The Toll of the Dead

The Toll of the Dead

Riarhin XII

Part I — The houses behind the line

Chapter 1 — The first harbour

Asta would not let her carry the sack.

It was a small sack. Riarhin had put her spare linen in it, the whetstone, a comb with three teeth missing, and a bundle of things which had begun as separate bundles and become one through damp. She had carried considerably heavier loads with considerably worse injuries. Asta stood on the quay with the sack in her hands and waited for her to stop explaining this.

“The other ship is there,” Riarhin said.

“I see it.”

“Thirty paces.”

“Then you won’t be waiting long.”

Asta gave the sack to Eyvind. He took it with the expression of a boy who had learned that agreement cost him less than being right.

The harbour smelt of fish heads and split pine. There were three ships at the outer quay, a row of boats drawn above the wrack, and a cooper’s shed with so many hoops hanging from its wall that Riarhin had mistaken them, coming in, for round windows. Between the shed and the tide stood a woman selling bread from beneath a sailcloth. A dog had put its head through the gap at the back. The woman struck it with a folded rag without interrupting her conversation.

Riarhin looked back at the Staðfast.

Hallbjorn was already giving orders about the freight. The men who had complained all morning of wanting solid ground were going ashore with loads and coming back for more. A wet patch on the quay grew beneath the salted hides. Orm knelt at the foot of the gangplank, trying to free a wedge which had been driven too hard into the timber. He had a hammer in his right hand and the left braced clear of the blow.

“Not that way,” Hallbjorn called.

Orm stopped, looked up, and hit it again.

Riarhin smiled. Asta was watching her.

“You can breathe properly?”

“Yes.”

“Then do it.”

She did. The old pain at her ribs had become a stiffness at the end of a full breath. Her thigh pulled if she turned on the planted foot. Both were improving. She had demonstrated this several times, which had only made Asta more particular about the circumstances in which she was willing to believe it.

“No lifting across yourself. If you have to turn, move your feet. If it hurts tomorrow more than today, stop whatever you were pleased with yourself for doing.”

“That’s a broad instruction.”

“You’ll find something.”

Eyvind came back without the sack.

“He wants to know if this is everything.”

“It is.”

“He says there’s room under the forward boards, but she can’t have the place beside the stove because that’s where they put the pans.”

“Tell him I shall try not to disappoint the pans.”

Eyvind looked at Asta, who waved him away.

“Go. He’ll understand.”

He went. Riarhin watched his feet on the planks, the small easy corrections his body made without his attending to them. When she had first come aboard, she had noticed the boy trying not to look at her injuries. Now he had opinions about where her belongings should go.

Asta took a little packet from inside her coat.

“Clean linen. It isn’t a gift. You’ve had all my good pieces and I want these back if you come this way.”

Riarhin took it.

“Thank you.”

“And eat before you sail. Not just something hard you’ve found in your pocket.”

“There is bread.”

“I have eyes.”

For a moment neither moved. Riarhin could hear the hammer again, and the creak of a cart making its way through the upper street. Asta put a hand on her shoulder, squeezed once, and turned towards the ship.

Riarhin caught her sleeve.

The old woman looked back. Riarhin kissed her cheek. It was warm, dry, and smelt faintly of the soap she used for everything, including people who complained that it took their skin off.

“Oh,” Asta said.

Then she pulled her sleeve free and went up the gangplank, telling Orm he was going to hit his foot.

Arngrim had a narrow face and a beard which seemed to have been cut by someone angry with him. He stood beside a pile of salt barrels checking the marks on their ends. The man who had brought them was explaining that a particular mark had always meant something else.

“On whose barrels?” Arngrim asked.

“On any barrels.”

“Then you’ll find a buyer easily.”

The man looked at Riarhin, perhaps hoping for a less difficult person, and looked away again.

Arngrim finished checking before he spoke to her.

“Sigrun says you keep a watch.”

“I do.”

“And won’t fall in.”

“I haven’t lately.”

He looked her over without lingering on the sword. His gaze stopped at the leg, then moved to the place where Eyvind had left her sack beneath the low rail.

“Passage to Payden’s. Food with us. You take the second watch when we’re under way, and help where Haldren says you won’t break anything. Twelve copper now.”

“Sigrun said ten.”

“Sigrun isn’t feeding you.”

“She has been.”

“And speaks warmly of your appetite.”

Riarhin took out the coins. He counted them into his palm, returned two, and nodded towards the bread stall.

“Bring your own dinner.”

She put the returned coins away. Behind Arngrim, a man had begun moving a barrel by working its edge along the planks. He was broad through the shoulders, grey at the temples, and wore a woollen cap folded over one ear. A wooden pin protruded from the corner of his mouth.

“Haldren,” Arngrim said. “This is her.”

The man put the barrel down and took the pin out.

“The one with the leg?”

“I have two,” Riarhin said.

“Then keep the good one off my hatch. It’s drying.”

He pointed with the pin. A board had been laid flat over two blocks, its pale repaired edge shining with fresh oil.

“The forward place is yours. If you hear knocking underneath, it’s the water. If you hear knocking above, it’s somebody wanting you to move.”

“And if it’s neither?”

“Wake Arngrim.”

“Wake him in any case,” Arngrim said. “He wakes quickly.”

Haldren gave him a look, put the pin back in his mouth, and returned to the barrel.

She could see where she would sleep: a narrow space beyond the stowed sail, dry for the present, with a rib of the hull crossing it at an inconvenient height. She measured it with her eyes and turned her sack on its side. It would do. She had a sudden, unreasonable dislike of the place.

“There’s a board you can lift at your head,” Haldren said. “For things that want keeping dry.”

“Thank you.”

“Not that one. The little one.”

She moved her hand.

“Yes. Unless you want to sleep with the onions.”

Temür had found a place to sit on the harbour wall where he could see both the road and the ships. The bone case stood between his boots. A woman with a pack pony had stopped nearby and was disputing the price of a repaired shoe with a man who had a horse’s foot between his knees.

“You’ve found your road,” Riarhin said.

“Her road.” He nodded at the woman. “She goes as far as the moor crossing. After that I know where I am.”

The woman said something forceful enough to turn two heads at the bread stall. Temür listened with appreciation.

“She knows what she’ll pay,” he said.

“Does the smith?”

“He’s learning.”

Riarhin sat beside him. She had bought a loaf still warm at its centre and a small smoked fish. Temür produced a piece of cheese from a cloth with a hole in it. The cheese had picked up lint. He scraped it with his thumb and handed it to her.

She divided the bread. He watched the unequal pieces.

“That’s yours?”

“I bought it.”

“I brought cheese.”

“You brought most of a cloth.”

He took the larger piece from her hand. She let him.

For a while they ate without talking. Below them the tide made a soft sucking sound between the stones. A boy was attempting to float a broken bucket, pushing it out with a stick and pulling it back whenever it threatened to demonstrate that it was broken. The dog from the bread stall had followed Riarhin. It sat at a respectful distance from Temür’s knee and breathed through its mouth.

“No,” Temür told it.

The dog remained hopeful.

“Borte will want the whole account,” Riarhin said.

“She’ll want to tell me which bits she already knew.”

“Did she?”

“She dreamed. I was there. We shall have a disagreement about which tells you more.”

He ran a finger along the case’s lid. There was dirt in the join which he had not yet cleaned out. Riarhin could remember him holding it with both hands in the ship’s bow. She could remember the angle of every cut on the pieces inside. He had shown her them once more on the voyage, not because she needed to see but because he wanted someone beside him while he laid them out.

“Will you go home after that?”

He swallowed.

“For a while.”

She looked at the fish in her hands. There was a fine bone lying white against the dark flesh. She pulled it free.

“Good.”

He watched her, then reached over and took the other half of the fish.

“You can say you’d like me to come.”

“I would.”

“I said I’d go back.”

“I know.”

The words came too quickly. She put the bone on the wall and looked at him properly.

“I would like you to come. I hope the moor road is dry and that she has something better than this fish when you get there.”

He examined the fish as though the possibility had not occurred to him.

“It’s good fish.”

“It was good before you took it.”

He grinned, and she found that she wanted to lean against him. Instead she sat with her shoulder an inch from his, close enough to feel him move as he ate. The dog laid its head on his knee. Temür looked down in disbelief at the audacity of it.

“It thinks you meant later,” Riarhin said.

“I meant no.”

“Explain.”

He gave the dog the end of the skin.

The woman with the pony won her argument by paying the smith and leaving while he was still explaining why the amount was wrong. Temür got down from the wall. He adjusted his pack, tested the case’s strap, and picked up his bow.

Riarhin stood more slowly. He saw her do it and waited without offering a hand.

“Ask at the east crossing,” he said. “If you come. They’ll know which road.”

“Will they?”

“They’ll know hers.”

She nodded. He had a crumb caught in the rough cloth at his throat. She picked it off before she could decide whether to. He watched her hand.

Then he put an arm round her.

She leaned into him carefully, her face against the side of his neck, and for several breaths neither of them tried to make it easier by speaking. He was warm from sitting in the sun. She knew the smell of his coat. When he let go she wanted to hold him again, which annoyed her enough to make her smile.

“Go,” she said. “She’s going to leave you.”

“She won’t. I have her meal sack.”

He showed her the second bundle behind his pack, which she had taken for more of his belongings.

“A sound arrangement.”

“I thought so.”

He walked away along the quay, caught the woman’s eye, and received a gesture which required no translation. At the corner he turned. Riarhin lifted her hand. He lifted his, and the buildings hid him.

The dog had gone with him.

Orm was still at the gangplank when she returned, but the wedge was out. He had it in one hand and was showing the damaged end to Hallbjorn, who appeared unmoved by the evidence.

“Leaving?” Orm asked.

“Yes.”

“Then hold that.”

She took the gangplank’s side while he put a new packing piece beneath it. He struck the piece home, tested the plank with his weight, and bent to look underneath.

“Now he’ll say it was the timber.”

“It was the timber,” Hallbjorn said from the deck.

Orm looked up at Riarhin.

“You hear what I bear.”

She set down her end. He wiped his hand on his breeches and held it out. She took it, then pulled him towards her and kissed the side of his head.

He went still.

“That’s ruined the verse,” he said.

“You’ll manage.”

“I might need another.”

“For the verse?”

“For accuracy.”

She laughed and let go. His face had coloured beneath the weathering. Hallbjorn called his name, this time with work in it, and he picked up the hammer.

Sigrun stood at the rail. Riarhin thanked her. The skipper nodded towards Arngrim’s ship.

“He’ll say three days. If you get four, thank the wind. If you get six, ask what he forgot to load.”

“And if I get three?”

“Keep your belongings where you can reach them. He’ll be leaving again.”

They clasped forearms. No one rang anything. Eyvind waved from behind a hide which was too big for him, and Riarhin waved back before the hide took his attention.

Arngrim was ready to cast off. Haldren held out a hand from the other deck. She took it and stepped aboard without turning on her bad leg.

“Better,” he said.

She looked at him.

“The plank. They fixed it.”

She put her remaining bread beside her sack and went aft to learn which line Arngrim wanted first.

That night she hit her head on the rib of the hull.

She had known it was there. She had arranged the folded blanket to put her shoulder below it and had then, half asleep, reached for something in the wrong place. The blow was sharp enough to bring tears to her eyes.

From farther aft Haldren said, “Little board.”

“I know.”

He chuckled. Someone told him to let people sleep.

The ship moved differently from the Staðfast. Its timbers had a higher complaint, and a barrel somewhere near her feet shifted against its chock whenever they rolled to port. She lay on her back with one hand against her forehead. Beyond the open hatch a square of stars tilted out of view and returned.

Her watch was not for another hour. She could hear Arngrim speaking quietly to the man at the steering oar, a word carried down, another lost in the water. She shifted until the sore place was clear of the blanket, shut her eyes, and waited for the new noises to become familiar.

Chapter 2 — The boat already tied up

On the fourth morning there was a bell in the fog.

Haldren had been telling Riarhin that the coast was close for the better part of an hour. He gave as evidence the taste of the wind, the colour of the water, and the behaviour of a bird she could not see. She had accepted the first two. The bird became a point of contention.

“It went in,” he said.

“Into what?”

“The fog.”

“Everything is in the fog.”

“Not everything knows where it’s going.”

He was sitting on a low chest with a piece of wood between his knees, shaving a thin curl from its edge. Each curl fell onto his left boot. When enough had accumulated he shook them into a bucket. Riarhin had been holding the spare piece against the joint he meant to mend. Her fingers were stiff with damp.

The bell sounded again, two clear notes with a pause between them.

“There,” Haldren said.

“I didn’t say there was no coast.”

“No. You doubted the bird.”

He took the wood from her and carried it aft. Arngrim was standing with one hand on the rail, looking into a whiteness which had begun to disclose patches of darker white. Riarhin could hear water striking something solid on their port side. Ahead, beyond the bell, a man shouted and another answered with a long wavering call.

A post appeared. It carried a hooded light, yellow behind horn. Another stood farther in. The posts were tall enough to remain above a high tide, black near the water and grey where weather had stripped them. Between them a raised bank crossed the view. Grass grew down its face in dark ribbons.

The ship slowed. Men moved to the lines. Riarhin took her place where Arngrim had shown her and waited for an order.

A small ferry lay beyond the nearer post, its bow held to a landing by a rope which trembled in the current. Four passengers sat inside. A fifth stood with a covered basket pressed against her chest. The ferryman had a long pole across the boat, one end in his hand, the other resting beneath a cleat.

Arngrim called to him.

The ferryman shook his head.

“The bell’s sounded,” Arngrim said.

“I heard it.”

“Then bring them across and clear the cut.”

“No.”

The ferry’s standing passenger looked from one man to the other. She had a yellow cloth tied over her hair. The basket moved against her coat; something inside made a small irritated sound.

Arngrim put both hands on the rail.

“Eddis. I need the outer berth before the turn.”

“So do several people.”

“Then move.”

The ferryman set the pole down. He was a long man, narrow everywhere except the hands, with a beard that grew sparsely along one side of his jaw. His cap had once been red. He looked past Arngrim towards the posts, counted something with a small movement of his lips, and picked up the pole again.

“Not yet.”

Haldren came forward with a coil of rope. Arngrim said something under his breath. Riarhin caught enough of it to know that Eddis had been impossible before the fog came in.

“Does he always do this?” she asked.

“Only when I’m here to hear him.”

From the landing beyond the ferry a woman called, “He was right yesterday.”

Eddis turned his head.

“Yesterday was yesterday.”

“And you were right.”

“Be quiet, will you?”

The woman remained beneath the landing roof, her face a pale patch above a dark shawl. Beside her stood a cart with one wheel lifted onto a block. Two men were trying to find something underneath it. One looked out at the ship and ducked back beneath the cart.

Arngrim ordered the sail taken in. The ship settled with a small sideways movement that brought the nearer post towards Riarhin’s hand. The line she held dragged across the wet rail.

“Not that post,” Eddis said.

Arngrim looked at him sharply.

“That’s the berth.”

“That’s where it ought to be.”

They backed water until the post was clear. It took longer than Arngrim liked. His ship was heavily laden, the current stronger below the still-looking surface, and the men had to work their oars without striking the ferry. Riarhin kept the line from fouling while Haldren leaned out to judge their distance.

When they were held off, Eddis brought the ferry to the landing on his own side and let the passengers out.

The woman with the basket began arguing immediately.

“I paid for the far side.”

“You’ll get it.”

“My sister’s waiting.”

“So am I.”

The basket gave a loud, indignant crow. Several people turned. The woman tightened her grip.

“Don’t you start,” she told it.

Haldren made a choking sound.

Arngrim sent him a look, then called to Eddis again. This time the ferryman raised a hand without answering. He tied the ferry properly, checked a ring in the landing timber, and walked out to the end where the ship could hear him without shouting.

“There’s one light too many.”

Riarhin looked. She could see three now, the last dimmer and low in the fog.

“How many should there be?” she asked.

“Two from here. Three from the grain stairs. You can see their tops from the high bank, but not their lamps.”

“Someone’s put one down,” Arngrim said.

“Then someone can go and pick it up.”

“Your boat is empty.”

“And stays empty.”

The woman beneath the roof came out. She walked to the end of the landing and looked at Riarhin.

“It’s her,” she said.

Arngrim shut his eyes briefly.

“What’s her?”

“The woman I told you.” She addressed Eddis as if the shipmaster had not spoken. “By the rope. There.”

Riarhin looked down at the rope in her hands.

“When?” she asked.

“This morning.”

“We’ve just come in.”

“Yes,” the woman said, impatiently. “Now. Before that you went out.”

One of the men beneath the cart crawled into view and sat on his heels. The ferry passengers had stopped arguing. Riarhin could feel Arngrim looking at her, not with suspicion but the exasperation of a man whose delay had found another way to prolong itself.

“What did you see?” she asked.

“This ship. Going out. You were there, by that rope, and a man was tying something on his wrist.”

Haldren glanced down. He had a strip of red wool round his right wrist. A splinter had scored the skin earlier in the morning; he had wrapped it to keep salt out.

“Lots of men tie things on their wrists,” he said.

“He dropped the end. You caught it.” The woman pointed at Riarhin. “With your left hand. You hadn’t put the rope down.”

Riarhin had done that. The strip had unrolled across the deck while Haldren held the first turn with his thumb, and she had caught it against the rail before it went over. They had still been outside the fog. No bank, no landing, no woman beneath a roof.

“What time?” she asked.

“Before the first ferry.”

“Had the bell sounded?”

“No. That’s why I thought it queer.”

“Did you speak to anyone?”

“I spoke to him.” She nodded at Eddis. “He said the salt boat wasn’t in.”

“It wasn’t,” Eddis said.

“Well, it was going out. I wouldn’t expect it to be in as well.”

The man by the cart laughed once, then looked embarrassed. The woman returned to the roof. Her shawl had begun dripping. She wrung one corner between her hands without looking at them.

Riarhin remembered the light when Haldren had cut himself: pale sunlight on the shavings, his annoyance at having to interrupt a job almost finished. She could place the ship’s movement and the words Arngrim had used from aft. It had happened. She had not imagined doing it because a woman told her she had.

Eddis watched her face.

“You know what she’s talking about,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Then leave the rope where it is.”

She let it rest along the rail.

Haldren fetched the sounding line. Its weight was smooth from use, with a little hollow in the bottom for tallow. He pressed fresh tallow into it, leaned over, and let the weight go.

Arngrim counted the marks as the line paid out.

“That can’t be right.”

“Come and feel it,” Haldren said.

Riarhin took the line from him. She could feel the weight touching something, lifting, touching again. They drifted a little and it dropped farther.

“A ledge?” she asked.

“Mud bank,” Eddis said from the landing. “Shouldn’t reach this far.”

When they hauled it in, the tallow carried black silt and a strand of pale root. Haldren held the weight under Arngrim’s nose. The skipper pushed his hand away.

“I can see.”

They sounded again off the other side. Deeper water. The current pulled the line slantwise beneath the ship. A fragment of reed floated past, stopped as though caught, and moved upstream along the hull.

Riarhin followed it with her eyes. Beyond it, the lowest lamp appeared to stand in water too deep for the post that carried it.

“Could that be a reflection?” she asked.

“Of what?” Eddis said.

She looked for an answering light above. There was none.

Arngrim called two men forward. They worked the ship away from the ordinary berth, along the face of the bank towards a set of heavier piles beneath a warehouse. Haldren and Riarhin kept sounding. This ground matched what Arngrim expected, though the current made them work for it. A shore hand came out with a line and stood waiting until Eddis called that the ship could come in.

“Glad one of us owns it,” Arngrim muttered.

The shore hand caught their rope. Riarhin took the strain, shifted her feet when her thigh objected, and paid out as Arngrim directed. Haldren drove a chock beneath the fender. The hull met the berth with a long grinding shudder.

Everyone stayed still for a moment.

Then a man aft said, “Are we here?”

“No,” Haldren said. “We went out this morning.”

No one laughed. He scratched beneath his cap and went to check the stern line.

The warehouse berth cost extra.

Arngrim learned this from a boy who arrived carrying a board with figures written on it. He disputed the charge, the boy’s authority to impose it, and the ancestry of whoever had sent him. The boy waited through all three objections and indicated the figure again.

“There was room over there,” he said.

Arngrim looked at Eddis.

The ferryman had begun testing his own crossing with the empty boat. He worked it out a few yards, held it, and brought it back. The woman with the cock sat on her basket. She had taken a piece of bread from her pocket and was eating it furiously.

“Pay him,” Haldren said. “You can complain indoors.”

“I am complaining here.”

“You’ve a gift.”

Arngrim paid.

Riarhin helped make the lines secure and waited for permission to go ashore. No one gave it. After a while she realised nobody thought she needed it. She fetched her sack, lifted it with both hands, and stood looking at the gap between deck and quay.

“Leave that,” Haldren said. “We’ll be here till the turn, perhaps longer. Have something hot.”

“Is the passage finished?”

“If you want to swim back, tell me first. I can sell your place.”

She left the sack.

There was a small shelter beside the warehouse where somebody had set a pot over charcoal. The woman tending it sold bowls of peas with onions and a thick heel of bread. Steam hung low beneath the roof. Riarhin sat on an upturned basket, the bowl warming her hands, and watched the harbour begin to emerge.

Behind the bank stood roofs of reed and dark tile. Some had stones tied along their eaves. A bell frame rose above a gate in the bank, its ironwork fitted into pale dressed blocks. Farther inland a second frame showed, then disappeared as the fog drew through. There were fields beyond the roofs: she could see a strip of fresh green, a cart moving along a raised way, the dark rectangle of ground recently turned.

She had expected more water. There was plenty of it, but the people had made places between. A woman in an apron came out of a low house, tipped a bucket into a drain, and leaned over her fence to exchange news with someone Riarhin could not see. Two children ran along the top of the bank. A man carrying a ladder shouted at them to get down.

Haldren joined her with his own bowl. He sat on a pile of rope, stretched out his legs, and blew across the peas.

“My wife makes this with bacon.”

“You should have brought her.”

“She’d have brought me. Different arrangement.”

“Does she travel?”

“To her sister’s. Comes home and says I’m a good man. Her sister’s husband has been useful to me all our married life.”

Riarhin laughed into the bowl. The peas were better than they looked. She ate slowly enough to enjoy the onions and still finished before him.

The bell sounded. Eddis brought his ferry out into the cut, tested the current, and this time continued across. The woman sat with the basket between her knees. At the far landing somebody waved both arms at her, then put their hands on their hips in a way that suggested the greeting had contained a complaint.

“There,” Haldren said. “Sister.”

“You know her?”

“I know the look.”

The fog lifted another few feet. Two lamps stood clear. The third was gone.

Riarhin set her empty bowl beside her boot and looked until her eyes began to hurt. Haldren followed her gaze. He wiped the bread round the bottom of his bowl.

“Don’t go looking for where it was,” he said.

“I was looking from here.”

“Best place.”

Eddis returned alone. He tied the ferry and came over for food. The woman beneath the landing roof had left while Riarhin was eating. Her cart was gone too.

“Who was she?” Riarhin asked.

“Which?”

“The one who saw us.”

“Lives beyond the third sluice. Comes in with eggs. Husband’s ill.”

“Does she often see things?”

“Everybody sees things.” He accepted a bowl from the woman at the pot. “She mostly sees eggs.”

Riarhin waited while he ate the first spoonful.

“Did anyone else see the ship?”

“Not that told me.”

“Can I speak to her again?”

“If you find her. Don’t go out after dark to try.”

His sleeve was wet to the elbow. There was a leather pouch tied close beneath his wrist, its drawstring worked through a little iron ring. She had seen such pouches on the people at the landing, all worn within easy reach. The boy who collected the berth fee had one hanging from his neck.

Eddis noticed her looking.

“You’ve got salt?”

“Cooking salt.”

“See Hesvar before you go inland.”

“I was going to see him.”

“Then do.”

He looked at her sword, the ears visible where she had tied her hair back, and the bowl beside her foot. She was accustomed to people making something of that collection. Eddis merely shifted his own bowl to the other hand and sat down on the landing step.

Behind them Haldren was trying to persuade the food seller that a second serving would be cheaper because he would use the same bowl. She refused. He said his wife always gave him more. She told him to go home.

Eddis laughed. After a moment Haldren did too, and paid.

Chapter 3 — The sacks under the stairs

Hesvar’s shop was where Coryd’s letter had said it would be, though the harbour steps had been rebuilt on the other side of it.

Riarhin found the old steps first. They led from a narrow lane to a wall. Someone had written a direction on the wall, which someone else had corrected, and a third hand had added a remark about the person responsible for the new harbour. She followed the correction. The lane brought her past two houses, a yard full of cut reeds, and the back of a shop whose open door smelt of hemp and oil.

A man inside was biting a length of cord.

“Front,” he said round it.

“I’m looking for Hesvar.”

He took the cord from his mouth.

“Still front.”

She went round. By the time she reached the proper door he had cut the cord and was wrapping it about a bundle of iron hooks. He was square, almost hairless, and had a habit of pushing his lower lip out before saying anything he expected to be unwelcome.

“I don’t sell from the back. People forget they’ve bought things.”

“I’m not buying.”

“That happens too.”

She gave him Coryd’s letter. He looked at the outside, then at her, then moved a tray of pins off a stool.

“Sit.”

She sat. The shop was narrow but went back farther than she had expected. Loops of line hung from pegs. There were candles of several colours, small lanterns, broad-ended nails, rolls of canvas, and a shelf of leather pouches beneath a board marked with prices. A curtain divided the rear space from the front. Behind it somebody sneezed.

Hesvar read the letter once, pursed his lips, and read the last portion again.

“She writes a small hand.”

“She has a great deal to say.”

“Family failing?”

“Not universally.”

He folded the letter and gave it back.

“Kelva is at the mill. Sivrik’s here somewhere, or gone back. He said he was going back, which means I haven’t seen him since.”

“Could you tell me the way?”

“I can take you. I have fittings for her.”

“Today?”

“If I don’t go today she’ll come tomorrow, and I won’t enjoy it.”

He called through the curtain. A woman’s voice told him she had heard everything and that the fittings were already on the cart.

Hesvar looked mildly injured by this efficiency. He turned to the shelf and took down a pouch.

“Eddis send you?”

“Yes.”

“You haven’t got one.”

“No.”

He opened the pouch, showed her coarse white grains and the dark stamped bead caught on its cord, then closed it again.

“Keep it dry. Don’t empty it into food. If somebody tells you it needs replacing every time the bell rings, they’re selling salt.”

“What does it cost?”

He named a sum she did not like. She paid it. He gave her two spare lengths of cord and pushed them across the counter without comment.

“Does everybody buy from you?”

“Enough do.”

“Where does it come from?”

“Pans south of the port. Blessed and sealed at the yard. I buy it sealed.”

He tapped the bead. She saw a little mark pressed into it, almost obscured by the cord.

“Don’t break that unless you need the salt.”

“And when I do?”

“Use it. People have died keeping the stamp pretty.”

Behind the curtain the woman said, “You might tell her where.”

“At the opening,” he said. “Door, window, place you mean to keep shut. Kelva will show you. She knows more than I do.”

He collected his cap, put it on, took it off to look for something beneath it, and discovered the thing in his other hand. Riarhin rose to help him close the shop.

“Leave that,” the woman called. “He closes it like that every day and I have to do it again.”

Hesvar gave Riarhin a wooden hook to carry. They left by the back.

The cart was drawn by a dun pony with a black stripe down its spine. Hesvar called it Captain, not fondly. It ignored him until he took the reins in both hands and moved its head. Then it set off with an expression of having thought of departure itself.

Riarhin walked beside the cart. The road followed a raised bank between cultivated strips, some green with young grain, others dark and ridged, with narrow watercourses gleaming between them. A woman stood ankle-deep beside a sluice lifting tangled roots from its grate. Farther out, two men were working a plough behind a pair of short-legged oxen. Their calls reached the road clearly through the damp air.

“You can ride,” Hesvar said.

“I’ve been sitting for days.”

“You’ll be walking for an hour.”

“Then I’ll sit.”

He accepted this. The cart wheels found each rut individually. A sack beneath the fittings rattled. Riarhin looked at the contents when the cloth shifted: little rods of dark metal, cut bone or horn, a coil of wire, and a flat wooden frame with holes bored along its edge.

“For the mill?”

“For what she’s putting round it.”

“Nereth?”

“You’ve been told.”

“Not much.”

“Then Kelva can tell you the rest.”

He said it with sufficient decision that she left the subject alone for a while.

The road crossed a drainage cut on a stone-backed wooden bridge. At each end stood a short post carrying an iron plate. Between them, above the cart’s height, a line bore small bells at unequal intervals. Captain slowed beneath them. Hesvar flicked a rein against his side.

“Nothing there.”

The pony walked on. Riarhin heard a thin note travel along the line, too high and brief for the cart to have made. She looked back. The woman at the sluice had straightened and was looking in the same direction.

Beyond the bridge stood a board painted with a sheaf and a mountain. Beneath it, in two scripts, were directions about loads and the maintenance of the crossing. Fresh marks had been added beside the delivery dates.

“How far does the line go?” she asked.

“Far enough if it’s kept.”

“And if it isn’t?”

Hesvar looked at the fields rather than her.

“The lower lanes first. People move up. Then the high road gets crowded.”

“Is that happening?”

He clicked at Captain.

“The mill’s beyond the old span. You’ll see.”

She did. The next bell frame stood empty on one side. The fixing was there, a hook of blackened iron beneath a little roof, but the bell itself was missing. A piece of red cloth had been tied around the post. The road beside it had been swept, though the grass beyond grew through abandoned wheel tracks. A gate stood open onto a yard where nettles had begun under the sill of a house.

“Whose?” she asked.

“Her aunt’s. They’re up by the grain yard now.”

“Nereth’s aunt?”

“No. The woman at the sluice.”

Riarhin looked back. She could no longer see the woman, only the bend of the bank and Captain’s wet hoofprints behind the cart.

The mill announced itself with a sound like a large creature clearing its throat.

It stood on the inner side of a cut, its lower walls dark with damp, its roof patched in three different materials. The wheel turned slowly beneath a wooden shelter. Water fell through its paddles in white threads and struck the lower pool with an uneven slap. A dog lay on the warmest part of the threshold, apparently unconcerned that both cart and visitors would need it.

Kelva came out carrying a floury cloth. She looked at Hesvar, the cart, and Riarhin in that order.

“You’ve brought the wrong frame.”

“I’ve brought what you asked.”

“I asked for one she could carry.”

“She can carry that.”

“Then you carry it to the back and see how willing you are.”

Hesvar got down from the cart. Kelva watched him long enough to be sure he was doing it, then turned to Riarhin.

“Coryd’s?”

“Riarhin.”

“I know your name. Come in.”

She was taller than Hesvar and narrower, with a long face, strong forearms, and a streak of flour at her temple where she had pushed her hair back. Her apron had been mended with blue cloth which did not match anything else she wore. She moved the dog with her foot, not gently but without disturbing its conviction that it belonged there.

The kitchen occupied the end of the building farthest from the wheel. Riarhin could feel the machinery through the floor. Cups on a shelf trembled very slightly each time the wheel caught its load. There was a fire banked beneath a pot, wet stockings on a line, and grain sacks piled under the stairs so high that the lowest steps could only be reached sideways.

Kelva pointed at the wash bowl.

“You can get the road off. Water’s cold unless you want to wait.”

Riarhin washed. The cold bit into her fingers pleasantly after the damp warmth of the walk. A child of perhaps eleven came down the stairs, stepped over a sack, slipped, caught the banister, and landed with a thump.

“Not like that,” Kelva said.

“It moved.”

“It has moved every time you’ve done it.”

The child looked at Riarhin, looked at the sword, and went out with a jug.

“Neighbours’ girl,” Kelva said. “She comes when her mother’s on the lower watch. Eats like three.”

“Does she have a name?”

“She has one her mother uses and six she answers to when she feels like it.”

Kelva took a loaf from a cloth. Riarhin waited. After a moment the woman laughed at herself.

“Tessa. I expect you’ll hear it enough.”

She cut bread, put cheese beside it, and poured small ale. Only when Riarhin had taken a mouthful did she ask for the letter.

Sivrik arrived while Kelva was reading. He stopped at the threshold, took in the stranger, and gave his sister a look which Riarhin recognised even before he spoke.

“You might have told me.”

“I told Hesvar.”

“That would help if I were Hesvar.”

“You’d be late with fittings instead of late with everything else.”

He stepped over the dog. He was a little younger than Kelva, or looked it because his hair had kept its colour. His hands were scarred across the knuckles and ingrained with black soil. Water darkened his breeches below the knee. He had a bundle of papers wrapped in waxed cloth beneath one arm.

“Riarhin,” he said. “Coryd writes about you.”

“She writes about you.”

“Nothing good, I hope. I should hate to disappoint.”

He held out his hand. She took it. His grip was careful, as if several fingers hurt.

Kelva folded the letter.

“She asks you to look at the girl.”

“To speak to her.”

“People say look when they mean speak and speak when they mean take away.”

“I haven’t come to take her away.”

“Good.”

Sivrik set his bundle on the table.

“Now ask whether she’s had dinner before you frighten her back onto the boat.”

“She is having dinner.”

“I meant Nereth.”

Kelva turned to the pot. Her face changed as she looked into it, a quick withdrawal Riarhin might not have noticed if she had been watching Sivrik.

“She hasn’t been in.”

“How long?”

“Three days.”

“She was at the lower frames yesterday.”

“Then yesterday she could have eaten.”

Sivrik took the lid from a crock and sniffed it. Kelva struck his wrist with the cloth.

“That is hers.”

“I was looking.”

“Look with your eyes.”

Hesvar came in carrying the frame. He put it against the wall and stood breathing a little harder than he wanted to.

“It is heavy,” he conceded.

Kelva handed him a cup.

“Sit.”

For a few minutes they ate. The wheel sounded steadily through the walls. Tessa returned with the jug full and an account of somebody’s hen which had got into somebody else’s bed. Hesvar wanted to know whether there had been anyone in it. Kelva told him not to encourage her. Tessa supplied the answer with relish.

Riarhin found herself smiling. She had been prepared for frightened cousins, a dangerous young woman, a road with the dead on it. Nobody in Coryd’s letter had mentioned the hen.

After Hesvar left, Sivrik opened his papers.

“I need these moved by tomorrow.” He pointed at the sacks beneath the stairs.

“They’re dry.”

“They’re due.”

“They were due with a working line.”

“We have a working line.”

“Yes,” Kelva said. “We do. And I know who put it there.”

He lowered his voice, not enough to keep Riarhin from hearing.

“I took the advance.”

“You spent it on the sluice.”

“It was still an advance.”

“You should have let them drown their own road.”

“And your ground with it?”

The dog lifted its head. Tessa left by the back door with the empty cups, though there was no washing place outside.

Sivrik flattened the paper with his palm. A broad dark thumbprint had appeared at one corner. He looked at it, rubbed it with the side of his hand, and made it worse.

Riarhin put her bread down. She had not meant to stop eating, but both of them noticed.

“It’s nothing to do with you,” Kelva said.

“I know.”

“He’s promised grain we haven’t threshed yet to men who took down our bell.”

“They took it for a new tongue. You know that.”

“It’s been gone since the frost.”

“They didn’t break the old one on purpose.”

“They didn’t hurry to mend it.”

Sivrik stood. His knee struck the table, tipping Riarhin’s cup. She caught it before much spilled. The ale spread beneath his paper.

“Oh, for—”

He lifted the sheets. Kelva fetched another cloth. For several breaths they worked together in angry silence, blotting the table, separating pages, finding somewhere flat to put them.

“I’ll help move the sacks,” Riarhin said when the papers were safe.

Kelva looked at her.

“To make room for my bed. If that’s where I’m sleeping.”

Her host looked towards the stairs as if remembering what stood beneath them.

“You’re in the little room above. There are blankets. We can put three sacks by the back wall.”

“Not that wall,” Sivrik said. “It sweats.”

He took the smaller sack before anyone could answer and carried it across the kitchen to a pallet near the hearth. Kelva watched him set it down, then pointed to the next one.

“That on top.”

Riarhin carried the empty baskets and moved the bench. She let Sivrik lift the grain. Kelva noticed this and said nothing, which she appreciated until she realised the woman had also noticed her appreciation.

The little room was barely wider than its bed. It contained a stool, a shelf, and a window looking across the garden towards a bend in the lane. The bedding smelt of dried herbs and somebody else’s soap. Riarhin set her sack on the stool and laid Remorse along the wall where she could reach it. Toothpick remained with her. The shortened Last Blade she put beside the sword.

Below, the siblings had begun speaking more quietly. She could not make out the words over the mill. She did not move closer to the stair.

The garden gate caught on something when Kelva opened it. Riarhin looked out. Her host knelt, lifted a little wooden piece clear of the lower hinge, and hung it from a peg. A thread passed from the piece to a post beyond the fence. It was almost invisible until the light caught it. There were others, running low along the garden’s boundary, held clear of the wet ground by shaped supports.

Kelva stood and looked up.

“You can see her work from there.”

“I was looking.”

“She’ll come to check it. She always does.”

“Tonight?”

“Morning, likely.”

Kelva turned towards the lane. Beyond the bend, a man had stopped beside an empty cart. He stood too far away for Riarhin to see his face. There was something odd about the way his arm lay along the shaft, the hand hanging below rather than gripping it.

Kelva closed the gate. She crouched to replace the wooden piece and waited until its thread lay still.

When she looked up again, her face was composed, though she had flour on one cheek where she had rubbed it.

“Come down while it’s warm,” she said. “There’s enough.”

Chapter 4 — Until morning

Kelva put out three bowls and left a fourth beside the pot.

Sivrik had gone back to his own house before dusk, taking the papers in a fresh cloth. Tessa’s mother had collected her from the mill yard. The dog occupied the place where either of them might have put a foot. Riarhin ate with Kelva and an old man who came in from the working room, washed his hands up to the elbows, and introduced himself as the one who did the work.

“He turns the wheel on when I tell him,” Kelva said.

“And off when she remembers.”

He was her mother’s cousin, had lived at the mill through three owners, and was having trouble with the thumb on his right hand. He showed Riarhin the joint without being asked. She looked at it, because he plainly wanted someone to, and said she had seen worse.

“So have I. They were on other people.”

Kelva gave him more stew. He held the bowl close to his face while eating, partly to see it and partly, Riarhin thought, to prevent anyone putting anything else in before he had finished. His name was Pell. She had no difficulty remembering it. Kelva called it often enough while trying to get him to bring a chair nearer the fire.

“I’m not cold.”

“You’re wet.”

“Then moving won’t mend it.”

He eventually moved. Riarhin made room for his feet. There were onions in the stew, soft barley, a little bacon cut so finely that each piece had to be searched for. She took another helping when Kelva offered it.

The wheel slowed after supper. Pell went through to close the feed, and the floor ceased its low vibration. For a little while the kitchen felt as though something had been taken from beneath it. Riarhin heard the garden: water dropping from the fence, a bird moving in the hedge, the scrape of Kelva’s shoe as she went out to check the gate.

A bell sounded far away. Another answered nearer the port. The notes did not repeat at even intervals. She listened until she could distinguish three separate distances, then stopped trying to make a pattern of them.

Pell came back carrying a small lamp.

“Windows,” he said.

Kelva set the shutters. Riarhin helped with the one above the sink, which had swollen in its frame. The wood gave suddenly beneath her hand and struck the wall.

“Sorry.”

“That one always does.”

Kelva slid the bar through. Above it hung a little strip of worked horn marked with close scratches. Riarhin had thought it a mended fastening until Kelva ran one finger beneath it to clear a spider’s thread.

“Part of it?”

“Yes.”

“Can I look?”

“In the morning. Don’t take it down.”

Riarhin put her hand back by her side.

The first sound came while Kelva was washing the bowls.

It was a slow dragging scrape beyond the garden. Pell lifted his head. Kelva continued washing, but the bowl stopped turning in her hands.

Riarhin stood.

“Sit down,” Kelva said.

“Who is it?”

“I don’t know yet.”

The scrape stopped. Something touched the outside of the gate: a knock without the quick withdrawal of a living hand. The wood gave another small sound under continued pressure.

Riarhin moved to the inner door. Kelva put the bowl down.

“Not that either.”

“I only want to see.”

“Through the little shutter. Up there.”

A square opening stood above the latch, covered on the inside by a sliding piece of wood. Riarhin lifted it a finger’s width. The garden was darker than the kitchen, but she could see the white edge of a path, the gate, the figure beyond it.

The man from the lane had come closer. His coat hung wrong from one shoulder. One sleeve was empty below the elbow; the other arm rested against the upper rail. His head lay at an angle which might have been fatigue if he had breathed.

She waited. Nothing moved beneath the open coat.

He leaned on the gate. The lower thread drew straight, catching a faint light from somewhere she could not locate. The man stopped leaning. His hand slid along the wood, slowly, until it reached the post. There it halted again.

Behind Riarhin, Pell cleared his throat.

“Which?”

“The one from the bend,” Kelva said.

“You’ve seen him before?” Riarhin asked.

“He comes some evenings.”

“Did you know him?”

“No.”

The man’s hand descended along the post. It stopped at the height of the thread. His fingers moved, not searching, exactly; they bent and opened against the air, as though whatever he expected to find had been moved a little beyond reach.

Riarhin closed the small shutter.

Kelva had picked up the bowl again.

“What do you do?” Riarhin asked.

“Finish these.”

“And then?”

“Go to bed.”

Pell put a split log on the fire. A spark caught the hair on his wrist. He brushed it away with a word Kelva pretended not to hear.

Riarhin remained standing. She could hear the pressure at the gate, a faint complaint from the lower hinge. Her left hand had gone to the place where Toothpick sat. She took it away.

“Has it ever failed?”

“Not here.”

“How long?”

Kelva dried the bowl and set it beside the others.

“Long enough for me to want my evening.”

Riarhin looked at her. The woman was tired. Not frightened in the way Riarhin had expected; tired of a question whose answer would make her watch the gate again. There were little cracks in the skin beside her nails. She had spent the day working while a stranger considered whether her home was safe.

“May I dry?” Riarhin asked.

Kelva handed her the cloth.

They sat by the fire afterwards. Pell fell asleep in his chair with his mouth open. Kelva mended a cuff. Riarhin worked at a loose stitch on her own coat, trying not to pull it so tight that the cloth gathered. The gate made no further sound for almost an hour.

The next noise came from the side fence.

Riarhin’s needle stopped. Kelva looked up just long enough to show that she had heard too, then put her head down over the cuff. A stick pressed against the paling, released, pressed again. The dog opened one eye and closed it.

“Does it bother him?” Riarhin asked quietly.

“He used to bark all night.”

“And now?”

“You can see.”

Pell snorted awake.

“What?”

“The dog,” Kelva said.

“What about him?”

“Go back to sleep.”

“I wasn’t.”

He sat up to prove it and began rubbing his thumb. After a while he told Riarhin about a millstone which had cracked when Kelva was a girl, and the man who had insisted he could repair it. The story involved three attempts, a borrowed cart, and an injury to the man’s pride which Pell still found satisfying. Kelva corrected the size of the crack. Pell said she had been too small to see it.

“I stood on the sacks.”

“You were always where you shouldn’t be.”

“And that’s how I saw it.”

Riarhin laughed. She had almost finished the stitch. Beyond the wall something brushed the shutter, so softly that neither of the others appeared to hear it. She pressed the needle through, drew the thread, and tied it off.

She did not sleep at once.

The little room was warm from the chimney beside it. She had arranged her things, tested the shutter, and laid the sword where she could take it without knocking the stool over. Her body was pleased with the mattress. Her mind kept returning to the hand against the gate.

Below, Kelva moved about for a while and then went quiet. Pell coughed once in the far room. Water passed beneath the stopped wheel, a low rush broken by drips from the paddles. Somewhere along the lane a bell gave a single soft note.

Riarhin got up.

She opened the little window shutter no wider than she needed. The garden lay silver-grey in the thinning fog. At the fence stood two figures. The man from earlier had moved to the corner. The other was shorter and difficult to distinguish from the hedge until it shifted its weight. Neither looked towards her.

She could have gone down, taken the lamp, studied the fittings. She wanted to. Instead she sat on the stool and watched the thread where it passed around the nearest support. A bead of water travelled along it until it reached the wood. It hung there, gathered another, and fell.

The shorter figure moved left. At the gate it stopped. The man stood as he had stood before. Time passed, measured by nothing more useful than the changing stiffness in her leg.

A gust brought the smell of the lower water through the opening. She shut it, went back to bed, and lay with her hand on the blanket.

When she woke there was sunlight on the wall.

For a moment she was angry. She had meant to hear if anything changed. The sunlight was already high enough to reach the shelf. From below came the sound of Kelva arguing with someone about an egg.

“You can’t tell until you open it.”

“You can tell that one.”

“It rolled under something.”

“It has hair.”

Riarhin put a hand over her eyes. Then she began to laugh.

Nereth stood in the kitchen holding an egg between two fingers. She was young enough that Riarhin’s first impression was of someone dressed in an older person’s clothes: long coat, sleeves turned back several times, a belt carrying pouches and a little case for narrow tools. The impression lasted until she spoke.

“If you want to keep it, keep it. Just don’t put it in mine.”

Kelva took the egg from her and dropped it into a crock by the door.

“I was going to give it to the dog.”

“The dog has done nothing to you.”

Nereth turned. She saw Riarhin, then looked at Kelva.

“You said she was asleep.”

“She was.”

“You could have said who.”

“I did. You were counting the spoons.”

“One was missing.”

“Pell had it.”

Pell, at the table, raised the spoon without looking up from his bowl.

Nereth’s hair had been braided badly and was coming loose above both ears. There was ink on the side of her hand and a mark beneath her jaw where something had rubbed. Her boots were wet. She had the pallor of someone who had spent too much of the morning doing work before eating.

“Riarhin,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Coryd wrote.”

“To me.”

“I know. Kelva told me.”

“I told you someone was coming,” Kelva said.

“You said Coryd’s sister. There aren’t that many.”

“There are enough.”

Riarhin took the place Kelva indicated. Nereth watched her sit, perhaps expecting a more elaborate arrival. Then she accepted a bowl and began eating too quickly.

“Bread,” Kelva said, putting it beside her.

“I see it.”

“Then use it.”

Nereth took a piece. For several minutes the most dangerous thing she did was drip stew onto her notes.

After breakfast they went into the garden.

Nereth brought the frame Hesvar had delivered and stood it against the wall. She tried to lift it over the water barrel, failed to clear the edge, and swore.

“He said you could carry it,” Kelva said from the doorway.

“He’s right. I can’t carry it through a wall.”

Riarhin took the other end. Together they moved it past the barrel. Nereth’s breath came out sharply with relief, and she gave Riarhin a quick, embarrassed smile.

“Thank you.”

“What is it for?”

“Spacing. If I have to measure each one again I’ll still be here at supper.”

She set it by a length of the lower thread and began checking the positions of the supports against its holes. The work was precise and physical. A support had sunk in the wet ground. She pulled it free, packed gravel beneath it, and drove it back with the butt of a small mallet. Riarhin held the frame steady.

“You had two here last night,” she said.

“I know. Kelva told me.”

“They couldn’t pass.”

“No.”

“Does it need doing every morning?”

“Looking at. Not remaking. Water moves the ground. Pell catches it with the barrow. The dog tries to get underneath, which isn’t necessary because the gate opens, but he has strong views.”

The dog stood in the doorway, listening to his name.

Nereth adjusted the thread with a tool shaped like a blunt needle. A faint tension passed through it. Riarhin felt the frame move in her hands, though the girl had not touched it.

“What happens if somebody breaks one?”

“Kelva knows how to join it. If a support comes out, she has the holes marked. If the horn cracks, she has a spare.”

“And if something happens she doesn’t know?”

“She sends for me.”

Nereth looked up. Her eyes were grey and direct.

“I don’t tell her it will do everything. Ask her.”

“I wasn’t accusing you.”

“People mostly don’t at first.”

She went back to the thread. Riarhin held the frame until the girl was ready to move it. When they had finished that section, Nereth sat on the barrel’s rim and flexed her fingers.

“You slept?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

The pleasure in it was plain. She looked towards the kitchen, where Kelva had begun singing under her breath while she worked.

“She used to sleep in her chair,” Nereth said. “So she’d be by the door. She said the bed hurt her back.”

Riarhin remembered the dry bedding above the kitchen, the warmth of the chimney. She set the frame down carefully.

“How did you learn?”

Nereth’s face closed a little.

“I read.”

“What?”

“A book.”

She got off the barrel.

“Can you hold the gate while I mend the hinge? It catches every time.”

Sivrik came along the lane with a barrow while they were finishing. He looked at the new gravel, the frame, and the girl’s wet boots.

“You’ve been at the lower bend.”

“Yes.”

“How many?”

“I didn’t count.”

“You should.”

“I was keeping them off the road.”

“Which part?”

Nereth put the mallet in her belt with more force than it needed.

“Your sister’s part. Is that all right?”

He looked towards the house. Kelva had stopped singing.

“I meant the bend.”

“I know what you meant.”

She took the frame and carried it to the wall, this time without asking for help. It struck the barrel. She paused, manoeuvred it round, and stood it exactly where it would not fall.

Riarhin waited until the girl had gone in before speaking.

“Show me.”

Sivrik gripped the barrow handles.

“I need to take these down.”

“I’ll walk with you.”

He nodded.

The lower bend lay beyond the empty house and the disused tracks. In daylight the road looked ordinary. Reeds grew close to the drainage cut. On the far side, beyond a low fence, the ground dipped towards a strip which had not been sown.

There were five figures standing in it.

Riarhin stopped beside the barrow. One was the man from Kelva’s gate. She knew the empty sleeve. Another wore something pale that had once been tied at the waist. All stood on the lower side of a thin line running from post to post beside the road.

The nearest moved when Sivrik pushed the barrow forward. Its head turned. Riarhin could not see eyes beneath the collapsed brim of its hat, but the movement followed them.

“They were farther down last week,” Sivrik said.

“Is there a house beyond?”

“Two. One empty. One isn’t.”

“Are they protected?”

“Old line. The bell hangs. Most nights it rings.”

He resumed walking. The barrow wheel squealed. Across the fence, the nearest figure took a step parallel to them and stopped where the ground rose.

Riarhin walked on with him. At the next turn she looked back. The five remained in the uncultivated strip. Beyond them, where the fog still lay low, smoke rose from a chimney.

Chapter 5 — A song with an answer

The dead man’s daughter had brought the wrong coat.

She stood in the chapel doorway with it over her arm while her brother insisted that their father had worn the other one to every occasion worth remembering. She said he had worn this one every day. Her brother said that was hardly the point. Behind them, a woman with a face like a shut drawer looked at the covered bell and waited for someone to be sensible.

Riarhin had come to return a bag of nails.

Sivrik had left them in the barrow when he went to look at the lower bridge, then remembered that the chapel keeper needed them before the afternoon. He had asked Riarhin if she minded taking them up. She did not. The chapel stood on raised ground beside a narrow causeway, its walls built from stone below and timber above, with a roof so thickly thatched that grass had begun growing on the shaded side. No graves surrounded it. A path went out beyond its bell-posts into mist and reeds. Two long carrying poles rested beneath the eaves.

The daughter moved aside to let Riarhin enter. Her brother stopped speaking at the sight of the sword.

“Not for us,” said a man sitting by the cold hearth. “She’s brought nails.”

He held out a hand.

Riarhin gave him the bag. He opened it, checked one nail against a board on the floor, and set them both aside.

“You’re early,” he told the family.

“You said before the bell.”

“I did. And you are. Sit down.”

The woman with the shut face sat. The brother remained standing. His sister laid the coat over the back of a bench and began taking off her wet shawl.

The man by the hearth was younger than Riarhin had expected a man who addressed the dead to be, though she could not have said why she had expected age. His hair was brown, his beard short and badly trimmed beneath one ear. He wore a plain dark coat over a shirt whose collar had been repaired with cloth a shade too white. A small stringed instrument lay in an open case beside him, but he did not reach for it.

“Sivrik said you’d know what they’re for,” Riarhin said.

“The seat. It tries to fold up under large mourners.”

He pushed himself to his feet.

“Esvin.”

“Riarhin.”

“Yes. I was told you’d come. Kelva hasn’t fed you to death, then.”

“Not yet.”

“She may be waiting for a festival.”

The daughter laughed once. Her brother looked at her. Esvin went to a jug on the shelf and poured water into four cups, then considered Riarhin.

“Staying?”

“If it’s private, I’ll go.”

He looked at the family, not answering for them. The mother shrugged. The daughter said she did not mind. Her brother said there was nothing private in what their father had to tell them.

Esvin took down a fifth cup.

The woman who came next carried no token. She had a white scarf over her hair and water on the hem of her coat. Esvin gave her the chair nearest the door.

“The lower ground?” he asked.

“Damp.”

“It is generally.”

“From underneath.”

He stopped arranging the cups.

“How far?”

“Not to the turn. I came round.”

“Good.”

She sat, holding her hands in her lap. Her fingers were bent at the last joints. The mother moved her own chair a little farther from the hearth, giving the newcomer room without looking at her.

Outside, somebody led an animal past. A bell on its harness sounded twice. Riarhin could hear water slipping from the thatch into a gutter. The chapel had a smell of wet wool, old smoke, and the sweet dusty scent of rushes not yet damp enough to sour.

Esvin checked the room. He closed the outer door but left its latch lifted, secured by a loop of cord. The cloth over the bell was grey, its corners hanging below the rim. A rope lay loose on the floor beside it. Beneath the bell stood a stool on which nobody had put anything.

“Who speaks first?” he asked.

The brother began.

“I’d like him to say what he intended about the boat.”

His sister looked at him.

“You said it was about Mother.”

“It is. Mother needs the boat kept.”

“I need you both to stop,” their mother said.

Esvin sat on the sound bench.

“Tell me what you want to ask him. Not what you think he’ll say.”

“The boat,” the brother said again.

The daughter took a breath.

“He said I could go.”

“When?”

“Before he died.”

“You keep saying that,” her brother said.

“Because he did.”

Riarhin looked at the coat. Its cuff had been worn through and turned back, exposing an older mend beneath. Someone had washed it thoroughly. A faint mark remained at the elbow where the cloth would not release whatever had stained it.

“Where?” Esvin asked the daughter.

“To the south yards. My aunt has work for me. Keeping measures, mostly, and helping with the books when the cargo comes in.”

Her brother made a small impatient movement.

“She has work here.”

“For you.”

“For all of us.”

“Enough,” said the mother.

Esvin took a little piece of charcoal from his pocket and made a mark on a board beside him. Riarhin thought he was writing the question until he turned the board and put it beneath the short leg of the bench.

“I haven’t asked him anything yet,” he said. “And I won’t while you’re answering one another.”

They settled. The daughter folded her hands tightly enough to whiten the knuckles.

“Your name?” Esvin asked her.

“Dena.”

He said it once. Then he asked her brother’s, and their mother’s, and the name of the man whose coat they had brought. He asked what each of them had called him at home. The answers differed. One made the mother smile reluctantly.

He looked at the coat.

“He wore that?”

“Every day,” Dena said.

“Then that will do.”

Before he sang, Esvin touched the cloth over the bell.

“Varnek Karn hears the offer. Leave the place clear.”

The brother moved his cup from beside the empty stool. Esvin waited until he had set it elsewhere.

“I’m asking him to hear you,” he said. “I don’t promise he’ll answer. If he does, let him finish. If you don’t understand, ask. Don’t make a promise because you dislike the silence after a question.”

The mother nodded. Her son glanced at the stool.

Riarhin sat with her hands open on her knees. She had been in rooms where magic arrived before anyone noticed the cost of inviting it. Here Esvin fetched the water closer, cleared his throat, and complained that the bench was still unsteady. The daughter shifted, giving him room. His knee cracked when he turned. He winced and began.

The first notes were lower than she had expected. They had little ornament, a measure shaped for walking while carrying something with another person. He sang the dead man’s name into it, then the name his wife had used, then an ordinary recollection she supplied when he left room for her.

A winter in which the boat had frozen to its mooring. A quarrel about a borrowed kettle. A neighbour who had fallen in and blamed the bank.

The wife spoke the last with a trace of pleasure. Esvin caught it and carried the measure on. His voice was not large. Riarhin found herself leaning forward to hear where it ended beneath the rain.

Dena held the coat.

Nothing happened for long enough that her brother began moving his foot. His mother put a hand on his knee. Esvin sang a verse again, more slowly. The woman by the door raised her head.

A sound came from the coat.

Not a voice at first. A breath drawn through cloth, followed by a little impatient exhalation. Dena’s fingers tightened. Her mother made a noise Riarhin could not have named.

Esvin let the measure continue beneath it.

“The kettle,” said a man’s voice. “She broke the handle herself.”

The wife began to laugh, and cried while she did it.

“I did not.”

“You did.”

“Don’t start.”

For a moment they sounded like two people beside a hearth after everyone else had gone to bed. Riarhin looked away, towards the rain-dark timber over the door. The brother leaned forward as if being nearer would let him see the mouth the words came from.

“Father.”

The voice stopped.

Esvin kept the tune, his eyes on the wife. She wiped her face with the heel of her hand.

“He wants to ask you about the boat.”

“What’s happened to it?”

“Nothing,” the son said quickly. “Nothing’s happened. But Dena says—”

Dena shut her eyes.

“Let her,” Esvin said, between notes.

The brother swallowed whatever he had been going to say.

“I want to go south,” Dena said. “To Aunt’s. We talked about it.”

A long pause. Water fell outside. Riarhin could hear Esvin taking breath through his nose, conserving the measure without making a show of it.

“There’s work here,” the voice said.

“You said I could.”

“When the accounts were straight.”

“They won’t be straight. There’s always something.”

“You don’t leave your mother with a boat she can’t use.”

“I’m not leaving her with it. He has it.”

Her brother looked at the floor. Esvin’s voice grew a little rougher. The woman by the door put both feet flat beneath her chair.

“Keep her here,” the voice said. It was directed somewhere past Dena, towards her brother or her mother or the room itself. “Until the work is done. There’s no sense in this.”

Dena bent her head. Her hands were shaking against the coat. Riarhin wanted to stand up. She had no idea what she intended to do once she had.

Esvin altered the measure. The change was small, a note left where another should have followed, a space in which the rain became suddenly loud.

“Dena?” he said.

She lifted her face.

“I’m going.”

The voice began again. Esvin did not raise his own over it. He held the opening while Dena took a breath.

“I wanted you to know. I wanted you not to be angry. But I’m going.”

Her mother put a hand on the coat. The brother’s head remained lowered.

The dead man said something about winter. Riarhin heard the word but not the rest. Esvin’s face had gone pale around the mouth. He waited, let another fragment come, then gave the wife a look.

“I’ll see to myself,” she said. “You know I will.”

The breath in the coat caught. The voice spoke her name. She answered, and whatever followed was too low for Riarhin to make out, even listening as she did.

Esvin closed the measure gently. The cloth lay still.

Nobody moved at once.

Then the brother stood and went outside. His mother watched him go. Dena kept holding the coat until Esvin asked if she wanted it folded.

“No. I can.”

She folded one sleeve across, then the other, aligning the worn cuffs. Her mother put the cups together and carried them to the shelf. She moved with the concentration of someone grateful for a small task she knew how to finish.

The woman by the door touched Esvin’s shoulder.

“Enough,” she said.

He looked up as if he had not heard her arrive beside him.

“Yes.”

Riarhin offered him water. He took it and drank too much at once, coughed, and wiped his beard with his sleeve.

Outside, Dena’s brother was speaking to the keeper. His voice rose, then lowered. Riarhin heard her own name and hoped he was not about to ask her to agree with him.

He came back in alone.

“You heard him,” he told Esvin.

“I did.”

“Can you tell her? Properly. She thinks because you let her say—”

“I heard her too.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Esvin set the cup down.

“Then ask it differently.”

The man looked at him, flushed, and left. His mother followed with the folded coat. Dena stayed a moment longer.

“Was it him?” she asked.

Esvin rubbed a thumb along the cup’s rim.

“You knew things in what he said. So did your mother.”

“Yes.”

“I asked for him. I believe he answered. I can’t give you more than that.”

She nodded. Her face was wet but composed. At the door she turned back.

“The kettle was hers. She never admitted it.”

For the first time since the song began, Esvin smiled.

“Then don’t tell your brother. Give him one thing less to be right about.”

She laughed weakly and went out.

Riarhin helped repair the bench.

Esvin held the new brace while she drove the nails. He had selected the wrong side to kneel on and had to move halfway through, complaining about the floor. By the time they finished, the chapel had become an ordinary small building in need of sweeping.

“Do you do this often?” she asked.

“Break benches?”

“The other.”

“When I’m asked. When I can.”

“Could you ask anyone?”

He looked at her then, more directly than he had since she arrived.

“Who?”

She had not intended to ask that. She had intended a question about how the thing worked, about names and tokens and the ability to hear an answer. Several names came to her, and she disliked how quickly they had.

“No one now.”

He nodded without appearing disappointed.

They put the bench back. The repaired leg sat a little high. Riarhin tested it with her weight and looked at him.

“We can take the packing out.”

“Or leave it for the next large mourner.”

She removed the board. The bench settled.

Outside, the rain had stopped. The white-scarfed woman stood at the head of the causeway looking towards the mist. Esvin joined her. They spoke too quietly for Riarhin to hear from where she stood, though she could have moved closer. After a while the woman left by the upper path.

“Is something wrong down there?” Riarhin asked when he returned.

“Water where she doesn’t expect it.”

“Is she usually right?”

“About water, often. About her daughter’s husband, she has been right three times and wrong for twenty years.”

He locked the chapel, then checked the latch with a pull which suggested he had once forgotten to.

“Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

“Recently enough to prevent it happening again?”

“No.”

“Good.”

They ate in a house beside the raised road where a woman sold meals to people who had business at the chapel or the sluice. Her husband was patching a net at the far end of the room. A young couple sat near the window pretending not to listen to the conversation of three older women at the next table.

Esvin was greeted with demands for a song before he had taken his coat off.

“Something cheerful,” the woman serving them said.

“I only know dreadful things.”

“Then one with a wedding.”

“That narrows nothing.”

He ate first, despite the protests. The food was thick soup with pieces of turnip and a dumpling large enough to occupy most of the bowl. Riarhin cut hers with the spoon’s edge. Esvin watched the steam escape and said he should have done that before burning his mouth.

Afterwards he sang without the instrument.

It began with a grain collector admiring a widow’s stores. Riarhin expected a familiar joke and found herself three verses later trying not to laugh while the widow explained precisely which sack he might inspect and why he would need to take his boots off. The net-mender knew a different fourth verse. Esvin let him sing it, considered it, and pronounced it slander upon an honest trade.

“Yours or his?” the woman asked.

“Mine. He gets paid whether anyone enjoys it.”

The young couple had given up pretending. The girl had her hand over her mouth. Her companion was looking at her instead of the singer, delighted by her delight.

Riarhin leaned back against the wall. The room was warm. Her thigh had stopped aching. For a little while nobody asked anything about the dead.

When they went out, Esvin stopped halfway down the steps.

“Yes?” he said.

Riarhin looked behind her. The doorway was empty.

He waited, head turned a little to one side. Then he rubbed his mouth and went down the last step.

“Which way?” she asked.

He did not answer until she said it again.

“Mill,” he said. “You’re going back to the mill.”

“Yes.”

“I’ll walk as far as the turn.”

They went slowly. At the turn he remembered a parcel he had left in the eating house and swore with considerable feeling. Riarhin offered to wait. He said he would only be a moment, then came back for his coat, which he had draped over her arm without noticing.

Chapter 6 — The open passage

Kelva needed two things from the port, and gave Riarhin a list of five.

“The last three can wait,” she said.

“Then why write them?”

“So they don’t.”

Riarhin folded the list into her coat. Sivrik was taking the barrow in as far as the grain stairs and had agreed to bring back whatever she could persuade him to carry. He inspected the list before they left and crossed out the largest item.

Kelva took the charcoal from him and put it back.

“Get Hesvar to deliver it.”

“He delivered the last.”

“And she’ll die before he delivers the next,” Sivrik told Riarhin.

“I heard that,” Kelva said.

“You were meant to.”

They left while she was still explaining where she wanted it put. The morning was close and damp. Fog lay in the lower cuts, but above the road the light had a yellow thickness which suggested sun somewhere too high to be useful. Sivrik pushed the barrow with his sleeves rolled up. On it lay a damaged gate fitting, a coil of chain, and a bundle of grain tallies he meant to compare at the store.

Riarhin had offered to take a handle. He had refused on the grounds that there was only one wheel and he preferred to know who was steering.

They passed the lower bend. The figures were gone from the uncultivated strip.

“Where do they go?” she asked.

“Somewhere else.”

“Do they leave when the sun comes?”

“Sometimes. Sometimes you find one standing in a shed at noon. Don’t open sheds you don’t need.”

He pushed the barrow over a raised root. The chain shifted.

“Nereth says they’ll stop coming once the line is whole.”

“Do you believe her?”

“I believe she believes it.”

He looked at Riarhin, saw the answer had annoyed her, and smiled without pleasure.

“I know. You didn’t ask that.”

“No.”

“I’ve seen four things she said would work. Three did. The fourth she mended before my sister heard of it.”

“What was it?”

“A post that wouldn’t hold its mark. Nothing got through.”

“Where?”

He looked down at the barrow wheel.

“Ask her.”

At the grain stairs, men were rolling empty casks onto a lighter. Sivrik left the barrow beneath a roof and went to find the keeper. Riarhin continued to Hesvar’s shop, bought two of the five things, agreed to have one delivered, and discovered that the other two were available at a price Kelva had not anticipated.

“Tell her,” Hesvar said.

“You tell her.”

He pushed his lower lip out.

“I’ll bring them.”

She left with lamp oil and a packet of needles. There was still time before Sivrik would be ready. She walked down to Arngrim’s berth.

The salt boat had been unloaded. Without the barrels it seemed larger and less important. Haldren knelt beside an opened section of decking, examining a board whose underside had gone soft. He looked up when her shadow fell across his hands.

“Come to pay for your bed?”

“I paid for it.”

“And went away before I could ask what you’d broken.”

“What have I broken?”

“Nothing yet. I like to know where people are.”

She sat on the rail while he tested the timber with the point of a tool. Arngrim stood on the quay in conversation with two men carrying measuring rods. Their voices rose and fell with the regularity of a dispute nobody expected to settle today.

“Cargo?” she asked.

“Grain. If it arrives. Wool, if it dries. A man promised hides and has since remembered another buyer.”

“When do you sail?”

“When Arngrim has finished insulting those men and asked them the same thing politely.”

“Tomorrow?”

Haldren laughed.

“You’ve lived with hopeful people.”

She watched him cut away the softened edge. His hands were broad, the fingers surprisingly quick. A little silver ring sat close to one knuckle, too tight to come off without effort. He turned it with his thumb whenever he stopped to consider the work.

“My wife says I should have it cut,” he said, catching her glance.

“Why don’t you?”

“She put it there. She can get it off.”

He shaved another strip.

“That’s what I told her. She fetched the shears.”

“Did you hold still?”

“No.”

Riarhin laughed. Then she heard the bell.

It came from inland, fast and uneven. A second answered near the gate, then stopped after half a note.

Arngrim turned. The men with the measuring rods had already begun walking towards the storehouse. A woman on the opposite quay lifted a child from the edge of the water and took it inside.

Haldren set down the tool.

“That isn’t the tide.”

A voice called from beyond the warehouse.

“Lower passage! Keep to the lower passage!”

The words travelled clearly over the water. Riarhin could not see who had shouted. On the bank above, two people changed direction. A cart turned across the mouth of a lane, its driver dragging the horse’s head round while a man behind pushed at a wheel.

Arngrim looked towards the gate.

“Stay aboard,” he told his crew.

Haldren had risen. He watched the cart trying to turn.

“He’ll put that wheel over.”

The voice called again, nearer now, though Riarhin had seen no one move along the quay.

“Lower passage! Upper line’s gone!”

A woman began running. Her bundle came loose, spilling something pale across the stones. She stopped, reached for it, looked behind her, and ran on without it.

Riarhin put the oil and needles beneath the rail.

“I’m going to look.”

Arngrim swore. Haldren took up his mallet and followed her ashore.

They found the crowd at the turn below the grain stairs. People were coming down from the raised road towards a narrow gate between the storehouse wall and the bank. Beyond it a timber passage led across a drainage channel to another landing. Riarhin had passed it that morning without attending to its shape. Now she saw how little room there was for two people abreast.
