A woman stood at the gate with an iron bar in her hands. She wore a dark hood thrown back, showing cropped hair streaked with grey. A bell hung above her beneath a roof of copper gone green at the edges. The rope lay beside her boot.

“Up,” she shouted. “Go up. This one’s closing.”

“They said lower,” a man answered.

“I’m telling you upper.”

The crowd pressed closer. A child cried somewhere within it. Haldren caught the turning cart’s wheel before it went over the edge and called for the driver to stop pulling. The horse reared its head, eyes showing white.

Riarhin climbed onto a low stone beside the bank. She could see the timber passage, the far landing, and beyond it a lane dissolving into fog. Three people were coming along the lane. Behind them moved others who did not seem to be hurrying at all.

“There’s a family coming,” she called to the woman at the gate.

“I see them.”

The woman’s gaze had fixed beyond the family. Riarhin followed it.

Something stood where the lane entered the mist. It was tall enough that its head should have been visible above the lowest roof. The fog made that difficult to judge. A pale vertical line moved, then another. People in the crowd below Riarhin stopped shouting long enough for her to hear a soft dragging noise from across the channel.

The woman seized the bell rope and rang it.

The sound struck Riarhin through the teeth. She flinched. Across the channel the nearest pale shape stopped. The three living people ran harder.

“Come on,” the woman said, not shouting now. “Come on.”

The father reached the far landing first. He turned back for a girl carrying something against her chest. The mother followed with a load on her back that caught against the corner post. She stumbled, recovered, and pulled the strap clear.

The woman at the gate lifted the bar.

“Wait,” Riarhin said.

The tall shape moved. The gate slammed.

A hand came through the gap at the hinge and vanished. The man screamed.

For a moment Riarhin could not see any of them. People had surged towards the shut gate. Haldren was trying to keep the horse from backing into the crowd, shouting at a man to get out from behind the cart. The bell rang again, painfully loud.

“Move back!” the keeper shouted. “Back to the stairs!”

Riarhin jumped down on the inner side of the bank. The landing beyond the gate was separated from her by water and a narrow strip of stone wall. She could see the father now, bent over his hand. His wife had the girl against her and was trying to drag both of them away from the outer lane.

“Open it,” Riarhin said.

The keeper shook her head.

“Can’t hold the span if I do.”

“They’re on it.”

“I know.”

Riarhin took one step towards her and stopped. The woman’s hands were white on the bar. The bell rope had wrapped around one wrist. She was looking at the opening beneath the gate, where the mist had begun pressing through like water rising behind a blocked drain.

There was another bell farther down, on the grain landing. It was still ringing at measured intervals. Its note reached them clearly through the greater noise.

Riarhin looked towards it. The grain stairs descended to a platform where loads could be lowered onto barges. A ramp lay along the edge, broad planks joined by iron straps, its end held on a roller. Between that platform and the family’s landing was a gap of perhaps twelve feet, most of it water, with the stone corner of the storehouse narrowing it on the inside.

“Haldren!”

He turned his head. She pointed. He looked once, then left the cart in the hands of its driver and ran towards her.

“The loading ramp,” she said.

“Won’t reach.”

“To the corner.”

He looked again. The keeper had heard. She followed the line of Riarhin’s hand, then pulled the bell rope free of her wrist.

“Stay this side of the black post,” she said. “The other landing’s outside the stroke.”

“Ring it,” Riarhin told her.

The woman caught her sleeve.

“The black post.”

Riarhin nodded and pulled free.

The ramp was too heavy for two.

Haldren shouted at the men sheltering beneath the stairs. One came at once. Another looked at the keeper, who nodded. The third remained with a woman who could not stop coughing. They lifted the shoreward end and swung it round while Riarhin freed the pin at the roller.

It jammed halfway.

“Pull,” Haldren said.

“I am.”

“Not you. Him.”

The man on the left changed his grip. The ramp shifted. Riarhin drove the pin clear with the heel of Toothpick’s hilt and caught it before it fell. Haldren had taken a length of rope from a pile beside the stairs. He passed it through the ramp’s iron ring and gave the end to the man who had come first.

“Round that. Twice. Don’t hold it in your hands.”

The man obeyed. Together they worked the ramp outward. Its far end scraped the storehouse corner and stopped short of the family’s landing.

“I told you,” Haldren said.

Riarhin stepped onto it. It dipped beneath her weight. She felt the movement run through her thigh and bent both knees.

“Get off.”

“Keep it there.”

She could reach the stone corner from the end. Beyond it ran a narrow ledge, slick with moss, no wider than her boot at its broadest. She put her left hand against the wall and tested it. Solid enough. The family’s landing stood a little lower and perhaps two paces away around the angle.

The father looked up at her. Blood had run down his wrist.

“Give me your good hand,” she said.

“My girl—”

“First you. I need you on that side.”

He looked towards the child. She had stopped crying. Her face was pressed into her mother’s coat.

Riarhin moved onto the ledge. Her ribs pulled when she reached. She let the sword’s scabbard hang clear rather than catch beneath her leg and held out her right hand while keeping her left against the wall.

The man took it.

“Step where I step. Not into the water.”

Behind him, beyond the outer rail, a figure in a pale coat had reached the far end of the passage. Its head moved from side to side. The bell above the gate sounded, and it stopped, then began again as the note faded.

Riarhin tightened her grip on the man.

“Now.”

He came awkwardly, his injured hand held to his chest. At the corner he looked down. His foot slipped. Riarhin drove her shoulder into the wall and took his weight through the arm, pain running hot beneath her ribs.

“Look at me.”

He did. His mouth was open, his breath coming too fast.

“There’s a plank behind you. Put your foot back.”

He found it. Haldren caught his coat and hauled him onto the ramp. The man fell to his knees. Someone on the grain landing pulled him farther in.

Riarhin stayed against the wall until her breath returned. The mother watched her from the other side.

“The child,” Riarhin said.

The girl would not let go of the bundle.

It was a small blanket wrapped around something hard. Riarhin could not see what. Her mother tried to take it, and the girl made a sound so frightened that the woman stopped.

“Bring it,” Riarhin said.

The mother shook her head.

“She can’t climb with it.”

“Give it to me.”

The girl looked at her. Riarhin put out her hand without moving nearer. The bell rang. The figure beyond the rail pressed its face against the wood, and the mother made a small involuntary sound.

The girl held out the bundle.

Riarhin took it and passed it round the corner to Haldren. He put it in the father’s lap. The father held it up where his daughter could see.

“There,” Riarhin said. “Now come.”

The girl was too small to make the step. Riarhin could have carried her if she had both arms free and somewhere to put her feet. She had neither.

“Haldren. Line.”

A loop appeared around the corner. She caught it, fed it under the girl’s arms, and tightened it enough to prevent slipping. The mother helped. Her hands shook so badly that Riarhin guided them through the last movement.

“Not round her neck. Here.”

The girl began crying again when the rope took her weight.

“You’re going to the plank,” Riarhin told her. “I’m here.”

She kept one hand under the child’s boot and the other against her back while Haldren drew the line. The girl’s foot scraped the wall. She kicked, found nothing, and screamed.

“Keep going,” Riarhin said to Haldren.

She went with the child as far as the corner, her own feet inching along the ledge. Haldren leaned out. His hand closed on the back of the girl’s coat. He lifted, grunted, and brought her against his chest. The line went slack.

The father reached for her. She clung to Haldren instead. He looked startled, then held her tighter and backed along the ramp.

Riarhin turned for the mother.

The pale figure had climbed onto the outer rail.

The mother saw it and made for the wall too quickly. Her load caught on a nail in the landing post. She pulled, stumbled back, and the thing on the rail turned towards the movement.

“Leave it,” Riarhin said.

“My—”

“Leave it.”

The woman struggled with the strap. Riarhin reached across, caught the edge of it, and cut it with Toothpick. The pack fell onto the landing. Something inside broke.

The woman cried out as though the knife had cut her. Then she took Riarhin’s hand.

She was heavier than her daughter and steadier than her husband. She kept her face against the wall and moved when told. At the corner the ramp shifted beneath Haldren, who had returned to hold it. The rope round the landing post creaked.

The bell sounded again. The pale figure dropped from the rail onto the family’s landing.

Riarhin put the woman in front of her and stepped onto the ramp. Behind them a hand touched the stone where hers had been. She could hear nails dragging over moss.

“Pull it in,” she said.

Haldren pushed the woman towards the men at the grain stairs. Riarhin turned at the end of the ramp, drew Remorse with her left hand, and held the point low enough to follow the movement coming round the corner.

The face appeared first. One side had been eaten away. The remaining eye did not look at the blade. A hand reached for the plank.

She cut the wrist as it took weight. The hand fell into the water. The body leaned farther, unbalanced, and she stepped back, letting it come beyond the ledge before cutting again at the shoulder. It struck the ramp heavily enough to jar it off the stone.

Haldren hauled on the rope. The near end slid. Riarhin went to one knee, caught herself with her right hand, and backed as the dead weight rolled into the channel.

Water splashed beneath the planks. Something caught at the lower edge, then lost it.

The ramp came in with a crash. Riarhin sat on it, breathing hard, the sword across her knees. The keeper was still ringing the bell.

“Enough,” Haldren shouted. “They’re here.”

The bell stopped. In the sudden gap Riarhin could hear the child sobbing against her father’s chest.

The keeper’s name was Maren Thorne.

Riarhin learned it from the father, who said it as soon as the woman knelt beside him.

“Maren.”

“I need to see your hand.”

“You shut it.”

“I know.”

“You saw us.”

Maren held out a clean cloth. Her own hands were shaking now.

“Let me see.”

He pulled away. His wife took the cloth instead and began wrapping it around his fingers. Two lay at an angle Riarhin did not like. The skin across the knuckles had split.

Maren did not try to take the hand again. She asked someone to fetch the woman who dressed injuries at the storehouse, then went along the people gathered beneath the stairs, asking names, asking who was missing. She repeated the questions whenever someone gave her an account of the alarm instead.

“After. Who came with you?”

Haldren sat beside Riarhin. His breath was loud.

“Your leg?”

“It held.”

“Not what I asked.”

She looked at him. There was a long scrape down his cheek, which he had not noticed.

“You’re bleeding.”

“So are you, somewhere. We can both enjoy being right.”

She laughed, then wished she had not. The ache under her ribs had become a hot band. She wiped Remorse with a piece of cloth he gave her and sheathed it carefully. Toothpick she had put away without thinking. She touched the hilt to be certain.

Across the landing, the girl opened the blanket. Inside was a clay bird, painted blue, its tail broken off. She pressed the broken place with her thumb. Her mother bent over her husband’s hand and cried without making any sound.

Riarhin looked away.

Nereth arrived with Sivrik while the last people were being brought from the upper stairs.

She had run. Her braid had come loose and her face was white. She looked first towards the garden of posts beyond the storehouse, then at Maren.

“Mine held.”

Maren turned.

“Who told you they didn’t?”

“Everyone’s saying the lower line failed.”

“The passage was called open when it wasn’t.”

“By whom?”

“That is what I’d like to know.”

Sivrik found Riarhin on the ramp.

“You were supposed to buy needles.”

“I did.”

He looked at the wet planks, her hand against her side, Haldren’s face.

“Kelva will ask where they are.”

“On the ship.”

“Good. Something’s where it ought to be.”

He went to help with the injured man. Nereth remained beside Maren, looking from the closed gate to the people beneath the stairs.

“The houses on the inside are all right,” she said. “I went past. The marks haven’t moved.”

“I haven’t said they have.”

“But you’ll say take them down.”

Maren looked tired enough to strike her.

“Not now.”

Nereth opened her mouth, shut it, and knelt beside the girl with the clay bird. She asked whether the child wanted the tail mended. The girl shook her head violently.

“All right,” Nereth said. “I won’t.”

She stayed there, sitting on her heels. After a while the girl leaned against her sleeve.

The voice that had called the lower passage open could not be found.

One man said it had come from the stairs. A woman said it was from the warehouse roof. The cart driver had thought it was Maren, though he admitted he had been looking at his horse. Maren listened to each account with the same fixed attention and wrote names on a scrap of board with the end of a burnt stick.

Eddis arrived from the ferry. He had heard the call from the water.

“Same words?” Riarhin asked.

“Lower passage. Upper line gone.”

“Who?”

He looked at Maren.

“No one I saw.”

Beyond the gate, the mist had thinned. The family’s pack lay where Riarhin had cut it loose. The mother stood looking through the small opening above the latch.

“Can I get it?” she asked.

Maren shook her head.

“In the morning.”

“My good pot’s in there.”

“I’ll have someone bring it.”

The woman stayed at the opening. Riarhin heard her draw a breath, hold it, let it go.

Sivrik had fetched the barrow. He had left the chain and fitting with the grain keeper and spread an old sail over the boards. The injured man sat on it reluctantly, holding his hand above his heart as he had been told. His wife walked beside him. Nereth carried the girl, who had fallen asleep at last.

“Mill?” Sivrik asked.

“Mill,” Riarhin said.

Haldren brought the oil and needles from the ship. He put them in her hands with exaggerated care.

“There. A successful morning.”

She looked at the packet, then at him. He was trying to make her smile. She managed it, and he looked relieved.

Chapter 7 — The breakfast they eat

By morning there were nine people in Kelva’s kitchen and none of them had enough room.

Pell insisted that the mill had to run. Kelva agreed. They disagreed over whether this meant he could have the bench, which was occupied by the injured man and his sleeping daughter. Sivrik had been awake most of the night and was making tea with the wrong pot. Haldren sat beside the hearth in borrowed stockings, his own hanging above him on a line. Every few minutes a drop fell from one onto his shoulder.

Riarhin moved the line.

“Thank you,” he said.

“It was annoying me.”

“I thought it might.”

The girl’s mother had finally gone upstairs. Nereth slept at the table with her forehead on her folded arms. Someone had put Kelva’s shawl over her shoulders. The dog lay beneath her chair, pleased with the increased chance of food falling.

Kelva put bread in front of Riarhin and told her to eat before she went finding something else to do.

“I wasn’t going anywhere.”

“You were looking.”

Riarhin ate. The bread was yesterday’s, softened against the pot’s steam before being cut. There was butter in a shallow dish and a dark conserve which Kelva said was plum. Haldren said it was whatever the plums had been stored beside. He took more when she offered it.

The injured man woke when the wheel began to turn. He looked around the room with the alarm of someone who had forgotten where he was, then at his daughter against his side. His bandaged hand rested in a sling made from one of Kelva’s cloths.

“How is it?” Riarhin asked.

He tried to move the fingers and stopped.

“Still there.”

“Leave it up.”

“Everyone says that.”

“Then you’re being consistently advised.”

He looked at her, uncertain whether she was making fun of him. She offered the bread. He took a piece with his good hand.

His name was Dorran. His wife was Min, the child Rella. They had told Riarhin these things the previous evening while Min helped wash blood from the sleeve and Dorran apologised for getting it on the floor. Rella had slept through most of it, waking once to ask for the bird. It lay now beside the butter dish, the broken tail set apart on a clean piece of cloth.

“She made that?” Haldren asked.

“Her uncle. In winter.”

“Good bird.”

“It was.”

Haldren bent closer without touching it.

“Still looks like one to me.”

Dorran considered this. Rella moved in her sleep and made a small sound. He put his good arm around her.

Esvin came with eggs and a headache.

He had heard about the gate late, gone to the port, discovered everyone he meant to find had left, and spent half the night helping a woman search for a boy who had been asleep behind the grain store the whole time.

“He didn’t hear us calling.”

“Was he all right?” Kelva asked.

“Until she found him.”

He set the eggs down carefully and held his head while bending to remove his shoes.

“Were you singing?” Riarhin asked.

“Calling. Loudly. Then she wanted me to tell her dead husband he’d been found.”

“Did you?”

“I told her to tell the living boy he was wanted first.”

Kelva pushed a cup towards him. He drank, closed his eyes, and made a grateful noise.

“That’s awful.”

“Sivrik made it.”

“I know. He makes it as though the leaves have insulted him.”

Sivrik raised one hand without turning from the stove.

Esvin found a place on the floor near Haldren. They regarded each other over the intervening boots.

“Ship?” Esvin asked.

“Salt boat.”

“Waiting for grain?”

“Waiting for men to admit they haven’t got grain.”

“That’ll be autumn, then.”

Haldren laughed. His laugh began in his nose and ended in a sort of wheeze, unattractive enough that Riarhin had disliked it on the crossing. Now she found herself waiting for it while Esvin described the boy’s hiding place. The story grew in the telling, not in its events but in the precise indignities of a grown man trying to look beneath a storehouse with a lamp that kept going out.

Dorran laughed and hurt his hand against the table. He swore. Everyone went quiet for a moment, and then he laughed again, angrier this time, at himself.

Rella woke. She looked around, saw the bird, and reached for it.

Kelva put a small bowl in her free hand before she could be troubled by anything else.

When Nereth woke, she had a crease from her sleeve across one cheek. She sat up abruptly, caught the shawl before it fell, and looked at the light as if it had betrayed her.

“I have to go.”

“You have to eat,” Kelva said.

“I ate.”

“Yesterday.”

“I need to check—”

“They can wait ten minutes.”

Nereth looked towards Riarhin, perhaps hoping for support from the person who had watched the lines with her. Riarhin broke an egg over a piece of bread and offered the plate.

“You helped carry Rella here. Sit down.”

The girl sat. She ate with the fierce concentration of someone determined not to enjoy being made to do something sensible. Esvin watched her for a moment and wisely said nothing.

Rella held out the bird.

Nereth looked at it.

“Now?”

The child nodded.

“I can’t make it as it was.”

Rella’s face changed.

“I can join it. You’ll see the line.”

After a moment the child pushed the tail across the table too.

Nereth fetched a little pot from her bag. Kelva looked suspiciously at it.

“Glue,” the girl said.

“I didn’t ask.”

“You were about to.”

She warmed the pot between her hands, tested the fit of the broken piece, and scraped a crumb from the join. Rella watched so closely that her hair kept falling across Nereth’s fingers.

“Hold it back.”

The child obeyed. Nereth brushed glue onto the break and pressed the pieces together. Her hands, unsteady with haste the day before, were perfectly still now. She set the bird in a folded cloth to keep the tail from taking weight.

“Leave it until after dinner. Don’t poke it to see whether it’s dry.”

Rella drew her hand back.

Nereth looked at her. The child looked back. They smiled at the same time.

Riarhin went into the yard while Kelva cleared the table. The air had warmed. She stood beside the water barrel and lifted both arms slowly, stopping before the pull beneath her ribs became pain. Her thigh was stiff from the rescue, but it bore her weight. Asta would have had opinions. Riarhin could imagine the exact shape of them and wished, unexpectedly, that she could hear them from the woman herself.

Haldren came out carrying his boots. He sat on the low wall and began putting them on.

“Arngrim will think I’ve found better employment.”

“Have you?”

“No one has paid me.”

“Kelva fed you.”

“She fed everybody. I’d like distinction.”

He pulled the second boot on and winced.

“Still wet?”

“Always wet. It’s a quality of boots.”

She sat beside him, leaving enough room for the sword. Across the cut, a man was trimming a hedge. Each stroke exposed pale wood beneath the dark outer growth. The regular sound was pleasant. A white bird stood in the lower field, motionless among the green shoots.

“Will you tell your wife?” she asked.

“About my breakfast? Certainly. She’ll want to know whether Kelva can make a man eat turnip before noon.”

“About yesterday.”

He worked at the buckle.

“Yes. Not on the first evening.”

“Why not?”

“She’ll have things to tell me.”

He glanced at her, perhaps expecting a joke. She had none ready. He bent back to the buckle and threaded the strap through.

“She’ll ask why my coat smells of somebody else’s hearth. I’ll say I was helping. She’ll ask who asked me to. I’ll say a woman with a sword. She’ll say that explains the speed of it.”

Riarhin laughed.

“You came before I asked.”

“Don’t spoil it. I’ve got a wife to entertain.”

He stood, tested the boots, and looked towards the lane.

“If you need boards, ask Arngrim. If you ask me, he’ll say I gave them away.”

“Thank you.”

He nodded, uncomfortable with the word now that it had arrived plainly, and went back inside to say goodbye to the child.

Maren arrived after he left.

She came without a guard or a bell. There was a packet beneath her arm and a dark bruise along one wrist where the rope had tightened. Kelva admitted her and said Dorran was sleeping. Maren stood just inside the door, looking at the place on the bench where he had been.

“His pack,” she said.

Kelva took it. The strap was cut, the cloth wet. Something inside had been wrapped separately.

“Pot’s broken.”

“I know.”

“Min will want the pieces.”

“They’re there.”

Kelva carried the pack to the foot of the stairs. Maren remained standing until she returned.

“Tea?”

“If there’s some.”

“There is. It isn’t good.”

“I don’t mind.”

Riarhin watched from beside the hearth. Maren’s gaze came to her and moved away before returning.

“You held the ramp.”

“Haldren held it.”

“You went out.”

“Yes.”

Maren nodded, looking into her cup.

“Thank you.”

Riarhin inclined her head. She let the thanks stand.

Maren drank the tea and did not visibly react. Kelva noticed and looked almost impressed.

“I need to compare the lines,” the keeper said.

Nereth had been in the working room with Pell. At the word lines she appeared in the doorway.

“Mine held.”

“I saw.”

“Then why?”

“Because the call was wrong, the water was wrong, and something reached my outer span from ground it shouldn’t have crossed. I don’t know why. I want to see yours working with someone at the farther marker.”

“You’ve seen it work.”

“I’ve seen people safe behind it. I want to see the far side.”

Nereth looked at Riarhin, and her face coloured.

“They were outside.”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

“Where they’re meant to be.”

“Outside which house?” Maren asked.

Kelva put her cup down hard enough to make the tea jump.

“You can ask without sounding pleased to find fault.”

Maren turned to her.

“I am not pleased.”

“No. You’re never pleased. It’s one of the things we pay for.”

For a moment Riarhin thought the keeper would leave. Instead she put the cup carefully beside the hearth.

“I closed a gate on a man yesterday. I heard him. I would like to know what I should have done before there’s another.”

Kelva looked towards the stairs. Nereth’s colour faded. The mill continued its steady work beyond the wall.

“Where?” Riarhin asked.

“The short span by the upper cut. Clear ground on both sides. Daylight. No houses directly behind the second marker.”

“When?”

“After the water settles. Tomorrow, if it does.”

Nereth rubbed at the crease still visible on her cheek.

“I’ll need to set it.”

“Tell me what you need.”

“And nobody touches anything because they think they know better.”

Maren nodded.

Riarhin saw Kelva begin to speak, stop, and fold her hands instead. The girl had agreed before her defender could refuse for her.

The official came while Maren was still there.

He was Aeler, short and broad, with a dark beard braided close beneath his chin and a coat whose shoulders had been reinforced against rain. He wiped his boots before entering. The two people accompanying him stayed in the yard, one beside the gate and the other looking at the millrace.

Kelva recognised him and did not offer tea.

“I’m here about the fittings,” he said.

“They’re fitted.”

“So I’ve been told.”

He looked at Nereth, then at the frame leaning against the wall. His eyes went to the little horn plate above the shutter.

“Unlicensed work on a protected span needs examination.”

“It wasn’t protected,” Kelva said.

“The repair is pending.”

“It has been pending through the winter.”

He drew breath, considered the room, and turned to Maren.

“You’ve inspected it?”

“Tomorrow.”

“I’d prefer the components held until—”

“No,” Kelva said.

The word was loud enough that Pell stopped whatever he was doing in the next room.

The official looked at the shut door, the stair, the small bird drying on its cloth. He seemed to become aware of how many people were sleeping in the house.

“I haven’t said remove the household protection this minute.”

“You were going to.”

“I was going to ask the keeper what could safely be held.”

Maren rubbed her bruised wrist.

“I don’t know yet.”

“Then who has the instructions?”

Nereth lifted her chin.

“I do.”

“I’ll need a copy.”

“No.”

He looked at her for a long moment. Riarhin could see him revising an estimate of the difficulty. Nereth stood very straight, too straight, her hands hidden in her coat pockets.

“A witnessed test,” Maren said. “Tomorrow if the water allows. You’ll receive the findings.”

“From whom?”

“Me.”

“And her?” He looked at Riarhin.

“I’ll watch the other end,” she said.

He glanced at the sword. She could not tell whether he knew her name or merely disliked a stranger being involved in something he had expected to settle locally. He asked it. She told him. He repeated it once, correctly, and took a small writing case from his coat.

“Then we’ll begin with that.”

He wrote the arrangement down. Maren read it before making her mark. Nereth read it twice and pointed to a word. He altered it after a brief argument in which Riarhin did not intervene. Kelva watched all of them and refused to sign anything.

When the official left, the room seemed to exhale.

Nereth went to the bird, touched the cloth beside it rather than the repair, and took her bag from the floor.

“I need to prepare.”

Kelva caught her arm.

“Dinner.”

“I’ll come.”

“Here.”

“Yes.”

Kelva released her. She left by the garden gate, replacing the lower piece carefully behind her.

Dorran came downstairs after the official had gone.

Min followed him carrying the sling he had removed. She told him to put it back on. He said he needed to know whether he could button his coat. She said he had found out three times already.

Maren stood to leave. Dorran saw her and stopped on the last stair.

For several breaths the only movement was the wheel beyond the wall. Kelva took a cup from the table and set it by the basin, making unnecessary room.

“I brought the pack,” Maren said.

“I saw.”

“The pieces are wrapped.”

“Yes.”

Min went past her to fetch the pack. She put it on the bench and opened the wrapping. The pot had broken into five large pieces and grit. She sorted the pieces on the table. Rella came to look, saw that it was not her bird, and went away again.

Dorran sat. His coat remained open. The unbuttoned side hung lower where he had stretched it trying to make his injured hand useful.

“Did you see us?” he asked Maren.

“I heard you.”

“Did you see?”

“After the gate was down.”

He looked at the bruising on her wrist. Riarhin stood by the hearth, far enough from both that neither had to look through her to reach the other.

“You could have lifted it.”

“Not without opening the lane.”

“We were in the lane.”

“Yes.”

He took one of the pot fragments in his sound hand and put it down again.

“What happens if it’s me next time?”

Maren’s gaze moved towards the yard, then back to him.

“I’ll have someone at the lower bend before the call. We should have had someone there yesterday.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

Min asked where their cart was. The ordinary question entered the room so abruptly that Riarhin almost answered, although she did not know.

“By the upper shed,” Maren said. “The wheel’s off. They carried the load in.”

“All of it?”

“As much as was on it when they reached it.”

Min pressed her lips together. She began fitting two pieces of the pot against each other, then abandoned the attempt and wrapped them again.

“We need that cart.”

“I’ll ask them to bring it.”

“Can they put the wheel on first?”

“I’ll ask.”

“Don’t ask. Find someone who can.”

Maren nodded. She picked up her empty cup, discovered it had already been taken away, and let her hand fall.

Dorran had begun trying the button again. His bad hand moved by fractions, the fingers stiff and pale. Riarhin watched Min watch him. Neither offered help. At last he gave up and looked at Maren.

“I don’t want you telling people I came late.”

“I won’t.”

“The bell was wrong.”

“Yes.”

He turned his face towards the hearth. Maren left without asking whether he accepted the answer.

Min put the sling back on him. This time he let her. She stood behind his chair to make the knot and smoothed the cloth where it had twisted against his neck. He reached up with his good hand and held hers for a moment.

Riarhin went into the yard. Maren was standing by the gate, looking at the fastening Nereth had replaced.

“Do you know a wheelwright?” the keeper asked.

“Not here.”

“Nor do I. Not one free today.”

They stood beside the closed gate. Then Riarhin said she could ask Haldren when she went to the quay.

Maren nodded. She did not thank her again. She opened the gate carefully and went towards the upper shed.

Riarhin went upstairs to fetch her coat. From the little window she could see beyond the bend towards the raised road. A line of carts was moving towards the port. Men walked beside them with poles over their shoulders. Behind the carts came six riders in dark coats, their horses stepping carefully on the wet surface.

She watched them turn towards the export stores. Two dismounted at the gate. The others remained outside, spread along the road where the carts had to pass between them.

Below, Rella woke and asked whether the bird was ready.

“After dinner,” Kelva called.

“Is it dinner?”

“Not yet.”

“When?”

“When I put it on the table.”

Riarhin took the coat from the stool. Her hand found the packet of needles still in its pocket. She had forgotten to give them to Kelva. She went down to do it before something else took her attention.

Part II — Their own protection

Chapter 8 — The earth under the grain

The test was postponed because the water did not settle.

At the upper cut it climbed the measuring post while the tide was falling. Eddis checked the mark twice, cleaned weed from the lower notch, and checked again. Maren watched him do it. Nereth waited with her case of tools beneath one arm, her anger at the delay becoming harder to maintain as the water rose visibly around a tuft of grass.

“Someone’s opened the west sluice,” she said.

“Perhaps,” Eddis said.

“Wouldn’t that do it?”

“It would bring water.”

She turned away from him. Riarhin could understand the irritation. She would have liked an answer with somewhere to put her feet in it.

Maren sent a boy along the high road to ask at the sluice. They waited until he returned, breathless and offended at having been doubted when he said it was shut. Nereth crouched by the water and looked at the underside of the bank.

“I can set it higher.”

“Not today,” Maren said.

“People are waiting.”

“So am I.”

The keeper closed her case. After a moment Nereth did the same.

Riarhin walked back towards the mill with her. The girl kept the case between them, perhaps accidentally. Her boots had acquired another layer of mud.

“Where do you sleep?” Riarhin asked.

“Different places.”

“Last night?”

“At the lower sheds.”

“Why?”

“Because I was there.”

The answer had enough exhaustion in it to quiet Riarhin’s next question. They walked in silence until the road divided. Nereth stopped.

“I have work.”

“Kelva expects you.”

“She always expects me.”

“She’ll keep dinner.”

The girl’s face softened briefly and then closed again.

“Tell her not to.”

She took the lower road, walking fast. Riarhin watched until the bend hid her. Then she continued towards the mill.

Kelva was going to the storehouse with three sacks and an expression that discouraged inquiry. Sivrik had hired a small cart. Dorran stood beside it, his injured hand elevated in its sling, giving advice nobody had asked for.

“You’ll have to shift the weight if that wheel goes into a rut.”

“I’ve pushed a cart before,” Sivrik said.

“Not that one.”

“Nor have you.”

“I can see it.”

Kelva came out with a little packet of papers. She put them inside her coat and looked at Riarhin.

“Postponed?”

“Yes.”

“Of course.”

“The water’s rising.”

“So is the price of waiting.”

She climbed onto the cart. Riarhin went with them, partly because Sivrik asked for help at the other end and partly because she had begun to recognise the look brother and sister wore when they needed someone else in the space between.

At the storehouse the Aeler official from the mill stood beside a weighing frame. His coat was folded over a chair. In his shirtsleeves he looked younger, though the beard disguised how much. Another clerk called figures while a man moved a counter along a board. The sacks waiting to be measured filled half the yard.

Kelva gave her name. The official found a page.

“Three?”

“For the advance. Sivrik’s share.”

He looked at the page, then at the cart.

“And the balance?”

“Still at the mill.”

“Delivery is overdue.”

“So is the bell.”

He picked up his pen, reconsidered, and put it down.

“Those are separate accounts.”

“They seem very close together from my kitchen.”

Sivrik had begun unloading. Riarhin helped guide a sack onto the waiting trolley while another man took the weight. She heard the official ask whether Kelva disputed the quantity. Kelva said she disputed the arrangement. He asked again about the quantity.

A low noise passed beneath the yard.

Riarhin felt it first through her boots: a shiver, not a blow, followed by a movement like the deck of a ship settling against a mooring. The trolley rolled away from her hand. She caught it before the sack tipped.

Someone laughed nervously.

Then the yard dropped.

It did not drop far. That was what Riarhin remembered afterwards, the smallness of the movement compared with the noise it made. The raised approach along the storehouse wall sank perhaps the breadth of her hand. The cart jolted. A horse screamed. Stone joints opened with a series of little cracks, and water came through them.

Inside the storehouse something heavy fell.

The official ran for the door. He reached it before Riarhin and put his shoulder against the frame as the lintel shifted. A man within was trying to drag a sack away from the opening. The door had jammed half shut against it.

“Leave that,” the official shouted.

The man pulled harder. Riarhin caught the top of the sack from outside, hauled it towards herself, and tore the seam on the door’s iron edge. Grain poured over her wrists. The man let go at last and stumbled through.

“Who else?” she asked.

He pointed behind him.

A girl stood on a stack at the far side, surrounded by fallen sacks. Water had begun spreading across the floor. She was looking at the roof.

“Come down,” Riarhin called.

“I can’t.”

“You can. This way.”

The official was still holding the frame. His feet slipped on spilled grain. Riarhin kicked a wedge of broken timber beneath the door, then went inside.

The smell was close and dry above the rising water, grain dust caught in damp air. A crack had opened along the inner wall. She could see daylight through it. The girl stood with one foot between two sacks, unable to free it without stepping where a shelf had fallen.

Riarhin climbed onto the lower pile. Her thigh protested. She moved the smaller sack, put her hand beneath the trapped foot, and lifted.

“Now.”

The girl came free. She was older than Tessa, perhaps fifteen, with dust in her eyelashes. She tried to jump. Riarhin caught her by the coat.

“Here first.”

They came down one level at a time. At the door the official’s face was grey with effort. The frame had moved again, pressing the timber wedge into the grain-covered floor.

“Quickly,” he said.

Riarhin pushed the girl ahead. The official reached for her without taking his shoulder away from the wood. Someone outside caught her. Riarhin came through, turned, and put her own weight against the frame long enough for him to get clear.

They let go together.

The door lurched inward and held crookedly, the wedge jammed beneath it. Water ran over the threshold, carrying a little yellow fan of grain into the yard.

The official sat down hard on a stone. His hands shook. He looked towards the girl, found her among the people by the weighing frame, and bent forward with his elbows on his knees.

Riarhin stood beside him, catching her breath. She wanted to tell him he had done well and could hear how intolerable that would sound. She asked whether his shoulder was hurt.

He moved it experimentally and winced.

“Only when I move it.”

“Then don’t for a moment.”

He laughed once, without amusement, and stayed where he was.

The shaking did not return.

People waited for it while doing the things which could not wait. The horse was led away. A woman counted those who had been inside. Men brought planks to keep the water from cutting farther into the yard. Sivrik was already kneeling beside the broken sluice grate, pulling out what the movement had packed against it.

Kelva’s three sacks had been set on the lower edge of the approach. Two were wet through at the bottom.

“We need them up,” she said when Riarhin joined her.

They found a dry trolley and lifted the first sack with help from a storehouse hand. It was heavier now, water clinging to the cloth. Grain had begun swelling through a split seam. Kelva pressed her palm over it while they moved.

“Those were weighed?” she asked Sivrik.

“Not yet.”

“Then they’ll say it’s water.”

“It is water.”

“It wasn’t when I brought them.”

He looked up from the grate. Mud streaked his face where he had wiped it with his wrist.

“I know.”

For once she did not answer. Riarhin held the torn sack while Kelva fetched a new one. They worked over a cloth, tipping slowly, catching the spills. The wet grain stuck to their hands. It would have to be dried quickly or used, and the storehouse was no longer a place to do either.

The official returned with his coat over the unhurt shoulder. He asked whether anyone had been missed, then spoke to the men preparing boards for the approach. Riarhin heard the word export, repeated twice.

Sivrik stood.

“The mill cut’s backing up too. I need two of them with poles.”

“After this crossing is secured.”

“If it breaks there, we’ve got water in the house.”

“The loaded barges are against this bank. If it goes, we lose them and the landing.”

Sivrik looked towards the harbour. A line of moored vessels rose and fell against the piles. Men were loosening ropes to allow for the changed water.

“Two,” he said. “Not the whole crew.”

“After this is braced.”

Kelva had heard. She straightened with the empty sack in her hands.

“There. Now you know.”

Sivrik turned on her.

“I need this landing too.”

“You have a house.”

“And grain on a boat against that bank, which pays for part of it.”

She opened her mouth and stopped. He took the empty sack from her, shook it, and held it while Riarhin poured.

The repair crew passed them carrying poles. One of the men nodded apologetically to Sivrik. He nodded back.

They spent the rest of the morning drying grain on borrowed sailcloth.

The sun appeared and disappeared above the fog, giving them warmth in brief intervals. Kelva knelt to spread the grain with both hands. Riarhin used a flat board. Sivrik went back to the mill on foot to check the bank, returning an hour later with wet boots and the news that Pell had opened the overflow in time.

“House is dry,” he said.

Kelva’s shoulders dropped. She did not thank him until he had begun helping again, and then so quietly that he almost missed it.

The cloth was not large enough. Kelva spread the grain thin at the edges and left a thicker ridge down the middle, turning it every few minutes. A woman drying her own load beside them said it would sour there before it dried. Kelva asked whether she had another sail to lend. The woman shook her head and went back to her own grain.

Riarhin asked at the nearest boat. Its owner had a spare sail, folded beneath a hatch, but would not put food on the dressing. At the second, the men were bracing the landing and nobody would stop to hear her. She reached Arngrim’s vessel with her boots clotted in grain paste.

He listened, looked at the sky, and said he could give her a cloth until the evening.

“Clean?”

“Clean enough for freight.”

“Grain.”

“I heard you.”

Haldren brought it up. They unfolded it across the deck. One corner smelled of fish oil. He sniffed, found another corner no better, and looked at Arngrim.

“Other side,” the skipper said.

Haldren turned it. The stain had soaked through.

“Unless the grain eats with its eyes.”

Arngrim swore at him and sent him below for an undressed length meant for a cover. Haldren returned with it under his arm. He made Riarhin carry the heavier end, telling her that an invalid had already lifted it twice. She took it without arguing and he looked disappointed.

Kelva was on her feet when they came back. A shower had begun over the western water, its edge visible as a grey fringe beyond the moored boats. People were looking for shelter. The woman beside them had gathered her grain into the middle of her cloth and was trying to make its corners reach.

“Under the awning,” Haldren said.

“That belongs to the weighers,” Kelva told him.

“Do they weigh in rain?”

They carried the new cloth there, swept the grit off the boards beneath it, and began moving the grain in shallow baskets. Riarhin knelt at one end, Kelva at the other. The weight of each basket changed as water drained from it. A boy slipped while carrying the third, and grain scattered beneath the bench. Kelva made a noise between a sob and an oath.

The boy froze.

“Fetch a broom,” she said. “Not that one. A clean one.”

He ran. Haldren went after him before the search could become another delay.

The rain reached the yard. It sounded first on the storehouse roof, then on the stretched awning. People pressed under it carrying sacks and coats and one small child who had become furious at being lifted. Kelva moved her knee out of the child’s way and went on spreading grain. There was barely room to stand.

Sivrik returned with news from the mill while the shower was passing. He stopped at the edge of the awning, looking for somewhere to put his wet feet. Kelva pushed the empty basket towards him. He understood and began filling it from the last thick patch on the old cloth.

The woman who had warned them about souring brought her own basket over.

“May I?”

Kelva measured the remaining space with her eyes. Riarhin thought she would refuse. Instead she drew a line through the grain with the edge of her hand and shifted her own portion closer.

“There. Keep it separate.”

They sat knee to knee until the shower had passed. The woman’s child fell asleep against Kelva’s side, damp hair leaving a patch on her sleeve. Kelva did not move him. When his mother reached to take him, she shook her head and pointed towards the drying grain.

The official came with the tally board. He had recorded the three sacks as received, subject to drying and final measure. Below that, in smaller writing, stood the quantity remaining at the mill.

“Still outstanding,” he said.

Kelva looked from the wet grain to his board.

“You could leave it for an hour.”

“I have to record what’s here.”

“And what isn’t.”

“Yes.”

She signed nothing. Sivrik read the figures, corrected one which had been copied from an older return, and made his mark beside the correction. The official accepted it without argument.

Riarhin noticed a line of blood along his collar. The door frame must have caught him beneath the beard. He had not felt it, or had decided it could wait. She took one of Asta’s clean pieces from her pocket and offered it.

He stared at it a moment before understanding.

“Thank you.”

He pressed it to his neck, looked at the stain, and put the cloth carefully between two fingers while he finished writing. She wondered whether Asta would count that as a reasonable failure to return her linen.

On the road back, Kelva sat beside the recovered sacks with her hands in her lap. The skin between her fingers had gone white from water. Sivrik walked at the horse’s head. Riarhin went behind until Kelva told her to climb up and stop looking as though she had been sent to prison.

The cart moved slowly. A wheel found a rut and everyone shifted. Beyond the road, the fields were unchanged except for a new gleam of water in a furrow that had been dry that morning.

“Would you have sent the men here?” Kelva asked.

Riarhin knew what she meant.

“I don’t know how close that bank was to going.”

“You saw the boats.”

“Yes.”

“So did I.”

Kelva looked ahead at her brother. His shoulders were rounded with fatigue, his hand resting on the horse’s neck.

“He isn’t a coward,” she said.

“I didn’t think he was.”

“He thinks I think it.”

The cart creaked. Riarhin waited, but Kelva said no more.

When they reached the mill, Pell had propped the garden gate clear of the mud with a broken tile. Nereth was crouched beside it, replacing a support the water had shifted. She looked up as the cart stopped.

“It held,” she said.

Kelva got down, went to her, and put both arms round her before the girl could stand. Nereth’s face disappeared against the floury apron. For a moment her hands hung awkwardly at her sides. Then she held Kelva back.

Chapter 9 — One span

By the following afternoon the water had fallen three marks.

Eddis called it enough to look at, not enough to trust. Maren accepted the distinction and sent for Nereth. They met at the upper cut with a measuring cord, two lamps, a box of fittings, and considerably more people than Riarhin had expected.

Kelva stood on the high side of the road with Sivrik. Hesvar had come to see whether his frame had been useful and brought a bag of spare pegs in case it had not. The official sent a younger clerk who looked relieved when told to remain well back. Two ward keepers carried a shuttered lantern between them. Esvin arrived last, having been asked by a family whose dead sometimes walked that stretch to make certain nobody treated the test as an excuse to damage a recognisable body.

“It’s not a hunting party,” Maren told him.

“I said I’d come. They didn’t say I had to enjoy it.”

Nereth had selected a short section where the bank narrowed between the drainage cut and an old fence. A little shed stood beyond, its door hanging loose. There were no occupied houses immediately behind it. Farther off, among the reeds, a figure moved slowly along a path too low for the living road.

Riarhin looked at the distances. The near end was open and firm. At the far end the bank curved towards a stone marker with a worn hollow on top. Between, Nereth had put three supports into the earth and stretched a thread through them at knee height.

“Not the whole working,” she said before anyone asked. “Just the refusal. The house fittings keep the shape round a place. This only turns an approach.”

Maren knelt beside the nearest support.

“May I touch it?”

“The wood. Not the mark.”

She turned it gently between finger and thumb without pulling it free. Nereth watched closely, then relaxed when the keeper set it back exactly as she had found it.

“Who stands where?” Riarhin asked.

Nereth pointed. Riarhin would take the far marker, Maren the near, Eddis the cut where he could watch the water. The others would remain behind the old line. If Maren rang the handbell, everyone was to withdraw by the same road they had used to arrive. Nobody was to cut the thread unless Nereth said to.

“And if you can’t say?” Maren asked.

The girl’s mouth tightened. She took a narrow knife from her case and laid it beside the near support.

“Lift that piece first. There. Then cut. Otherwise the end returns through this one.”

Maren made her show it twice. Nereth resented the second demonstration, but gave it. Riarhin watched from the far marker until she could see the movement clearly.

The figure took a long time to come.

It moved along the lower path, stopped beside a patch of water, and remained there while the living stood waiting. A fly settled on Riarhin’s wrist. She brushed it away. The sun came out, warmed the back of her neck, and disappeared again. Somebody behind the old line coughed.

Nereth checked the little plate at the centre of the thread. Maren told her to leave it alone. The girl looked up angrily.

“I’m checking.”

“You’ve checked. Let it stand.”

She stepped back.

Riarhin could hear Esvin speaking quietly to the family member who had come with him. The man believed the figure might be his wife’s uncle. Esvin asked whether he recognised the coat. The man could not say. They continued watching.

At last the figure turned towards the bank.

It was a woman, or had been dressed as one. Her skirts were dark with water to the thigh. Something pale showed at the side of her head where the hair had gone. She climbed the lower rise without using her hands, her feet moving in a slow, dragging sequence which made the ascent look impossible until she had completed it.

Nereth stood very still.

The figure reached the thread and stopped.

For several breaths nothing changed. Then it turned left, following the line. At the centre support it stopped again, leaned forward, and drew back. The movement was slight, but unmistakable. It could not cross.

Kelva let out a breath behind the old line. Riarhin heard it from where she stood.

Nereth looked towards Maren.

The keeper nodded once.

The figure continued along the thread towards the far marker.

Riarhin first noticed the handhold.

It appeared where there should have been the dark water of the cut, a worn curve in a pale stone wall. For a moment she thought she was seeing a reflection from the marker beside her. Then a stair formed beneath it, each tread visible at an angle which did not fit the bank.

She moved her head. The stair remained.

Beyond the first turn lay another turn, descending. The light on it was dry and colourless. There was no sky. She could see a smear along the handhold where many hands had darkened the stone, and a crack across the edge of the third tread.

“Stop,” she said.

Nereth looked up.

“Don’t move anything.”

“What?”

“There’s a stair.”

Eddis stood a few yards below her on the cut. He looked where she pointed.

“Water.”

“Beside the marker.”

“I see water.”

Riarhin lifted a small stone from the bank and held it at arm’s length towards the opening. The air beyond her fingers was cold. She did not let go.

“Step back,” Maren said.

Riarhin did, slowly. The stair narrowed, as if the angle from which it could be seen had changed. The figure beyond the thread turned its head towards the place.

Nereth ran a few paces towards Riarhin and stopped when Maren rang the handbell.

“Back,” the keeper said. “All of you.”

“I need to see—”

“Back.”

Riarhin withdrew by the bank. At each step she kept the stair in view until it became a line of pale light beside the marker and vanished. The figure did not follow. It stood with its head bent towards the water.

Nereth had gone pale. She looked at her fittings, then at the far marker, then down at the notebook she had taken from her coat.

Maren noticed.

“You know it?”

“No.”

“You looked.”

“I wrote what the ground was before. I was checking.”

“Show me.”

The girl closed the book.

“No.”

They argued behind the old line while the dead woman remained on the other side of the new.

Maren wanted the test ended and the fittings held for examination. Nereth said the test could be ended but not by removing everything at once. The working had joined to the ground farther than she intended. She could release it, but would need to establish where it had gone first.

“You said one span,” Maren said.

“It is one span.”

“Then why can’t you close one span?”

“Because the ground moved yesterday.”

“You chose it today.”

Nereth’s eyes filled. She blinked hard, furious with herself for it.

“I know where I put it.”

Riarhin waited until both stopped for breath.

“She didn’t cross,” she said.

Maren turned to her.

“I saw.”

“And something opened beside the far marker. I saw a stair. Eddis didn’t. If we take it down, I want someone watching there while she does it.”

“That was the arrangement,” Nereth said.

“Then we can keep it.”

Kelva had come closer. Her hands were tightly clasped.

“Is the mill safe?”

Nobody answered quickly enough.

“Nereth.”

“Yes. It hasn’t changed.”

“Do you know?”

The girl’s face changed at the question. Not anger this time. Hurt, so immediate that Riarhin almost wished Kelva had not asked.

“I’ll go and check.”

“You stay here,” Maren said. “Someone else can go.”

Nereth looked at the notebook in her hands.

“They won’t know what to look for.”

“Then tell them.”

It took the rest of the afternoon.

Nereth explained the household marks to Kelva and one of the ward keepers, using the spare frame and a piece of damp ground. She began too quickly, stopped when Kelva asked her to, and began again. The keeper repeated each step. Nereth corrected the position of his fingers, then apologised for the manner of it without looking up.

Riarhin stayed at the far marker while the test was released. Eddis stood below. She could see no stair now, only the dark water and the opposite bank. When Nereth lifted the first fitting, the water appeared to move uphill for a moment. Eddis swore.

“You saw that?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes.”

The second fitting came free. The figure turned away from the line and began walking towards the lower path. Its movement was no different from before. Riarhin felt a small release of tension she had not noticed holding.

At the third fitting, the pale handhold showed again.

“There,” she said.

Nereth stopped with the support half lifted. Maren stood beside her, one hand on the girl’s shoulder to prevent another movement.

“Put it back?” Nereth asked.

“Hold it,” Riarhin said.

She watched. The handhold remained. Beyond it, on the third tread, lay something dark and flat which had not been there before. She could not tell whether it was cloth or a shadow.

“Now slowly.”

Nereth lowered the support. The stair diminished. Riarhin moved closer by one step and saw the crack in the tread align with the edge of the stone marker. She told Maren exactly where.

The keeper marked the place on her cord. Nereth wrote something in her notebook. When Riarhin looked back, she had turned a page over the one she was using.

They left the final support in place, attended by two keepers. The line no longer turned the whole approach, but the opening stayed closed while the little fitting held its position. Nereth did not call it a success. Maren did not call it safe.

At dusk, Kelva returned from the mill.

“Nothing moved,” she said.

Nereth shut her eyes briefly.

“I told you.”

Kelva put a hand against the girl’s cheek. Nereth leaned into it for a moment before remembering the people watching.

Before leaving, she went back to the watched support.

The crowd had dispersed. Without the people behind the old line, the place looked smaller, three lengths of wood beside an ordinary drainage cut. One keeper sat on an upturned box. The other stood near the marker, careful to remain on the side Riarhin had shown him.

“Anything?” she asked.

“Nothing I can see.”

It was the younger of the two who answered. He had a red mark on his cheek where he had rubbed it with the edge of his glove. He wanted her to look again. She could tell by the way he made room without asking.

Riarhin went to her former position. The water reflected a strip of grey sky. She moved her head until the stone marker lay at the angle from which she had seen the handhold. Nothing appeared.

“No stair,” she said.

He relaxed a little.

The older keeper asked whether she had seen anyone on it.

“No.”

“A shadow?”

“Something on a tread. I couldn’t tell what.”

“Not someone standing?”

“No.”

He looked at his companion. The young man stared at the water.

“What did you see?” Riarhin asked.

“I thought there was a person. Before you came back. Just the shoulder, there.”

She looked. A dark length of reed lay against the far bank. It might have made the shape he described. It had not been there when she watched the test.

“Do you still see it?”

“No.”

She asked him to show where he had stood. He had been farther downhill, carrying the box for the older keeper. They went there together. The ground was soft beneath the grass. From that angle the reed vanished and a pale patch of bank appeared in its place.

“That’s where,” he said.

Riarhin stayed long enough for her eyes to become used to the fading light. She could see nothing which justified leaving him reassured, or frightened.

“Tell the next watch exactly that. Where you stood. What you saw. Don’t let them make it more definite.”

He nodded. The older keeper looked relieved to have an instruction he could give someone else.

Riarhin helped move the box to firmer ground. They had brought food but no covering, expecting a short test. She fetched a spare cloak from the mill and came back with it. The young keeper received it with such gratitude that she wondered how much of the afternoon he had spent cold.

The dead woman had gone from the lower path. Esvin’s companion remained at the bend, looking after the place she had last been visible. Riarhin stopped beside him on her way home.

“It wasn’t your wife’s uncle,” she said.

“No.”

“Do you know who she was?”

He shook his head.

“My wife might. She’s better with faces.”

There had been very little face left to recognise. Riarhin let the remark pass. The man rubbed the back of his neck and said his wife would be angry that he had stayed so long when they had shutters to mend.

“Tell her you were waiting for me,” Riarhin said.

“Were you waiting for me?”

“No.”

He smiled faintly. They walked back towards the houses together. At the first gate he stopped to collect a mallet he had left there before the test, and Riarhin went on alone.

Riarhin drew the stair that evening.

She was not good at drawing, but she could put the handhold in relation to the three visible treads and mark the crack. Esvin watched from across the table. He did not offer an interpretation. After a while he turned the paper a little so the light fell more evenly on it.

“Is that how far down you saw?”

“Yes.”

“What was below?”

“A turn.”

“And then?”

“I don’t know.”

He nodded.

Pell came in and asked whether it was a plan for the new sluice. Riarhin said no. He examined it anyway and said the rise was too steep for a barrow. She found the observation unexpectedly comforting.

Nereth had not come to supper. Kelva kept a bowl beside the pot until the fire was low.

Chapter 10 — The man who came back

Riarhin knew the voice before she saw him.

“Perhaps she could sit down first,” it said.

The speaker sounded as though he was making a small, reasonable suggestion to people who would certainly agree once it had been explained. Riarhin stopped on the chapel step. Inside, someone protested that the questions were necessary. The voice agreed that they very probably were.

“Chairs are useful for questions too.”

She went in.

Cadan stood beside the repaired bench with a woman’s arm across his shoulders. His coat was wet to the waist. There was mud on his cheek and a tear in one sleeve. He turned his head when Riarhin entered, and his face opened in unmistakable pleasure.

“Oh,” he said. “There you are.”

The woman sagged against him. Riarhin crossed the room and took her other side. Together they lowered her onto the bench. She was middle-aged, narrow-faced, with lips so dry that the lower one had split. Her feet were wrapped in pieces of sackcloth. One hand clutched a leather strap that had been cut from whatever it once held.

Cadan knelt to lift her feet clear of the floor.

“A cushion, if there is one.”

The chapel keeper fetched a folded blanket. Riarhin put it beneath the woman’s legs. Esvin came from the little room behind the hearth with water.

“Slowly,” he said.

The woman reached with both hands. He let her hold the cup but kept his own hand beneath it.

Cadan stood. For a moment he simply looked at Riarhin. She had expected the old unease to arrive first. Instead she was glad, so glad that it embarrassed her.

She took his forearm.

“Cadan.”

“Yes.” He smiled. “I had wondered whether I’d find you here.”

“Why?”

“People were describing you.”

“Unkindly?”

“With a great deal of variation.”

She laughed before she meant to. He looked pleased with that too.

The woman on the bench began to shake. Both of them turned back at once.

Her name was Vessa. She had gone out carrying fittings for the new ward posts with three other people. The keeper who had hired Cadan had a record of their names and what each had been carrying. He brought it from a chest and held it in both hands while Esvin tried to get Vessa to drink.

“They were due back six days ago.”

Cadan looked at him.

“Five when I left.”

“Six now.”

“Then I was gone longer than I thought.”

The keeper’s mouth tightened. He was a small man with an old burn along the side of his neck, and had the air of someone who had repeated the same instructions until he no longer trusted his own wording. Maren had sent Riarhin to see him about the farther ward route. He now appeared to have forgotten that anyone had come for any reason except Vessa.

“Did you find the others?”

“I found where they had been.”

“But not them.”

“Not yet.”

Cadan said it without reassurance. He took off his wet coat, folded it so the mud lay inside, and placed it beside the door. Beneath it his shirt was dry at the shoulders and dark lower down. Riarhin could see where something had rubbed the skin at his throat.

“Where?” she asked.

He looked towards Vessa.

“She can hear us.”

“Outside, then.”

He nodded. Before leaving he asked Esvin whether she needed anything else. The singer said warmth and less company. Cadan went out at once.

They stood beneath the eaves beside the carrying poles.

The keeper followed with the record. He wanted answers, but Riarhin first asked what he had asked Cadan to do.

“Find them. Bring back anyone he could. Find out what had changed the route.”

“Which route?”

“The old way beyond the western frames. He used it before.”

Cadan nodded.

“A place I know. I thought I knew it rather better.”

“Why you?” Riarhin asked.

The keeper looked at her as if the answer were visible.

“He came through when the lower path was shut. Last autumn. Brought a man out with him. I asked at the places he said to ask.”

“And you were nearby?” she asked Cadan.

“Near enough for the message to find me. Not near enough to be prompt.”

He brushed mud from his sleeve. It came away in flakes.

“The mortal entrance is still there. Two fallen stones and a lintel with roots through it. Beyond that, the passage divides sooner than it did. I marked the division. On the left I could hear someone working. On the right I found her.”

“Working at what?”

“I couldn’t establish it.”

“What did you hear?”

“A repeated strike. Wood, perhaps. A pause. Then another.”

Riarhin looked at the closed chapel door. From within came Esvin’s low voice, ordinary speech rather than song.

“How did she get there?”

“She says the path turned. I haven’t asked her to explain it again.”

“And the other three?”

“One pair of shoes. A carrying frame. Fresh marks on a wall where someone had tried to climb. A voice further in, but I couldn’t reach it and bring her out within the same interval.”

“What interval?”

He showed her a short cord with knots along it, tied to a little metal weight. The cord was damp and discoloured at one end.

“I can keep the approach available for a while. It doesn’t remain obliging indefinitely. I had told him when to stop waiting at the entrance.”

The keeper nodded. He had waited longer.

Cadan turned to him.

“Don’t do that again.”

It was said pleasantly. The keeper looked at the ground.

Maren came before noon. Nereth came with her, having heard that one of the carriers had returned. The girl went straight to Vessa, stopped at the sight of her feet, and put her case down so abruptly that something inside rattled.

“I thought you’d gone to your sister’s.”

Vessa looked up.

“I wasn’t going to my sister’s.”

“I know. I meant after.”

Vessa’s hand tightened around the severed strap. Esvin put a stool beside the bench for Nereth. She sat but did not seem to know what to do with her hands.

Maren read the keeper’s record. Cadan answered the same questions again, adding no certainty through repetition. When he described the changed division, Nereth looked towards them.

“There shouldn’t be a division there.”

“No,” Cadan said. “I thought not.”

“What did the marks look like?”

He described them. She took out her notebook, found a page, and held it so only he could see.

“Like that?”

“Yes.”

Her face became still.

“Those aren’t meant to admit anything.”

“They did.”

“They turn away.”

“Something answered from the other side.”

Nereth shut the notebook. Riarhin saw Maren notice the movement.

“Show us,” the keeper said.

“No. You’ll copy it without knowing what it’s attached to.”

“Then tell us.”

“I can’t tell you all of it standing here.”

Vessa made a small sound. Everyone stopped.

“Not here,” Esvin said.

Maren looked at the woman on the bench, then at Nereth.

“Tomorrow. At the mill.”

The girl nodded once, looking sick.

The keeper had sent for Vessa’s sister. Until she arrived, the chapel seemed full of things being done for a person who had no strength to refuse them. Someone brought another blanket. Someone else wanted the window shut. A third visitor had heard that she should not sleep until she had eaten and stood calling her name each time her head sank.

Riarhin asked that visitor to wait outside.

“I know her.”

“So does he.” She indicated Esvin.

“He doesn’t know what she likes.”

“Then tell him from the door.”

The woman looked at her with open dislike. Riarhin moved aside so she could see Esvin, and the woman told him at length that Vessa could not bear broth with fat floating on it. Esvin thanked her and asked whether she would skim what had been brought. Given a bowl and a purpose, she quietened.

Vessa opened her eyes.

“My shoe.”

“Cadan has it,” Riarhin said.

“Don’t throw it out.”

“He’s repairing it.”

“Don’t.”

Riarhin looked towards him. His needle stopped.

“The other one’s gone,” Vessa said. “They’re not a pair.”

“We can find another,” the keeper began.

She shut her eyes. Riarhin crouched by the bench, leaving enough room that Vessa could turn away from her.

“Do you want it where you can see it?”

A small movement of the head. Cadan brought the shoe and set it beneath the bench, toe showing. Vessa looked at it until the little furrow between her brows eased.

The visitor returned with skimmed broth. Esvin lifted the bowl. Vessa took two mouthfuls, then put her hand against his wrist. He lowered it at once.

Outside, the keeper’s questions had acquired an audience. Riarhin could hear the changed division being described, each retelling making it more definite. Someone said the girl had put a gate in the wood. Someone else said the Aeler had always known it was there. Maren went to speak to them. The sound diminished without entirely stopping.

Vessa’s sister arrived with a folded nightdress, a comb, and nothing to say about gates. She looked at the bench, then at the keeper, and asked where the other bed was.

“There isn’t—”

“There will be.”

The keeper fetched a pallet. Riarhin helped him bring it into the little room behind the chapel. They had to move a chest, sweep the floor, and take a hook out of the wall where it would catch anyone rising in the dark. By the time they were done, Vessa was asleep again.

“Leave her,” her sister said.

They left the made bed waiting. The sister sat beside the bench and began combing the loose ends of Vessa’s hair, a few strands at a time, holding them above the knot so she did not pull at the scalp. Riarhin watched until the sister looked up.

“Food,” she said. “For later. Something she’ll recognise.”

Riarhin stood.

Riarhin went to buy food.

She had begun to understand why people sent her on errands. Going somewhere, obtaining a thing, and bringing it back made a shape she could complete without deciding what to do about a voice on the other side of a wall. She bought soft bread, a little honey, and broth in a covered pot from the house where Esvin had sung. The woman there knew who had returned. She refused payment for the broth and accepted it for the bread.

“Don’t let him sing over her all afternoon,” she said.

“He hasn’t.”

“Good. Sometimes he forgets people need sleep even when they have something interesting to say.”

Riarhin carried the pot carefully. The road was busy with ordinary work. A cart passed carrying reeds, the driver balanced high on the load. Two children argued over a hoop. The one who owned it was losing, which made the argument considerably more violent.

At the chapel, Cadan was repairing the strap on Vessa’s remaining shoe. He had washed his hands. The mud on his cheek was gone, leaving a scratch beneath it.

“I brought food.”

“How thoughtful.”

She gave him the bread and sat beside him while Esvin took the broth to the woman. Through the half-open inner door she could hear the cup touching the bowl, a small scrape, Vessa asking whether there was enough for someone else.

“Who?” Esvin asked.

“He was with me.”

Cadan’s needle stopped.

“Did you bring someone else?” Riarhin asked quietly.

“No one I could see.”

She looked at him. He did not soften the answer.

Vessa spoke again, too quietly for Riarhin to make out. Esvin asked her to repeat it. Cadan resumed sewing.

Later the woman slept.

Esvin came out and sat on the chapel step with his cup between his knees. Riarhin joined him. Cadan remained inside with the keeper, drawing the entrance and the altered division on a board.

“She heard a man,” Esvin said. “Walking beside her after the others went ahead. He told her when to stop.”

“Who?”

“She doesn’t know. She couldn’t see his face.”

“Did you hear him?”

“No.”

“Could he have followed?”

Esvin rubbed the cup with his thumb.

“I didn’t hear him,” he said again. “That’s what I can tell you.”

Riarhin watched a drop gather at the end of the thatch. It fell onto the step between her boots.

“You don’t like Cadan.”

“I don’t know him.”

“You look as though you don’t like him.”

“He says he’ll go back.”

“Yes.”

“As though somebody asked him to fetch a coat.”

Riarhin looked into the chapel. Cadan was listening while the keeper corrected the position of a stone. His hands rested patiently beside the drawing.

“He went back for her,” she said.

“I know.”

After a while Esvin stood and took the empty cup inside. Riarhin stayed on the step until Cadan came to sit beside her.

“It is good to see you,” he said.

“It is good to see you too.”

He seemed content with that. They watched the rain begin again, light enough that the people on the road did not hurry.

Then he asked, “What would you like me to do?”

She had been expecting the question. It still changed the afternoon.

“For now? Eat. Sleep. Tomorrow tell us exactly where you went.”

“And then?”

“I haven’t decided.”

“Very well.”

He took the piece of bread she offered him. It had honey on one side. He held it carefully so none dripped onto his repaired sleeve.

Chapter 11 — What can be kept

Kelva put the meeting in the working room because she wanted everyone to see what happened when the wheel stopped.

Pell objected to this interpretation of his workplace, to the chairs brought in from the kitchen, and to the number of people standing near a belt he had just dressed. Kelva told him to stop the wheel for an hour. He said it would take that long to persuade it to start again. She said he could begin persuading it while they talked.

He closed the feed. The machinery slowed through a sequence of familiar complaints and came to rest.

The silence was enormous.

People looked at one another, suddenly aware of the noises they made: a boot scraping grit, a chair leg, a cough which its owner had intended to lose beneath the wheel. Riarhin stood near the open doorway with Cadan. Nereth sat at the table Kelva had cleared from beneath a heap of sacks. Maren put her case beside it, within reach but unopened.

There were eleven people besides the mill household. Riarhin knew some by sight now: a woman from the upper gardens, the man who maintained the long drain, the family member who had watched the test. Two came from houses still under the Aeler line. One of those carried a folded receipt which he took out repeatedly and put away without reading.

Hesvar set a little box on the table.

“Common fittings. If we’re doing it.”

“Doing what?” asked the man with the receipt.

“Keeping our own.”

“That wasn’t what I was asked here for.”

“What were you asked?” Kelva said.

“To hear whether it was safe.”

Everyone looked at Riarhin.

She disliked the ease of it. She had stood at one end of a thread for an afternoon, and now her unfamiliarity made her suitable to settle what they had lived with for years.

“The ward turned a dead woman from the bank,” she said. “At the far marker I saw a stair where there should have been water. We stopped the test. One fitting is still attended because lifting it showed the stair again.”

“Did it turn her?” Kelva asked.

“Yes.”

“Then it works.”

“Something else happened too.”

“I heard.”

Nereth looked down at her hands. Maren opened her case and laid the marked cord beside the box.

“This is the distance it reached. Not what it was meant to reach. What we observed.”

The man with the receipt leaned closer. He could understand a cord. Several others moved with him.

They began with the grain.

Kelva wanted the withheld balance kept until the old repairs were made or a share of the maintenance payment returned. Sivrik wanted the grain already advanced against sent downriver while the argument continued. A woman from the upper gardens asked whether that meant everybody should pay except those who had already borrowed.

“It means some of it isn’t ours to hold,” Sivrik said.

“It was ours when we grew it.”

“And then we took money for it.”

“Not all of us.”

“No. Not all of us.”

He sounded tired before the meeting had properly begun. Riarhin watched him spread the corrected return on the table. The wet thumbprint remained at its corner. He put a small stone on it to keep the paper flat.

The man with the receipt said the Aeler crossing had stayed open during the tremor. Kelva said the mill approach had been left to Pell. Pell, from beside the motionless wheel, said he had opened an overflow and would appreciate people ceasing to call it a miracle.

Someone laughed. The argument loosened a little.

Riarhin listened while they separated what was already sold from what was promised for maintenance, what belonged to individual households from what had been collected together. The figures did not all agree. A boy was sent to fetch another return. Hesvar lent charcoal. Nereth remained silent until a woman asked whether the new fittings would cost less.

“Yes,” she said.

“How much less?”

The girl named a sum. Hesvar looked at her.

“For the first span,” he said.

“For the fittings.”

“And the replacements?”

“If they’re maintained—”

“They’ll still need replacing.”

Nereth took a breath. Riarhin saw her force herself to look at the woman who had asked rather than at Hesvar.

“I don’t know the yearly cost yet. The oldest has been up through one winter. Some pieces lasted. Some didn’t.”

That answer brought more attention than the promise of cheapness. The drain keeper asked which pieces. Nereth showed him.

The night watches caused worse trouble.

Everyone agreed they were necessary. Nobody agreed whose sleep should be interrupted. The woman from the gardens already tended a sick husband. The man with the receipt worked before dawn. Sivrik sometimes spent nights on the lighter. Kelva said Pell could not stand outside in the damp. Pell said he could stand wherever he pleased, then spoiled his argument by coughing until he had to sit down.

Riarhin fetched water. When she returned, the young woman from one of the protected houses was speaking.

“We could take the late watch. My sister and me. We close the drying room at midnight anyway.”

“Your house isn’t joining,” Kelva said.

“Not yet.”

“Then why should you—”

“Because the lane’s still our lane.”

Kelva looked at her. The girl held her gaze, then looked away first, but did not withdraw the offer.

Maren wrote the names down. She asked whether they could recognise a displaced mark in rain. They could not. Nereth began explaining. After a minute the girl held up a hand.

“Can you show us somewhere? Later. I won’t remember it like this.”

“Yes,” Nereth said.

She sounded almost relieved.
