They arranged two afternoons of teaching. Hesvar said he could provide broken fittings for practice, which earned him several comments about whether there were any other kind in his shop. He accepted the first, objected to the second, and threatened to charge for the third.

Riarhin sat on an empty sack beside the doorway. Cadan remained standing. She asked quietly whether he wanted her place.

“I am quite comfortable.”

“You’ve been standing an hour.”

“Yes.”

She gave up. Across the room Nereth was showing the young woman how to hold a support without touching its mark. For a moment both forgot that they were at a meeting.

When Maren asked for the instructions, the girl took the support back.

“I’m showing them.”

“I mean the whole working.”

“They don’t need that to keep it.”

“What if you’re ill?”

“They send for someone who knows.”

“Who?”

Nereth looked at the open doorway. She had been enjoying the teaching. The change in her was painful to watch.

“I can teach more people.”

“Then do.”

“Not with him taking a copy back.”

She pointed at the man with the receipt. He flushed.

“I haven’t said I’d take anything.”

“You still use their line.”

“So does half the port.”

“They’ll call it theirs.”

The room quietened. Riarhin heard water spilling beyond the stopped wheel. Kelva moved nearer the girl’s chair.

“Who’s they?” the drain keeper asked.

“The people who can make you pay for knowing it. The people who tell Hesvar what stamp he can put on salt. They’ll take it and say it was unsafe until they had it, and then we’ll buy it back.”

Hesvar did not laugh at that. He turned one of the pegs in his hands.

Maren set the cord down.

“I need to know how to stop a failure when you aren’t there.”

“I know.”

“Then we must find a way you can show me.”

Nereth looked at her notebook. Her thumb pressed the worn edge of its cover.

“I’ll show you the stopping sequence. Not the rest. Not yet.”

Maren accepted that with visible reluctance. The drain keeper asked whether the test could proceed before more houses were joined. Kelva objected, then looked at Riarhin and stopped herself.

Riarhin had not spoken. She was glad she had not had to.

Esvin arrived halfway through the argument about salt.

He had been at a house where an old woman wanted to speak to her husband before allowing a fence moved. Her husband, once invited, had said the fence was in the wrong place to begin with. Esvin had left them arguing about who remembered the tree correctly.

“Useful,” Kelva said. “Perhaps he can settle ours.”

“He liked the old levy. Said it was cheaper than losing cattle.”

“That was before the price went up.”

“He died before the price went up.”

“Then tell him.”

“I did. He asked whether the cattle had got cheaper.”

Sivrik laughed. Kelva did not. Esvin took a piece of bread from the plate without asking and sat beside Riarhin.

“Have I missed a decision?”

“Several attempts.”

“Ah.”

A man near the table asked whether he would sing at a gathering for the independent line. Something about keeping their own ground. Something people could remember.

“I can sing,” Esvin said.

“And the old words? The ones about choosing your keeper?”

“Which old words?”

The man gave him a fragment. Esvin shook his head.

“That one chooses the Aeler.”

“It needn’t.”

“It does.”

“You could change it.”

“I could write another. I won’t tell you your grandfather sang it.”

The man looked annoyed, then embarrassed. He said a new one would do. Esvin asked whether he wanted a tune people knew or one they would forget before the second verse. The discussion moved, gratefully, to something several people could disagree about without fearing for their homes.

Pell started the wheel before the meeting ended.

He warned them twice. The third warning was the sound of water taking the paddles. The floor stirred. Conversation rose to compete, then broke apart as people stood and moved chairs out of the way.

Kelva protested. Pell pointed at a waiting sack. Its owner had been standing outside for half an hour and needed meal before dark.

The meeting continued in pieces. Maren arranged the watched release with Nereth. Sivrik spoke to the two sisters about the lighter’s return. Hesvar took back the fittings no one had yet agreed to buy and left the practice pieces on the table.

Riarhin helped the man with the receipt move a bench. He looked as though he wanted to explain himself.

“My mother won’t sleep without the old bell.”

“You don’t have to tell me.”

“I know. I wanted to.”

She stopped moving the bench and looked at him.

“She hears it from her bed,” he said. “She’s heard it all her life. I can’t tell her it’s somebody else’s now.”

Riarhin nodded. They lifted the bench again and took it into the kitchen.

By the time everyone left, the table was covered in crumbs and marks in charcoal. Kelva rubbed one with her thumb, then fetched a damp cloth. Nereth sat beside her, copying the agreed watch names into a page which could be torn out without revealing the rest of her book.

“Eat here tonight,” Kelva said.

“I have to go back.”

“After.”

The girl hesitated, then put down the pen.

“After,” she agreed.

They had fish that evening, small ones which needed more attention than they returned in food. Pell complained about the bones until Kelva offered to mash his portion. He accused her of trying to hurry him into old age. She told him he had been there longer than she had been at the mill.

Nereth sat between the two sisters from the drying room. They had stayed to eat because the lesson had gone on until supper was ready, and because one had brought the fish. They were discussing a young man Riarhin did not know. His chief offence appeared to be asking each of them separately whether the other would attend a wedding.

“He could have asked her himself,” Nereth said.

“He did. She told him to ask me.”

“Then why are you angry?”

“I’m not angry. She is.”

The other sister threw a fish bone at her. Kelva pointed at the floor and made her retrieve it.

Riarhin took another piece of bread. The household argument continued around the table, accumulating detail until the young man’s intentions became less clear rather than more. Nereth laughed with her mouth full, covered it, and laughed harder when one of the sisters imitated his elaborate care in returning a borrowed bucket.

“I’ve never seen anyone court a bucket so well.”

“He washed it.”

“It had water in it before.”

“Clean water.”

“Not after he’d washed it.”

Pell said the young man had once borrowed a mallet and returned it with a new handle, which ought to count in his favour. This brought the argument briefly into a sphere where he felt entitled to take charge. Sivrik asked whether the handle had been broken before the loan. Pell said that was not the point.

Riarhin found herself laughing. She could not have said at which remark. Her food had gone cold while she listened.

Afterwards, the sisters persuaded Nereth to show them the practice fittings by lamplight. They set the frame on the kitchen floor and took turns finding the piece she had shifted. In daylight both had been quick. Now a shadow concealed one notch and made another seem twice as deep. The younger sister chose the wrong support, took her hand away when Nereth drew breath, and swore.

“You won’t be there making that noise,” she said.

Nereth closed her mouth.

They tried again. Riarhin stayed to hold the lamp. She thought her sharp sight would make the exercise easy and was wrong: she could see every scratch without knowing which scratches mattered. Nereth took the lamp from her and moved it to the height a watch keeper would carry it. The apparent damage changed.

The older sister sat back on her heels.

“I need to come tomorrow.”

“You’ve got the washing,” the younger said.

“Then you do it.”

“I’ve got work too.”

They argued about the hour. This time nobody laughed. Nereth offered to go to the drying room, and the sisters accepted so quickly that Riarhin wondered how many other walks the girl had promised to make.

When they had gone, Kelva began clearing the fish bones. Nereth gathered her practice pieces into the case. One had split during the lesson. She held the two halves together, checking the break, and put them in a separate pocket.

“Another one,” she said.

“Leave it till morning,” Kelva told her.

“It’ll still need doing in the morning.”

“Yes. And you’ll have slept.”

Nereth looked towards the door. Riarhin expected another departure. Instead the girl put the closed case beneath the bench and took the cloth Kelva held out to her. They washed the table together, moving around the places where the wood had risen and caught threads in the rag.

Riarhin went upstairs while they were still working. Below her, the wheel kept turning. She lay awake long enough to hear Nereth laugh once more.

Chapter 12 — No answer at the door

The house had been quiet for twelve nights, and its owner wanted something done about it.

Riarhin went with Esvin because he asked her to carry a lamp. He had two, and a small bag of things which clinked when he walked. The house stood beyond the upper gardens, close enough to the new line that its fence shared one of Nereth’s supports. The owner met them at the gate with her sleeves rolled up and a floury handprint on her skirt.

“She always came on the fourth,” she said.

“Your sister?” Esvin asked.

“My mother. My sister’s here.”

A woman in the doorway raised her hand. Esvin apologised, then asked whether the fourth meant the day of the month or the evening after the wash. Riarhin thought this an odd question until the owner answered, “After the wash, of course.”

Inside, a stool had been set beside the hearth. A little cup stood on it. There were fresh flowers in a crock, several already wilting in the warm room.

“She didn’t drink it,” the owner said when Riarhin looked. “We know that. We put it there.”

“I wasn’t going to ask.”

“People do.”

Esvin knelt to inspect the threshold. Above it hung a horn plate like the one at Kelva’s window. He did not touch it.

“When did this go up?”

“Thirteen days.”

“And she hasn’t come since.”

“No.”

He sat back on his heels.

“Did Nereth tell you what it kept out?”

“The dead.”

The woman heard herself and looked towards the stool.

“Not Mother.”

Nereth came after midday. She was carrying the practice frame and had been on her way to teach the two sisters when a child found her with the message. She set the frame by the fence and stood listening while the householder explained.

“It doesn’t know which you want,” she said at last.

“But you do.”

“I can’t put that into it.”

“Why not?”

The girl rubbed her forehead with the back of her wrist.

“Because wanting her doesn’t tell it what she is.”

The sister in the doorway folded her arms.

“Then take it down for an evening.”

“No,” said a voice from beyond the fence.

The neighbour had been weeding. He stood with a handful of roots, dirt falling onto his boots.

“You take that down, you’re taking ours down. That post’s common.”

“It’s our mother.”

“And I’ve no objection to her. I’ve an objection to whatever comes behind.”

Esvin asked them all to stop talking for a moment. They looked at him.

“We can speak outside the line.”

The owner stared.

“At night?”

“Before dusk, with people attending the approach. We don’t have to open your door to everyone.”

Nereth looked relieved and then defensive about being relieved.

“That would work.”

“Would it?” the neighbour asked.

“It would leave the ward alone.”

He considered this, then offered to stand at his side of the fence. He would not go outside, but he could keep the lamp there and call if anything moved behind them. His wife would have to agree. He went in to ask her.

The owner looked at the stool again.

“She liked it by the fire.”

Esvin’s face softened.

“I know.”

There was a smaller dispute about what to bring outside.

The householder wanted the hearth stool, its little cup, the flowers, and the shawl her mother had worn during her last winter. Her sister said the shawl had been given away. This was news to the householder.

“To whom?”

The sister named a cousin.

“The one who leaves things on the floor?”

“She was cold.”

“So was Mother.”

Esvin bent over his bag with an absorption Riarhin distrusted. He was letting the argument proceed. She moved the lamp from one hand to the other and waited until he looked up.

“Do we need the shawl?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then—”

He shook his head a little. The sisters were still talking. Neither had asked him to settle it.

Riarhin went out to examine the place beyond the line. The neighbour had fetched a spade and was lifting a sod where one leg of the stool would stand. His wife leaned from the window to tell him he was making it worse. He asked whether she wanted to come and do it. She said she had a loaf in the oven and an opinion was quicker.

He looked at Riarhin, hoping for an ally. She crouched to look at the ground.

“The board will bridge that.”

“I know.”

He filled the hole again, tamping it with the back of the spade. She helped him pull a bramble away from the path. Its thorns caught in her sleeve. He offered to cut it and she told him to wait while she freed the cloth.

“You’re not from here.”

“No.”

“Thought not. You looked surprised.”

“At what?”

“All this for a visit.”

She freed the sleeve. There was a tiny hole in it, scarcely worth mending until it grew.

“I’ve had visits take more arranging.”

He grinned, assuming a joke she had not intended. Then his expression settled.

“My wife used to go in there when the mother came. She liked the old woman. I did too. It’s no pleasure saying no to them.”

“Your fence joins theirs.”

“Exactly.”

He stood the spade against the post and went to fetch more boards.

When Riarhin returned indoors, the sisters had put the flowers back on the shelf. The shawl would remain where it was. The cup, however, was going outside. Esvin took it as gravely as he had taken the clasp. The householder watched him wrap it against breakage and thanked him in a voice which made Riarhin glad she had not interrupted the argument.

Preparation took longer than the conversation.

They chose a place beyond the far support where the ground was firm and the path back could be seen from the neighbour’s window. Nereth checked the line, marked where nobody should step, and explained to Riarhin which thread would catch a skirt. Riarhin cleared the low brambles. The householder brought a dry board to put beneath the stool, then another when the first rocked.

Esvin inspected the token: a small brass clasp worn almost smooth. The daughter explained where her mother had kept it. He asked whether she had used it herself. No, it had been put away after the funeral. He listened to the account of that day, including a disagreement about who had carried the outer pole, and asked the sister for her memory as well.

Riarhin held the lamp while they talked. A fly kept landing on the same spot on the back of her hand. She let it remain for a while, watching it rub its front legs together, then shook it off when it crawled towards her sleeve.

The afternoon cooled. The neighbour took his position and complained that he could see nothing except them. Nereth said that was the intention. His wife brought him a stool, which he accepted without acknowledging that he had said he would not need one.

At last Esvin began.

This song was different from the chapel’s. It had a little turn in it like a remembered household tune, which the daughters supplied and disagreed over. Their mother had sung it while cleaning fish. One remembered a line about a sailor; the other said it was a thief. Esvin tried both. The sisters laughed, briefly, then fell quiet.

A voice answered from beyond the lamp.

“It was your father.”

The owner put a hand over her mouth.

“He wasn’t either,” her sister said.

“He was both.”

They laughed again. The sound of it went out across the lower ground and came back changed by the water.

Riarhin stayed where she had been told to stand.

She could hear enough to know that the mother’s first concern was a garment which had been given to a cousin who would not look after it. The daughters defended the decision. The voice complained, then asked after a child. The answers were ordinary and detailed. A cough which had passed. A new tooth. A young man whose attentions had been noticed and were being discouraged.

The lamp smoked. Riarhin trimmed the wick with the small tool Esvin had given her. It brightened without flaring. Beyond its reach, mist lay low over the furrows. She could see a shape moving far away, too indistinct to tell whether it was a person.

Nereth came to stand beside her.

“I couldn’t make it choose,” she said quietly.

“I heard.”

“They thought I should.”

“Yes.”

The girl pressed her lips together.

“If I let one through, I have to make something it can answer. A place. A permission. Then something else can find it.”

Riarhin looked at her. Nereth kept watching the mist.

“Is that what happened at the test?”

“I don’t know.”

This time the uncertainty sounded unforced. She stood beside Riarhin until Esvin’s measure began to close, then went to check the path back.

The sisters carried the stool home together. They were talking about the cousin and the garment, still disagreeing, but without the anxious urgency that had filled the house when Riarhin arrived. At the door the owner turned.

“Will we have to do this every time?”

Esvin had lowered himself onto the board where the stool had stood.

“If you want to speak to her while the line is up.”

“I’ll make a better place.”

“Somewhere dry,” he said.

She nodded and went inside.

They packed in the dusk.

Nereth left first, late for the lesson and annoyed with herself. The neighbour waited until Riarhin carried the last lamp through, then took his stool in. His wife thanked them from the window. The new ward had not been moved.

Esvin remained outside the fence, looking towards the place where the voice had answered.

“Lamp,” Riarhin said.

He did not turn.

“Esvin.”

“Yes, I know,” he said softly. “But she didn’t ask—”

He stopped. Riarhin waited.

He looked at her, blinked, and put a hand over his mouth.

“Sorry.”

“What did you hear?”

“I thought she was still speaking.”

“Was she?”

“I don’t know.”

He picked up the bag. It slipped from his fingers. She caught it before the contents struck the board and gave him the lighter lamp instead.

They walked back slowly. He was tired enough to stumble twice on ground she had seen him cross without looking that morning. At the eating house she made him sit while she ordered food. He objected that she had bought the last. She said he could pay, which settled him.

At the eating house, a boy brought them a jug and asked whether the mother had said anything about rain. Esvin told him she had been more interested in clothes.

“Mine always is,” the boy said.

“Yours is alive.”

“That’s how I know.”

He went back to the kitchen. Riarhin watched Esvin’s hand tremble against the side of the jug and poured for him. He let her, looking away while she did it.

“You were still listening,” she said.

“Yes.”

“To her?”

“I thought so.”

The room around them had grown busy. A man was attempting to move a bench with two people still sitting on it. At the hearth someone complained that the fish had been better yesterday. The woman serving said he could wait until tomorrow and try saying it again.

Esvin turned his cup with both hands.

“There’s a moment after,” he said. “Sometimes. You hear something and you want to give it another breath. There may have been a word caught in the end of the song. Something they meant to say.”

“Is there?”

“Sometimes. Sometimes I remember asking a question I didn’t ask.”

She let that sit between them while the bowls arrived. He sniffed at his soup before taking up the spoon, hungry despite the exhaustion.

“Does it frighten you?” she asked.

He looked at her as if the question itself had surprised him.

“When I notice it.”

He began eating. Riarhin did too. After a few mouthfuls he put his spoon down and said, “If I do that in the wood, don’t wait until I answer politely.”

“All right.”

“I may object.”

“I’ve noticed.”

He smiled, then went back to the soup. She watched until the next spoonful reached his mouth without spilling.

The Aeler specialist arrived while they were eating.

He had come to speak to Esvin about an old agreement, having heard that the singer had addressed one of its witnesses years before. He was older than the grain official, with a beard gone silver near the mouth and a leather case scarred by travel. He introduced himself to the woman serving food before approaching their table, and waited until Esvin finished swallowing.

“I won’t keep you long.”

Esvin looked at the bowl.

“People generally say that before they do.”

The specialist smiled as though he had expected it and unrolled a copy of a compact beside the bread.

“There. The keeping of the outer line. You heard the witness?”

“Yes.”

“He affirmed the undertaking.”

“He said they asked for the line and agreed a share of the harvest.”

“So the present removals are against it.”

Esvin wiped his spoon carefully and put it down.

“I didn’t say that.”

“The agreement continues.”

“Does it? This is a copy. What happened to the promised repairs?”

“They are being undertaken.”

“When he spoke, he said the keeper would come before the autumn storms. That was what he remembered agreeing.”

“The circumstances—”

“May be different. Yes.”

The specialist glanced at Riarhin, then back at Esvin.

“His descendants continue to benefit.”

“And continue to pay. You asked what he said. I’ve told you.”

There was no anger in the singer’s voice yet, only fatigue making him less willing to help the man reach the desired conclusion. The specialist looked at the copied compact. He asked whether Esvin would put the remembered words in writing.

“As I heard them.”

“Of course.”

“Not with that added at the end.”

He pointed to a sentence on the copy. The specialist read it, nodded reluctantly, and rolled the document up.

When he had gone, Esvin looked at his soup. A skin had formed across it. He broke it with the spoon and tasted it.

“Cold.”

Riarhin pushed her warmer bowl towards him. He looked at it, then at her.

“I can ask for more.”

“I know. Take it before I change my mind.”

He did. Outside, the bell marking the evening passage sounded over the roofs. Neither of them got up until the food was finished.

Chapter 13 — The clearing order

The order was read in the storehouse yard because there was no place at the mill where its readers could stand without looking like visitors.

Riarhin went with Kelva and Sivrik. Nereth had been summoned separately and was late. Maren stood beside the weighing frame with the younger clerk and a maintenance officer whose coat carried the same mountain mark as the delivery board on the road. He had brought four people with tools and two with spears.

The grain official had a bandage beneath his collar. When he saw Riarhin he touched it, almost in greeting, then returned to the paper.

The order required unlicensed fittings attached to the common line to be surrendered for examination. Household work not touching the common line would remain pending inspection. Nereth was to provide the stopping instructions for the span left under watch. No one was to extend any new work until the keeper had reported.

Kelva heard the first sentence and stopped listening to the qualifications.

“You’ll pull them out.”

“Where they meet the common line,” the officer said.

“And leave us with the gap.”

“We’ll establish a temporary keeper’s span.”

“For how long?”

“As long as it takes.”

She laughed harshly. Sivrik put a hand on her arm. She shook it off.

Riarhin watched the tool bearers. They had poles, a covered box, a coil of wire, and two small bells padded in cloth. Whatever else the order meant, they had not come intending to leave the opening entirely bare. One man was examining the ground as if already deciding where the temporary supports could go.

Nereth arrived with mud on her skirt.

“Those won’t hold the lower bend,” she said.

The officer turned.

“You’ve tried them?”

“They’ve been tried. Before mine.”

“We’re not putting them where the old ones were.”

“Then where?”

He pointed on the plan. She stepped closer, looked, and put her finger beyond his.

“Water comes through there when the sluice backs. You’ll have to put it higher.”

“That leaves the outer house.”

“Yes.”

For a moment they were both looking at the same problem. Then the clerk asked for the notebook, and the girl’s hand withdrew.

The alarm began while they were still arguing over the outer house.

This time Riarhin saw the bell being rung. A keeper on the high bank pulled twice, paused, pulled again. Maren lifted her head, counted, and went to the yard gate. The maintenance officer followed.

“Western frames,” she said.

He called to the tool bearers. They lifted the padded bells and went with her.

The clerk looked at the order in his hands.

“Do we continue?”

The officer turned back.

“Not through occupied houses. Keep what is holding until we have the people in.”

He pointed the two spear carriers towards the export stores. They took position at the doors while workers brought the outer shutters down. Kelva watched them, then looked at Sivrik.

He had already gone to fetch the cart.

Riarhin ran after Maren. Her leg loosened after the first dozen strides. The pain under her ribs remained a reminder to breathe rather than hold herself tight. Nereth kept pace beside her, lifting her skirt out of the mud with one hand and holding the case against her body with the other.

“What failed?” Riarhin asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Is it yours?”

“I don’t know.”

They reached the high bank together.

Below, a line of people moved towards them from the western lane. A woman pushed a handcart with a child sitting on a sack. An older boy carried a cage containing two hens. Behind them came a man leading a cow which objected to every step. Farther back, where the lane narrowed, figures stood in the water on either side.

The old bell at the western frame rang once and stopped.

Nereth made a sound in her throat and ran down the bank.

The outer settlement consisted of seven houses, a drying shed, and a shared well on a raised patch beyond the maintained western span. Riarhin had seen its roofs from the mill road. Up close the houses had gardens, washing lines, and the small accumulated repairs which told her people had expected to remain.

The nearest family had left their door open. Wind moved a curtain within. A pot sat on the hearth beside a low flame.

“Put that out,” Riarhin told the boy with the cage.

He looked at her, frightened.

“I’ll hold them.”

He gave her the cage and went back. The hens shifted, offended by the change of bearer. His mother had stopped the cart at the narrowest part of the lane, unable to turn it past a fallen pole. Riarhin handed the cage back when the boy returned, lifted the pole, and held it while the woman pushed through.

“Which way?” the mother asked.

“High bank. They’ve opened a place by the stores.”

“We can’t stay there.”

“For now.”

The woman looked back at her house. Riarhin could see the calculation arriving too late: what had been left, what might be carried, how long it would take to go back. She put a hand on the cart’s side.

“Keep moving. I’ll look behind you.”

The child on the sack held a wooden spoon like a weapon. As the cart passed, he struck Riarhin’s sleeve with it, not hard. His mother apologised without turning. Riarhin told the child he could keep it ready.

He looked at her solemnly and lifted it again.

At the last occupied house, a man refused to leave until his wife had found a packet of seeds.

“They’re in the blue jar,” he said through the doorway.

“They aren’t.”

“Then the brown one.”

“That has salt.”

Riarhin stood at the gate, watching the water beyond the fence. A body had caught against a willow root. Its face was down. One arm moved slowly beneath the surface, stirring a little fan of mud.

“You need to go,” she said.

The man looked at her.

“She brought them from her mother’s.”

Riarhin went inside. The kitchen had a shelf of jars, four brown and one blue. She asked what the packet looked like. The woman described an oilcloth wrap tied with a green thread. Riarhin moved two pots and found it behind a stack of wooden bowls.

The woman snatched it, then apologised.

“Go,” Riarhin said.

They went. She checked the rear room, shut the inner door, and followed.

Outside, the body had freed itself from the root.

She kept her distance and took the upper side of the lane. The figure rose from the cut, water pouring from its coat. It turned towards the house she had just left. She did not stop to fight it. The living couple were still within reach, the man helping his wife over the fallen pole. She joined them and kept them moving.

At the bank, Maren’s temporary bell began to sound. The note was thin but clear. People crowded beneath it, watching the lane they had come from.

Nereth stood beside a new support below the bank. Her hands were wet. A line ran from the support towards the old frame and vanished where the grass dipped.

“Who moved that?” she demanded.

A young keeper looked away.

“Who?”

“I did. You said if it leaned—”

“Not that one.”

“It was in the water.”

“You were to leave that one.”

The keeper’s face reddened. Maren stepped between them.

“What happened?”

Nereth looked from the support to the lower lane. The figure from the cut had reached the first garden. Another stood behind it.

“It’s taking the refusal across the wrong ground.”

“Can you stop it?”

“Yes. But then that side—”

She pointed beyond the houses, towards another occupied rise farther west.

Maren followed the direction. Her jaw tightened.

“How long has it been doing that?”

Nereth did not answer.

The mother with the handcart came back down the bank looking for the boy with the hens.

He had gone to help someone else with an animal. She could see his cage beside the temporary bell, abandoned with the door still tied. Riarhin left Maren and Nereth arguing over the post and went to look along the lane.

The boy was beside the first garden, pulling at a goat which had got its tether round a fence stake. He was trying to drag it in the same direction that tightened the rope. The goat braced all four feet and yelled. Its owner stood in the doorway with a bundle under each arm, giving incompatible instructions.

“Let the rope go,” Riarhin called.

The boy turned. The goat took the opportunity to twist farther round the post.

“Let it go.”

He dropped it. Riarhin stepped over the fence, unwrapped the tether, and passed the free end through the gap to him.

“Walk. Don’t pull.”

The goat came two paces, stopped to eat something, and had to be pulled after all. Riarhin went to the woman in the doorway.

“Those the last?”

“My husband’s coming.”

“Where is he?”

“At his brother’s.”

“Inside the line?”

“Yes, but he’ll come here.”

Riarhin took one bundle so the woman could lock the door. The lock refused its key. They stood while she tried twice, her hands growing less capable with the need to hurry. Riarhin watched the lower water. The figure by the willow had moved out of sight, which she liked less than seeing it.

“Give it to me.”

The woman held the key tighter.

“I can do it.”

“All right. Lift the door.”

Together they lifted. The bolt moved. The woman pulled the key out and put it into her mouth while tying a knot in the bundle’s loose wrapping. Riarhin waited until the knot was finished before setting off. The boy and the goat were already on the upper bank.

At the bell the mother slapped her son’s shoulder, caught him by the coat, and said his name three times. He tried to explain. She would not let him. Riarhin put the bundle down beside the cage and left them to it.

The man with the cow had tethered it to the cart. Now he wanted to know who had room for milk that evening. His cow had to be milked whether or not the stores admitted cattle. Kelva, arriving with empty baskets, pointed towards the mill shed.

“Not in the yard,” Sivrik said. “The gate won’t hold her.”

“Then mend the gate.”

“With what time?”

She looked past him towards Dorran, who had come despite his injured hand and was already examining the cart’s loose side. He met her eye and lifted his sound hand.

“I can look.”

“That isn’t mending,” Sivrik said.

“It’s the bit before.”

They took the cow. The goat followed it without further difficulty, to the boy’s obvious resentment. His mother made him carry the hens as well.

Riarhin watched the little procession move towards the stores. Behind it, the temporary bell stopped while its keeper changed hands. In that brief silence she heard the low splash of something moving beyond the houses. Maren heard it too. She went back to Nereth.

The keeper who had moved the support was the same person who had helped her mend the earlier failure. Riarhin learned this in fragments while they worked. He had thought the sinking post was the same problem. Nereth had told him last time that shifting it higher would keep the lane clear. Something had gathered beyond the farther house afterwards, but nothing had entered.

“You knew it moved them,” Maren said.

“I knew they went round.”

“Where?”

“Where they could.”

“Nereth.”

The girl looked up angrily.

“What was I supposed to do? Let them through here?”

Maren had no answer ready. A child was crying behind her. The temporary bell sounded, stopped, sounded again under another keeper’s hand.

Riarhin looked towards the farther rise. Smoke came from one chimney. A door stood open. Whoever lived there had not joined the people on the bank.

“Who can get to them?”

“Upper track,” the young keeper said. “Round the back.”

“How long?”

“Too long if they come this way.”

Riarhin turned to go. Nereth caught her sleeve.

“Wait.”

From the empty old bell frame a voice spoke.

“Use the outer mark.”

Nobody stood beneath it. The frame’s little roof had slipped sideways. Riarhin could see daylight between its supports.

Nereth went still.

Maren lifted her handbell.

“Who is that?”

“Use the outer mark,” the voice repeated. “There is ground enough.”

It sounded neither distant nor loud. It had the ordinary carrying tone of someone giving advice from across a yard.

Nereth looked at the far support. Riarhin saw recognition in her face, not of a person but of a possibility.

“Don’t answer,” Maren said.

“I haven’t.”

“Have you heard it before?” Riarhin asked.

The girl pulled her sleeve free.

“I can keep that house.”

“Have you?”

She knelt beside the support and opened her case.

Riarhin took the upper track.

She did not know whether Nereth would accept the advice. She knew there were people on the rise who had not come in. The young keeper followed her carrying a lamp, though it was still daylight. They went behind the drying shed and along a narrow bank thick with nettles.

At the far house an old woman had put a chair against the door and sat behind it holding a kitchen knife. A man lay in bed in the inner room. He had been unable to walk for months.

“We heard the bell,” she said through the shutter. “We can’t come.”

“We can carry him,” Riarhin said.

“Through them?”

Riarhin looked back. Along the lower approach three figures had begun climbing. The bank she and the keeper had used remained clear, but not wide enough for two people carrying a bed without difficulty.

She asked for a blanket and two poles. The woman said there were bean poles behind the house, too thin. Riarhin went to look. Two could be lashed together on each side. It would take time.

The old woman came out, still holding the knife, and helped cut the bindings from the trellis.

Below them, the figures stopped.

Riarhin looked up from the knot she was tying. A pale thread of light had appeared between the far support and the house’s lower fence. Nereth stood at the distant frame with both hands raised to the fitting. She lowered one, made a small adjustment, and the figures turned away from the rise.

The old woman began crying. She was still holding the bean poles steady.

Riarhin finished the knot before she let herself look again.

Chapter 14 — The house she saves

They carried the man out while the new line held.

He was heavier than Riarhin expected, his body wasted in some places and swollen in others. The young keeper took the front. Riarhin took the back and found the weight pulling through the sore place at her ribs. She changed her grip. The old woman followed with a bag over one shoulder and the knife wrapped in a cloth.

At the narrow bend they had to stop. One bound pair of poles began separating where the wood tapered. Riarhin set her end down, retied it, and asked the man whether he could bear being tilted for a moment.

“I’ve borne worse beds,” he said.

His wife made an angry sound.

“Not mine.”

“No, love.”

He closed his eyes while they moved him. Riarhin looked towards the lower fence. A figure stood beyond the pale line, its face turned towards them. It did not cross.

They reached the bank slowly. People made room. Sivrik had brought the cart again, and they eased the man onto the folded sail. His wife climbed beside him, put the bag beneath his head, and took his hand.

Nereth watched from the old frame. Her face had lost all colour. When the old woman called thanks, she lifted one hand and then let it fall.

Maren went to her.

“What did you do?”

“Extended it.”

“With what?”

“The outer mark.”

“Whose?”

Nereth looked at the empty frame.

No voice answered now.

Riarhin saw the crack from the high bank.

Pall Watch-Tower stood across the water, a dark square ruin above the western approaches. Until that afternoon she had taken the line down its side for a long vine. Now it opened pale from top to bottom, no wider than a man’s arm, and within it she saw the stair.

The angle was wrong for the tower. The handhold showed at a height where there could be no landing. Something dark fell from the opening and struck the stones below.

“Look,” she said.

Maren turned in time to see the pale line close.

“What?”

“The same stair.”

Nereth looked up sharply.

“Where?”

Riarhin pointed. Cadan, who had arrived while the households were being brought in, stood beside the keeper with his head slightly tilted.

“I saw something fall,” he said.

“Can we get there?”

Eddis shook his head.

“Not across that. Round by the high bank.”

They sent two people while the rest remained with the families. It took long enough that Riarhin began to think the object would be gone by the time they arrived. The waiting people drifted towards the shelter by the stores. Some went back to fetch animals from places the keepers had checked. Others stayed where they had first stopped, unable to begin deciding what came next.

Riarhin found the woman with the seeds sitting on an upturned trough. She was holding the oilcloth packet in both hands.

“Did you get the cow?” Riarhin asked.

“My brother took her.”

“Good.”

The woman looked towards the distant roofs.

“I left the bread to rise.”

Riarhin sat beside her for a little while. Neither said anything useful. When the woman’s husband returned with a blanket, she got up and went to meet him.

The shelter by the stores had been built for sacks. Its floor was raised and dry, its roof low at the edges, and its only convenient entrance faced the yard where carts needed to turn. By the time the families had settled beneath it, the carriers were having to step over bedding to reach their loads.

A boy put a bowl on a crate. A storehouse hand moved it. The boy moved it back. Riarhin arrived as the man was beginning to shout.

“There’s bedding inside,” she said.

“I can see it. That’s the trouble.”

He showed her the mark on the crate and the matching mark on a board in his hand. It had to go onto the next barge. There were six more behind it.

The boy’s mother said nobody had told them.

“I didn’t know you’d be here,” the man answered.

They made a passage together. Riarhin moved the bedding while the mother gathered their things. The boy took his bowl and sat on an empty basket with it in his lap. He was the child who had struck her sleeve with his spoon. He recognised her and held the spoon up again, less fiercely this time.

“Still ready?” she asked.

He nodded.

The man carried the first crate through. Its corner caught the edge of a hanging blanket and pulled it down. He stopped with the load in his arms, waiting while Riarhin put the blanket back. They looked at each other over the crate.

“It needs another hook,” he said.

“Have you one?”

He pointed with his chin. She found it on a shelf and drove it into an existing hole with a stone. It was too large, but the blanket stayed up. Behind it the old woman was settling her husband on the folded sail. His cart had been brought beneath the awning until a better place could be found.

“Can you get me down?” he asked Riarhin.

“Where?”

He looked towards his wife, embarrassed. She understood first and sent Riarhin for Sivrik. Between them they helped him to the far end of the shelter and held up the blanket while he relieved himself. Afterwards he apologised for taking their time. Sivrik told him the grain would not grow legs in the next five minutes.

When they had him settled again, Nereth came with water. The man’s wife would not let her leave until she had sat down.

“You saved him.”

The girl looked towards the lane.

“I need to—”

“Sit. Drink some yourself.”

She sat. The old woman took her hand and held it between both of hers. Riarhin saw Nereth’s face alter under the gratitude, the frightened concentration briefly giving way. She drank when the woman pressed the cup on her.

The man on the sail told her about his wife’s knife. She had insisted on bringing it, he said, as if she intended to hold the lane alone. His wife replied that she could at least cut their supper with it, which was more than his walking stick would do. Nereth smiled into the cup.

Riarhin left them together. At the entrance the storehouse hand had finished moving his crates. He carried two empty ones back and set them where the family could use them as seats.

The object from the tower was a small iron tool with a split wooden handle.

Cadan recognised it. He had seen its pair beside the carrying frame on the other side of the altered passage. The keeper who had hired him confirmed that both belonged to one of the missing carriers. There was a notch in the handle where the man had fitted his thumb, and a little wire repair above it.

Riarhin turned it over in her hands. It was dry. Even the hollow around the rivet held dust rather than mud.

Maren looked at Nereth.

“You’ve opened something.”

“I kept the house.”

“Yes.”

The agreement seemed to unsettle the girl more than accusation. She looked towards the cart where the old man lay beneath blankets.

“I can make it secure.”

“Here?”

“No.”

“Where?”

Nereth did not answer. She took out her notebook and began writing, her hand moving too quickly for neatness. Riarhin watched the page fill and wondered how much of what the girl knew existed nowhere else.

Maren brought the fragment in a box padded with old wool.

It was a length of dark root or fibre, threaded through with something that caught the light yellow rather than white. At one end the weave had broken. At the other it disappeared into a small piece of worked stone worn smooth by handling.

Nereth looked at it with immediate hunger.

“Where did you get that?”

“It was kept at the old station.”

“You had it all this time?”

“I had a broken piece and no reason to think it could hold anything on its own.”

“Did you try?”

Maren met her eyes.

“Yes.”

They stood beneath the storehouse shelter, the evacuated households close enough to hear but occupied with food and dry bedding. Esvin had taken two children to fetch water. Cadan stood by the door. Riarhin could smell the wet wool beneath the fragment and the grain drying in the next yard.

“The Chord,” Maren said. “What remains of a pattern. The old accounts call it the First Curse, which has made people do foolish things with the name. It was part of the keeping before these bells were hung.”

“Can it close the opening?” Riarhin asked.

“I want to see what it can hold.”

Nereth put out a hand and stopped herself.

“May I?”

Maren nodded. The girl touched the broken end with one finger. She drew breath, very slowly.

“There’s more of it.”

“Supposed to be.”

“At the fortress.”

Maren looked at her.

“You’ve been there?”

“Near it.”

“How near?”

Nereth took her hand away.

“Near enough.”

They tested the fragment at a damaged stretch of the temporary line.

Maren chose a place where the ground was firm and the approach could be watched from both sides. Nereth helped place the supports. This time she spoke each movement before making it. Riarhin took the far end again. Eddis watched the water. Cadan remained close enough to the keeper to see the fitting but did not touch it.

The fragment lay between two pieces of stone. Maren passed a thread through the surviving weave, joined it to the temporary support, and rang the little bell once.

The air changed.

Riarhin could not have said at first how she knew. The distant sound of water seemed to settle into its proper direction. A reed which had been bending against the wind straightened and moved with the others. Beyond the mark, a wandering figure stopped, turned, and went down the lower bank.

No stair appeared.

They waited. Maren checked the near fitting. Eddis sounded the cut with his pole. Nereth stood still enough that Riarhin could see the effort of it in her fingers.

“Far side?” Maren called.

“Unchanged.”

They let it hold until the sun had moved clear of the storehouse roof. Then Maren released it in the reverse order. The figure did not return immediately. The water continued moving in the expected direction.

Nereth knelt beside the fragment.

“With the whole pattern,” she said, “we could keep more than this.”

“Perhaps.”

“And its seat.”

Maren’s hand paused over the box.

“What do you know about the seat?”

The girl was looking at the gold caught in the root.

“I’ve read about it.”

After the test, Nereth helped Maren wrap the fragment. Neither hurried. The keeper laid one fold of wool over the broken end and the girl moved it back to show how a fibre might catch. Maren examined it, agreed, and folded the wool differently.

Riarhin watched from the bank while Eddis took one more sounding. The water was no longer climbing, but the mud at the bottom had shifted. He marked a place where the ferries should not turn until he had checked it from a boat.

Behind them a dispute had begun over which evacuated households would sleep at the mill. Kelva could take two upstairs if the children shared. Another family had an offer across the lane, but the woman would not leave her mother in the shelter. Her mother, hearing this, insisted she was quite happy in the shelter. Neither sounded happy.

Nereth went to help with the bedding. Riarhin followed with two bundles. They carried them up the mill stair one at a time because the turn was too tight for both. The girl knew which door stuck and how to lift it. She knew where Kelva kept the spare chamber pot. She found a blanket Riarhin would not have recognised as spare because it was already serving as a curtain.

“You sleep here often?” Riarhin asked.

“When she catches me.”

They spread the blanket over a pallet. Nereth smoothed it, then took one corner back because she had covered the little boy’s spoon. She set the spoon beside the pillow.

“Does your mother know where you are?”

“She knows Kelva.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Nereth straightened. Her face closed, and Riarhin regretted the tone before she could improve it.

“Someone can take a message.”

“I send messages.”

“All right.”

The girl picked up the empty sack and brushed dust from it. Riarhin held the door while she went through. They met the family on the stairs and had to squeeze into the doorway to let them pass.

The child saw his spoon on the bed and ran to it. His mother thanked Nereth by name. Downstairs Kelva called that supper would be ready soon and anyone who meant to eat should wash. Nereth went to the basin. Riarhin watched her scrub her hands, her attention fixed on ink beneath one nail which would not come off.

By evening Nereth was gone.

She had eaten with Kelva, helped wash the bowls, and said she needed to inspect the western marks before night. Kelva had made her take bread. When she did not return, nobody was immediately alarmed. She had slept at other places often enough.

It was the young keeper who brought the note.

She had given it to him at the western frames and told him to take it to Maren after the household watch was changed. He had waited, as instructed. By then she had been gone more than an hour.

The note said she knew where the pattern had been anchored. She would make her work safe there. She had taken the journal because she would need the original sequence, not the parts she had copied. She asked Maren not to remove the outer mark while people depended on it.

Kelva read it twice. Then she put it on the table and began clearing the plates, though they had already been cleared.

Riarhin caught one before it fell.

“Kelva.”

“She took bread.”

“Yes.”

“She knew she was going.”

Riarhin put the plate down. Kelva stood with her hands open, looking at them as though she had forgotten what they were for.

Sivrik came in and read the note. He swore, once, with an exhaustion which made it worse than shouting.

Maren sent for the keepers.

They met without chairs this time.

The immediate work was divided first. Two people would stay at the altered span. The sisters from the drying room would take their promised watch with a trained keeper. Hesvar would supply replacements for the wet supports. Sivrik would check the lower sluice before dawn and arrange food for the families at the stores.

Then Maren said she was going after Nereth.

“You’re needed here,” Kelva said.

“So is what she knows.”

“Send somebody.”

“I need to see the seat.”

Cadan described the route he had used and where it no longer led as expected. Eddis knew the outer cuts and the approach to the wood. Esvin had two names among the missing, people whose households he visited. He would come.

Riarhin listened until they turned to her.

“I’ll go,” she said. “But we prepare in daylight. We don’t run after her through a wood none of us can see.”

“She’ll have another night ahead,” Kelva said.

“I know.”

Kelva looked as though she might strike her. Riarhin let her look. After a while the woman sat down on the edge of the table.

“Bring her back.”

“I’ll try.”

“Don’t say it like that.”

Riarhin moved closer. She had no promise she could make better by speaking it differently.

Kelva caught her hand, held it hard for a moment, and released it.

Arngrim refused to spare Haldren until he learned whose tool had fallen from the tower.

One of the missing carriers had taken his advance for a load which had not arrived. Another had worked his shore freight for years. The unsafe crossing had already cost him time, and the western alarm had stopped three households from bringing wool to the port. He listened while Haldren explained this, then said he understood his own accounts.

“Then understand where the man is,” Haldren said.

Riarhin stood by the rail and let them argue. Haldren had heard of the expedition from Sivrik. He had come to find her carrying a coil of line and asking whether they intended to cross wet ground with nothing to mend it.

Arngrim looked at the coil.

“That’s mine.”

“I know.”

“You’ve packed it already.”

“It packs quickly. It’s rope.”

The skipper looked at Riarhin.

“You’ll bring him back?”

“I’ll be trying to bring everyone back.”

He disliked the answer. So did she. Haldren set the coil down and waited.

At last Arngrim went below and returned with a small packet of nails, a wrapped saw, and a piece of oilcloth.

“Not the good adze,” he told Haldren.

“I wasn’t taking it.”

“You were thinking about it.”

Haldren accepted the tools. Riarhin helped him carry them ashore.

At the mill, Kelva had spread food and spare cloth on the kitchen table. Esvin was attempting to persuade Pell that a pot with a sound handle was worth lending. Pell said every pot had a sound handle when it left his keeping. Eddis sat by the door, mending a strap with a needle he held too close to his eyes.

Cadan sorted the loads as people brought them. He asked what each person could carry, accepted the answers, and then made Riarhin remove two things she had added to her own bundle when he was not looking.

“I can carry them.”

“Yes. But Haldren can carry one, and I can carry the other.”

She let him take them.

Kelva brought the loaf Nereth had cut at supper. A little flour remained on its dark crust where the girl had handled it after moving the frame. For a moment Riarhin thought Kelva would put it in the expedition food.

Instead she wrapped it and set it beside the pot.

“Breakfast,” she said.

They went on packing. Outside, the garden gate stood shut, the lower thread straight and still beneath it.

Part III — The people beside the path

Chapter 15 — Beyond the writ

Eddis would not leave until he had seen the ferry cross twice under its temporary keeper.

The woman taking it had worked the cut before he did. She reminded him of this while he stood on the landing pointing out a change in the current. She reminded him again when he told her about the nearer ring. On the second crossing she brought the bow so neatly against the fender that there was no sound at all.

“Will that do?” she asked.

“For now.”

“It’ll have to.”

He gave her the key to the little shed. She held out her hand for the other. He looked affronted, then fetched it from beneath the landing rail.

Riarhin waited with the packs. Haldren sat on one and ate the last of the bread Kelva had put into his hand after breakfast. Esvin was still at the chapel. Maren had gone to the western frame to hand over her duties. Cadan stood beside a post with his eyes closed, looking so restful that Riarhin wondered whether he was asleep until he moved aside for a passing cart without opening them.

Eddis came up the steps.

“She’ll be all right.”

“She seemed confident.”

“She is. That’s what worries me.”

He shouldered his pack and walked ahead of them towards the gate. Haldren offered Riarhin the end of the bread. She took it. It tasted of onion because he had stored it with his dinner.

Before the chapel, Riarhin stopped at Hesvar’s back door.

She had written a short inquiry to Aurine after the test span, using the address from their last correspondence. There had been too little in it to justify the cost: a stair which did not belong, an effect which continued after its supposed source was removed, a request for anyone worth asking about either. Hesvar had agreed to pass it with the factor’s papers. Now she asked him to hold any reply until she returned.

“Here?” he said.

“If you can.”

“People are always asking me to keep things.”

He put her name in the little book beside the door and asked whether the reply was likely to require feeding. She said she hoped not. He added a mark beside the name.

“Then it can go on the shelf.”

She paid for the forwarding before joining the others.

At the chapel Esvin was speaking to Vessa. She sat outside with her feet in clean wrappings, a cup of broth between her hands. The keeper had put a stool beneath them. Her face had filled a little, though she still looked startled whenever somebody moved quickly.

Esvin introduced Riarhin to the people waiting beside her. One was the wife of the man whose tool had fallen from the tower. Another was a younger brother of the last carrier. Neither asked Riarhin what she intended to do. They asked whether she had enough food and whether the return would be by the same road.

“If we can,” she said.

The wife handed Esvin a folded scrap of cloth. He opened it far enough to see the corner of a little carved piece and closed it again.

“If,” she said.

“I know.”

“Don’t use it if there’s another way.”

“I won’t.”

He put it inside his coat, separate from the other things in his bag.

Vessa caught Cadan’s sleeve as he passed.

“Don’t follow the tapping.”

He bent to hear her.

“I heard you.”

“It sounds like work.”

“Yes.”

“Then it stops.”

Cadan waited for more. She let his sleeve go and looked into her cup.

At the western frame Maren made the young keeper repeat the stopping sequence.

He had learned it overnight. He could show the first movement and the second without hesitation. At the third he paused and looked towards the lower support. Maren waited. He corrected his own hand before she spoke.

“If the water covers that mark?”

“Call the upper watch. Don’t raise the post.”

“And if nobody comes?”

“Move the people. Leave the post.”

She nodded. He looked older than he had during the alarm, though only by tiredness. Nereth’s notebook lay open on a borrowed stool, one copied page weighted with a stone. She had left enough instructions for this much. Riarhin watched the keeper fold the page carefully and put it inside a waxed cover.

The maintenance officer stood beneath the gate with a lantern in a metal frame. A piece of folded parchment was attached to its handle by a sealed cord. He gave it to Maren.

“Up to the old road station. The garrison has the matching mark.”

“Beyond?”

“Beyond, no one of mine is posted.”

Maren read the writ. It authorised passage, named the people going, and required any collected fittings to be presented on return. She pointed to the last clause.

“We’re bringing survivors first.”

“It doesn’t prevent that.”

“It will if someone wants it to.”

The officer took the paper back, added a line, and made the clerk witness it. Maren read it again before accepting the lantern.

The guards opened the gate.

Kelva had come to see them out. She stood beside Sivrik with her hands tucked beneath her apron, as if otherwise she might catch hold of somebody. Riarhin went to her.

“Tell her I have her bowl,” Kelva said.

“I will.”

“And don’t let her pretend she doesn’t know which.”

Riarhin nodded. Sivrik gave Eddis a last account of the water at the upper cut. Haldren shifted his pack. Esvin discovered a strap had caught beneath the pot and asked Cadan to free it.

There was no single moment at which the farewell finished. They moved through the gate while people were still speaking. The guards closed it behind the last pack. Riarhin looked back once and saw Kelva lift a hand, not waving, only holding it where it could be seen.

The first day’s road was cultivated.

They passed strips of grain, a field left to rest beneath weeds, a drainage ditch where men were setting new boards. The empty houses stood among occupied ones. A child watched them from a doorway while his mother hung cloth across a fence. Farther on, a gate had been tied shut with wire and the yard beyond was full of water.

Maren stopped at each maintained frame. Sometimes she spoke to a keeper. Sometimes she merely looked and made a mark on her cord. Riarhin let her set the pace until Haldren began shifting the weight of his tools from one shoulder to the other.

“We need a halt,” she said.

“I have one more.”

“It’ll still be there after we eat.”

Maren looked at the others. Esvin was pretending not to be out of breath. Eddis had found a dry place before anyone agreed to stop.

They sat beside a little wall and opened their food. Haldren took off his cap and wiped his head with it. The weather had become warm beneath the cloud. A thin mist remained over the lower water, moving as though dragged by a wind they could not feel.

“You walk quickly,” Esvin told Riarhin.

“Maren walks quickly.”

“I was being polite to her.”

Maren, eating at the other end of the wall, gave him a look.

He smiled. She almost did.

Cadan divided a piece of cheese with exquisite care, making portions so similar that Haldren accused him of using measures. He said it seemed discourteous to give one person less. Haldren told him to give himself less and save time. Cadan considered this seriously enough that Riarhin had to turn away to hide her amusement.

They rested until everyone had stopped trying to pretend they did not need it.

The low road was drowned beyond the last occupied frame.

Eddis tested it with his pole. The first few yards were sound. After that the pole sank nearly to his hand without finding a firm bottom. He withdrew it slowly, watching the mud cling.

“We go round.”

Maren looked towards the rising ground on their right. It was not high, but its stony shoulder carried trees and an old track which had not been used for carts in years. Brambles grew across the opening.

“How far?”

“Farther.”

Esvin made a small sound of agreement with whatever complaint she did not voice.

Riarhin went ahead to find the line of the track. There were hoof marks beneath the brambles, old and softened. A wheel rut lay filled with leaves. She cut the worst of the growth with the shortened Last Blade and bent the rest aside, tying one length back so the packs would not catch. The knife worked well for it. She cleaned it before putting it away.

The detour took them above the water. Through the trees they could see the roofs behind them and, farther off, the pale movement of a sail. The port had become a thing small enough to overlook. Riarhin disliked that. She had left a woman with seeds in a packet and a man with a hand he might not use properly again; from here they were no more visible than the grain stored beside them.

She turned back to the track. Haldren had stopped beneath a branch which caught his pack. She went to lift it clear.

The road station had no garrison inside its wall.

The guards were camped on the dry side, beneath a roof they had made from spare doors. Their sergeant examined the lantern seal, compared it with a stamp in a wooden case, and asked Maren who was keeping the western frame. She told him. He nodded, satisfied with the name rather than the paper.

“No farther cover than here,” he said.

“We know.”

“People don’t always.”

He offered water and a place to rest. They stayed long enough to refill their skins. Riarhin looked through the station’s broken gate at the inner yard. A tree grew from a crack beside the well. Its roots had lifted one edge of the paving.

“Why outside?” she asked.

The sergeant followed her gaze.

“Inside we keep hearing the relief arrive.”

“Does it?”

“No.”

He returned to his seat and began mending a buckle.

The sergeant had two empty water skins hung beneath the borrowed roof. He filled one for Maren and refused to fill the other from the station well.

“It’s good water,” Haldren said, looking through the gate.

“It looks good.”

“You’ve tried it?”

“My predecessor did.”

“What happened?”

The sergeant worked the repaired buckle through its strap.

“He stopped recognising his brother for a week. The physician said fever. Perhaps it was.”

Haldren looked at the well again and went to refill his own skin from the garrison’s barrel.

A soldier no older than Werrin came over to examine the lantern seal. He did not touch it. Riarhin saw him mouth the names on the writ as he read them, giving his own name silently the same careful treatment when the sergeant called for him.

“Is it always like that?” she asked after the young man had gone.

“What?”

“The relief.”

“Most nights.”

“How do you know it isn’t your own?”

“Mine come to the outside post and complain about the walk. The others go into the yard and sound pleased to be here.”

He threaded the buckle, pulled hard, and found it wanting. A stitch snapped.

“Would you hold that?”

She held it while he made another hole. His hands were broad and deft. He asked where she had learned to stand watch. She gave him the name of a company long dissolved. He knew the name by reputation and looked at her again, recalculating something which he was polite enough not to put into words.

“Was it better?” he asked.

“Some days.”

He grinned. She held the leather tighter while he drew the stitch through.

They left him mending under the roof. The young soldier waved them on from the outer post and reminded them that if they returned in darkness they should call from the road, not enter by the old gate. Riarhin repeated the instruction. He looked relieved that she had listened.

Beyond the station the ground fell again. Trees gathered close to the track. Their leaves were larger and darker than those on the rise, the trunks furred with moss where damp lay against them. Cadan led them to two standing stones with a fallen third between.

“Here,” he said.

Riarhin marked the approach with a strip of cloth and walked back until she could no longer see it. She returned, changed its height, and tried again. Eddis watched from the stones, then suggested a second marker lower down where a person coming from the water would look.

She put it there.

They checked the line together. When the others were ready, she counted them without speaking, touched the mark once to fix its place in memory, and followed Cadan between the stones.

Chapter 16 — Wrong places

The path smelt of wet leaves and old iron.

At first there were signs of ordinary passage: a cut branch, the print of a boot in mud, a place where somebody had stopped and laid a load against a tree. Cadan identified the last as his. He had set Vessa there while he went back for the strap she would not leave.

“Why did she need it?” Haldren asked.

“She thought someone was holding the other end.”

Nobody asked whether there had been.

They walked in pairs where the path allowed it and one behind another where it narrowed. Riarhin went ahead far enough to see obstacles, not far enough to lose the next person. Maren followed with the lantern. Its light looked feeble in daylight but did not go out in the damp. Eddis used his pole more often than his feet to decide where the ground began.

By midday the trees had changed. Their roots ran over worked stone. A square edge showed beneath moss, then another, too regular to be a natural outcrop. Riarhin knelt and brushed leaves from one corner. The stone held a shallow groove worn smooth on either side.

“A drain?” Haldren asked.

“Perhaps.”

“It ran somewhere.”

He looked down the slope. The groove ended beneath a tree whose trunk was wider than his shoulders.

“Not lately.”

They went on. Once Riarhin saw a pale mark tied beneath a low branch, one of Nereth’s new fittings. Maren examined it without touching. The thread led away towards ground none of them could see.

“She came this way,” Esvin said.

“Or put it here before,” Riarhin answered.

He nodded. The distinction did not improve his expression.

They halted where the path broadened around a fallen trunk.

Haldren set his pack down with a grunt and flexed the fingers of his left hand. Maren checked the lantern. Esvin drank, passed the skin, and discovered he had been carrying the pot with its handle digging into his back for the last hour. He asked why nobody had told him. Eddis said he had assumed the arrangement had religious significance.

Riarhin laughed. Then she heard the voice.

It was not a word. It was the movement before one: a breath, a small hesitation, the beginning of an amused objection. She knew it so immediately that she turned before remembering why she could not have heard it.

Beyond a tree to the left, someone seemed to be speaking at a great distance.

She stayed where she was and listened.

Esvin said something behind her. Haldren answered. Their voices obscured the other, and she felt a flash of irritation so strong that it frightened her afterwards. She moved one pace aside to put the tree between her and their noise.

There it was again.

Na’ar, before he told her she was making a simple thing complicated. Not the words. She could not hear the words. But the rise and fall were his, the roughness low in the breath, the way he left room for somebody to interrupt and went on when nobody did.

She stepped around the tree.

The path disappeared.

Riarhin stopped with one foot still moving.

She put it down carefully. There was moss beneath it, deep enough to hide a root. The fallen trunk should have been to her right. She could see no trunk. The tree she had rounded stood behind her, but its far side was covered in a pale fungus she had not seen on the approach.

The voice remained distant.

She turned her head, trying to place it. It seemed to come from beyond a low fold of ground. She could reach the top in a dozen steps. From there she would see—

She closed her left hand hard enough for the nails to hurt.

“Esvin?”

Her own voice sounded flat. No answer came.

She looked at the tree, the root beneath her foot, the shallow print she had made. She had walked perhaps four paces. She could remember each. That should have been enough to go back.

She moved towards the tree. The voice paused. She almost stopped to hear whether it would continue.

“Riarhin.”

Esvin’s voice, ordinary and much nearer, came from a direction she had not considered. She turned towards it and saw nothing.

“Say it again.”

He did.

She moved slowly, testing the ground. A branch brushed her face. She pushed it aside and saw the lantern.

Maren stood no more than six feet away. Behind her Haldren had one arm raised to hold a sapling out of the way. Esvin was standing on the path with his hands empty at his sides. Cadan looked beyond her into the trees.

Riarhin stepped through. The fallen trunk was where it had been. Her pack lay against it.

Nobody spoke at once.

“What did you hear?” Esvin asked.

“A man.”

“Did he call you?”

“No.”

“Words?”

She shook her head.

“Then don’t follow it.”

“I know who it was.”

The words came sharply. She heard the anger and could not put it away quickly enough.

Esvin looked at her, not flinching.

“The mist wants people in wrong places.”

“I know his voice.”

“I didn’t hear it.”

“Then how can you say?”

“I can say you went round a tree and couldn’t find us.”

She looked at the tree. From here its trunk was dark and wet, without the pale fungus. Haldren lowered his arm. The sapling sprang back into place, hiding the narrow gap through which she had returned.

Riarhin sat on the fallen trunk because she did not trust herself to remain standing without going somewhere. Her hands were shaking. She put them flat on her knees.

Esvin sat a little way from her.

“I didn’t say you don’t know him.”

She looked away.

“I heard him.”

“Yes.”

The agreement hurt more than she expected. For a moment she wanted him to tell her it had been a trick with enough certainty that she could be angry at him instead of sitting here with it.

He did not.

They ate before continuing.

Riarhin could not taste the bread at first. Then the salt in the cheese became almost painfully strong. She drank, returned the skin, and asked how long she had been out of sight.

“A little while,” Maren said.

“How long?”

“Long enough for us to call twice.”

She had heard neither. Cadan watched her without speaking. She wondered what he saw when he looked into a place like that and decided she did not want the answer just then.

Haldren moved his pack nearer hers.

“If you want quiet, tell us to shut up. Eddis likes doing it.”

Eddis nodded.

“I do.”

Riarhin looked at them. Haldren’s face held no pity she could resent. His cap had slipped crookedly again. She reached for her bread and ate another mouthful.

“I wanted to hear,” she said.

No one tried to make that smaller.

The afternoon path crossed a hollow where the water was clear enough to show pale stones beneath it. Eddis stopped them before anyone stepped down.

“Wrong bottom.”

Riarhin knelt beside him. The stones looked close. She could see a little dark leaf resting on one. Eddis lowered his pole. It passed the apparent surface of the stone and continued down until his arm was almost fully extended.

He drew it back. There was black mud on the end.

They went around the hollow.

At the first climb Eddis drove his pole into the mud above him and used it to get one foot onto a root. The root held. The next one came away in his hand. He sat down hard, slid nearly to the water, and stopped with his boot wedged against the trunk.

Nobody laughed. He lay still long enough to test each leg, then told them exactly what he thought of the ground.

Riarhin went up on the other side of the trunk. She used branches where she could see their living join, keeping her weight close until she reached a shelf big enough to kneel on. A narrow animal track continued above it. She tested the next two yards before letting anyone follow.

The packs had to come first. They were too broad to wear through the roots. Cadan tied one to a cord, and she drew it up hand over hand while Haldren kept it from catching below. Esvin’s pot struck stone three times. He apologised for each blow as though the pot had manners he had failed to teach it.

Maren came next, passing the lantern up before finding her foothold. Riarhin offered a hand. The keeper took her wrist, hauled herself too quickly, and struck her knee against the shelf. She stayed crouched beside Riarhin while the pain passed.

“You can swear,” Riarhin said.

“I have a mouthful of mud.”

“Afterwards, then.”

Maren spat, wiped her mouth, and supplied a phrase Riarhin had not heard before. She asked what one word meant. Maren told her. They both laughed, briefly and without pleasure, and then the next pack came up.

By the time everyone had reached the shelf, they had used most of an hour. Eddis had mud down the back of his collar. Haldren’s right hand would not close comfortably after catching the pack at the wrong angle. Riarhin made him hold out the fingers one at a time. They moved, and he told her she looked disappointed not to have found something to mend.

“Give me time.”

He took his hand back and shouldered the tools with the other.

Above the shelf the track narrowed again. They passed a tree with a deep scar down one side, sap dried black along its edges. Haldren stopped to look. Riarhin waited with him, expecting some judgment about lightning or rot.

“Somebody’s tried to cut a step,” he said.

She could see it when he pointed: a shallow flat place, old enough for bark to have begun growing round it. Another lay farther up, then a third beyond her reach.

They looked into the branches. Nothing moved. The steps led towards a cleft where the trunk divided, and the cleft was empty.

Haldren went on. Riarhin looked back twice before they lost sight of the tree. Riarhin carried Esvin’s pot for the steepest part so he could use both hands. He thanked her at the top, then complained that she had made it look easy and would now expect him to continue.

She told him she had expected that before.

He laughed. The sound was ordinary and close. She found herself walking nearer him afterwards, not enough to impede him, only enough that she could hear him breathe when the path climbed.

Near evening they found a narrow strip of firm ground beside an old wall.

The wall was no higher than Riarhin’s waist in most places. A tree had grown through one corner. Haldren examined the stones, pushed at two, and declared them unlikely to fall on anybody unless encouraged. They made camp on the inside, where the ground lay a little higher.

Riarhin took the first watch with Maren. The others ate, arranged their blankets, and argued quietly over which pack had carried the onion. Esvin claimed it had been entrusted to Haldren. Haldren said no such undertaking had occurred. Cadan found it inside the pot.

The argument continued after this, now concerning how it had got there.

Maren looked at Riarhin. The keeper’s face relaxed for the first time that day.

“Would you like to ask them to stop?”

“No.”

They stood together by the wall. The fog moved between the trees beyond it. Riarhin heard water, leaves, an occasional settling sound from the old stone. She did not hear Na’ar again.

When her watch ended, she lay down beside the others and listened to the small, familiar noises of people making themselves comfortable in an uncomfortable place. Haldren turned over, swore at a root, and turned back. Esvin began to snore, stopped, and apologised though nobody had spoken.

Riarhin put her face into the folded coat beneath her head and slept.

Chapter 17 — A roof for six

Rain found them before breakfast and accompanied them for two days.

It did not fall heavily. It drifted between the trunks, gathered on leaves, and waited until somebody passed beneath to empty itself down a collar. The path remained passable in stretches. Between them, Eddis tested the ground and the others watched him acquire more mud. Haldren began referring to particular puddles as old acquaintances.

Riarhin’s coat never quite dried. She woke with the same damp patch against her shoulder that she had warmed with her body the evening before. Her boots remained sound, which became an object of gratitude out of proportion to their appearance. Esvin’s did not. He wrapped one sole with cord and said he had wanted to know whether he could hate an inanimate thing.

On the second day they came to a place where the path had been paved.

The stones appeared one at a time beneath the mud, regular enough that Haldren began stepping on them deliberately. After a dozen paces he was walking as though through a market square. Riarhin watched the ground ahead. At the end of the visible stretch a row of young trees crossed the way. Beyond them the paving continued, cleaner and paler.

She put a hand on his pack.

“Wait.”

He stopped with one foot raised.

“What?”

“The trees.”

“They’re trees.”

“The ground between them.”

He lowered his foot beside the other. Eddis came forward and pushed the pole through the clean paving. It entered without a sound. The pale surface remained around it, unbroken.

Haldren looked back at the stones beneath his boots.

“That’s unpleasant.”

Eddis withdrew the pole. Black mud clung to its lower end.

“Round,” he said.

They went around. It took less time than Riarhin feared and more energy than anyone wanted to spend. A low branch caught Esvin’s repaired boot. He freed it with such elaborate restraint that Haldren offered to swear for him. Esvin accepted. Haldren supplied several phrases, each more specific than the last, until Maren told him she now knew more about his cousins than she needed.

At the far end they found a little raised place beneath an old tree. The ground was dry enough to sit. Cadan took the packs without asking and set them on exposed roots. Eddis lay back, shut his eyes, and said he had never wanted a bed so badly in his life.

“You wanted mine last night,” Haldren said.

“You put it on me.”

“Only a corner.”

“Your feet were in the corner.”

Riarhin took off one boot and shook out a small stone. She had felt it for the last hour and refused to stop for it. Now she looked at the redness it had made and wondered why she had been so determined to carry a stone.

Maren watched her rub the foot.

“Blister?”

“Not yet.”

“Good.”

The keeper had a small bag of grease. She offered it. Riarhin used a little and passed it to Esvin, whose heel needed more attention than he wished to admit.

Haldren took his own boots off. His socks were an extraordinary green, bright even through the mud.

Eddis opened one eye.

“What happened to those?”

“Wool,” Haldren said.

“I can see that.”

“My wife was given dye. More than she needed.”

“So you were punished.”

“Everyone was. The dog looked ill for a fortnight.”
