Riarhin laughed with her boot still in her hand. Haldren looked pleased and went on to explain that the dog had not been dyed deliberately; it had stolen the cloth in which the dye had been strained and carried it around the yard until the children persuaded it to exchange the cloth for a piece of fish. By then the colour had travelled well beyond its mouth.

“Did it come out?” Maren asked.

“Eventually. Less successfully from the children.”

Cadan listened with grave attention. He asked whether the dog had been harmed. Haldren said only in its dignity, which it had never possessed much of. Cadan nodded as though that settled an important point.

For a little while no one looked beyond the tree.

Then a drop fell from the canopy directly into Eddis’s open eye. He sat up swearing, and they began putting their boots back on.

The keeper’s old path failed them again toward evening, this time without deception. A bank had washed out. The remaining ledge was too narrow to take the packs safely, and below it lay a tangle of branches which would have made a fall difficult to survive. Riarhin went ahead without her load to see whether the ledge widened beyond the bend.

It did. She could have crossed it alone. She had crossed worse, years ago, with Ghere ahead of her and no interest in whether she disliked the height. She put a hand against the bank, felt the wet earth move beneath her palm, and came back.

“We go above.”

Maren looked at the slope.

“How far?”

“Until we find somewhere it holds.”

They climbed on hands and feet. Cadan passed the loads up one at a time, and Riarhin received them where the roots made a shelf. Halfway through, Esvin’s boot cord broke. He sat down very carefully and stared at the exposed sole.

“Don’t,” he told Haldren.

“I hadn’t said anything.”

“You were preparing.”

Haldren gave him another piece of cord. This time he showed him how to take it over the instep rather than around the weakest part of the leather. Esvin accepted the instruction without wit. He was too tired.

At the top they had to lower everything again. By the time they reached level ground, Riarhin’s arms shook with effort. The party had travelled perhaps fifty paces along its intended way.

They stopped where they were. Supper was cold. Nobody proposed a song.

Riarhin took the first watch and spent most of it listening to six different kinds of uncomfortable sleep. Haldren’s feet twitched beneath the blanket. Eddis complained once, without waking. Maren lay curled around the fragment’s box. When Riarhin moved a fallen leaf away from Esvin’s face he caught her wrist, frightened before he knew where he was.

“Only me,” she said.

He let go and apologised. She told him to sleep.

She remained beside him for a while because the ground there was no worse than anywhere else, and because he fell asleep more easily when she stayed.

On the third afternoon they found a shelter.

It had once been a keeper’s house, built against a low rise with a wall of stone at the back and timber elsewhere. Half the roof remained. The other half lay inside, covered in leaves. A little hearth stood beneath the surviving portion, its chimney stopped with a bird’s nest old enough to have grown moss.

Haldren looked through the doorway and took off his pack.

“Here.”

Maren glanced at the light.

“We could go farther.”

“We could. Then we’d be farther and wet.”

Riarhin stepped inside. The back wall was dry above knee height. There were no recent marks in the floor, no smell of occupied bedding, no sign of the disturbed repetition Cadan had warned them to notice. She checked the small space behind the hearth while Haldren tested the roof supports.

“Can you mend it?” she asked.

“For tonight.”

“And tomorrow?”

“If it doesn’t rain harder.”

She looked outside.

He followed her gaze.

“I heard it.”

They cleared the fallen roof in pieces.

Riarhin took the heavier timber with Cadan. Haldren sorted what could be reused, cutting away rot with quick, economical blows. Esvin and Maren carried leaf litter out. Eddis found the place where water had been entering beneath the wall and dug a narrow drain with a flat piece of wood.

When Riarhin offered an opinion about the roof angle, Haldren gave her the end of a pole.

“Hold that.”

“I meant if we—”

“Higher.”

She lifted it.

“There. Keep it there.”

He lashed a crosspiece, tested it, and laid the lighter timbers across. The angle was the one she had meant to suggest. She was almost certain he knew it. Her arms began to ache.

“How long?”

“Until I say.”

She looked at him. He glanced back with innocent attention.

“Or you can tell me about angles again.”

She laughed, lost half an inch of height, and was told to put it back.

They stretched the oilcloth over the frame and weighted the outer edge with trimmed branches. The covering sagged in one place. Haldren went outside, came back wetter, and adjusted the support. Water began running off where he intended.

He stood beneath it with his hands on his hips.

“Now you may advise me.”

“Eat something.”

“A sound woman.”

Esvin cooked.

He had opinions about the amount of water required, the order in which things should enter the pot, and the danger of stirring at the wrong time. These opinions had not been evident while other people cooked. Haldren observed this. Esvin said he had been allowing them experience.

The result was thick, hot, and burnt faintly at the bottom.

“That gives it flavour,” Esvin said.

“So would food,” Eddis answered.

Everyone ate. The smoke escaped through the cleared chimney, mostly. A little gathered beneath the new roof and found its way out through gaps Haldren had left on purpose, according to him. Riarhin sat with her boots near the hearth and her damp hair loose over her shoulders. She had taken off the coat and could feel the warmth reach the linen beneath.

Cadan found a clean splinter in the debris and used it to lift mud from the buckle of her pack while she ate.

“You don’t have to do that.”

“I know.”

He continued. The buckle had been sticking since the wet hollow. She watched his hands for a moment, then let him.

Maren opened the fragment’s box and checked the wrapping. The root-and-gold lay unchanged. She closed it before anyone could ask for another examination.

“Not tonight,” she said.

“No,” Riarhin agreed.

The keeper put it beneath her rolled coat, within reach, and accepted more of Esvin’s cooking.

The fire needed tending before it could be enjoyed.

Eddis had brought a load of branches from beneath the fallen roof, some dry at the heart and wet everywhere else. They stacked them close enough to steam, turned them when one side began to blacken, and moved the driest pieces nearer the hearth. The work had a soothing futility: there was always another wet branch, another edge of blanket needing to be turned towards the heat.

Riarhin unplaited the last knot in her hair and found a little clot of burrs near the nape. She had been carrying it since the climb. She worked at it until Maren, watching from the other side of the fire, asked whether she intended to remove the hair with it.

“I can reach it.”

“You can reach the wrong part very well.”

The keeper came to sit behind her. Riarhin handed over the comb. Maren examined its missing teeth without comment and used the sound end, drawing out a few hairs at a time while Riarhin stared into the fire.

It was difficult to remain still. She knew what was being done and could hear every small movement of the comb. She nevertheless had to suppress the wish to turn and see the hand near her neck. Maren waited whenever her shoulders tightened, then went on without asking why.

Haldren began telling Esvin that people in the north could predict weather from the way smoke came off a fire. Esvin asked what this smoke foretold. Haldren looked at the roof, the wet blankets, the water dripping from his own stockings, and said there was some chance of rain.

Maren laughed. The comb tugged.

“Sorry.”

“Worth it,” Riarhin said.

The burr came free at last. Maren held it up for inspection: a little dark knot, scarcely large enough to account for the trouble. She dropped it into the fire and began making the loose hair into a plait.

Riarhin reached for the comb, then let her hand rest on her knee. Maren tied the plait with a spare thread and patted her shoulder once before going back to her place.

Eddis was examining his boots. He had taken the insoles out and arranged them beside the fire, then forgotten which belonged to which foot. Haldren told him to put the larger one in the smaller boot and spend tomorrow composing a complaint. Esvin offered to set it to music.

Riarhin stretched her legs towards the hearth. Her hair lay comfortably between her shoulders. She could feel the neatness of the plait without needing to see it. When Haldren asked for the water, she passed it and kept hold a moment longer so he had to ask again. He accused her of hoarding. She let go and laughed.

Haldren sang when he had eaten enough to forget his wet boots.

The song concerned a woman crossing a sea with a golden spear, which Riarhin recognised only after the second verse because his tune wandered. She had heard a version in the north, though not lately, and remembered it as considerably shorter.

“In mine,” Haldren said when she mentioned this, “she has to arrive before she can kill anybody. People leave out the rowing.”

“That is often merciful,” Esvin said.

“You’d leave out the eating.”

“I should like to leave out your singing.”

Haldren ignored him and continued. The woman carried a sceptre beneath her arm and a spear in her hand. Her ship had nine—he stopped, frowned, and began the line again with eight. Eddis, trying to sleep beside the wall, opened one eye.

“Was that correction necessary?”

“Different verse.”

“Sing the one that ends.”

Haldren finished the stanza, then explained that his mother had sung a different one in which the thing beneath the woman’s arm was a caulking mallet and she was angry because someone had used it to crack nuts.

Esvin sat up.

“That one.”

“It doesn’t fit the tune.”

“Neither did the sceptre. Go on.”

He sang it. The mallet required an extra beat. The nuts required two. By the end, Maren was laughing so hard that she put down her bowl before she spilled it.

Maren caught Riarhin watching and tried to stop laughing. She managed a breath, looked at Haldren, and had to put her face in her hands.

“It’s a dreadful song.”

“It is,” Riarhin said.

“I know a worse one,” Esvin offered.

Eddis put his coat over his head.

Esvin did not sing. He told a story about a daughter who had been sent to fetch rain in a basket.

He told it with the formality of a man standing before a large company, though his audience consisted of five damp travellers and one pot. The daughter had three sisters, each wiser than the last, which caused the family considerable difficulty when anything needed doing. Their father had nine reasons for wanting the rain and would tell them to anyone foolish enough to ask.

Riarhin recognised the kind of telling. He let phrases return, each altered a little by what had happened between. Haldren interrupted to offer an unsuitable solution. Esvin incorporated it and made the next sister try it, to general satisfaction. Maren guessed the ending incorrectly and was made to wait longer for the right one.

By the time the daughter reached the rain, the basket had been mended, stolen, recovered, used to carry three chickens, and returned to her by a woman who did not admit stealing it. The rain refused to enter until the daughter stopped asking politely and put the basket down somewhere it wanted to go.

“That can’t be the end,” Haldren said.

“It isn’t. Her father wanted it in another room.”

Even Eddis laughed beneath the coat.

They sat after the telling with the fire settling into red hollows. Riarhin had forgotten to be cold. She knew the roof would need checking and the watch arranging. For a few minutes she let those things wait.

While the others settled, Maren took the empty pot outside to rinse it. Riarhin followed with a lamp. The rain had diminished to the soft continuous fall of water from leaves. Beneath the patched roof they could hear Esvin protesting that he had cooked and should therefore be exempt from everyone else’s assessment of the result.

Maren laughed once as she tipped the pot. Burnt food clung to the bottom.

“Sand,” Riarhin suggested.

“Find me some dry enough to be sand.”

She used a handful of wet grit from beside the step. Riarhin held the light while she worked it round the blackened place. The keeper’s sleeves had been rolled above her bruised wrist. The swelling from the gate rope was gone, leaving yellow at the edges.

“Still hurt?”

“Only when I remember it.”

Riarhin raised an eyebrow. Maren looked at the hand in the pot and smiled reluctantly.

“And then.”

She let Riarhin take the pot. It required more force than it looked. Esvin had burned the meal thoroughly. By the time the bottom came clean, Riarhin’s arms had begun to feel the roof work again.

Maren sat on the step. The lamp between them lit one side of her face and left the other almost invisible.

“Do you always do that?” she asked.

“What?”

“Find the next thing.”

Riarhin stopped rubbing.

“The next thing needs doing.”

“Usually.”

She took the pot back, rinsed it, and inspected the result. Riarhin waited for the rest of the judgment. None came. Maren set the pot upside down on a stone and flexed her wrist.

“When we go back, I need to change the lower watch. People will say I’m admitting the call failed.”

“It did.”

“I know. They mean admitting that it can fail again.”

Riarhin looked beyond the lamp. The trees had become a succession of wet surfaces, visible only where the light struck them. She could hear Haldren and Eddis disputing the amount of room occupied by a pair of feet.

“Will they refuse to keep watch?”

“Some. Some will insist on keeping it themselves and get offended when I tell them they have to learn.”

“What will you do?”

“Teach them while they’re offended.”

Riarhin smiled. Maren looked back towards the house.

“My mother used to sit outside her door waiting for me to come home from the frames. I told her she made me look incapable. She said she was entitled to sit outside her own door.”

“Was she?”

“Of course. That was what made it difficult.”

Maren picked up the pot and glanced towards the shelter. “I still go round the back when I’m late.”

They went in together. Esvin examined the pot and thanked them with such solemnity that Maren threatened to make him clean the next one with his sleeve. Riarhin put the lamp where it would not shine into Eddis’s eyes and went to find her blanket.

Cadan took the first watch.

Before going out he passed Riarhin her pack. The buckle worked smoothly. He had also retied the short strap so it would not catch under the flap.

“You should sleep,” he said.

“So should you.”

“I shall. Later.”

He looked towards the others, then back at her.

“It is pleasant when people know where they mean to lie down.”

She could not decide what answer he expected. Perhaps none. He picked up the lantern and went to the doorway.

Riarhin arranged her blanket beside Maren’s. Haldren was humming the mallet verse under his breath, trying to fit it to the tune. Esvin supplied a word.

“Worse,” Haldren said.

“Shorter.”

The new roof held. Riarhin put the folded coat beneath her head. Rain tapped on the oilcloth above her, and Haldren began the verse again.

Chapter 18 — The way the patrol turns

They left the shelter cleaner than they had found it, which was not difficult, and drier, which had taken most of the evening.

Haldren checked the roof before they went. He adjusted one weight, drove a peg farther in, and stood looking at the result with a satisfaction he tried to conceal when Riarhin noticed.

“It’ll keep somebody else out of the rain.”

“Yes.”

“Unless they pull it down.”

“Then they’ll get wet.”

He accepted this and shouldered his tools.

For two days the path ran through ground which had once been organised. Low walls appeared and vanished beneath roots. A raised channel crossed above them, dry in one stretch and overflowing in the next. They passed three doorways without buildings and a building without any opening Riarhin could find. Maren marked their route on the cord. Eddis began leaving paired stones where the ground might look different on the return.

Cadan found the carrying frame where he had left it. The straps had been cut, one recently, the pale inner leather still visible. He lifted the severed end, turned it over, and let it fall before moving on.

The cut frame could still carry weight. Haldren turned it over, pressed the joints, and looked at Cadan before touching the severed straps.

“Were they cut when you left it?”

“No.”

“Both?”

“One.”

Riarhin looked for prints in the wet ground. There were several kinds of disturbance, none clear enough to put a person to. A drag ran from beneath the frame to a root and stopped. Water had collected in it. She knelt without setting her pack down.

“Which strap was yours?”

Cadan pointed to the older cut. The edges were dark and stiff. She touched the newer one with the back of a finger. It had been pulled tight when cut; one corner remained whole, stretched thin before it tore.

“Someone trying to free something,” Haldren said.

“Or take the strap,” Eddis said.

The rest of it was gone. Riarhin lifted the frame enough to see beneath, then lowered it into the same depressions. A beetle worked its way out of the wet wood and disappeared under her boot.

Cadan was looking along the track.

“Did you leave anything on it?” she asked.

“A blanket. The water skin was already empty.”

“Where?”

He showed her. They found neither. Haldren wanted to bring the frame. Cadan said there was another farther on, where the passage had failed, and Haldren asked whether he expected that one to have kept its straps.

They took the frame.

It made the next hour worse. The path narrowed between young trees, and the bare uprights caught every low branch. Haldren tried it across his shoulders, then on one side, then gave it to Eddis and watched with satisfaction as the ferryman discovered exactly the same difficulties. At last they lashed it flat against the outside of Haldren’s pack. Riarhin cut off a protruding length of broken rail with the carpenter’s saw while he sat patiently facing away from her.

“Leave me some of it,” he said.

“I’m considering leaving all of you.”

“Then you can carry your own repairs.”

She brushed the sawdust from his collar. Some had gone down his back. He stood wriggling his shoulders and swearing at the frame as if it had arranged the inconvenience.

They came to a channel crossing before noon. Its water travelled beneath the path through a stone opening scarcely wide enough for a dog. On the far side, roots had lifted two paving slabs and left a little pocket of dry ground under them. Someone had slept there. Riarhin could see the depression in the dead leaves, the fire kept small enough to fit behind a flat stone. Three half-burned sticks had been laid parallel rather than kicked apart.

Maren looked at the place with the expression she had worn over the frame.

“We should look farther.”

“How far?” Riarhin asked.

“Until we know whether someone needs us.”

Riarhin took off her pack. She went round the lifted slabs, keeping the channel in sight, and found a scrap of wet cloth on a thorn. It was brown, or had become brown. She could not tell whether it belonged to a sleeve, a blanket, or a sack. Beyond it the ground ran away into a hollow full of young alder.

There were marks on a tree at the edge: two shallow cuts and a deeper one. She put her fingers against them. The spacing might have been deliberate. The bark had begun to close over the lowest.

“Anything?” Eddis called.

She returned with the cloth. Maren examined its weave, then passed it to Cadan. He shook his head.

Riarhin left it on the flat stone beneath the slabs, weighted so it could be seen. She told the others what she had searched and where she had stopped. Maren stood a moment longer at the mouth of the shelter. When they moved on she went last, looking back until a turn of the channel hid it.

That afternoon they saw the patrol.

Six figures came along a paved strip below the path, turned at a broken column, and went back behind a wall. Riarhin raised her hand. The company stopped. She knelt behind a root and watched until they returned.

The first carried a length of wood which might once have been a spear. The second had no visible weapon. The third dragged one foot, making a soft repeated scrape on the paving. The last wore a piece of metal at the throat, green-black with age.

They reached the column. Turned. Disappeared.

Riarhin waited through four circuits.

“They don’t look up,” Haldren whispered.

“Not yet.”

The path they needed crossed the paved strip beyond the column and entered a narrow opening in the wall. Nereth’s pale marks ran along its near edge. Riarhin could see three supports, the thread between them held clear of the moss. The patrol pressed close to that edge when it turned, as though the marks had narrowed the place it expected to use.

Cadan looked at the opening.

“That was clear before.”

“When?”

“When I came for her.”

Beyond the wall, in the direction they needed to go, something struck wood. A pause. Another strike.

Vessa’s warning returned to Riarhin with unpleasant clarity.

She went down alone to examine the crossing.

The paved strip was wet but sound. The opening beyond it had a fallen beam across the lower part, easily stepped over. Above, branches and a length of old timber hung from the broken wall. The timber swayed slightly whenever the patrol passed beneath, though none touched it.

Riarhin tested the beam with her foot. It held. She could put the company over one at a time while the patrol was behind the wall, provided nobody caught a pack or stopped to look. She returned and explained the movement without making it sound easy.

“Maren first with the light. Eddis. Esvin. Haldren. Cadan, stay with me until they’re across.”

“Why me before him?” Esvin asked, pointing at Haldren.

“So he can push if you stop.”

“I won’t stop.”

“I hope not.”

He looked at the crossing and swallowed.

They waited for the turn. Riarhin counted by movement, not aloud. When the last figure disappeared, she brought Maren down. The keeper stepped over the beam, lantern held high, and waited beyond. Eddis followed. Esvin caught the pot against the wall, freed it with a breathless apology, and climbed through.

Then the beam shifted.

Maren had set one foot on the far side and found ground which no longer held. Her boot sank. She grabbed the beam to steady herself, and the far end dropped into the hollow beneath the opening.

Haldren stopped with one leg over it.

“Back,” Riarhin said.

Behind the wall the scraping foot had already begun its return.

Haldren tried to lift the beam. It had wedged against his boot. Riarhin caught his pack, pulled him clear enough to free the foot, and pushed him through towards Eddis. The patrol appeared beyond the broken column.

The first figure turned its head.

Riarhin drew Remorse. She stepped to the wider paving where she could move, keeping the wall off her left shoulder. The broken spear came towards her chest. She let it pass outside her arm, cut at the supporting wrist, and moved before the body could fall against her. The second figure reached past it.

There was no cry, no breath, only the scrape of material on stone.

She cut the reaching arm, thrust where a living throat would have been, and felt the point strike something hard behind it. The body did not fall. She withdrew and gave ground, her feet finding the places she had tested on the first approach.

“Cadan. Through.”

He was helping Haldren lift Maren from the soft ground beyond the beam. He looked back, judged the distance, and obeyed after the keeper was clear. Riarhin let another thrust go past, cut low at the knee, and heard the joint give.

The figure fell across the narrow part of the paving. Two behind it stumbled. She turned towards the opening.

A woman stood beyond it with a golden spear.

For an instant Riarhin thought she was living.

She had the stance of someone who knew the weapon, weight settled, hands apart, the spear’s point occupying the place Riarhin needed to enter. Her clothes were dark beneath pieces of old armour. Hair lay close against one side of her face, wet or bound. She looked past Riarhin towards the figures on the paving with an expression of furious concentration.

Then she struck at Esvin.

He fell back against the wall. The spear took the place where his throat had been. Eddis pulled him sideways. Maren raised the lantern and called a title Riarhin recognised from Haldren’s song.

“Golden Thorn!”

The woman turned on her.

The movement was so fast that Riarhin barely saw the shaft cross the space. It hit Maren’s measuring case and drove it from her hands. The case struck the wall, opened, and spilled cord and rods into the hollow where the beam had fallen.

“Don’t call her that,” Riarhin said.

She came over the beam low, bringing Remorse across to turn the next thrust from Maren. The contact rang through her left arm. The spear’s blade slid along the sword and freed itself before she could bind it.

The woman looked at her.

There was recognition in the face, but Riarhin did not think it was recognition of her. Behind those eyes something else was happening, some approach being defended, some person being kept from a place they had no right to enter.

Riarhin moved sideways into the broken room beyond the opening. The spear followed.

She kept the distance. She could not close safely against that reach, and there were too many people behind her. She needed the woman turned, not defeated.

“Left wall,” she told the others. “Under the hanging timber.”

Haldren understood first. He pushed Esvin towards the gap beneath the slanted beam. Cadan took Maren by the arm. Eddis followed, looking back as though compelled to keep seeing what he feared.

Riarhin stepped towards the woman, invited the thrust, and withdrew diagonally. The spear’s point passed close enough to part the cloth at her shoulder. She felt no wound. Remorse caught the shaft for the length of one breath. She used that breath to turn the woman farther from the gap.

“Go.”

The hanging timber was held by roots and an old binding.

Riarhin had seen it sway from the other side. Now she saw the strain where the binding crossed a broken stone. Haldren ducked beneath the low end. Esvin’s pot struck it and set it moving. The woman glanced towards the sound.

Riarhin threw a piece of fallen masonry against the opposite wall.

The spear followed the louder sound. She moved for the gap, drew Toothpick with her right hand, and cut the taut binding where it lay across the sharp edge of the stone. It resisted the first stroke. She cut again, feeling the tension part beneath the blade.

The timber dropped.

She went under it as the far end struck, roots tearing from the wall in a cascade of dirt. Haldren caught her coat and pulled. The beam crashed between them and the woman. A golden point came through the falling leaves, stopped short of Eddis’s face, and withdrew.

They ran until Riarhin made them stop.

Not far. Far enough to put two turns of ruined wall between them and the crossing, not far enough to lose the path altogether. Esvin was gasping. Maren had the lantern clutched against her body with both arms. Haldren bent with his hands on his knees.

Riarhin listened.

No footsteps came after them. From the crossing, faintly, came the old scrape. Then the repeated wooden strike began again, at its former interval.

Maren wanted the case.

“No,” Riarhin said.

“I need the measure.”

“Can you make another?”

“Not that one.”

“Can you work without it?”

The keeper stared at her, breathing hard.

“Less well.”

“Then less well.”

Maren looked away. Riarhin knew she had spoken too harshly and did not know how to improve it without allowing the discussion to continue. She checked Esvin’s throat. The spear had grazed the skin below his ear. Blood ran in a thin line to his collar.

He held still while she pressed cloth to it.

“I said I wouldn’t stop,” he managed.

“You didn’t.”

“That should count for something.”

“It does.”

His laugh shook the cloth. She told him to stop moving.

Cadan opened his hand. In it lay a small metal piece, no larger than the last joint of Riarhin’s thumb, threaded through a broken cord. Marks had been cut into one face.

“It caught on her case,” he said. “When it fell.”

“Where from?” Maren asked.

“The wall. There were things hung there.”

Riarhin remembered a row of dark shapes behind the woman, taken in without attention while she watched the spear. She had thought them roots.

Maren reached for the piece, stopped when Cadan did not release it, and held her palm open. He gave it to her.

The keeper turned it towards the lantern.

“A keeper’s mark. Old.”

Haldren straightened slowly. He looked at it over her shoulder.

“Not yours,” he said.

Maren glanced at him.

“Linn,” he said. “I think.”

They stayed until everyone could walk steadily. Riarhin cleaned the blades and checked the cut in her coat. There was only a red line on the skin beneath. She put Toothpick away, then Remorse, and took the lead along the path away from the crossing.

Chapter 19 — The names on a key

The man in the reeds watched them for some time before speaking.

Riarhin saw him first as a break in the pattern of stems. She stopped. He remained still, perhaps hoping she would mistake him for another kind of shape, until Eddis said, “Put the hook down. We can see you.”

The man rose with difficulty. He was old enough to move carefully but not old enough to enjoy being treated as old. A reed hook hung from his hand. Bundles lay tied behind him on a little sled.

“I wasn’t hiding.”

“You were doing it badly, then,” Eddis said.

The man looked at the company, their packs, the cut on Esvin’s neck, the lantern.

“You’ve been at the crossing.”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

“Then you should have gone round.”

She waited. After a moment he looked embarrassed by the uselessness of the advice and pointed towards a rise beyond the reeds.

“My shelter’s there.”

They followed at a distance he appeared to prefer. The shelter was a low roof over a dry patch, half hidden beneath the bank. Bundles of reeds formed one side. A small fire smoked beneath an opening lined with flat stones. Beside it stood two cups, though he seemed to be alone.

“My nephew comes,” he said when he saw Riarhin notice them. “When he can.”

“Is he expected?”

“Not today.”

He gave them room near the fire and asked for payment before offering food. Riarhin was relieved by the directness. She paid. He added water to the pot and divided what had been cooking into smaller portions without pretending it was plenty.

The little sled had caught its runner between two roots outside. The reed-cutter had been trying to free it when they arrived; he kept looking through the entrance while the pot came back to the boil. At last Haldren set his cup down and went out.

“Don’t pull that end,” the man called.

Haldren stopped with his hands already on it.

“Then come and show me.”

The man went. From inside Riarhin heard their exchange, first suspicious, then technical. Eddis took his food outside to watch. He returned for his pole and did not come back for some time.

The sled arrived at the entrance with one bundle untied and the freed runner leaving a groove in the mud. Its owner put both hands against his back.

“Rotten underneath.”

“Only at the fastening,” Haldren said. “The runner’s sound.”

“I meant the ground.”

“So did I.”

The man stared at him, then gave a short unwilling laugh. He fetched a dry strip of willow for the fastening. His fingers shook too much to push the end through the narrow gap. Riarhin reached towards it, stopped, and asked him to turn the sled so she could see.

He showed her how the strip must go flat before it twisted. She did the first turn wrong. He clicked his tongue, unwound it, and let her try again.

“Pull it now. While it’s wet.”

She pulled. The strip held. He tested it with his hook and grunted.

Inside, Maren had moved one of the reed bundles to make room for Esvin’s feet. The man put it back as soon as he saw.

“That one’s sold.”

“I haven’t damaged it.”

“I didn’t say you had.”

He counted the bundles with a touch to each, looking particularly hard at the one she had shifted. Maren drew her feet under her. Riarhin watched him check the knot in the binding, loosen it, and tighten it to the same place.

They ate the thin stew with bread from their own packs. He had a little salt wrapped in a leaf and was generous with it after Esvin asked. The singer complimented the stew. The reed-cutter looked into the pot.

“It had more in it yesterday.”

“Then I wish we’d come yesterday.”

“You’d have had to pay more.”

Esvin smiled. The man appeared pleased that the joke had been recognised. He put the salt nearer him.

After the meal he set Riarhin to holding the loose reeds while he tied the opened bundle. She sat by the entrance with the stems across her knees, feeling them slide against one another whenever she relaxed her hands. There were more varieties than she had noticed: some hollow and ringing, some soft enough to flatten beneath her thumb. He sorted them by touch.

“These go to roofs?” she asked.

“Some. These don’t.”

He showed her the finer ones. People used them for screens, baskets, the inside of cheap doors. A good buyer kept the bundles separate; a bad one mixed them, then complained that the roof leaked.

“Your nephew sells them?”

“When he comes.”

“You could take them down with him.”

“I could.”

She waited for another sentence. He drew the binding tight, passed it round, and asked her to lift the bundle so he could get underneath. She did. By the time it was finished her knees were numb.

The man stood the bundle beside the others. There was a space behind them where a person could sleep, protected from the draught. He pulled a folded coat out of the way before setting the reeds down. It was too small for him.

He had stayed for his daughter’s belongings.

She had gone farther into the wood with a group gathering old fittings before the new disturbances. Two returned. One had seen her leave the path towards a bell sound. They had brought back her outer coat and a basket. Her reed knife, a pair of earrings, and a little keeper’s key had not been found.

“Not worth much,” he said. “Except the earrings. My wife’s.”

Riarhin asked how long he had been searching.

“Long enough.”

He stirred the pot, scraped the bottom, and looked at Esvin.

“You could ask.”

“Do you know she’s dead?”

The man stared at him.

“No.”

“Then I’d rather not begin by calling her among them.”

For a moment the reed-cutter looked angry. Then his face changed in a way Riarhin found difficult to watch.

“No,” he said. “No, don’t.”

Esvin asked where the coat had been found. The man described a stand of trees beyond an old water gate. Eddis knew the approach but not the ground after it. They spoke for a while over the man’s scratched map in the dirt.

Maren took out the metal token.

“Have you seen a mark like this?”

The man leaned closer. He held it at an angle to catch the light, then went to a bundle beneath his bedding and fetched a long, narrow piece of metal with a loop at one end.

“My father’s. Hers was the same kind.”

The keeper’s key had marks running along its length, some nearly worn away, others cut more recently. Maren compared them with the token. Near the loop was a shape made from three strokes joined at an angle.

“There,” she said.

Haldren shook his head.

“Turn it.”

She did.

“Not a number. That’s part of a name.”

Haldren knew the form because his wife’s family had kept old boat marks.

He was not certain of every stroke. He said so before Maren could begin treating his memory as a book. The reed-cutter supplied the sequence of the key’s keepers as far back as he knew it. Beyond that were names spoken in his father’s stories, people who had maintained crossings before the coast’s present lines.

One name sounded close to what Haldren read on the token.

“Asket,” he said. “Or something worn into that. I’d like a better light.”

They moved the lamp. Riarhin held the key while Maren turned the token. The joining marks matched, not perfectly but in the same order, and a cut beneath them which the keeper had thought damage appeared on both.

“A house?” Riarhin asked.

“A keeping,” the reed-cutter said. “People joined it. Married into it. Took the duty. Not all one blood.”

“From the Linn?”

“Some. A long time back.”

Haldren traced the air above the token without touching it.

“Asket of the crossing. That’s as much as I’d swear.”

The man looked towards the reeds.

“There was a woman with them. In my father’s telling. She brought people downriver.”

“Golden spear?” Esvin asked.

“Yes. But he told it differently when he was mending. I liked that one better.”

“Why?”

“She swore more.”

Haldren smiled. Maren did not. She was looking at the token as though the name had made it heavier.

The reed-cutter would guide them to the old water gate.

He named a price for the journey and a separate sum if they wanted him to wait there. Riarhin asked him to wait only long enough to be sure they had the next path. He accepted that. He would not go farther, whatever they offered.

“I’ve been that way.”

“What happened?”

“I came back. I don’t want to discuss how near a thing it was.”

She let it rest.

Before dark, Riarhin went with him to move the last of the cut reeds above the damp. The bundles were light until she had carried several; then the cut ends began to abrade the inside of her arm. He told her to turn them the other way and watched while she discovered why that was worse.

“You’ve never carried reeds.”

“No.”

“You looked as if you had.”

“What does that look like?”

“As if you don’t want telling.”

She shifted the bundle onto her shoulder. He took the other end without asking and they carried it between them.

From the bank she could see the narrow lanes he had cut through the standing reeds. One went much farther than the others, out towards a patch of water reflecting the evening sky. At its beginning the stems had been tied back rather than cut.

“That way?” she asked.

He looked where she was looking.

“No.”

She did not ask what question he had answered. They put the bundle on a rack. One end of the rack had sunk, and she helped him pack stones beneath its foot. He turned each stone until it sat as he wanted. A longer one required both their hands.

Her sleeve rode up. His eyes rested briefly on her wrist, then on her face. She waited for the question about her age, or her ears, or whether she could hear something he could not.

“Mind the sharp side,” he said. “It’ll cut through that coat.”

They finished before the light failed. On the way back he asked her to leave the hook by the door where he could reach it from his bed.

They slept beneath his roof and a second sheet Haldren rigged between the reeds. It was cramped and smelled strongly of smoke, but the ground was dry. The man talked after supper, not about the forest but about a dispute over the price of cut reeds. He believed the buyers at the port had agreed among themselves to pay less. Eddis thought them too quarrelsome to agree about anything for long. The discussion became animated enough that Esvin asked whether they could settle it after he had slept.

Riarhin lay listening. She had expected the man to speak of his daughter again. He did not. Near dawn she heard him move, pour water into one cup, and leave the other where it stood.

The route to the gate ran along firmer ground than any they had used since the station.

The reed-cutter knew where the old bank had sunk and where a fallen tree could be crossed without breaking through. He pointed out a hollow which looked harmless and would have taken a person’s leg to the thigh. Eddis tested it anyway. The man looked pleased rather than offended.

At the water gate, he stopped.

Beyond lay a narrow stone passage beneath roots. A little chamber opened to one side, its floor above the damp. The man pointed to a place where the wall had been scratched clean of moss.

“They used to leave things there. For the keepers.”

Maren looked at the token.

“Here?”

“If it’s theirs.”

The reed-cutter accepted his payment. Riarhin asked what she should do if she found the knife or earrings.

“Bring them to the port. Hesvar knows my nephew.”

“And if we find her?”

His mouth moved once before he could answer.

“Tell her I’m angry. She’ll believe that.”

Riarhin nodded. He went back along the bank, carrying the hook over one shoulder. At the first bend he turned to point out the sound footing once more, then disappeared into the reeds.

They stood for a while after he had gone. Esvin looked at the chamber, then at the token in Maren’s hand.

“Before we go farther,” he said.

Chapter 20 — The companion’s complaint

Esvin asked for time to learn the name.

He said it as Haldren had said it, listened while the carpenter corrected a vowel, and repeated it until Haldren told him that was as close as he could make it. Then he asked Maren what the keeping mark meant. She knew only the order of duties it appeared beside on the key.

“That will have to be what we offer,” Esvin said.

“Is it enough?”

“I don’t know.”

They made the chamber ready. Riarhin and Eddis checked the passage. Haldren moved loose stones from the floor and found a place where the lantern could stand without spilling. Cadan remained at the outer opening. Maren set the token on a dry piece of cloth.

The chamber had been used for other purposes since its keepers left. Haldren found a nest tucked behind the lamp shelf and moved the lantern to the floor instead. Maren discovered fish bones in a crack and a piece of fired clay too recent to belong to the stonework. She laid the fragment outside rather than putting it in her bag.

“How long?” Riarhin asked Esvin.

“How long to prepare, or how long to call?”

“Both.”

He rubbed his face with his palms.

“To prepare, until I can do it without rushing. To call, I need someone else to keep the time.”

Haldren offered. Esvin showed him the length of a phrase, let him hear it twice, and asked him to count its repetitions on his fingers. Haldren said he counted better on wood. He set six little chips on the stone beside his knee.

“If I haven’t begun to close when you move the last, say my name.”

“And if you don’t answer?”

“Say it again.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“And then?”

“Don’t touch the token. Take the water away from it, if you can. Tell me we’re leaving.”

He glanced towards the passage as though checking that there would be somewhere to leave to. Cadan was testing a loose slab with the edge of his boot. He turned it over and put it down flat.

Maren took a place near Esvin. Her damaged supply of measuring cord lay in her lap, repaired with the knot Haldren had made the night before. She wound it round one hand until Riarhin asked her to stop.

“Why?”

“If you have to move, you’ll catch it.”

Maren looked at the cord and loosened the turns. She set it beside her instead.

They agreed on the questions. There were too many. Maren wanted to know what the old keeping had guarded, how the high place could be approached, whether the woman with the spear had always struck at strangers. Haldren asked which words Esvin needed him to supply. Eddis wanted to know whether the water gate still opened and to what.

“Who he is first,” Esvin said. “Then whether he’ll speak to us.”

“He may not be able to tell us,” Maren said.

“Then we may not be able to ask.”

She pressed her lips together. Riarhin expected a reply and was grateful when none came.

Esvin took off his outer coat. The room was cold, but he said the collar hindered his breath. He folded it twice, reconsidered, and spread it beneath his knees. Riarhin placed the water where he could reach it with his eyes closed. He reached for it once to make sure.

Haldren set his first chip apart from the other five.

Esvin did not use the instrument. He listened to the water beyond the gate and shaped his first notes around its uneven movement. Riarhin had begun to recognise the preparation in his breathing, the care with which he settled himself before giving a name into the space.

“Asket,” he sang.

Nothing answered.

He sang the keeping mark as a phrase Haldren supplied, not words Riarhin understood. The carpenter frowned at his own memory and corrected the phrase once more. Esvin began again without complaint.

The lantern flame leaned towards the token.

The first reply was angry.

It came from near the floor, sharp enough that Maren drew back. Riarhin understood none of it. Haldren caught one word and said it aloud, uncertainly. Esvin let the reply finish and asked again, changing the address.

This time the voice used words they could follow, though some were old or oddly placed.

“Late.”

“We’ve come to ask,” Esvin said.

“Always after.”

The singer waited. Riarhin could hear his breath beneath the silence. Cadan had turned his head slightly towards the chamber but remained at the opening.

“Who sent you?” the voice demanded.

“We found your mark. We want to know who kept the crossing.”

“I kept it.”

“And the woman?”

A sound like a laugh, cut short.

“She left it.”

Maren leaned forward.

“With the golden spear?”

The lantern shook. Esvin lifted a hand, stopping her next question.

“Let him.”

The voice broke, returned to the same grievance, lost words which seemed to matter to it. A boat had been brought too far upriver. Wounded people needed the crossing. Someone had ordered withdrawal. The woman had refused, then gone back towards the high place when Asket believed she should have remained with those she had brought.

“She said one more,” the voice said. “Always one more.”

Riarhin looked at the token. There was no face to read, no body whose pain she could judge. The anger sounded human. That did not tell her whether it was fair.

“Did she come back?” Esvin asked.

“Not to us.”

The voice asked how many they had brought.

“Six,” Esvin said.

“Walking?”

“Yes.”

“No wounded?”

“Not who need carrying.”

Riarhin saw Haldren touch his shoulder. A load had rubbed the skin there raw beneath the shirt. He took his hand away when Asket spoke again.

“Then they can wait.”

Esvin looked towards the token. His next question was gentler.

“Who had to go first?”

The answer was a confusion of people described by what they carried or could no longer do. A man holding his own foot. A child too quiet to wake. Someone with a bundle which had to be taken from her before she would step into the boat. The voice called one person by a name and another by a place. It corrected itself angrily when the place was wrong.

Maren had charcoal ready. She wrote only a few marks before Riarhin could see that she was losing the sequence. The keeper turned the cloth and started again on a clean corner.

“Where were you sending them?” Esvin asked.

“Down.”

“Past the lower crossing?”

“Down.”

A drop struck the token. Riarhin looked up. The ceiling above it was dry. Water gathered along the metal edge, trembled, and ran onto the cloth.

At the doorway Cadan changed his footing. She turned enough to see him without breaking Esvin’s view of the mark.

“What?” she whispered.

“The way is still here.”

“That wasn’t an answer.”

“It has become something I need to watch.”

She went to stand beside him. Beyond the opening, the water gate seemed deeper than it had when they entered, the far wall more distant. Eddis stood on the other side of the threshold, his pole set across a place she could no longer see. He raised his hand when she looked towards him. The gesture was ordinary. She held on to that while returning to the lamp.

Asket was speaking about a child again. He said the child had refused to leave because someone had promised to come back. Esvin asked whom. The reply broke into words neither Haldren nor Riarhin understood. One sound repeated until it became difficult to hear as speech.

“Enough,” Maren said quietly.

Esvin held up a hand. He waited for the sound to finish, then asked whether the boat had gone. The voice returned to the quarrel at once, speaking so clearly that Riarhin could hear the catches in its anger.

He had given them his boat, it said. He had not given them leave to break it.

Haldren leaned forward.

Haldren asked about the boat.

The question changed the reply. Asket described a sprung seam, a tool borrowed and not returned, a quarrel with the woman’s sister over whether the hull could take another passage. His words became clearer when he complained about work.

“What tool?” Haldren asked.

The answer was unfamiliar. Haldren tried another word. The voice rejected it impatiently, then described the thing in enough detail that the carpenter nodded.

“A caulking mallet.”

“She carried it like a lord’s staff,” the voice said. “Wouldn’t put it down. Said we’d lose it.”

Haldren looked at Riarhin. She remembered the song in the shelter, the extra beats for the nuts. She kept her face still and listened.

The woman had a sister who wanted the boat taken downriver. They had argued with people waiting to cross around them. Asket remembered the sister’s hands, split from work. He remembered the woman’s promise to return before the water changed. He did not know what had happened at the high place. When Maren asked about the druid, the answer became confused and bitter again.

“Ask her.”

“We don’t know how,” Esvin said.

“Then learn.”

The singer bowed his head, not in agreement with the rebuke but to take another breath. Sweat had gathered beneath his hair despite the cold chamber.

Riarhin moved the water cup closer to his hand.

Haldren moved the fifth chip.

The voice had become caught on a rope. Asket was describing where it should have been fastened, how many people had passed it without seeing that it was running against the stone. Each time Esvin tried to return to the woman, the reply brought them back to the rope.

“At the ring,” Asket said. “I told him. At the ring.”

“Who?” Maren asked.

No answer came to that. The water beyond the gate slapped once against something hollow.

Riarhin watched Haldren’s hand hover over the last chip. His brow had furrowed. He was listening to the account of the rope with the same attention he gave an actual defect in a boat.

“Was there a second fastening?” he asked.

The voice rose sharply. Esvin lifted a hand, asking Haldren to wait. The carpenter withdrew his hand from the chips and set it on his knee.

“No more,” Riarhin said softly.

Haldren looked at her, then at the stone. He moved the last chip.

“Esvin.”

The singer nodded. He had heard. His next phrase fell differently, its last note lower, leaving a space which Riarhin had not noticed in the earlier exchanges. For a moment the chamber seemed to become only a chamber again. She heard Eddis shift his foot in the passage. A little dry mortar fell near her hand.

Then Asket spoke of the sister, and Esvin kept the space open for the answer.

The name came almost by accident.

Asket repeated something the sister had shouted. The first part concerned the tool. Then a short name, spoken in the altered intimacy of someone quoting a family argument.

Yrdis.

Riarhin heard it clearly.

Maren wrote it on the edge of her cloth with charcoal. The mark held for a moment. Then the letters seemed to lose their relation to one another. Riarhin looked from them to the keeper’s hand. Maren stared, rubbed the cloth, and tried again.

The second attempt became a dark smear.

Esvin’s voice faltered. The reply grew faint.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said to Maren.

The keeper stopped writing.

Asket was asking for something now. A message. A woman at the lower crossing was to be told that he had not left with the boat. He had stayed where he said he would. The name of the recipient came broken, repeated until Haldren thought he had it.

“I’ll look for her,” Esvin said.

“Tell her.”

“I will try to find who can receive it.”

“Tell her.”

Esvin closed his eyes. Riarhin saw the struggle before he spoke again.

“I won’t promise she’s waiting.”

The voice thinned to a sound beneath the water. He held the measure a little longer, long enough for another reply if one came. None did. At last he let the final note end.

The lantern stood upright. Maren’s cloth lay beneath the token with a patch of black across one corner.

They stayed in the chamber while Esvin recovered.

He drank, ate a little, and insisted he was capable of standing before discovering that he was not. Haldren caught his elbow. Esvin thanked him and sat down again with an expression of profound annoyance.

“Did you hear it?” Maren asked Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“What was it?”

Riarhin said the name. She did not write it.

Maren looked at the cloth.

“Say it again.”

She did. The keeper listened, then tried to repeat it and stopped halfway, uncertain of the sound she had just heard.

Haldren could retain part of it, enough to recognise the shape when Riarhin spoke. Eddis had heard the argument but not the name. Cadan said the passage had grown narrower while it was spoken. He did not know why.

Riarhin remembered every inflection. She could hear the sister’s quoted impatience, carried in Asket’s voice. It had not sounded like a title or an invocation. It had sounded like a woman tired of asking another woman to put down a tool.

“Is it hers?” Maren asked.

“The person he was quoting spoke it to her.”

“That could mean—”

“Yes,” Riarhin said. “It could.”

Esvin wanted to call once more before they left.

“He may have more of the sister’s account.”

“He said goodbye,” Riarhin said.

“He faded.”

“You ended it.”

The singer looked at the token. His face was tired and stubborn.

“I could ask one thing.”

Maren began to speak, then stopped. Haldren busied himself with the lantern fastening. Riarhin sat beside Esvin rather than standing over him.

“You promised to look for the message’s recipient.”

“I know what I promised.”

“Can you do that if you can’t walk?”

He turned towards her sharply, ready to answer. Then he looked at his hands. They were still shaking a little.

“I dislike that being a good question,” he said.

“I know.”

After a while he wrapped the token in the clean part of the cloth and gave it to Maren. They ate before moving on.

At the outer gate Riarhin looked back once. The little chamber was empty except for the stones Haldren had cleared from the floor. She could still hear the angry voice in memory, clearer now than the water which had carried it.

Chapter 21 — The other room

The sound came while Riarhin was repairing a strap.

They had stopped beneath a wall whose stones leaned inward without falling. Haldren had tested it, disliked it, tested the ground beyond, and disliked that more. They settled on the dry strip at its base with their packs ready to move if anything changed.

Riarhin sat apart by no more than the length of a blanket. She had taken the strap from Esvin’s bag because he kept pretending the split was not widening. The needle was too small. She had to pull it through with a piece of cloth wrapped around her fingers.

From the other side of the wall came a voice.

A woman’s voice, distant and indistinct, rising at the end of a phrase as though asking someone to pass something. Riarhin’s hands stopped.

She knew the sound before she allowed herself the name.

Ailsa.

She remained sitting.

The wall had no opening. Beyond it lay a drop into trees and water. She had looked before choosing the place. She could remember the moss on the lower stones and a branch lying against them. There was no room beyond like the room her body expected: a fire, a bed, a woman occupied with something she had no wish to interrupt merely because Riarhin had arrived.

The voice came again.

No words. A pause, then the low movement of laughter, almost lost beneath water. Riarhin could not tell whether anyone answered. She put the needle down carefully so she would not lose it.

Her throat hurt. She had not expected that. She had expected, if she thought of it at all, the sharp desire to move which Na’ar’s voice had brought. Instead she wanted to remain exactly where she was. If she did not move, perhaps the sound would not stop.

Behind her, Esvin asked Haldren for the pot. Haldren gave it to him. The ordinary exchange seemed to come from a great distance too.

Ailsa had said something like that once while reaching past Riarhin for a bowl. Not the words—she could not hear words—but the impatience softened by knowing she would be obeyed eventually. She remembered the sleeve against her face. She remembered refusing to move quickly because it amused her to be in the way.

The voice faded.

Riarhin waited.

Esvin came and sat near her without speaking.

She knew he was there by the sound of his clothes and the faint smell of smoke they had all acquired. He did not ask what she heard. For a while she resented him for knowing enough not to ask, then was glad of it.

“It was her,” she said at last.

He looked towards the wall.

“I didn’t hear.”

“I know.”

The light changed. Somewhere above, a gap in the cloud let warmth reach the top of the stones. A drop ran down through moss and fell beside the needle.

Riarhin picked it up.

“Don’t tell me.”

“All right.”

He remained while she took another stitch. Her fingers would not perform the small movement properly. She pulled the thread too hard, stopped before it cut into the leather, and loosened it.

After a while she handed him the strap.

“You can finish it.”

“I thought you said I’d do it badly.”

“You will.”

He took it. The familiar small argument made it possible to stand.

She went to find her cup and could not remember where she had put it.

The pack was where she had left it. Her blanket lay across the top, folded to keep the underside from the wet. She looked beneath it, then in the side pocket, then beneath the blanket again. Cadan watched from a few paces away without offering help. She hated him for that until she realised he was looking past her at the wall.

Eddis had the cup. He had taken it to fill with water and set it beside the pot. He handed it to her without comment.

“Thank you,” she said too sharply.

He shrugged. She waited for him to go away so she could regret it in peace. Instead he sat down on the other side of the pot and began taking mud out of the stitching of his boot with a twig.

Riarhin drank. The water was warm from standing by the fire. There was a leaf in it, and she removed the leaf, held it between finger and thumb, and found that she had no idea where to put it. She dropped it on the ground.

Eddis’s twig broke. He looked at the two pieces as if one of them had betrayed an agreement.

“Do you have a pin?” he asked.

She gave him one from her sewing things. He took it carefully and worked the mud free. She watched his hands. They were broad and split across the knuckles. One nail had a dark crescent beneath it where the ferry rope had trapped it; she remembered noticing the injury on the coast.

“Does that hurt?”

“Only when I use it.”

He held up the hand as if inviting a suggestion about how to avoid doing so. She almost smiled.

When he had finished he wiped the pin on a clean edge of his sleeve and returned it. She put it away before she could lose it. The familiar sequence steadied her enough to go back to Esvin.

He had made a knot in the thread where she would not have made one. She saw it at once. There was another, farther along, which would rub the strap when he wore the bag. He was working slowly, with the point of his tongue caught between his teeth.

Ailsa had done that over a difficult fastening. Riarhin had once leaned over her and kissed the exposed tip of her tongue, which had ended the repair for some time and caused an argument afterwards about whose fault that was.

The memory arrived whole. For several breaths she could feel the warmth of that room, the press of Ailsa’s knee against hers, the discarded fastening underfoot. They had quarrelled that morning. She could recall the words; she would not bring them into this place.

Esvin looked up.

“Too loose?”

“No.”

She sat beside him. He drew the next stitch through. A little later she said, “She would have laughed at that.”

“The sewing?”

“At me watching you do it.”

“Then I like her.”

Riarhin pressed the heel of her hand against one eye. Esvin returned to his work. When she lowered her hand, he had managed three more stitches and none of them were particularly good.

They stayed another night because the ground ahead could not be trusted in the fading light.

Eddis had found a crossing which seemed to lead towards higher ground. Riarhin went with him to examine it while there was enough daylight. It was a narrow stone causeway, broken at one edge, with a low marker at the far end. Water showed beneath it on both sides.

She knew it.

Not from the outward journey. From somewhere that came into memory complete: standing at the far marker, looking back towards the wall, seeing Haldren’s cap above the reeds. She had never stood there. She was standing on the near side with Eddis beside her.

She stopped.

“What?” he asked.

“I remember this from there.”

He followed her gaze.

“The far end?”

“Yes.”

“What’s behind you?”

“A broken step. A tree leaning left. There’s a white stone under the root.”

Eddis looked at her for a long time.

“I saw that from the ferry,” he said.

“When?”

“Before you came. In the fog beside the landing. I thought it was the old stair showing through.”

They stood together at the causeway’s edge. Riarhin could remember being at the marker and could remember walking here from the wall. Neither memory dissolved when she attended to the other.

“Do we cross?” Eddis asked.

She looked at the light. It was failing.

“Not tonight.”

He nodded so quickly she knew he had hoped for that answer.

On the way back, Eddis stopped at a tree Riarhin had used as a marker.

She had cut nothing into it. A split low branch and a pale patch of bark were enough for her. He looked at the tree, then at her.

“Which side did we pass?”

“Here.”

He moved to the other side. She waited while he examined the ground.

“Eddis.”

“I know.”

“What are you looking for?”

“The boot mark I remember making.”

She went with him, leaving the tree between them and the causeway. There was a print in the soft ground. Its heel had a nick at the edge which she had noticed in his boot that morning. He crouched, held his foot beside it without putting weight down, and looked up at her.

“We didn’t pass here,” she said.

“No.”

The print might have been older. Eddis had travelled in the outer wood before. Riarhin almost said so, then looked back at the tree they had both remembered passing on the other side.

He took a little twig and laid it across the print.

“What for?”

“So if I see it again I can ask whether the twig is there.”

She nodded. He rose slowly, looked once towards the causeway, and turned his back on it.

They walked nearer each other than before. At a patch of roots he put out an arm to stop her although she had already seen the danger. She thanked him rather than saying so. When the camp lamp became visible he halted again.

“If I tell you I’ve been somewhere,” he said, “ask when.”

“I will.”

“Don’t let me make it easy.”

He turned the pole in his hands, setting its muddy end against the place where he stood. She had been glad to leave the ground to him while she listened for her dead. It had not occurred to her that he might be frightened of what he remembered too.

“I’ll ask you to show me,” she said.

He accepted that and went towards the fire. Riarhin stayed a moment to mark which side of the last tree she actually passed.

Maren wanted to see the crossing herself. Riarhin described it and the failing light. The keeper argued for only a few breaths before looking at the others and agreeing to wait.

Haldren built a small cover over the packs. Cadan fetched water from the place Eddis had marked. Esvin gave her the finished strap to test. She pulled against the stitching. It held, though she could see where she would have unpicked it and begun again.

“Good,” she said, and handed it back. His pleasure made her wish she had not spent so long looking for faults. He fitted it to his bag.

At supper nobody sang. The silence was companionable in patches and merely tired in others. Maren ate with the fragment’s box against her knee. Cadan asked whether Riarhin wished to take the middle watch.

“I’ll take the first.”

“You had it last night.”

“I know.”

Eddis looked at her across the fire.

“I’ll do it.”

“You’ve been testing ground all day.”

“And you can test it tomorrow. Lie down.”

She began to object, then looked at Esvin putting his sewing things away with unnecessary care, Haldren already asleep against his pack. She was tired enough that the thought of another hour listening to the trees made her want to cry.

“All right.”

She lay down before she changed her mind.

At dawn the fortress stood beyond the crossing.

Riarhin saw it from beside the wall while pulling on her boots. It rose above the trees in fragments: a broken upper tower, a long lower wall, the dark shape of something growing through the masonry. It had not been visible the evening before. The light then had shown open ground where the lower wall now stood.

She did not wake the others by shouting. She went to Maren, touched her shoulder, and pointed.

The keeper sat up. For a while they looked together.

Cadan joined them. He examined the line of the causeway, then the place where it met the far bank.

“We should mark this side before we go.”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

They did. Two stones, a strip of cloth at a height visible from the far marker, another lower for Eddis. Riarhin walked back along the approach until she could place the old wall and the new view together without pretending they had always agreed.

The others ate while she checked. When she returned, Haldren gave her bread and told her to swallow it before deciding anything else.

She ate standing beside the packs. Across the water, a bird lifted from the ruined tower and disappeared into the mist. The causeway remained where they could see it.

When everyone was ready, Eddis went first with the pole. Riarhin followed at a distance which left him room to turn back.

Part IV — The instructions beneath the roots

Chapter 22 — Before the forest

The first door was too narrow for the packs.

Eddis found it in the side of the causeway wall after the way above had ended in a clot of roots. He had gone down three shallow steps to test what looked like a drain, and his pole had passed through the darkness without touching a back. Riarhin followed him. Water stood around their boots. Something small moved away beneath it, leaving a succession of rings which reached the wall and returned.

“Could be a culvert,” he said.

“Could be.”

“I’d rather not crawl.”

She put her hand against the opening. The jamb was warm. Above her fingers, under a film of slime, a shallow cut ran straight into the stone and turned twice. She cleared it with her thumb. There was another on the opposite side, level with her shoulder.

“A door.”

“For small people.”

“It’s sunk.”

Eddis looked down at the water. He put the pole between his feet and pushed. Mud accepted another hand’s breadth of it; below that was something hard. He worked the tip along the surface.

“Steps,” he said. “More steps.”

Above them Haldren was arguing with the root which had caught his pack. The root won. He took the pack off and lowered it by its strap, requesting that neither of them stand beneath it. Riarhin moved aside. It landed on the wet step with a noise that made him close his eyes.

“My blanket,” he said.

“We can lift it through.”

“My dry blanket.”

They made a chain. Eddis remained outside, Haldren passed down the loads, and Riarhin went through sideways with Remorse held flat against her thigh. Inside she found enough room to stand. A pale strip of morning fell from a break high above, illuminating a floor of fitted stone beneath the silt. There was a bench along one wall. No roots crossed it. The chamber smelled of wet lime and something faintly sour, rather like a jug which had held milk and been washed too soon with cold water.

She set the packs on the bench. Maren entered with the box clutched against her belly and refused help until the lid had cleared both jambs. Esvin bent almost double. Cadan came last, after Eddis, carrying Haldren’s blanket separately. He had kept it above the water.

Haldren took it from him.

“I owe you a drink.”

“I should like that.”

“Not here.”

Cadan looked around the chamber, apparently considering the possibility.

“No,” he said. “I suppose not.”

There had been cupboards. Their doors were gone, but the rebates remained in the walls, and in one of them Riarhin found the green stain of a hinge. The stain followed a crack all the way to the floor. Something had been written above each recess. She brushed a sleeve across the nearest inscription and brought out four shallow groups of marks.

Maren stood beside her with the coastal notebook open.

“Can you read it?”

“Some.”

“What does that one say?”

Riarhin followed the first group with a finger held just clear of the stone. She knew its shape. She had seen it cut beside bowls in which Ghere kept resin, and once scratched by a child on the underside of a sleeping platform. There it had meant something like what remained after taking. Here the next mark altered it. Or might have. The last was a word she knew for a hand, placed where she expected a relation between the first two.

“Something kept apart,” she said. “Possibly. That last word may mean the person responsible.”

“What is kept apart?”

“I don’t know.”

Maren looked into the recess. There was nothing in it except a strip of pale matter curled against the back.

“Don’t touch that,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were leaning.”

“I lean when I look.”

Riarhin moved away and let her look.

At the far end Haldren had found a stone table. Its surface tilted enough that a drop of water placed at the upper end ran through a cut channel into a bowl-shaped depression. He tried another drop. Then, with a more deliberate air, he tipped half a spoonful onto the dry part beside the channel.

It ran the other way.

Eddis came to watch. Haldren repeated the experiment. Water in the groove travelled toward the bowl; water on the plain surface travelled toward the wall.

“Don’t drink anything off that,” Eddis said.

“I was not intending to.”

“You looked pleased.”

“I like a thing that does what it was made to do.”

“Know what that was, do you?”

Haldren dried the spoon on his cuff and put it away without answering.

A passage led out behind the table. They could see daylight at the far end, interrupted by regular dark bars. Riarhin went ahead, checking the floor. The bars were roots. They had grown across another doorway and into the opposite wall, not filling it entirely but leaving gaps too narrow for shoulders. Beyond them lay a larger room with a broken roof. Rain fell into it although the strip of sky above was clear.

She listened. The drops struck different surfaces: stone, shallow water, something which gave a hollow wooden note. No footsteps answered hers.

Maren squeezed in beside her.

“This one,” she said, turning the notebook. “Look.”

The page contained a copy of measurements made in a hand larger and more confident than the annotations beneath. Maren had marked one number twice.

“The width. The height. Those two cuts beside the sill. It’s the dry door.”

Riarhin looked through the roots at the rain.

“What does that mean?”

“It means the person who measured it could get through.”

Haldren came up behind them with a length of cord. Since the loss of Maren’s case he had been tying knots into whatever line they could spare, measuring each against the same stretch of his forearm. He passed an end through the roots and asked Riarhin to hold it against the left-hand cut. Then he stretched it to the right.

“Near enough,” he said.

“Near enough to what?” Maren asked.

“Your width.”

“You haven’t looked at it.”

“You read it aloud.”

She gave him the page. He compared it anyway, and she looked less irritated.

The roots were thick as wrists, pale beneath their wet bark. Haldren pressed one. It did not move. Farther down, where it entered the wall, the masonry had split around it in a long star. A patch of old plaster remained between two cracks. On it someone had painted a blue line, straight until the root had pushed its middle outward.

Riarhin crouched. Below the blue line was a later mark cut through the plaster into the stone. It resembled the signs on Nereth’s horn plates. Beneath that, almost on the buried sill, a third hand had scratched something with a narrow point. The strokes were hurried and too close together.

She could make out a direction. Or a plea to return. Without the missing beginning it might have been either.

“Can we go around?” she asked.

Haldren withdrew his cord.

“I’d prefer it to cutting.”

They found the way around through a room with no outer wall.

Trees grew below it in a hollow which should, from the approach, have lain within the causeway. Eddis spent some time at the edge, looking down and then back at the door through which they had entered. He said nothing until Riarhin joined him.

“There’s too much ground,” he said.

“How much?”

“Enough.”

She did not ask him to improve the answer. Across the hollow, a broken colonnade rose among the trunks. The columns were pale and slender, with collars of darker stone beneath their capitals. Two stood at an angle. A third lay in sections on the earth, each section carrying a single letter of an inscription which had once wound around the whole.

Beyond the colonnade was a wall made of a different stone. It had been built across the older work without much concern for windows or doors. Roots entered it, emerged, and entered again. Some were black. Others had been stripped of bark and polished by contact, and in the places where several met Riarhin saw the dull white of bone.

Esvin came to stand on her other side.

“Oh,” he said softly.

She followed his gaze. A narrow face projected from the newer wall above a doorway. It might have been a carving until the mist moved behind it and showed the empty space beneath the cheek.

Haldren made a sound in his throat.

“We aren’t going through there,” he said.

“Not if we can help it,” Riarhin said.

The carpenter stepped back. He had been rubbing his thumb over his wedding ring. He stopped when he saw her notice, then resumed a moment later.

Maren had opened the box. The fragment lay within its padding, gold dull beneath the branching matter attached to it. She lifted one corner of the wrapping. A thread which had hung slack now curved toward the newer wall.

“That way,” she said.

“Does it show a safe way?” Riarhin asked.

“No.”

“Then close it for now.”

Maren watched the thread for another breath before covering it.

Before they ate, Maren found a set of measurements cut into the underside of a fallen lintel.

She called Haldren over. He had been looking for somewhere to put the packs clear of the water and came reluctantly, wiping both hands on his coat. The lintel lay with one end raised upon the broken capital of a column. A later root had grown through the gap beneath it and split into three, each branch thickening around a different fragment.

“Older than that,” Maren said, pointing to the cuts.

“Most things are older than what grows over them.”

“Come and look.”

He came. Riarhin held the lamp while the two of them crouched beneath the stone. The cuts ran in two parallel rows. One had been partly erased when the lintel was fitted into a second doorway. Riarhin could see a socket cut across the end of a word, and beside it the pale outline of mortar which had protected the rest.

“They used it twice,” Haldren said.

“At least.”

Maren compared the spacing with her copy. Nothing matched until she turned the page upside down. Then she looked annoyed with herself.

“Here.”

Riarhin bent to read.

The words were clearer than those above the cupboards, but not easier. The marks described a quantity which had been moved from one place to another, then altered by what Riarhin first took to be a measure of cold. She read farther and changed her mind. It concerned the interruption of a repeated act. Beyond that stood a row of strokes, some crossed through.

“How many?” Maren asked.

“I can count the marks.”

“What do they count?”

“I can’t tell you.”

Haldren waited while she tried another line. She knew the word for flesh. The next mark could have been a possession or a direction, depending on what the first word included. The writer had not troubled to say. His reader would have known. Riarhin heard Ghere’s voice in memory, impatient with a question whose answer lay in everything she had failed to attend to before asking it.

She straightened too quickly and struck her shoulder against the stone.

“Enough?” Haldren asked.

“For now.”

He did not insist.

On the other side of the fallen lintel, Esvin had found a small recess. Within it lay a disc of stone with a depression shaped to receive a hand. There were five separate hollows for the fingers and a worn place beneath the heel of the palm. Riarhin could not tell whether it had been used by someone holding themselves still or someone held still by another person.

Beside it, worked into the wall, was a channel which could be closed with a sliding plate. The plate remained. It had been repaired with a metal strip. Above the repair someone had cut a brief complaint into the plaster, then plastered over it badly.

“This is a workroom,” Maren said.

“Yes,” Riarhin answered.

“Before the roots. Before the later wall.”

Haldren looked up through the broken roof.

“Long before.”

Riarhin followed his gaze. A tree had grown against the first wall, died, and left its shape in another growth which had overtaken it. The newer masonry had been built around the hollow. Above that lay the root-and-bone structure they had seen from the passage. She could distinguish the order without knowing how many lives had passed between one alteration and the next.

Esvin stood with his hands behind his back, looking at the hand-shaped hollow.

“Was this his?” he asked.

“The writer’s? I don’t know.”

“Or someone after.”

“Or before. We have the measurements of rooms. That doesn’t tell us everyone who used them.”

He nodded. After a moment he took a folded cloth from his pocket and covered the hollow before sitting down to eat.

Haldren found a dry place for the packs at last. He set them down with more care than usual, then asked whether anyone had kept the cheese separate from the oil. Eddis said he had thought someone else was doing it. The cheese, when found, tasted faintly of the lamp.

They ate in the room with the missing wall. Nobody wished to light a fire. Haldren cut bread and passed it along; Esvin shook the last crumbs from a bag into his palm and ate them before offering the bag to Eddis for packing. Riarhin drank sparingly. Her mouth remained dry.

Cadan had gone back along the passage while they settled. He returned before the food was finished, holding a short piece of wood.

“From the other frame,” he said.
