Riarhin recognised the rounded end and the hole through which a carrying strap had been threaded. There was a fresh splinter at the other end.

“Where?”

“Two rooms behind us. There is a stair below the floor. Someone has opened it since I was last here.”

“Nereth?”

“Perhaps. I found this on the stair.”

“Tracks?”

“Several. Water has entered since.”

He turned the wood in his hands. Along one side were two small cuts, then a gap, then another. Riarhin had seen similar marks made by men counting trips.

“Could they have come back?” she asked.

“I hope so.”

That was all he would say about it. He sat, accepted bread, and put the piece beside his knee where he could see it.

The stair was older than the floor above it.

Its opening had been covered with slabs laid across a shallow frame. Two slabs now stood against the wall. Their undersides were clean. Riarhin could see the places where hands had lifted them, dark thumbprints in the pale dust.

The first four steps were worn in the centre. Below those, a later channel had been cut down one side to carry water. The channel ran away from the old rooms and disappeared beneath the new wall. It should have been draining them. Instead a thin flow climbed its edge and spread across the seventh step.

Eddis knelt and touched it with the pole.

“Again,” he said.

Maren lowered a lamp. Its reflection lay in the water, long and wavering. For a moment the reflected flame appeared farther down than the lamp itself, around a turn the stair did not have.

She raised it quickly.

“Don’t do that again,” Eddis said.

“I didn’t intend to do it the first time.”

Haldren examined the frame over their heads. He pressed the end of his knife into one joint, withdrew it, and rubbed the blade between finger and thumb.

“Still sound. But keep off the left slab. It’s been undercut.”

They descended one at a time. Riarhin went first, with the lamp now in her right hand and her left free. Halfway down she smelled smoke. Good smoke, a little greasy, with something cooking over it.

She stopped so abruptly that Maren bumped her shoulder.

“What?”

“Food.”

They listened. Below them a pot scraped across stone. Someone coughed, a young, irritated cough, and a woman said, “If you put that there again I shall stand on it.”

A man answered, too low for the others to hear.

“I don’t care where you put your boots at home.”

Riarhin lowered the lamp. Beyond the foot of the stair, light showed beneath a hanging cloth. It moved as someone passed behind it. There was a pause, then the sound of a spoon striking the rim of a bowl.

“Nereth,” she called.

The spoon stopped.

For several breaths nothing moved behind the cloth. Then it was pulled aside, and the girl stood looking up at them with a bowl in her hands.

Her hair was tied back with a strip torn from some much larger piece of clothing. Soot marked one side of her nose. She looked first at Riarhin, then beyond her at the lamp, the packs, the line of tired faces on the stair.

“You took a long time,” she said.

Chapter 23 — The margin in her hand

Nereth had made a home beside a hole in the wall.

The hole served as a chimney, though badly. Smoke wandered across the ceiling before remembering it, and the upper shelves were black. Below the smoke the room was warm. A hearth of loose stones held a little pot, a larger pot, and an iron plate on which three flat cakes were beginning to burn. Nereth set down her bowl, snatched the plate sideways with a folded rag, and swore.

“I told you to watch them.”

“I was watching the door,” said the man seated beside the hearth.

“You can hear a door.”

“Not that one.”

He was younger than Riarhin had expected from his voice, perhaps in his middle twenties, with a narrow face and a moustache which looked as though it had been encouraged beyond its abilities. One boot was off. The foot beneath it was wrapped in a dirty strip of linen. He started to stand, saw how many people were entering, and pulled the bare foot under the bench instead.

“Stay where you are,” Riarhin said.

Nereth had picked up the least burned cake. She looked at it, at the company, and broke it in half.

“There’s enough broth. Not enough of these.”

“We have bread,” Haldren said.

“Good. Put it anywhere dry.”

There was nowhere dry which did not already contain something. The long bench held folded clothing, a bundle of rushes, two bowls, a brass measure, and a book wrapped in oiled cloth. Beneath it stood three pots of earth. Thin rods projected from the earth, each carrying a loop of thread. The loops leaned in different directions although Riarhin could feel no draught.

She set her pack beside the door.

“Not there,” Nereth said at once.

Riarhin looked down. A line of pale powder crossed the threshold, turned around a stone, and continued beneath the bench.

“Where?”

“Beside the chimney. Mind the blue cup.”

Haldren moved the cup before anyone could break it. It contained nails. He looked into it with interest, then set it on a higher shelf.

“Are these in use?” he asked.

“Some of them.”

“Which?”

“The straight ones.”

He began sorting them.

The man’s name was Werrin. He came from the houses beyond Kelva’s mill, and his mother had sent the beans in the larger pot. He had also brought meal, lamp oil, two shirts, a packet of needles, and a message Nereth had not yet opened because she knew what it would say.

“Then why send it?” Eddis asked.

“She likes me to have it in writing.”

“What will it say?”

“That I should eat more.”

Werrin, who had been staring at Cadan, looked away quickly.

“It says other things.”

Nereth reached for the packet. He put a hand over it.

“After you’ve eaten.”

She stared at him. He stared back. At last she took up her bowl and sat on a low stool with an expression Riarhin recognised from Tessa at the mill.

“How did you come?” Riarhin asked Werrin.

“By the lower cut. Then her marks.”

“How long?”

“Left yesterday.”

Eddis turned his head.

“Yesterday?”

“The day before, if this is another morning. I slept once.”

“We slept rather more than once,” Esvin said.

“You came the old way. I saw the marks at the fork.” Werrin shifted his foot. “She told me not to use that. There’s a place where you turn at a white root. Looks as though you ought to go straight. You don’t.”

“And it was clear?” Maren asked.

“Clear enough. There were things outside.”

“What things?”

He looked at Nereth.

“People,” he said. “Some of them.”

Nereth ate another spoonful. She was holding the bowl so tightly that the ends of her fingers had gone white.

“You should have waited until the morning mark,” she said.

“I did.”

“Then they weren’t inside.”

“I didn’t say they were.”

The broth was good. Riarhin had expected thin water and beans, but someone had put fat into it and a bitter leaf which left her mouth feeling clean. She ate too quickly, slowed when the heat caught her throat, then finished the bowl before Nereth had reached halfway through hers.

Esvin asked for the pot. He filled everyone’s bowl before his own and left a portion beside the hearth.

“Who’s that for?” Nereth asked.

“Anyone still hungry.”

“Oh.”

She looked at the portion twice while they ate. When Esvin pushed it toward her, she took it without speaking.

Werrin asked Riarhin to pass him his coat from the bench.

She fetched it. He searched an inner pocket, took out a small flat parcel, and stared at it as if he had forgotten its presence until that moment. Then he called Nereth over.

“What now?” she asked.

He gave it to her. Inside were four pieces of soap, shaved thin and pressed back together around a thread. They were not new. Someone had collected the remnants which became too small to hold comfortably.

“My mother says you can’t call it washing just because the water changes colour.”

Nereth looked at the pieces. She put one by the basin and wrapped the others again.

“Tell her thank you.”

“You can tell her.”

The girl tucked the parcel into a jar without answering. Werrin watched her return to the bench.

“How much are you carrying for her?” Riarhin asked him quietly.

“Whatever they send.”

“Do you know what the fittings do?”

“The ones by the houses. Not all of these.”

“Has anyone asked you to stop?”

He shifted the sore foot, making a face which delayed his answer.

“People ask me to fetch her. Then she gives me something to take back and they keep it. I don’t know which bit they mean me to stop.”

Riarhin sat on the edge of the bench. He looked no longer quite so young when he spoke without trying to sound useful. The faint moustache did him a disservice.

“Was it safe when you came?”

“I got here.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I know. It’s what I can tell you.”

She looked at the bruised foot and the mud dried into his coat. He turned the coat inside out to show a tear near the hem.

“That caught on a root. I thought someone had me. I ran until it tore. She says I shouldn’t run between marks.”

“She’s right.”

“I know that too.”

He folded the coat back so the tear was hidden. Riarhin wanted to tell him that fear did not make the attempt foolish. She also wanted him to stop making it. She asked how he had found the next mark.

He described it in detail, where the pale part showed beneath a root, how the path seemed to turn uphill until he looked back. Eddis came over while he was speaking and asked him to begin at the last water. Werrin did, more confidently now, drawing with a finger on the dusty edge of the bench. They listened until he reached the room they sat in.

Nereth passed behind them carrying bowls. She paused at the scratched route, opened her mouth, and then pointed to one turn.

“Here. You went too low.”

“That was the part I could see.”

She looked at the drawing for a moment longer before changing the position of her finger.

“I’ll put another above it.”

Werrin nodded, accepting the repair as if it were already done. Riarhin kept looking at the space between the two marks.

The workshop had two doors besides the stair. One led to a small room where Nereth slept. The other was closed with a bar which looked new, though the sockets holding it were old. Across its centre someone had tied a twist of red thread.

Riarhin approached only far enough to see the floor beneath it.

“That stays shut,” Nereth said.

“For what reason?”

“Because the other side doesn’t always stay where it is.”

Maren set down her bowl.

“You knew that before you came here.”

“I knew something happened. I didn’t know this was where it began.”

“Did it begin here?” Riarhin asked.

Nereth rubbed the soot on her nose and made it worse.

“I think so.”

“Show us what you know.”

The girl looked at the wrapped book on the bench.

“You aren’t taking it.”

“No one has touched it.”

“You’ll say it has to be kept safe.”

“It probably does.”

“That’s what they say when they mean kept from me.”

Riarhin sat on the bench, leaving the book between them.

“Bring the lamp.”

Nereth remained standing. Outside the barred door something brushed the stone, a slow dragging sound. Werrin stopped spooning the last beans from his bowl. Haldren, still sorting nails, looked up.

“It does that,” Nereth said.

“How often?”

“Sometimes.”

“Nereth.”

The girl snatched the lamp from its hook and brought it over.

The outer covering was recent. Beneath it lay a book whose boards had warped in opposite directions. A strip of green material held one corner together. The pages were thin, pale, and patched along their edges with paper which had browned faster than the original leaves. Several had been folded back and forth so often that the folds were dark.

Nereth opened it near the beginning. Her own writing crowded the margins, small and quick, changing direction where it ran out of room. On one page she had continued across a stain, then repeated the obscured words above it. Riarhin could read those words easily enough. The text between them took longer.

“This is where I started,” Nereth said.

“How old were you?”

“Twelve.”

“Where did you find it?”

“In a case. The roof had come down over it. I was looking for copper.”

“Alone?”

“Not then.”

She turned a page before Riarhin could ask who had been with her.

“The pictures helped. There are measurements. If you put the same things together, some of them work without understanding every word.”

Riarhin thought of the tilted table upstairs.

“Some.”

“Yes.”

Nereth held the page flat. In the centre was a drawing of a figure seen from the side, arms extended, with lines running from the hands to two posts. Beneath it another drawing showed the same figure turned toward a smaller one. The smaller figure had been added in a different ink.

“The first part concerns movement,” Nereth said. “Repeating it. Keeping it within a place. I didn’t use that.”

Riarhin worked through the line below the second drawing. She knew the word for return, and the one which could mean work or the thing worked upon. A mark beside the smaller figure gave her trouble. She read the sequence again, silently this time.

“Someone spoke to it.”

“Yes.”

“A person it knew.”

“His wife.”

“Does it say wife?”

“There. And again on the next page.”

Nereth turned the leaf. The mark recurred beside a longer description. Riarhin could see why she had chosen wife, though it might also have described someone responsible for a shared house. The writer had cared about what the voice interrupted. He had counted the interruption. There were numbers beside each attempt.

Below them a short line had been underlined twice.

“What have you made of that?” Riarhin asked.

“A defect in retention.”

Riarhin looked at the drawing of the smaller figure. One hand had been raised toward the larger one. Perhaps that was part of the experiment. Perhaps the writer had merely drawn what happened.

Nereth was watching her face.

“I know what he was doing.”

“Do you?”

“He wanted them to keep working. He thought anything which made them stop was a mistake.” She pulled the page toward herself. “But they did stop. They could tell one approach from another. That’s what mattered.”

She pointed to her own writing. Here the hand was younger, the letters larger and less evenly spaced. Several words had been scratched out hard enough to score the page.

“At first I thought you could make them remember. Then I thought you could make the boundary remember instead. The difference between someone arriving and someone being allowed through.”

Maren leaned over Riarhin’s shoulder.

“Those are the two outer loops.”

“Yes.”

“And the return?”

“Not return. Refusal. I had it wrong at first.”

They bent over the page together. Riarhin shifted to let Maren sit. The keeper took out her notebook, laid it beside the ancient drawing, and traced a line without touching either.

“That is why the third plate has to be lower.”

“Only on wet ground.”

“You didn’t tell us that.”

“I told you to keep it below the water mark.”

“That isn’t the same instruction.”

“It was for the mill.”

“And when someone uses it elsewhere?”

Nereth pulled the book back an inch.

“They should ask.”

Maren looked at her, then at the page. She wrote something in her notebook. The scratching of her pen went on for several breaths.

While they worked, Haldren repaired the stool.

It had been rocking since Nereth sat on it. He waited until she stood to fetch another lamp, turned it over, and found one leg held in place by a wedge made from folded paper. He removed the paper, read a few upside-down figures, and offered it to her.

“Oh,” she said. “I’ve been looking for that.”

“Was it important?”

“Not now.”

He made a better wedge from the end of a broken spoon. Werrin held the stool while he fitted it. The younger man asked whether Cadan had really come back from the place below the stair. Haldren said he should ask Cadan.

“I don’t like to.”

“He won’t bite.”

Cadan, from beside the hearth, said, “No.”

Werrin dropped the stool. Haldren caught it against his knee and looked at him with considerable patience.

“Hold this end,” he said.

Riarhin went to Nereth’s sleeping room while the girl fetched blankets. A narrow pallet lay beneath a shelf full of jars. Beside it stood a basin, a comb with three missing teeth, and a little clay figure which might have been a cat before its ears broke. The room had no window. On the wall above the pallet Nereth had scratched a row of marks, some grouped, others standing alone.

“How long have you been coming here?”

“Since the thaw.”

“Sleeping here?”

“Sometimes.”

“Kelva knows?”

“She knows I go into the wood.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Nereth pulled a blanket from the shelf. A folded sheet came with it and fell at Riarhin’s feet.

She picked it up before the girl could reach it.

There were dates on it, or intervals counted from a date. Beside each stood a place. Riarhin recognised the mill, the lower passage, Pall Watch-Tower. Another entry described a lane she had helped carry people out of. Beside that Nereth had written: held here; gathering beyond the west post, no return yet.

Farther down: voice at empty frame. Same direction as before.

The last entry had no place, only a drawing of a stair and the words more room than yesterday.

Nereth took the sheet from her hand.

“I was keeping track.”

“How many times did it happen before the lower passage?”

“I don’t know what you mean by it.”

“People gathered where your ward sent them.”

“They were already in the country.”

“How many times?”

“Three that I saw.”

“Who did you tell?”

The girl folded the paper along its old creases.

“If I had told the bell keepers, they would have stopped everything.”

“Maren didn’t stop the mill.”

“Maren came after you. After it had worked. After there were people who would speak for me.” Her voice rose. “You weren’t there when they took the plates from my mother’s door.”

Riarhin waited.

“They took them in a sack. They didn’t even mark which side faced out. One broke. I heard it.”

“What happened to your mother?”

“Nothing. They gave us a bell allotment.”

The answer seemed to anger Nereth more than the question. She shoved the blanket into Riarhin’s arms.

“Nothing happened. So they were right. That was how they told it.”

From the other room came the sound of the stool being tested, Haldren’s weight settling onto it with a grunt. Werrin laughed at something Esvin said. The ordinary noise made the sleeping room feel very small.

Riarhin looked again at the marks above the pallet.

“Did you hear the voice before you came here?”

“Yes.”

“What did it ask?”

“It told me where to put the outer mark.”

“And since?”

Nereth’s eyes moved toward the barred door beyond the hanging cloth.

“It knows the way things fit.”

“Does it know the people whose houses are outside?”

“I know them.”

Riarhin wanted to take the folded sheet back. She wanted to bring Nereth into the larger room and make her read it aloud to everyone. The girl was eighteen, hungry again despite two bowls of broth, and watching the doorway as though Riarhin might stand in it and never move.

“Bring that with you,” she said. “Maren needs to see the changes.”

“You’ll make it sound worse than it is.”

“Then you can tell her where I’m wrong.”

Nereth stayed in the little room after Riarhin left. When she came out she carried the sheet, folded so tightly that it would not lie flat on the bench.

They did not finish before the lamp needed filling.

Eddis fell asleep sitting upright. Werrin stretched his bad foot toward the hearth and admitted, when Esvin asked, that he had felt the blister before leaving home and thought it would settle. Haldren said blisters generally settled by getting worse. Nereth laughed once, sharply, then covered her mouth as though she had done something inappropriate.

Maren and the girl had stopped arguing aloud. Their pens continued. Sometimes one pushed a page toward the other; sometimes the other pushed it back. Riarhin watched the barred door. Nothing had brushed against it for some time.

At last Nereth reached past her for the blue cup.

“Where are the bent ones?”

“Haldren’s straightening them.”

“I needed one bent.”

“For what?”

“The lamp hook.”

Riarhin looked at the lamp. Its hook had opened under the weight. She took a straight nail from the cup, laid it across the edge of the stone bench, and bent it with the butt of her knife.

Nereth watched her make the second turn. When Riarhin handed it over, the girl said, “That’ll do.”

She climbed onto the repaired stool and hung the lamp. Its light spread farther across the room, catching the steam from Werrin’s drying boot.

They arranged the blankets without much discussion. Riarhin lay nearest the barred door. Nereth saw where she put her pack and said nothing about it. For a while the girl remained at the bench, turning pages in the raised light. Then she closed the book, wrapped it, and carried it into her sleeping room.

The cloth fell behind her. Someone coughed. Haldren’s breathing deepened.

Riarhin listened to the new place around them until the small noises became familiar enough to sleep through.

Chapter 24 — A stair on the other side

In the morning Werrin could not put his boot on.

He tried while the others pretended not to watch, first pushing his foot straight into it, then turning the boot sideways and pulling at the heel. Sweat appeared above his moustache. He stopped with the leather half folded beneath him.

Esvin held out a hand.

“Give it here.”

“I can do it.”

“Yes. Tomorrow, perhaps. Give it here.”

The heel had dried hard beside the fire. Esvin worked it between his palms while Nereth fetched water for the foot. The blister had opened. There was a red patch above it which Riarhin disliked, though it was no larger than two fingers held together.

“You aren’t walking on that today,” she said.

“I have to get back.”

“Not today.”

“My mother’s expecting—”

“Your mother sent enough food for three days,” Nereth said. “She knows you.”

He looked hurt. She knelt and washed his heel more gently than she had spoken.

They breakfasted on the remaining cakes, scraped. Haldren called the black parts seasoning. Nereth said he was welcome to them and gave him hers. He ate it, which seemed to surprise her.

Riarhin had slept badly. Nothing had touched the barred door, but at some point in the night she had heard water below the floor and spent too long trying to decide whether it was running toward the sea. Now, with food in her stomach and the lamps lit, she could hear only an occasional drop in the passage outside.

Nereth took the bar down after breakfast.

“Stand back from the line,” she said.

Maren already had her notebook open.

“Which line?”

“The red one. The white is for the door.”

“There are three red ones.”

“The nearest.”

“Write that down.”

Nereth made an exasperated noise, then wrote it down.

The room beyond had once been much larger.

A wall of roots occupied its middle. They had grown through a long stone trough, lifting one end and breaking the other, and continued upward into a mass which disappeared through the ceiling. Between them something dark shone with moisture. Riarhin could not decide whether it was stone until a drop fell from it and showed a hard edge beneath.

The floor near the door was swept clean. Nereth’s marks covered it in a pattern of short lines and circles, with gaps which had been left deliberately. She stepped over two lines, knelt beside a little iron bowl, and checked its contents with the end of a wooden stick.

“Don’t move that,” she told Haldren.

“I haven’t moved anything.”

“You were looking at the wedge.”

“It looks loose.”

“It has to be loose.”

He folded his hands in front of him.

Farther into the room, beyond the swept part, a stair descended beneath the roots. Only its first three steps were visible. They were made of the same pale stone as the old workrooms and had the same shallow wear in their centres. Riarhin could see silt on the lowest.

Nereth placed the stick across the iron bowl. Nothing happened.

“Is that it?” Eddis asked.

“No.”

She waited. Her eyes stayed on the roots rather than the stair. After a while Riarhin saw one of the thin loops beside the bowl turn. It moved slowly, as though someone had breathed across it.

The fourth step appeared.

It was darker than the others. It had a high lip along one edge and a crack which crossed it from corner to corner. Beyond it lay a fifth step, then a sixth, turning away beneath an arch where there had been no room for one.

Eddis swore under his breath.

Riarhin knew the crack. She had looked at it across the test span while water climbed against the current.

“That is the stair,” she said.

“Yes,” Nereth said.

“Not a likeness.”

“I don’t think so.”

Cold air moved across Riarhin’s ankles. It carried a dry smell which did not belong beneath wet roots. She had smelled something like it in tomb passages and in a place where old mortar had been crushed beneath a fallen wall, but there was another scent mixed with it, sweet and faintly spoiled.

Maren came up beside her, stopping short of the nearest red line.

“How long can you hold it?”

“I’m not holding it. This is what it does now.”

“Then what did the bowl do?”

“Kept this side still.”

Maren looked down at the pattern beneath their feet.

“For how long?”

“Until the loop turns back.”

“How long is that?”

“It varies.”

Haldren unfolded his hands.

“I think I’d like to know where to stand.”

Riarhin moved him behind her, then moved herself back as well.

Cadan watched the opening without approaching it.

He had put his pack down outside the room. The short piece of carrying frame lay beside it, and above that he had arranged a cord, three strips of cloth, and a little object wrapped in a square of dark fabric. He unwrapped the object now. It was a smooth, flat stone with a shallow hollow in its centre.

Nereth glanced at it and then looked away.

“Will that let you go through?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then what is it for?”

“So I know when to come back.”

She opened her mouth, closed it, and returned to watching the loop.

Cadan spoke quietly to Riarhin. He would go only to the turn, look beyond it if the passage remained open, and return before the third length of cord had been drawn through her hand. He demonstrated the pace. The cord was long, marked at intervals which could be felt without looking. Drawing its full length at the agreed rate took several minutes; they timed it twice by the steady dripping into a measure before trusting their hands. Riarhin would still have chosen longer for someone searching a strange place.

“If I pull twice?” she asked.

“I return.”

“If it slackens?”

“You call me.”

“If you don’t answer?”

“Do not follow me without another way back.”

She disliked the calm with which he said it. She had heard him use the same tone over breakfast when asking for the salt.

“Can you bring someone?”

“If they can come.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It is the one I have.”

He tied the cord around his wrist. Then he removed a second loop from it and tied that around his waist. Both knots were ordinary. Riarhin checked them.

“You’ll have to carry anyone who can’t walk.”

“Yes.”

“You can’t—”

“No,” he said. “Not with someone else.”

She let go of the knot.

Eddis took up position beside the door, where he could watch both the stair and the passage behind them. Haldren found a place to brace himself if the cord needed hauling. Maren stayed with Nereth. Esvin stood beyond the white line, his hands empty.

Before he went, Cadan asked for a different end on the waist fastening.

Riarhin had tucked the loose part neatly beneath the turn. He drew it out and left enough showing to be found in darkness. She understood, then looked towards the stair.

“If you have to free yourself—”

“I will try to tell you first.”

“What if you can’t?”

“Do not keep hauling an empty cord because you hope I am at the end of it.”

Haldren looked up from the place where he meant to brace his feet. He opened his mouth, then closed it. Riarhin turned the fastening once more in her fingers.

“Would you leave it?”

“To get clear, perhaps. To bring someone with me, I need the way open and the line intact.”

“And if you have to choose?”

Cadan looked at the stair. For the first time since laying out his things, he did not seem ready with an answer.

“I would rather make that choice when I know what it is.”

She released the fastening. He had made similar answers before while she demanded particulars no one could supply. She disliked them most when she knew they were reasonable.

“Show me the pull again.”

He did. She answered. They changed places so he could feel her signal and tell her whether it was distinct from the ordinary movement of the line. Her first attempt was too quick. Haldren had taken up the slack and blurred the two pulls into one.

“Wait until I give it,” she told him.

“I thought—”

“I know. We both thought.”

They tried again. This time Cadan answered at once. Haldren shifted his grip so the cord could run without cutting into his palm.

Nereth watched from beside the bowl. She had looked impatient when the practice began. Now she asked whether Cadan wanted another cloth marker, something he could see on the way back even if the lamp went out.

“What colour?” he asked.

“I have white.”

“The stone is white.”

She searched a pocket and found a strip of red thread wound round a splinter. It was too thin to see at a distance. Riarhin took the cleanest scrap from her sewing packet and drew a broad cross on it with the charcoal Maren used for notes. Cadan tied it beside the entrance at his own shoulder height.

“From the turn,” he said. “I should see that from the turn.”

Riarhin went down only far enough to judge the angle. The mark showed above her through a gap between Haldren and the door. She asked the carpenter to move his shoulder. He moved it, checked the line again, and stayed clear.

Cadan touched the mark once before facing the stair.

Cadan stepped onto the first stair.

Nothing changed at once. His foot made a familiar sound on the stone. On the fourth step the sound was different. Riarhin felt the cord grow colder as it passed through her hand.

He turned and looked back. She raised her free hand. He nodded and went down.

The arch took his head, then his shoulders. The cord continued moving.

At the first cloth strip Riarhin could still hear him.

At the second she could hear something else.

A dragging noise came through, followed by two dull knocks. Then silence. The cord stopped.

She called his name.

No answer.

Nereth bent closer to the bowl. Maren caught her sleeve before she could touch it.

“What are you doing?”

“Keeping it—”

“You said not to move it.”

“The loop is turning.”

Riarhin looked. The loop had begun to lean toward the door.

She pulled twice.

For a moment the cord resisted. Then it moved toward her, an inch, another inch, and stopped again. She could feel a weight at its far end. Not the sharp resistance of someone pulling against her. A heavy, uneven drag.

“Haldren.”

He took the cord behind her and began to gather it without jerking. Eddis came forward from the door. Riarhin held up a hand to keep him back; they did not need a third person crowding the same line.

A shoe appeared beneath the arch.

It was facing the wrong way. A leg followed it, the knee bent, and then Cadan’s hand came around the side of the stair. He was backing upward with someone under the arms. The person’s feet caught on the high lips of the steps.

Riarhin went down as far as the third stair and knelt. The cold reached through her trousers. She took the stranger’s ankle, lifted it clear, and felt almost no weight beneath the cloth.

“Again,” she said.

Cadan climbed another step. Haldren took in the cord. The stranger made a small sound, more breath than voice.

They got him over the threshold just as the fourth step shortened.

Riarhin saw it happen. The lip remained visible, but the distance between it and the step above folded until there was room only for a hand. Cadan’s heel struck pale stone. He fell backward against Haldren, who swore and caught him under one arm.

The foreign arch was gone.

Nereth removed the stick from the bowl with fingers which shook badly enough that she dropped it.

The carrier’s name was Brenn.

He could say that much. He could also say no when Esvin tried to give him too much water at once. Afterward he lay on Nereth’s pallet with his eyes open, following the movement of the lamp.

Vessa had described him as a broad man. He was still broad across the shoulders, but the skin beneath his shirt hung loosely, and his beard had dried into small points around his mouth. One hand was wrapped in a piece of sacking. When Esvin unwound it, two fingers were swollen and dark at the tips.

Haldren looked away, then looked back and fetched the basin without being asked.

“Don’t rub them,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t going to.”

The room filled too quickly. She sent everyone out except Esvin, then went out herself when Brenn flinched at the shadow she cast across him.

In the workshop Werrin was trying to make more space.

He had moved the stool, then moved it back because it obstructed the hearth. Now he stood with Nereth’s spare blanket in both hands, looking for someone to tell him where it should go. Riarhin took it and laid it beside the sleeping-room door.

“Can you warm water?”

“Yes.”

“Not hot. Warm.”

He nodded and found a pot.

Nereth had begun clearing the bench with unnecessary speed. A jar fell. She caught it against her knee and stood holding it while the loose lid rolled across the floor. Haldren stopped the lid with his boot and picked it up.

“Here.”

She took it without looking at him.

“There’s a cup with a narrow lip,” she said. “Blue. No, the other blue. He won’t have to lift his head as much.”

Riarhin found it. When she carried it in, Brenn watched her as though she had been gone a long time.

“Vessa?” he asked.

“Home. At the coast.”

“Went?”

“Cadan brought her.”

Brenn’s face changed. He tried to speak, swallowed, and tried again. Esvin lifted a hand to stop Riarhin from filling the silence.

“She said she’d go,” the carrier managed.

“She did.”

“I told her not to.”

Riarhin set the cup where he could see it.

“She got out.”

He began to shake. It started in his hands and spread through his shoulders. Esvin asked him whether he was cold. Brenn shook his head, then nodded, then made an angry sound because neither answer seemed adequate. Riarhin fetched the blanket and laid it over him without trying to tuck it beneath the injured arm.

Through the door Haldren asked whether they needed anything else. Esvin requested another folded cloth for beneath the head. Nereth brought it. She stood at the foot of the pallet, watching Brenn shake.

“I didn’t know you were there,” she said.

He looked at her without understanding.

“I thought the voices—”

“Nereth,” Riarhin said.

The girl stopped. Riarhin held her gaze until she went back into the other room.

Brenn had closed his eyes. His lips moved, not quite making words. Esvin sat beside him and began to tell him where he was: a workshop, a pallet, people nearby, water ready when he wanted it. He named no promises beyond the room. After a while the shaking lessened.

Riarhin moved the lamp so its flame no longer shone directly into the carrier’s eyes. Then she went out to Cadan.

Cadan sat on the floor beside his pack. There was grey dust on his knees and a cut on his chin. In his hand he held a little piece of patterned stone.

“What happened?” Riarhin asked.

“He was behind the turn. I heard him moving.”

“Was anyone with him?”

“He says one is farther down.”

“And the fourth?”

Cadan looked at the stone in his palm.

“He does not know.”

“What is that?”

“A piece from the wall. It came away when I leaned on it.”

The pattern was shallow, almost worn smooth: a band of repeating shapes interrupted by one figure turned the other way. Riarhin had seen something similar on a traded funerary cup, years before, though the cup had been small enough to fit in two hands and this was part of a much larger design.

“Obishaal,” Esvin said from the sleeping-room door.

She turned.

“You know it?”

“I know the border they put around some memorial things. That might be an imitation.”

“Brenn?”

“Asleep, I hope.”

Nereth had gone very still beside the bench. Maren noticed before Riarhin did.

“You’ve seen that name.”

“In the observations.”

“Show us.”

The girl fetched the book.

The name was there, in a different passage from the instructions she had used.

Riarhin read it twice. The marks were old, but the sound survived their arrangement well enough. Around it the writer had made observations about an answering surface, a pressure which increased when a vessel was opened, and something Nereth had translated as the farther lodging. Riarhin was less certain of that. The word could have meant a place occupied, or a thing which accommodated another. She had heard Ghere use it for a hollow in a tree and for the space a guest was expected to leave around someone else’s belongings.

There were sketches. One showed a high-sided stair. Another showed a wall marked with repeated shapes. Neither was exact enough to be a plan.

“You didn’t use this part?” she asked.

“No.”

“But you read it.”

“I read all of it.”

“Did you understand all of it?”

Nereth gave her an angry look.

“No.”

From the sleeping room Brenn called out. Esvin went to him. Riarhin stayed with the book, listening to the singer ask whether he wanted the lamp moved.

“No. No. Behind that.”

“Behind what?”

“The turn. She couldn’t get her leg out.”

Esvin’s answer was too quiet to hear.

“She told me to go. I didn’t go far.”

Riarhin closed her eyes briefly. When she opened them, Nereth was looking toward the hanging cloth.

“The place is already open,” the girl said. “It was open before I found it.”

“And now?”

Nereth did not answer.

A voice came from beyond the barred door.

“It can be made wide enough.”

Everyone stopped.

The voice was near, conversational, without any of the distance which had attended the whispers in the wood. It might have belonged to someone standing in the passage waiting to be invited in.

Nereth turned toward it before she seemed to realise she had moved.

“For them,” it said. “If that is what you require.”

Maren put her hand flat on the book.

“Don’t answer.”

Riarhin watched Nereth’s face. The girl looked frightened, but beneath the fear there was an attention Riarhin knew too well: the look of someone who had just been offered a way to do what she could not bear to leave undone.

She went to the sleeping room instead of the door.

Brenn was trying to sit up. Esvin had one hand behind his shoulders and was asking him to wait. Riarhin knelt on the other side of the pallet, low enough that he did not have to lift his head to see her.

“What is her name?” she asked.

His eyes moved to her.

“The one behind the turn.”

“Leth.”

“And the other?”

“Davin.”

“Did you see where Davin went?”

Brenn shook his head. The movement hurt him. He lay back with a small gasp.

Riarhin stayed until he had stopped trying to explain. Then she held the cup while he drank, a little at a time.

Chapter 25 — Yrdis

They went looking for a boat.

Haldren proposed it after examining the stone Cadan had brought back and the little metal token from the patrol’s passage. He sat with both objects on his knees, turning one and then the other, while Maren and Nereth disputed a measurement at the bench.

“The cuts aren’t a house,” he said.

Riarhin looked over.

“You said they were keeping marks.”

“They are. But see this end.”

She sat beside him. The token had a raised lip along one side, worn smooth where a cord had passed over it. Beneath the lip three strokes converged at a point.

“A boat mark?”

“Could be. The way my grandfather cut a stem before fitting it. So he knew which side he’d already pared.”

“A common mark?”

“Among people who learned from him.”

“Which wasn’t everyone.”

“No.”

He grinned a little, relieved that she had not made him claim more.

“Asket kept the boat,” she said.

“And the thing in the song wasn’t a sceptre.”

“According to whom?”

“My mother, mostly. She disliked the version with a sceptre. Said nobody could steer anything worth having while waving one.”

Esvin, listening from the hearth, said, “People have managed kingdoms that way.”

“Badly,” Haldren said.

Nereth told them there was a lower chamber with wood in it. She had taken only what was loose. The rest was too wet, or too large to move. A doorway beyond it led toward the place the guardian watched.

“She doesn’t come into the lower room,” she said.

“Why?” Riarhin asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Have you tried speaking to her?”

“She tried to put the spear through me.”

Riarhin remembered the point coming toward Esvin’s throat.

“Yes,” she said. “She does that.”

The lower chamber smelled of old water.

It lay beneath a wide opening which had once admitted something much larger than a person. Silt covered the floor in ripples. Against the far wall rested a curved timber with pegs projecting from it, and beside that several thinner lengths had collapsed together in a shape which suggested ribs. Haldren approached slowly. He touched one peg, then put his palm against the curved surface.

“Boat,” he said.

“How old?”

“Old.”

“How is it still here?”

He looked up at the dripping roots.

“I’d like to know that myself.”

Eddis crouched beside the lowest timber. He cleared silt with the edge of a broken tile and found a line of dark fibre pressed into a seam.

“Caulking.”

Haldren knelt beside him. For a while they spoke in terms Riarhin half understood, disagreeing about the way the planks had overlapped and whether the curve belonged near a bow or amidships. She let them. Esvin walked around the chamber without touching anything. Maren stayed at the entrance, watching the little loop of thread she had carried from Nereth’s room.

Riarhin found the tool beneath a shelf of fallen stone.

At first she thought it was part of the boat. A short handle projected from the silt, swollen at the end. She uncovered it carefully. The head was broad, with one face worn almost flat and the other nicked along its edge. A narrow band of metal held a split in the wood together. Someone had cut three marks into the handle above the grip, then crossed one out.

Haldren took it from her and weighed it in his hand.

“There,” he said.

Eddis smiled despite himself.

“Your mother’s sceptre.”

“Near enough.”

Riarhin looked at the crossed-out mark. It had been cut deeply enough that she could feel it through the dirt.

“Don’t clean it yet.”

Haldren lowered the tool.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were rubbing.”

“I rub when I look,” he said, and Maren laughed from the doorway.

The guardian stood beyond the next arch.

She was farther away than Riarhin had expected, on the opposite side of a long chamber whose floor had broken into uneven shelves. Between them lay roots, shallow water, and the remains of a tiled channel. Behind the woman something rose which Riarhin did not want to look at directly yet. The spear ran from the woman’s hand toward it, or from it toward her. Distance and the intervening roots made the arrangement difficult to understand.

Gold showed where her fingers closed. Farther back, within the roots, another length of gold remained at the same angle when her hand moved. Riarhin looked from one to the other. For an instant the two seemed joined by a shaft which could not have fitted in the room; then the intervening dark concealed the connection. She kept her eyes on the point nearest them.

Riarhin stopped before the arch. Haldren remained behind her with the tool wrapped in a cloth. Esvin stood to one side, visible but not advancing. They had agreed he would not begin with a song.

The woman’s face turned.

“Yrdis,” Riarhin said.

Nothing changed.

“Asket spoke to us.”

The spear lifted a little.

“He said he kept the boat.”

“Go.”

The voice was low. Riarhin could not tell whether the word had been meant for them or someone behind them.

“He asked us to find—”

“Go.”

Haldren shifted. The metal band on the tool struck the buckle of his belt.

The guardian moved so quickly that Riarhin had barely drawn Remorse before she was at the nearer edge of the broken floor. The spear came level. Riarhin stepped sideways, keeping the arch between its point and Haldren.

“We’re going,” she said.

The woman watched them retreat. She did not follow into the boat room.

Once they were clear, Haldren sat on a stone and put the tool across his knees.

“That went well.”

Esvin wiped his upper lip.

“We’re all still here.”

“I had hoped for more.”

“So had I.”

Riarhin sheathed Remorse. Her hand was steady; she could feel the effort of keeping it so.

They returned to the workshop without trying again.

Brenn had eaten three spoonfuls of broth and been sick.

Nereth was furious with herself for giving him too much. Esvin told her it might have happened with less and took the basin outside. Werrin had mended a torn strap while sitting with his foot raised. He offered the strap to Haldren as though it might compensate for remaining behind.

The carpenter thanked him and examined the stitches seriously.

“Good. Better than mine.”

“That isn’t difficult,” Nereth said.

Haldren looked at her.

“You’ve seen my stitching?”

“Your sleeve.”

He inspected it, discovered the repair had opened again, and held it out to Werrin without further defence.

Riarhin ate by the door. She had been trying to fit Asket’s broken account to the woman across the roots. The name had produced nothing she could recognise. The message about the boat had angered her, or frightened her, or recalled an instruction stronger than the one they wished to give. Riarhin disliked how readily she thought of giving an instruction.

Esvin sat beside her when the basin was clean.

“I could try the name within an address,” he said.

“Would that make her listen?”

“It might make her able to answer.”

“And if she doesn’t wish to?”

“Then we stop.”

“She already said go.”

“Yes.”

He ate a little, looking into the bowl.

Riarhin watched Haldren show Werrin where the sleeve needed reinforcing. The young man laughed at something the carpenter said. Across the room Nereth had fallen asleep with her cheek beside the book, one hand still on the page.

“Tomorrow,” Riarhin said.

Esvin nodded.

In the morning Haldren went back to the boat.

He asked Riarhin whether she intended to stop him. She asked what he intended to do. He said he wanted to see how it had been put together before the room changed its mind about remaining accessible.

She went with him.

They took Eddis and a lamp. Esvin stayed with Brenn. Maren and Nereth were comparing the fragment with a fitting in the root chamber, and Cadan had begun marking the route between the workshop and the broken approach. Riarhin had made him show her each mark before he put it in place. Some were too unobtrusive for her taste.

The boat room had not changed. Haldren seemed pleased by that. He laid the mallet on a dry stone and cleared the seam Eddis had exposed the day before.

“Hold the lamp here.”

Riarhin held it.

“No, lower.”

She lowered it.

“Can you see that?”

“I can see several things.”

“The scarf. There, beneath the peg.”

She saw the join then, two pieces fitted with such patience that the line between them had almost disappeared beneath the damp. Haldren ran a finger along it without pressing.

“Someone knew their work.”

Eddis had found a loose strip of fibre. He rolled it between his fingers, smelled it, and shook his head.

“Wouldn’t use that now.”

“Nor would I.”

“Would you patch this?” Riarhin asked.

Haldren looked along the broken curve.

“To float? No. To keep it from falling farther apart, perhaps.”

“Why?”

He shrugged.

“It’s falling apart.”

They found a loose board which could be laid beneath the weakest section without disturbing the rest. Haldren needed Riarhin to lift the curve a fraction while Eddis pushed the board under. She tried, found the timber heavier than it looked, and changed her footing.

“Don’t wrench it,” Haldren said.

“I’m not.”

“Your face says otherwise.”

She set it down, took off her pack, and tried again from her knees.

The board went under. Eddis withdrew his hands. Haldren eased the timber onto it and sat back on his heels.

“Better.”

“It still leaks,” said the woman at the arch.

Riarhin turned without reaching for her sword.

Yrdis stood in the doorway. The spear was upright beside her, its lower end hidden by the threshold. Her face looked older in the light of their lamp and less damaged than it had across the broken floor. Water darkened the hem of her clothing. There was no sound of dripping beneath her.

“Yes,” Haldren said after a moment. “It does.”

“You’ve raised the wrong part.”

“For floating,” he said. “We aren’t floating it.”

The woman looked at the board. Her attention moved slowly along the curve of the timber. Riarhin stayed on her knees.

“Who gave you that?” Yrdis asked.

“The mallet?”

“It isn’t yours.”

“We found it here.”

“You brought it.”

“Only to the next room. Then back.”

Yrdis looked toward the arch behind her, as though trying to place a sound heard in sleep.

“Asket,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“He always struck too hard.”

Haldren glanced at the split held by the metal band.

“Did he break it?”

“I did.”

There was such immediate irritation in the reply that Eddis almost smiled. He bent over the seam to hide it.

“I told him not to put it there,” she said. “He put it there.”

“Where?” Haldren asked.

“Under the thwart.”

She looked at the room again. The word appeared to have brought something with it which the room did not provide.

“Where is the thwart?”

Haldren pointed to the remains of a crosspiece. Her gaze followed his hand. For a long time she said nothing.

Esvin came when Riarhin sent Eddis for him. He did not bring the bell.

Yrdis had moved into the room, though not far. She stood where she could see both the broken boat and the arch. When Esvin appeared she watched his empty hands.

“I spoke with Asket,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He asked me to seek someone for him.”

“He should have gone when I told him.”

“He said you left him at the crossing.”

“I did.”

Esvin sat on the stone where Haldren had laid the mallet.

“He was angry.”

“He was often angry.”

“About this in particular.”

The spear shifted in her hand. Riarhin saw the motion and moved her own hand away from her sword rather than toward it. Yrdis noticed. Her eyes stayed on Riarhin for several breaths.

“There were people in the boat,” she said.

“He remembered.”

“They couldn’t walk. One had no foot. My sister said to take them downriver.”

“Did you?”

“As far as the crossing.”

Esvin waited. The room was cold enough that Riarhin’s knees had begun to ache through her trousers. She wanted to stand. She remained where she was.

“My sister could steer,” Yrdis said. “She always said she couldn’t. When I was there.”

Haldren looked at the boat and then at the woman.

“Did she take it on?”

“I don’t know.”

The answer came with difficulty. Yrdis’s mouth moved again, but no further words followed. She looked down at the mallet beside Esvin.

“They put that in the song,” Haldren said. “Made it a sceptre in one telling.”

“A what?”

“A thing a ruler holds.”

“I know what a sceptre is.”

He lowered his eyes.

“Sorry.”

“Did they give Asket one too?”

“No.”

“He held the other end while I worked. They might have given him something.”

Esvin reached for the mallet, then stopped short of it.

“May I move this?”

She nodded. He placed it between them, where she could see the three cuts and the one crossed out.

“What did you mark?”

“Trips.”

“Across the river?”

“Back to the boat.”

Her fingers tightened on the spear.

“The last one wasn’t finished.”

Yrdis asked Haldren to move the mallet closer to the broken crosspiece.

He did it. Her hand followed his until the fingers passed through the wood. Riarhin saw the change in her face and looked away too late.

“Again,” Yrdis said.

He moved it back, then forward. She watched the tool rather than her own hand. Her fingers remained on the spear.

The chamber grew colder. Esvin set one palm against the stone beside him, as if measuring the change through his skin. He did not sing.

“Where would you like it?” Haldren asked.

Yrdis pointed. He set it there, its handle towards the place where a person kneeling in the boat could have reached it. She looked at it for a long time.

“My sister hated sitting there.”

“By the seam?”

“She said I put her in the wettest place.”

“Was it?”

“Yes.”

Haldren nodded solemnly. Eddis had returned and stood beneath the doorway, keeping his boots out of a shallow puddle. Yrdis looked at his pole.

“Too short.”

“For what?”

“The lower crossing.”

“It reaches what I need.”

“The water’s higher now.”

Eddis glanced towards Riarhin. She gave him nothing to settle whether now meant their day or another. He turned the pole in his hands.

“I’ll test before using it.”

Yrdis seemed satisfied. Then she looked again at the broken boat and asked where the people had gone.

“Which people?” Esvin said.

“The ones who couldn’t walk.”

“We don’t know.”

“They were here.”

“I believe you.”

Her gaze moved across the silt. Riarhin could follow the places where it stopped: enough space for a body beside the hull, another beneath the arch, a third on the higher stone where the water would not reach. She had been judging the room in much the same way without wanting to notice it.

“My sister took the boat,” Yrdis said.

“You hoped she did,” Esvin answered gently.

“She could steer it.”

“Yes.”

Yrdis turned towards him. The spear’s point moved a little and Riarhin had to keep herself from shifting to cover him.

“Don’t speak as though you know her.”

Esvin lowered his eyes.

“I don’t.”

The point steadied. Nobody moved. Somewhere beneath the silt a bubble broke with a small wet sound.

At last Yrdis asked Haldren who had taught him the song. He told her again about his mother. She asked whether the woman had known Asket. Haldren hesitated. Riarhin felt his temptation to say something kind and untrue as if it were her own.

“No,” he said.

“Then why did she sing it?”

“To pass the time. Sometimes while she worked.”

Yrdis looked at the mallet. Her expression was difficult to watch, anger and something less settled passing through it without arriving at an answer.

“Did she work well?”

“She thought so.”

“Did you?”

“I thought she worked too long.”

That brought a little movement at the corner of Yrdis’s mouth. Riarhin could not call it a smile. The woman turned her attention to the small support they had put beneath the hull.

“Leave that,” she said.

They left it.

When they asked about passage she became difficult again.

The room beyond the roots was hers to keep. No one was to touch what stood within it. Riarhin said there were living people trapped beyond another stair. Yrdis answered that there had always been living people somewhere beyond something. Esvin did not correct her.

“We need to reach them,” Riarhin said.

“Then go.”

“You stopped us.”

“You came toward him.”

“Who?”

Yrdis turned her head toward the inner chamber. Riarhin could see the effort of remembering and the effort of refusing it contend in her face.

“The one who would not stop.”

“The man in the roots?”

“Don’t touch him.”

“We aren’t asking to.”

“You will.”

Riarhin had no honest answer ready. The fragment in Maren’s box had turned toward that chamber. Nereth’s whole attempt depended on the wound beneath it. She would very likely have to ask.

“For now,” she said, “we need only the passage along the wall. We can show you where.”

Yrdis studied her.

“You say for now.”

“Yes.”

The woman looked at Esvin, then Haldren. Last she looked at the boat, supported by the new board with no prospect of ever floating again.

“The wall,” she said. “Not the middle.”

“Will you let the others pass?”

“If they stay there.”

“And after?”

“No.”

Riarhin inclined her head. She rose carefully, feeling the stiffness in both knees. Yrdis watched the movement without helping.

At the arch the woman paused.

“I didn’t sing that song,” she said to Haldren.

“I didn’t think you did.”

“My sister sang. I couldn’t keep it.”

“The tune?”

Yrdis looked impatient again.

“Yes. The tune.”

Then she went back through the arch. Riarhin heard no footsteps after the first two.

Haldren remained kneeling beside the boat. He picked a little mud from the metal band around the mallet and set the tool down where it had been found.

They left the lamp burning while they checked the permitted passage. When they came back, the wick had begun to smoke, and Eddis complained that nobody but him ever remembered the oil.

Chapter 26 — The settlement beyond the line

Maren spread the map on the floor because the bench was too short.

It was not much of a map. Nereth had drawn the coast as a crooked line, put the mill too close to the channel, and left out several places which Maren considered indispensable. The keeper added them while Nereth watched, saying nothing until Maren moved one of the outer marks.

“That can’t go there.”

“The house is there.”

“The mark isn’t.”

“Then draw the mark separately.”

Nereth took the charcoal and did so, hard enough to tear a small hole in the paper.

Riarhin sat beside them. Werrin was on the stool with his foot raised, Haldren behind him mending the heel of his boot. Esvin had gone to Brenn, who could now keep down a little food. Through the hanging cloth they could hear the singer talking about something which had happened in a kitchen. Brenn made a sound which might have been a laugh.

Nereth drew a second line beyond the mill.

“This is where it can go.”

“What can?” Riarhin asked.

“The approach. What we’ve been turning. It doesn’t have to spread everywhere.”

She added three strokes, joined them, and set a stone on the intersection.

“If I take this down and move the return here, the mill holds. So does the lower lane. The water mark stops pulling against the tower.”

Maren stared at the stone.

“The dryingshed houses.”

“They’re empty.”

“For now.”

“They’re under the bells. You put them there.”

Riarhin remembered the woman pushing a handcart through the mud while a boy held two hens against his chest. The younger child had carried a spoon, gripped in a fist all the way to the shelter. She leaned forward and moved the stone enough to see what lay beneath it.

“Those seven houses?”

“And the ground beyond. There’s room.”

“For how long?”

Nereth looked at Maren rather than Riarhin.

“Until we can make the whole line.”

“And until then?”

“They stay where they are.”

It took some time to make Nereth show them the full extent.

She had not concealed it on purpose, she said. She had left out fields because fields were not thresholds, and the well because the well did not have an admission, and the little path between the dryingshed and the raised gardens because she had never used it. Maren put them back. Each addition made the spared area on the other side of Nereth’s line look more generous.

The girl watched the paper fill.

“You’re making it look like a village.”

“It is one,” Maren said.

“It’s seven houses.”

“Eight if you count the old place.”

“No one lives in that.”

“Mistress Aven keeps her seeds there.”

The name brought back the oilcloth packet bound with green thread. Riarhin had found it behind the bowls while the woman and her husband argued about which jar held it. At the shelter the woman had kept both hands around it.

“Can they reach the gardens?” Riarhin asked.

“Not by that path,” Nereth said.

“Another?”

“Perhaps from the east.”

“Maren?”

“In dry weather. With someone keeping the lower gate.”

“And the well?”

The keeper shook her head.

Nereth put down the charcoal.

“There are people below us now.”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

“There are people at the mill now. There are children sleeping because I made somewhere they could sleep. If we wait for everyone to have everything they had before, we won’t do anything.”

Werrin looked away from the map. His mother’s house stood on the protected side. Riarhin could see him finding it, though he had no reason to point.

“Do they know this is what you’re proposing?” she asked.

“They’re not here.”

“Have you sent word?”

“There hasn’t been time.”

“You had time to draw it.”

Nereth flushed. Riarhin regretted the line almost as soon as she said it; it had been meant to hurt. The girl gathered the loose edge of the paper as if to fold it, then stopped because Maren’s knee held it down.

“I can keep them alive,” she said. “That is what I am offering.”

Maren’s voice was tired when she answered.

“You can keep some of them alive in someone else’s shelter. I can’t promise they will accept that.”

“They’ll be dead if we don’t.”

“You don’t know that.”

“You don’t know they won’t.”

From behind the cloth Brenn coughed. Esvin asked him to turn a little. The conversation on the floor stopped until the coughing did.

Riarhin rubbed her eyes. She wanted a plan which did not begin by sending someone away to seek permission while a woman lay trapped behind a narrowing stair. Nereth’s lines were neat. They had the attraction of all maps on which the difficult ground belonged to somebody absent.

“No,” she said.

Nereth looked at her.

“Not this.”

“Then what?”

“We work with what can be held here.”

“You don’t know if that will be enough.”

“No.”

The girl stood so quickly that her heel caught the edge of the paper. It tore farther. She walked into her sleeping room and pulled the cloth across. Brenn was still in there. After a moment she came out again, angrier for having nowhere private to go, and went through the outer door instead.

Riarhin let her go as far as the stair. Cadan, sitting beside the entrance, looked up. She shook her head at him.

“Just the stair,” she said.

He nodded and remained seated.

Werrin put his boot on after midday.

The leather had been softened and the heel let out. He stood carefully, tested his weight, and tried to make the first step look easier than it was. Haldren watched without comment until the younger man reached the door.

“You’re limping.”

“I’ll loosen up.”

“You’ll rub it open.”

“I can’t stay forever.”

“No,” Riarhin said. “But you can sit while we decide what you’re taking.”

She asked him to carry a warning.

He wanted to know whether Nereth knew. Riarhin said she would. He wanted to know whether it meant taking down the wards. Maren said no, and explained the distinction twice, in different words, until he stopped looking at the map as though it might turn into an order to dismantle his mother’s door.

They wrote the message on the back of a clean account sheet. Maren named the houses, the well, the gardens, and the path. She told the keepers not to send anyone home through that approach until someone had checked it in daylight from both ends. She did not tell them the danger had already been directed there. It had not. Riarhin made her strike out a phrase which sounded as though it had.

Werrin read the page slowly.

“I don’t know which Mistress Aven this means.”

“The one with the seeds,” Riarhin said.

“There are two.”

Maren added the husband’s name.

“Will this do?”

“Yes.”

“And Kelva receives a copy.”

“I’ll go there first.”

“No,” Maren said. “The keeper first. Then Kelva.”

He shifted his sore foot.

“The mill is on the way.”

Riarhin looked at the map. It was on the way if his return followed the same marks. If they had changed, there was no reason to believe any familiar order would hold.

“Who is the first person you can reach?” she asked.

“My mother’s cousin keeps the upper frame.”

“Then her. Tell her to send someone on. Don’t carry the only copy all the way to the mill if there’s help at the frame.”

Maren wrote another copy. The delay irritated her, but she handed it over without saying so.

Nereth returned while they were folding the pages.

“His foot isn’t fit,” she said.

“He says it will take him,” Riarhin answered.

“It will take me,” Werrin said, less confidently now that two people had disputed it.

Nereth knelt and checked the boot herself. He let her. She loosened the fastening above his ankle, pulled the leather away from the heel, and told Haldren he had stitched it too close at the side.

“I can let it out again,” he said.

“We haven’t time.”

She looked at Riarhin as she said it. Then she fetched a narrow strip of horn from the shelf and wrapped it in cloth.

“For the white root,” she told Werrin. “If the mark isn’t showing, put this down facing the cut. Wait until you can see both sides. Don’t go through one.”

“What if neither shows?”

“Come back.”

He took the wrapped horn.

“What if I can’t come back?”

Nereth had no immediate answer. Riarhin watched the certainty leave her face.

“Then stay on the old ground,” Maren said. “Don’t follow anything which has only just appeared. Ring if you have to.”

Werrin touched the little bell at his belt.

“I thought I wasn’t to ring.”

“If you have to,” she repeated.

Riarhin went to look at the return before Werrin put on his pack.

She asked Nereth to come with her. The girl was sitting beside the hearth, pressing the edge of the torn map flat against a board. She looked up as though expecting another argument.

“I need to see what we’re sending him through.”

“You’ve seen him come through it.”

“No. I saw him here.”

Nereth set down the board. She took a lamp, then changed her mind and left it. Riarhin fetched her own. They passed through the old workrooms to an opening she had mistaken for a collapsed cupboard. In the morning it had been full of roots. Now there was room for a person to pass sideways, and beyond it a strip of firm earth led between two pale trunks.

“Keep your shoulder toward me,” Nereth said.

“Which?”

The girl almost made an impatient gesture. Then she looked at the opening and pointed.

“Your right. The wall’s higher on that side.”

Riarhin went through. The back of her coat scraped stone. A short drop waited at the other side, no more than the height of a stool, but the place to set her foot was narrow and wet. She lowered herself with one hand against the jamb.

Beyond the pale trunks the way ran beneath a low arch of growth. She could walk it bent over. She could see the next mark from the first, a pale wedge set near the ground. Beyond that another turn disappeared into mist.

“Does it remain this narrow?”

“Some of it.”

“How much?”

“I haven’t measured every pace.”

Riarhin knelt to look at the ground beneath the arch. There was room for one person crawling. No room for someone to carry another person on a shoulder. A litter would have to turn where its bearers could not stand beside it.

They had discussed sending Brenn with the courier. Esvin had refused to say the man would survive another journey so soon, but Riarhin had wanted to see whether the way itself offered any kindness. She put down the lamp and took two pieces of fallen wood, setting them at the width of Brenn’s shoulders.

Nereth watched without speaking.

Riarhin tried the turn with the pieces held across her arms. One end caught beneath the arch. She changed the angle, found another obstruction, and stopped.

“You couldn’t lift him there,” Nereth said.

“No.”

“He’d have to help.”

“He can’t.”

The girl bent beside her. For a moment both looked through the passage at the clear space beyond, close enough to make the obstruction seem spiteful.

“We could alter this mark,” Nereth said.

“What would that move?”

“I’d have to try it.”

Riarhin set the pieces down.

“Leave it.”

Nereth’s mouth tightened, but she left it.

The first pale wedge was loose.

Riarhin noticed because the girl put out a hand to it, stopped, and wiped her fingers on her skirt instead. She crouched beside it and waited until Nereth said, “The water’s been under.”

“Can you make it sound?”

“Yes.”

“What happens while you do?”

“Nothing, if the next one’s still carrying.”

“Is it?”

Nereth looked ahead. The second wedge showed beyond the low arch. Its pale edge was clear. She went toward it, paused, and came back.

“Hold this. Here.”

She placed Riarhin’s hand against the nearer wedge. Then she took a little flat stone from her pocket and slid it beneath the loose side. The fitting settled with a click.

Riarhin felt something pull against the heel of her hand.

“Keep it there,” Nereth said quickly.

She pressed. The resistance increased, more like a door leaning into her palm than a piece of stone. Beyond the arch, the second pale mark grew indistinct. Riarhin could still see the ground around it. Only the wedge had become difficult to place.

“Nereth.”

“I see it.”

The girl withdrew the little stone halfway. The resistance eased. She changed its angle, tried again, and swore when the wedge tilted toward Riarhin’s fingers.

For several breaths neither moved.

Riarhin could hear a faint sound beyond the arch. It had the rhythm of steps, but there was no visible movement on the path. She fixed her eyes on the girl’s hands.

“Do you want me to let go?”

“No.”

“Tell me before you change that.”

Nereth nodded. She used a second sliver beneath the opposite side, working more slowly now. The pressure beneath Riarhin’s palm diminished. The farther wedge appeared plainly again, its edge catching the small light.

“Now,” Nereth said.

Riarhin lifted her hand. Nothing moved.

The girl sat back on her heels. She was breathing too quickly. Riarhin flexed her fingers, feeling a soreness in the palm which had no visible cause.

“How often does that need doing?”

“Not often.”

Riarhin waited.

“More now,” Nereth said.

She gathered the unused pieces into her pocket. One fell. Riarhin picked it up and gave it back.

“You could have asked me sooner.”

“I thought you’d tell me not to touch it.”

Riarhin looked at the secured wedge, then at the narrow way which would take Werrin toward the coast. The girl was right about that. She had been ready to stop her before knowing what the repair required.

“Tell him this one’s been reset. Show him which side stays dry.”

“I will.”

They brought Werrin as far as the low arch.

Haldren had made him a short staff from a straight length found upstairs. Werrin had said he did not need it, then taken it when no one argued. He used it now to lower himself from the old doorway. The tip slipped once. He corrected it and tried to make the second movement look ordinary.

Nereth checked the band around his heel while he stood. He complained that she had already done it. She said she would stop when he learned how to tell whether it was rubbing. He said he had learned that very thoroughly.

Riarhin had the two messages in separate wrappings. She gave him the first for his inner pocket and the second for the small pouch at his belt.

“If one gets wet, you still have the other.”

“I know.”

“Read the names back.”

He looked irritated. Then he did it. He remembered the households and the well, forgot the gardens, and added them after Maren prompted him. His face grew red.

“You won’t be reading it from memory,” Riarhin said. “I want you to know what matters if something happens to the pages.”

He looked past her toward the workshop.

“And if they ask about him?”

“Brenn is alive. Too weak for this way. We’re bringing him.”

“His wife will ask.”

“Yes.”

Werrin gripped the staff. He was older than Nereth by several years, and for a moment looked younger than she did. Riarhin wanted to take back the papers and find a messenger who did not know the people waiting. There was nobody to find.

“Don’t promise a day,” she said.

“I won’t.”

“Tell her you’ve seen him.”

He nodded.

Nereth made him look at the reset wedge. She pointed out the dry side and the little sliver beneath it, telling him not to try to improve it. He listened more carefully than he had in the workshop. His pack was lighter than on his arrival. He had left the food and taken the messages, two empty oil bottles, and a shirt Nereth wanted washed. The shirt seemed to embarrass her more than the warning.

“Kelva said she’d do it,” she explained.

“I didn’t say she hadn’t,” Werrin said.

He embraced her awkwardly, one arm around her shoulders and the other trapped beneath the pack strap. She stiffened, then pressed her face briefly against his coat.

“Eat,” he said.

“Go.”

He went.

Riarhin watched until he reached the second turn. He limped, paused to adjust the pack, and disappeared behind a pale trunk. For a while she could hear his uneven steps. Then the wood took the sound.

Nereth remained beside her.

“If he doesn’t get there,” she said, “will you still say we had to ask?”

Riarhin looked at the empty path. She did not trust herself to answer without saying something cruel or useless.

“We need to go back,” she said.

The girl walked ahead of her, taking care not to step on the marks.

Chapter 27 — A smaller shelter

The Chord fitted the place in the wall as though it had been cut from it.

Maren did not put it in at first. She held the box beneath the hollow while Haldren raised the lamp and Nereth stood on the other side with her hands tucked under her arms. The hollow was shallow, branching at one end. Dark gold ran through the stone around it. Some of the gold had been worked into fine lines; some seemed to have grown through the grain of the stone without regard for a tool.

Riarhin could see the resemblance before Maren took off the wrapping.

“That doesn’t mean it belongs there now,” she said.

“No,” Maren answered. “It doesn’t.”

The keeper measured twice with Haldren’s knotted cord, then once with a strip of paper. She compared the branching ends. Nereth pointed out a turn which Maren had missed, and Maren thanked her without looking up.

“It was taken from a longer run,” Nereth said.

“How do you know?”

“The end. It doesn’t finish.”

Riarhin examined it. The broken lines continued toward the wall’s deeper centre. Beyond them, partly hidden by roots, lay a dark rectangular stone set into the floor. Its surface was smooth except for a shallow depression at one end. Around it the later growth had bent without crossing.

“The seat,” Nereth said.

“The Keystone?”

“Yes.”

Haldren crouched beside it. He put no weight on it.

“That’s not coming out easily.”

“It isn’t coming out at all,” Maren said.

Nereth began to object, then stopped. Maren looked at her.

“If you lift it, what holds this end while the other is open?”

“The central—”

“The central thing is what we’re trying to stop using.”

Riarhin looked through the broken arch into the inner chamber. Yrdis stood beside the raised mass at its centre. They had not approached close enough to see the body clearly. The spear lay at an angle between the woman’s hand and the roots. Now Riarhin understood why it had seemed to extend differently each time she looked: Yrdis moved, but the point remained fixed.

Maren followed her gaze.

“Until that’s finished, this stays.”

“And afterward?” Riarhin asked.

“Afterward we see what is left to stand on.”

They made the first trial around an alcove no larger than Nereth’s sleeping room.

Haldren cleared fallen stone from its entrance. Eddis carried the smaller pieces and complained about every one, which reassured Riarhin more than silence would have. Cadan helped lift a slab, set it down when Haldren told him, and went back to checking the return marks.

Maren laid the Chord fragment against the hollow without seating it fully. Nereth ran a temporary connection from the nearest branch to a flat plate on the alcove floor. She used one of the nails Haldren had straightened, then selected a bent one for a second position.

He watched her do it.

“I’ll stop improving those.”

“Please.”

For a while nothing happened. A dead beetle lay near the plate. Riarhin found herself watching it and made herself look elsewhere.

At the far end of the passage a figure moved between the roots.

It was one of the patrol. She recognised the torn cloth hanging from its shoulder. It came toward them in short, uneven steps, turning its head at the same point on each side as though responding to someone who walked beside it. Riarhin drew Remorse and waited outside the alcove.

“Don’t strike unless it crosses,” Maren said.

“How close?”

“The plate.”

The figure approached. Its foot dragged through loose grit. A hand opened, closed, opened again. At the plate it stopped.

Riarhin could see an old cut across its cheek. The skin around it had dried back from the teeth. It stood within reach, head still turning from side to side.

Then it stepped away.

Not far. Only enough to leave the entrance clear. It continued down the passage toward the broken arch and passed out of sight.

Eddis exhaled noisily.

“Do that again,” he said.

“We will,” Maren answered.

They did. They altered one fitting, watched the line fail, restored it, and watched another figure turn. The work took most of the afternoon. Riarhin’s sword arm grew tired from waiting. Haldren brought her water and held the lamp while she drank.

Nereth remained intent, quick with her hands, forgetting to defend herself when there was something immediately useful to do. Twice Maren made her slow down and explain a change. The second time she did it without protest.

By the end they had a space which could hold eight people closely, more if nobody needed to lie down.

Nereth looked from the sheltered floor to the supplies still stacked outside. When Brenn was brought in on the pallet, she moved her own things to make room for him.

The route to the alcove crossed a broken gallery.

They could stand at one end and see the other clearly: a gap no wider than a room, with a surviving strip of floor along the wall and a fall beneath it into darkness. A root as thick as Riarhin’s body lay across the lower part, taking some of the weight of the stone. Water ran along its upper surface and vanished through a crack.

Haldren spent a long time looking before he said anything.

“We can take weight along the wall. Not the middle.”

“Brenn can’t walk it,” Riarhin said.

“No.”

“Can we carry him?”

“Not on that.”

He pointed to the surviving strip. Its edge had broken away in flakes. Farther along, where the root entered the wall, the stone above it had lifted enough to admit a finger.

“What do you need?”

“A beam which isn’t rotten.”

The first beam broke while they were moving it.

They had found it beneath an upper stair, dry on the face which showed and hollow underneath. Haldren had tested the end and disliked it. Eddis said it only needed to lie across the short gap, not hold up the whole fortress. The carpenter asked him to help lift it.

They raised one end between them. The beam split along its hidden side, spilling dry fragments and a nest of something which had long since gone. Eddis dropped his end and stepped back.

Haldren looked at him.

“I take it back,” the ferryman said.

They went on looking.

Riarhin found the second in an old doorway, half sheltered by a fallen slab. It was longer than they needed and lodged at one end beneath the masonry. She could see how it might come free if they lifted the nearer corner of the slab. She could also see where the slab would fall if they lifted carelessly.

Haldren crouched and worked his knife into the wood. At the first place he tried, the outer surface came away in damp curls. He made a face. Farther along the blade met resistance.

“Help me turn the light.”

Riarhin moved the lamp. He tested another place, then another. His knuckles scraped the underside of the slab. He shook the hand once and continued.

“Sound inside.”

“Can we free it?”

“We can try.”

Cadan came up the stair carrying two short props. Maren had found them while clearing the alcove. One still had a shallow socket in its end, cut to receive something round. Haldren examined them, accepted one, and sent the other back down.
