“That one’s twisted.”

“It would hold for a moment,” Cadan said.

“I need several moments.”

They cleared loose stone first. Riarhin passed pieces to Eddis, who placed them against the sound wall rather than throwing them down the stair. Each journey took him out of sight for a little while. She waited for his return before offering the next piece. There was too little space to accumulate mistakes.

Under the third stone she found a shoe.

It was little more than leather pressed flat against the floor. The stitching had survived along one side, and the heel remained thick enough to show where it had worn unevenly. She laid it aside without looking inside.

Eddis came back for the next stone. His eyes went to the shoe.

“Anything else?”

“Not that I can see.”

He nodded and took the weight.

By the time they could reach the whole beam, Riarhin’s thighs had begun to tremble from crouching. She stood at the doorway and straightened slowly. Through the gap beyond the missing wall she could see the tops of trees, their wet leaves dull in the failing light. Somewhere below, water ran through one of the old channels. She could not tell in which direction.

Haldren set the sound prop beneath the slab.

“Cadan, there. Hold it upright. Don’t lift until I say.”

Cadan did as he was told. Riarhin found a lever long enough to keep their hands away from the edge. The carpenter put it in place, tested the fulcrum, and told Eddis where to pull the beam.

“Riarhin and I lift. You pull only when it’s clear.”

“How clear?” Eddis asked.

“I’ll tell you.”

They lifted.

The slab rose less than a finger’s breadth. Dust fell from beneath it. Eddis pulled. The beam moved, stuck, and moved again with a squeal of wood against stone.

“Enough,” Haldren said.

They lowered the slab onto the prop and stopped to breathe. Riarhin’s hands had begun to sweat despite the cold. She wiped them on her trousers. Eddis sat on the beam’s freed end, putting no more weight on it than he had to.

“One more?”

“Two,” Haldren said.

The second lift went more easily. That made Riarhin nervous. She watched the fulcrum instead of the moving slab. It shifted a little when they lowered the weight, and she made Haldren wait while she cleared the crumbling edge beneath it.

He looked, nodded, and changed the angle.

The third lift freed the beam. Eddis pulled it toward the doorway while Riarhin and Haldren held the slab up. Cadan steadied the prop until the beam was clear. Then they lowered the stone into the space it had occupied.

The sound went through the floor and returned from somewhere beneath them.

All four stood still.

Nothing else moved.

Haldren rubbed the back of his neck, leaving a pale streak of dust along the skin. He looked at the beam on the floor and smiled, briefly and without an audience in mind.

“There you are.”

Moving it downstairs took longer than freeing it. They could not turn the full length at the first landing. Haldren cut away the useless end while the others supported it, the saw making a patient, rasping noise which Riarhin expected something to answer. Nothing did. He wrapped the sawn end before they lifted again so it would not tear their hands.

At the narrowest turn she had to stand beneath it while Eddis brought the upper end around. Her ribs hurt. She kept her breath shallow and waited for Haldren’s direction. When he said lower she nearly let it drop too quickly. Cadan took more of the weight without comment.

They got it to the gallery and put it down.

For a while no one wanted to move it again. Haldren knelt beside it, tested the newly cut end, and examined the grain in the lamplight.

“It’ll take the span,” he said.

“Will it take two people?”

“Two moving carefully. Not four jumping on it to prove me wrong.”

Eddis rubbed his shoulder where the beam had pressed.

“One of us will have to fetch more oil if you’re planning a demonstration.”

They slid the beam into place, braced it against sound stone, and laid narrow boards across the worst break. Haldren cut a notch to keep one board from shifting. Riarhin held the lamp. Her wrist trembled toward the end, and she changed hands without being asked.

“This end is good,” he said, tapping the wall. “The other is good enough. If that root moves sideways, don’t stand on it.”

“How will we know?”

“You’ll see the board lift. Here.”

He pressed beneath the edge to show her. It rose a little.

“Like that.”

“And if someone is already crossing?”

“Get them off.”

He said it plainly, without the reassuring addition Riarhin had half hoped for.

They tested the crossing with empty loads, then with Eddis carrying a sack of stone. Haldren went beside him on the sound strip. At the far end Eddis set the sack down and sat on it.

“That’s my journey for the day.”

“You have to come back,” Haldren said.

“I’ll send for my things.”

Riarhin laughed. It surprised her enough that she laughed again, briefly, while both men looked pleased with themselves.

Cadan showed her the proposed recovery route after supper.

From the stair he had used, a passage turned twice before dividing. Brenn had been near the first turn. Leth was beyond the second, where part of the wall had come down. Davin had last been seen farther on, before the division. Cadan drew it with charcoal on a loose tile. He left the farther reaches blank.

“How much time?” Riarhin asked.

“I do not know.”

“How much can we give you?”

“That is different.”

“Yes.”

Before they agreed on anything, Riarhin went with him as far as the third step.

The opening had quieted since Brenn’s return. Nereth watched the loop while Maren checked the bowl. They allowed no change to the fittings. Riarhin crouched on the pale stone and held the lamp low enough to see where its light ceased to belong to the room behind her.

Cadan put a hand against the wall beyond the fourth step. His fingers were visible. Their shadow fell upward.

“That’s where he was,” he said.

Brenn had described being behind a turn. The turn was visible now, apparently no farther away than the workshop hearth. Riarhin wanted to go and look. She could cover the distance quickly. She could hear a faint movement beyond it, which might have been someone shifting against stone.

“Can you hear that?”

“Yes.”

“Leth?”

“I don’t know.”

She leaned a little farther. The far side of the arch came into view, then the high edge of a step below. Her memory of the first test supplied the rest with such confidence that for a moment she was ready to put her foot on a surface she could not see.

Cadan’s hand closed on the back of her coat.

She sat back at once. Anger came before gratitude, hot and embarrassing. He released her when she was steady.

“I wasn’t going down.”

He waited.

She looked at the dark step again.

“I hadn’t decided.”

“I know.”

That answer irritated her more than a correction would have. She stood, keeping her hands clear of the wall, and went back to the room.

Cadan followed. He had brought the little hollowed stone again. He set it on the tile beside his sketch and turned it with one finger until a narrow scratch faced him.

“What does it tell you?” she asked.

“Where I put it.”

“And if it moves?”

“I have a reason to doubt the way.”

“It doesn’t keep you safe.”

“No.”

She sat beside him. Her pulse had not settled. She could still hear that faint movement around the turn, whether from the room or from the memory of listening.

“Brenn told us someone was there,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That isn’t the same as what I heard.”

“No.”

Behind them Nereth called that the loop was turning. The arch shortened while they watched, shutting off the sound beyond it. It had been open for less time than they had needed to move Brenn up three steps. Cadan looked toward the silent room, then moved the sketch so both of them could see it and asked which point she wanted him to return to if the opening altered before he found anyone.

They worked through the possibilities. One turn blocked, the other clear. A voice from an opening which had not been there. A body unable to move. Cadan knew more of such places than she did and declined to invent a certainty when she pressed him. She had to stop asking the same question in slightly different ways before he would draw the next mark.

At last she said, “If you can see one of us, come toward that person. If you can only hear us, use the line.”

“And if the line leads elsewhere?”

“You don’t follow it farther to find out how much elsewhere.”

He smiled faintly.

“That I can agree to.”

They settled on signals which could be made without relying on a voice travelling in the expected direction. Two pulls to return; a continued pull if the approach was closing; three measured knocks through the line to say he had reached someone and was beginning the return. He would not spend the remaining interval searching an unknown branch without coming back to report.

“If you hear someone?” she asked.

“I may have to choose.”

“You come back.”

“If they are near—”

“You come back and tell us where.”

Cadan looked at her. His face was courteous, attentive, and for once she could not read satisfaction in it.

“All right.”

She made him repeat the instruction. He did, exactly.

When they returned to the workshop, Esvin was sitting beside the door with the metal token in his hand. He had not brought it out for another address. He was simply holding it, rubbing the worn edge with his thumb.

“Will she hold the passage?” he asked.

“We haven’t asked her yet.”

“What are you offering?”

“An end.”

“To what?”

Riarhin leaned against the wall. She was tired enough to dislike him for asking.

“To being here.”

“When?”

“When we’ve got them out.”

“And if we can’t?”

She looked at the token. Asket had stayed with a boat because someone had to, and was still angry about the person who left.

“We’ll have to ask her,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Will you sing?”

“If I know whose promise I’m carrying.”

He closed his hand around the token and put it away. From the alcove Brenn called for water. Esvin rose before Riarhin could move.

Before lying down, Riarhin returned to the lower room with Esvin.

There were remains beneath the overturned bench near the boat. She had seen them earlier and mistaken part of the curve for another broken timber. Now Esvin held the lamp while she cleared loose silt from the edge. Enough lay together to show where someone had fallen or been placed. Nothing gave them a name.

They took what could be lifted without disturbing the hull. Esvin wrapped the small bundle in cloth and marked where it had come from. Riarhin looked at the rest, held beneath the old wood, and wanted to try moving the bench.

“It will bring that down,” he said.

She followed his finger. A narrow stone above the boat rested on the bench’s far end.

“All right.”

She helped him carry the bundle to the packs. It was lighter than she expected. She checked the knot twice and did not disturb it again.

Late that night Maren sat inside the smaller shelter with her back against the wall.

Nereth had gone to fetch another page. Haldren lay asleep on his folded coat, one arm over his eyes. Eddis was checking the outside mark. For the first time since they had entered the fortress, Riarhin could hear only ordinary movement around her.

She sat beside Maren.

“Does it need watching?”

“Yes.”

“Every moment?”

“No. Every change.”

Riarhin looked at the keeper’s hands. They were stained with charcoal and pale powder, the skin cracked beside two nails. Maren flexed them slowly.

“Kelva could learn this,” she said.

Riarhin thought of the miller’s broad hands, the bowl she kept beside the hearth for Nereth.

“I expect she could.”

“Not tonight.”

“No.”

They sat without speaking until Nereth returned. She had brought the page and three cups of water. She handed one to each of them, then lowered herself to the floor with a sigh so young and exhausted that Riarhin had to look away for a moment.

The three of them drank. Beyond the entrance, a figure passed without turning in.

Chapter 28 — The expansion

Brenn woke asking for Leth.

Esvin told him they were making a way. Brenn listened, then asked again. He did not seem to understand that the answer belonged to the same question. After the third time Esvin sat beside him and stopped offering the plan. He asked whether Leth had family at the coast.

“A sister,” Brenn said. “Not there. South.”

“What is her name?”

Brenn closed his eyes. The effort of finding it made his mouth move.

“Don’t know.”

“All right.”

“She’ll know.”

“Yes.”

Nereth stood in the doorway, listening. Riarhin saw her there when she went to relieve Eddis. The girl had a bowl in one hand and a folded page in the other. She moved aside without looking up.

At the outer mark Eddis complained that the water had risen while he was watching it. He had marked the level against a crack. Now it stood above the crack, though no rain reached that part of the ruin.

“Which way?” Riarhin asked.

He pointed toward the inner chamber.

“Not all of it. That’s what I don’t like.”

A thin stream ran across the floor in the ordinary direction. Beside it, separated by a ridge of old mortar, another moved the opposite way. Riarhin crouched and watched a fragment of leaf turn at the division. It circled twice, caught against a stone, and remained there.

“Tell Maren.”

“I did.”

“What did she say?”

“She wanted another hour without anything changing.”

“So do I.”

Eddis went to wake Haldren. Riarhin remained at the mark, listening to the slow movement in the roots.

Nereth brought her a message after a while.

It was the paper Werrin had delivered from his mother. Nereth had opened it the night before. The edges were already soft from handling.

“Read the bottom,” she said.

Riarhin took it. The first lines concerned food, a shirt, someone who had borrowed a pot and not returned it. Below them the hand grew tighter. An Aeler party had visited two houses, inspected the new wards, and left instructions that no further fittings were to be installed. Someone had been asked to surrender a copy of Nereth’s working. The writer did not know whether they had done so. There was a line about grain which had been crossed out, then written again more carefully.

They will wait until you come back. That is what they say now.

Riarhin handed it back.

“Werrin didn’t tell us.”

“He didn’t want me to leave.”

“Leave here?”

“Come home to argue.”

“Would you have?”

Nereth folded the paper.

“If they take the book, I won’t get it back.”

“We aren’t giving them the book.”

“You don’t live there.”

Riarhin could have said she had refused powerful people before. It would not have answered the girl. At the coast there were houses which needed repair, grain already owed, and people who might prefer the certainty of an old bell to waiting for a stranger’s promise.

“We have part of a working line,” she said. “Maren can show them that.”

“A room.”

“A room which doesn’t send the dead through someone else’s garden.”

Nereth’s face closed.

Riarhin wished she had said it differently. She was too tired to find the words before the girl turned away.

Riarhin found Nereth washing bowls in the workroom.

The girl had carried water from a place Eddis marked safe. She was using it too sparingly, wiping each bowl with a damp cloth and turning it against the light. The cloth had ceased to clean anything. Riarhin fetched another and put it beside her.

Nereth did not thank her. She changed cloths.

For a while Riarhin dried what the girl handed over. The blue cup had nails in it again. There was a chip in one bowl which caught the cloth each time she passed it around the rim. She turned the chip inward on the shelf so nobody would cut a finger reaching for it.

“That was Werrin,” Nereth said.

“The bowl?”

“He dropped it.”

“I expect it’s been dropped before.”

“Not while I had it.”

The girl examined the next bowl, dissatisfied with a mark which would not come away. She rubbed harder. Riarhin waited until the rubbing ceased.

“You could come home with us,” she said.

Nereth looked up.

“After we bring them through. Let Maren take the pattern.”

“And what do I do?”

“Sleep.”

The girl gave a short, disbelieving laugh.

“For how long?”

“As long as you can manage.”

“You sound like Kelva.”

“She seems to get some things right.”

Nereth put the bowl down carefully. Riarhin could feel the talk slipping toward the argument they had already had. She picked up another bowl instead of pursuing it.

After a while Nereth said, “The first one wasn’t at the mill.”

“The first working?”

The girl nodded.

“An old woman at the reed cut. Her husband used to come to the window. Every night. It wasn’t always him, she said, but she couldn’t stop looking.”

Riarhin set the dry bowl on the shelf.

“Was there a bell?”

“A little one. The rope had to be kept moving. Her hands hurt too much by morning to close the latch. She tied the rope to her ankle, but then she couldn’t sleep because she had to keep moving that instead.”

Nereth spoke toward the basin. Riarhin watched the edge of her cheek and let her continue.

“I put a plate at the window. I thought it would turn anything that looked through. It didn’t do anything the first night. I went back and changed the return. The second night she slept.”

“How did you know?”

“I was there.”

“You stayed?”

“I wanted to see.”

The girl lifted the basin and emptied the dirty water into a crack which ran toward the old drain. She kept the bottom clear of the pale marks, though Riarhin had not seen those marks before.

“She woke angry. Thought I’d put something in her drink. I told her I hadn’t. She said no one could sleep through that bell.”

“Had you rung it?”

“No.”

For the first time since the letter, Nereth’s mouth softened.

“She went outside and hit the plate with a spoon. To see what would happen.”

“What happened?”

“She bent the spoon.”

Riarhin laughed. The girl glanced at her and almost smiled.

“Then she told everyone I knew what I was doing. I hadn’t told her to say that.”

She laid the cloth over the edge of the basin. Her hands were reddened by the cold water. Riarhin wanted to ask where the old woman was now, but Nereth had begun setting the bowls back in their accustomed places, ending the conversation with the concentration of the task.

Riarhin helped. When the last one was put away, the girl took it down and moved it to another shelf.

“Brenn can reach it there.”

“All right.”

They went back to work.

They spent the morning preparing the return.

Haldren went over the gallery a second time, trimming the edge of a board which caught his boot. Eddis put a handline along the sound wall. Maren checked the shelter and wrote instructions large enough for anyone to read by a poor lamp. Cadan laid out what he would take and removed two things at Riarhin’s request because they would catch in a narrow passage.

Nereth worked in the root chamber.

She had said she needed to secure the outer connection before the rescue. Maren had agreed, provided she changed nothing at the Keystone. Riarhin heard the agreement. Later she would remember hearing it and would be unable to make the memory yield any warning she had missed.

Riarhin carried two filled water vessels to the far side of the gallery. Haldren had asked for them there in case the first passage had to be made quickly. On the way back she stopped to look at the handline Eddis had put up.

It ran at a useful height for someone standing. Leth would be carried below it; Brenn might have to be helped upright. She asked the ferryman for another length lower down. He said there was not enough rope.

“How much?”

He showed her. A shortfall of less than an arm’s length. Enough to matter at the broken end.

She took the long strap from her pack. He examined it, made two new holes with the awl, and joined it to the line. The leather would stretch when wet, so they set it tight and tested it with her weight before leaving it.

“That’s the good strap,” he said.

“I have another.”

“It’s not as good.”

“I know.”

He went to fetch the remaining cord. Riarhin stood alone at the far end and looked back along the crossing. She made herself imagine a body between her and the floor, the places she would be unable to see while carrying it. She would have to trust the person at the other end to tell her when a foot was badly placed. She practised one backward step, then another, stopping before the weak part.

From below came a single knock.

She froze. It had sounded close, almost beneath the board. She knelt, keeping her weight on the sound strip, and looked through the gap. Water moved over the root. Nothing else showed.

“Cadan?”

No reply.

The second knock was farther away. Then a scrape. She put her hand against the stone and felt no vibration.

Eddis returned. She asked whether he had heard it. He had heard something and assumed it was Haldren working at the other end.

They waited together. Nothing followed. At last Riarhin stood and went back to the workshop, resisting the impulse to look down through every crack on the way.

Cadan was there. He had not yet begun his passage. The little hollowed stone lay beside his pack.

He listened while she described the knocks, then went with Eddis to inspect the gallery from the safe end. Riarhin stayed at the shelter. Brenn was awake, trying to turn himself so he could see the entrance. She helped him and gave him the cup from the lower shelf.

“Was that her?” he asked.

“We don’t know.”

He held the cup without drinking. She sat beside him until he remembered what it was for.

At midday the nearest ward grew stronger.

A figure which had been lingering beyond the plate stepped back. Another followed. The thin loop on Maren’s rod straightened. Eddis, coming in from the gallery, said the water had stopped climbing over the lower lip.

Maren looked pleased for half a breath.

Then she looked toward the root chamber.

“Nereth?”

No answer.

Riarhin was already moving.

The passage between the alcove and the workshop had shortened. She knew it before she reached the turn. The lamp which ought to have been beyond a doorway was directly ahead, and the doorway itself lay flat against the wall as though someone had shut a book upon it.

She stopped so hard that Cadan nearly ran into her.

“Back,” she said.

From the root chamber Nereth called, “Don’t cross the line.”

“What have you done?” Maren shouted.

“It’s holding.”

The girl’s voice was high, breathless. Riarhin could see her through the altered doorway now, kneeling beside the dark stone. She had connected three of the prepared outer marks with a continuous line. A fourth ran toward the mass in the inner chamber, disappearing beneath the roots.

Maren made a sound Riarhin had never heard from her before.

“You said you wouldn’t move that.”

“I haven’t moved it.”

“You’ve joined it.”

“It was already joined. I only—”

The floor moved.

Not much. Riarhin felt the change through her boots before she saw anything. A pressure came beneath her left foot, then passed beneath the right. Somewhere behind her a pot fell and broke.

Brenn cried out.

Esvin answered from the alcove, but Brenn’s next cry came from below the floor.

The singer went still.

“Brenn?”

“Here.”

The answer came from two places. One was the pallet. The other was farther away, beyond the direction in which Cadan had gone down the foreign stair.

Riarhin looked into the root chamber. Behind Nereth the fourth step had returned. Above it stood the dark arch, no longer confined beneath the roots. Its edge occupied part of the workshop wall. The blue cup lay broken at its foot, nails scattered across a surface which had not been there that morning.

Nereth looked back at it.

For a moment she seemed unable to understand what she was seeing.

Then she reached for the line nearest her knee.

“Don’t pull that,” Maren said.

The girl froze.

A voice spoke beside the arch.

“You have enough room now.”

Riarhin could not see its owner. The words seemed to come from the little shadow between the arch and the wall, a space too narrow to conceal a person.

Nereth looked toward it.

“For them?” she asked.

“For what you intended.”

Maren seized Riarhin’s sleeve.

“If she breaks the return first, it comes through the shelter.”

“What do I stop?”

“Her hand. Until I can see the sequence.”

Between Riarhin and the girl, a figure stepped from behind the roots. Then another. The patrol had returned, but not along its old circuit. They came toward the workshop, toward the new opening, pressing into one another when the passage narrowed.

Haldren appeared behind Riarhin with the handline over his shoulder.

“The gallery’s still open.”

“Keep it open.”

He looked past her at the bodies.

“For how long?”

“As long as we need.”

It was a useless answer. She saw him know it, and then he went to fetch the beam he had set aside for bracing the turn.

Riarhin drew Remorse. Cadan moved beside her, waiting for the instruction they had already agreed upon.

She looked toward the alcove. Esvin had one hand on Brenn’s shoulder. Maren was gathering the Chord’s loose connection, keeping it clear of the advancing water. There was still space to reach the side passage.

“Go,” Riarhin told Cadan. “Same limits.”

He nodded once and slipped past her.

Ahead, Nereth was crying without making any sound. Her hand hovered above the line she had nearly broken. Behind her the foreign stair continued to become more solid, step by step.

Part V — The interval they have

Chapter 29 — Where the bodies gather

The first body wore a keeper’s coat.

One sleeve was missing. The other hung past the hand, heavy with water. It came around the turn with its shoulder against the wall, feeling its way along the stone in the same motion with which a living person might find a stair in darkness. Riarhin waited until it had committed its weight, then cut across the reaching arm and stepped away from the wall.

The hand fell. The body kept coming.

She turned the point into its upper chest, felt it strike something which would not give, and withdrew before the weight could trap the blade. Behind it another figure pressed forward. The first stumbled under that pressure and fell against her.

Haldren drove the bracing timber between them.

“Back,” he said.

Riarhin stepped clear. He shoved, using the wall behind him, and the body toppled sideways into the shallow channel. Its remaining arm caught at the edge. She struck through the elbow while Haldren held it down.

The next one came over it.

There was no room for the sweep she wanted. She shortened her motion, struck at the knee, and moved to the other side of the timber as the leg folded. The point of Remorse touched stone. She felt the vibration in her wrist and swore.

“Lower,” she told Haldren.

He lowered the timber. The fallen figure struck it with its chest. Riarhin cut where neck met shoulder, twice, because the first cut did not finish the movement. It sagged across the obstruction, still moving one hand.

They pushed it away together.

For a few breaths there was room to see the turn.

Four more figures stood beyond it. Behind those, in the dark, something pale shifted at a height which made Riarhin think of a person climbing over other people. She could hear Maren working behind her, metal touching stone, Esvin saying Brenn’s name again and again until the answer came only from the pallet.

“Don’t let them turn us,” Riarhin said.

Haldren had understood. He set his feet and braced the timber diagonally, leaving her a narrow space beside the wall through which to work.

The third body came low.

It had fallen behind the others and continued on its hands. Riarhin saw the head beneath the timber just before its fingers reached Haldren’s boot. She brought Remorse down at an angle, drove the point through the shoulder toward the floor, and felt the blade flex as the body kept moving.

“Lift your foot.”

Haldren lifted the wrong one.

“The other.”

He hopped, nearly lost his balance, and caught himself against the wall. She withdrew the blade and stamped the reaching wrist onto the stone. Bone gave beneath her heel. She had to do it again before the fingers opened.

The carpenter looked down at what had gripped him. Then he thrust the timber farther out, using it to keep the next body from climbing over the first.

Riarhin wanted a wider place. In a wider place she could have kept the point between herself and the approaching weight, made the bodies turn, cut from the side as they passed. Here each fallen figure made the space narrower. She could see how they would lose the turn without either of them being overcome: the bodies would fill it to the height of the timber, then come across the top.

“Don’t push them into the corner,” she said.

“Where, then?”

“The channel. My side.”

“You’ll be in it.”

“I’ll move.”

He hesitated only a moment. Then he changed the angle of the timber.

They let the next figure come nearer. It struck the wood at chest height. Riarhin moved behind its shoulder, cut through the tendons at the back of the knee, and pushed with the flat of her forearm against its neck. Haldren turned the timber with her. The body toppled into the water instead of across their retreat.

The movement worked. The next did not. A strip of cloth caught on the timber and held the body upright while the legs folded beneath it. Riarhin drew Toothpick, cut the cloth, and nearly lost the knife when the freed weight struck her wrist. She kept it by turning with the blow rather than pulling against it. Her shoulder hit the wall.

Pain ran down the arm. She stepped clear and tested the fingers once before closing them again.

Haldren had seen. His face changed.

“Can you—”

“Yes.”

She had no wish to discover whether that was true while discussing it. She put the knife away and changed the distance, standing half a pace farther back so she could finish each movement before the next body reached her.

At the turn a woman in a long coat raised one hand, palm outward.

Riarhin had seen the gesture at ferry landings, on roads, from people who wanted a stranger to wait. She almost did. Then the woman struck the timber with the same outstretched hand and kept walking into it.

The sound brought Haldren’s eyes up to Riarhin’s.

She looked away first, toward the floor she needed to keep clear.

“Again,” she said.

The floor grew worse as they fought.

Water entered from the channel, carrying pale dust. Where the two mixed, the stone became slick. Riarhin changed her footing after the first slip and kept her steps short. She could not retreat far without bringing the bodies against the shelter entrance. She could not advance without losing Haldren’s barrier.

One figure stopped before reaching them and lifted both hands to its head.

It made the gesture twice. A man’s gesture, familiar in its irritation: pushing something back from the brow which was no longer there. Then it reached toward Haldren.

The carpenter hesitated.

Riarhin struck the wrist aside and put her shoulder against his arm.

“Hold.”

He held. The figure pushed into the timber with no apparent concern for what it did to the flesh beneath. Riarhin cut low. Its foot turned beneath it. Haldren shoved, and it went down with a noise which made him bare his teeth.

“I know,” she said, though he had said nothing.

A hand caught the strap hanging from her pack. She felt the pull before she saw it, a sharp twist which dragged her left shoulder back. She could not bring Remorse around without striking Haldren. She drew Toothpick with her right hand and cut the strap.

The pack dropped.

She let it go, turned, and drove the knife into the hand which still held the severed end. The fingers opened. She pulled free and stepped back into the narrow space beside the timber.

Haldren kicked the pack behind him.

“Got it.”

“Leave it.”

“It’s clear.”

She had no breath to argue. Another body had reached the turn.

Esvin appeared behind her. He held the small token from the keeper’s coat, which must have come away when the first body fell. Riarhin had not seen him retrieve it.

“Once,” he said.

She moved enough to let him see.

He spoke a name. The body beneath the others continued trying to draw itself out of the channel. Esvin spoke again, differently, the name carried within a phrase Riarhin could not follow. His voice was steady, low, not the carrying voice he used in a room full of listeners.

Nothing answered.

He put the token away and went back to Brenn.

A second approach opened behind the broken door.

Riarhin heard the scrape before she saw it. The door had rested against the wall since they entered the ruin. Now its lower corner shifted. A hand appeared beneath it, then part of an arm. Someone was pushing from a space she had thought too shallow to hold anyone.

“Haldren. Keep this.”

“What?”

“The turn.”

She went toward the door. He called something after her which she did not catch.

The door moved again. Riarhin planted her right foot against its lower edge and put her shoulder behind the upper half. The wood was wet and heavy, its old facing loosening beneath her coat. Pressure came from the other side.

She could hold it for a little while. Not while watching the turn, not while helping Maren, not while doing any of the things which had seemed separate until they needed doing together.

“Eddis!”

The ferryman came out of the alcove carrying a bowl. He put it down so carefully that for a moment she wanted to shout at him. Then he saw her position and ran.

“What’s behind it?”

“Don’t know. Brace it.”

He looked around, found a short length of wood, and drove it beneath the lower edge. It snapped. He threw the pieces aside and took the iron rod from the stack of loose fittings.

“Does she need this?”

“Ask her later.”

He put the rod through a gap in the old hinge strap and forced its lower end into a crack in the floor. The door shifted against it. Riarhin eased her weight away a little, felt the pressure settle into the makeshift brace, and took another step back.

A face showed through the widening gap near the top. It was close enough that she could see water caught in the eyelashes.

She did not strike it. She took the edge of a loose shutter from the floor, passed it to Eddis, and helped him wedge it across the opening so they no longer had to look into it while working.

Behind her Haldren shouted.

She returned to the turn at a run. He had kept the bodies back by lowering the timber and retreating two paces. The movement had brought him almost to the shelter plate. Sweat ran down his face. One hand had slipped from its grip and he was holding the wood awkwardly against his forearm.

Riarhin cut across the reaching arm nearest him and stepped into the gap she made.

“Change hands.”

He did. The timber steadied.

“Door?” he asked.

“For now.”

He nodded. Neither had breath for more.

Maren needed them to move.

“Three steps,” she called. “Toward me. Not all at once.”

Riarhin looked over her shoulder. The plate which had protected the alcove now lay just inside a larger curve. Maren had extended the pattern toward the gallery, using the Chord’s branching end and one of Nereth’s rods. The connection was incomplete. Eddis stood with his boot on a loose board to keep the water from lifting it while she worked beneath.

“Ready?” Riarhin asked Haldren.

“No.”

“Good enough?”

He nodded.

They retreated a step. The bodies followed. Riarhin cut at an ankle, withdrew, and let the falling weight block the next. Haldren moved the timber, struck the wall with its far end, and lost a moment freeing it.

A figure came around the low end.

Riarhin stepped into it before it could get beside him. She used her hip and shoulder to turn it toward the channel, Toothpick still in her right hand, Remorse held clear in her left. The body was heavier than it looked. Its mouth moved beside her ear. She smelled wet cloth and old decay.

She drove the knife beneath the jaw, not expecting pain to stop it, and used the buried blade to turn the head while she kicked its supporting foot sideways. It fell against the timber. Haldren pushed it off her.

She withdrew Toothpick with difficulty.

“Now,” Maren said.

They moved the remaining distance. The nearest figure crossed the old position of the plate and stopped at the new one. The body behind pressed against it. For a terrible moment Riarhin thought the weight would force them all through.

Then the first turned.

The others turned with it, clumsily, striking one another and the wall. They moved along the edge of the new shelter toward the inner chamber.

Haldren lowered the timber. His arms shook.

Riarhin caught her breath against the wall, both blades still in her hands.

“Will it hold?” she asked.

Maren did not look up.

“While the seat holds.”

“And Nereth?”

“Still at it.”

There was a way through along the wall Yrdis had permitted.

It was narrow and partly flooded, but the bodies now moved across its far end rather than down it. Riarhin could reach the root chamber if she went alone. Haldren saw her looking and began to lift the timber again.

“No,” she said. “Stay here.”

“You’ll need—”

“They may turn back. Keep this end clear.”

His mouth tightened. Then Brenn cried out again, and the carpenter looked toward the pallet.

“All right.”

Riarhin wiped Toothpick on the inside of her coat before sheathing it. Remorse she kept drawn. She checked the shortened Last Blade at her other hip, then stepped into the passage.

The water rose above her ankles. At the far end Yrdis stood beside the roots, watching the bodies pass. Her face turned toward Riarhin.

“The wall,” she said.

“I remember.”

“Don’t come nearer.”

Riarhin kept her shoulder close to the stone. Something beneath the water touched her boot. She did not look down until she had passed it.

Behind her, Haldren called Eddis a name which made the ferryman answer indignantly. They were moving Brenn. The sound of the argument followed her as far as the next turn.

Chapter 30 — The hand at the stone

Nereth had a chisel beneath the edge of the Keystone.

She was not strong enough to lift it. Riarhin saw that at once. The girl had set a piece of wood beside it as a fulcrum and was leaning her weight on the tool, trying to gain a movement too small to see. Her hair had come loose. Dust clung to the wet tracks on her cheeks.

“Stop,” Riarhin said.

Nereth looked up. Her hands remained on the chisel.

“It has to turn.”

“Let go.”

“If I turn it back—”

“Let go.”

The girl pushed harder.

Riarhin crossed the room in three steps, avoiding the line nearest the bowl. She caught Nereth’s wrist, turned it away from the lever, and drew the girl backward. Nereth struck at her with the other hand. The blow caught Riarhin across the mouth.

She tasted blood.

Nereth tried to bite the hand holding her. Riarhin changed her grip, folded the arm behind the girl’s back, and put her down on her knees beside the stone. It was quick and ugly. Nereth made a high sound which stopped when Riarhin eased the pressure.

“Don’t move.”

“You’re breaking it.”

“Then stay still.”

The girl did. Riarhin kicked the chisel beyond reach and set Remorse against the wall, close enough to take up again. Her lip was swelling. She could feel Nereth’s pulse through the wrist she held.

“I can stop it,” Nereth said.

“How?”

“Turn the seat back.”

“You didn’t turn it to begin.”

“The connection moved it.”

“Did you know it would?”

“No.”

The answer came as a sob. Riarhin eased her grip another fraction.

Maren arrived carrying the looped rod. She stopped at the doorway, took in the girl on her knees, the chisel, the shifted wedge beside the stone.

“Don’t lift it,” she said.

“I wasn’t lifting it,” Nereth cried.

“Don’t turn it either.”

“It won’t stop.”

“I can see that.”

Maren knelt on the other side of the Keystone. She did not touch it. Riarhin watched her eyes move from the seat to the joined lines, then toward the arch which occupied part of the workshop wall.

“What did you join first?”

Nereth shook her head.

“What did you join first?”

“The outer return.”

“Show me.”

“I can’t.”

Riarhin released the wrist but kept a hand on the girl’s shoulder.

“Slowly.”

Nereth pointed.

The order mattered.

Maren made her name each connection before touching it. The girl stumbled over one, corrected herself, and began again. Her breath kept catching. Once she said a number and then pressed both hands over her mouth, shaking her head. Maren waited until she could speak.

Riarhin stood beside them with Remorse in her hand. Through the altered doorway she could see part of the alcove and, beyond it, a piece of the gallery where Eddis had tied the handline. The view shifted when she moved her head. She stopped moving it.

“That’s holding,” she said.

Nereth looked up.

“The shelter. Brenn’s inside. Haldren and Eddis are with him.”

“For now.”

“Yes. For now.”

Something struck the other side of the wall.

Nereth jerked toward the sound. Maren caught the loose line before the girl could drag it with her. Both women froze, hands almost touching above the same mark.

“Look at me,” the keeper said.

Nereth looked.

“Which hand can you move?”

“The left.”

“Move that one away. Put it on your knee.”

The girl obeyed. Riarhin saw how hard she had been gripping the other end. Her fingers were pale around it.

“Now tell me what happens if you let go.”

“It returns.”

“Where?”

“The first place.”

“Which first place?”

Nereth’s eyes filled again. She blinked fiercely, looking from the bowl to the stone and back. There were too many lines beneath them, old trials left beside later ones. Riarhin had watched her draw some of them and could distinguish their order by memory. She could not tell which order mattered now.

“The one under your foot,” Nereth said.

Maren did not move. Riarhin looked down. A faded line ran beneath the keeper’s right boot, almost concealed by the water she had carried in.

“Will you stand?” Riarhin asked.

“Not yet.”

Maren leaned her weight onto one knee. She lifted the boot a fraction, kept it raised, and asked Nereth whether the little loop had changed. The girl said no. The keeper lifted farther and set her foot outside the line.

The sound beyond the wall came again. This time it was followed by a faint scrape overhead. Riarhin looked up and saw a crack opening beneath the old ceiling moulding. Dust fell onto the stone beside Nereth’s knee.

She could shelter their heads, perhaps. She could pull them out. She could do neither without breaking the position they had so carefully reached.

“Tell me if you need moving,” she said.

Neither answered. Both were looking at the line.

Maren asked Nereth to release a finger. Just one. They watched. Then another. At the third, the loop shivered, and the girl closed her hand again with a frightened gasp.

“There,” she said. “You see?”

“I see.”

“You can’t just stop it.”

“I haven’t asked you to.”

Riarhin heard an answering thump from the alcove. Eddis called that the braced door was holding. She did not know whether he meant to reassure her or himself. She answered so he would know she was still there.

Nereth looked up at her voice.

“If it goes back through the sleeping room, Brenn—”

“He’s in the shelter.”

“My things.”

The girl flushed immediately, ashamed of saying it. Riarhin looked through the changed doorway. The comb, the little clay figure, the jars above the pallet: none were visible from here. The book lay on the workbench where Maren had put it. One edge of the oiled wrapping trailed toward the growing arch.

“I’ll get the book when we can move.”

Nereth stared at her.

“We need the instructions,” Riarhin said.

“I know.”

The girl looked down again. Her hand stopped shaking enough for Maren to begin the next attempt.

This time Nereth asked for a loose plate from the shelf. Riarhin could reach it without crossing any line. She brought three, because the girl had said the flat one and two were flat. Nereth selected one, turned it twice in her free hand, and gave it to Maren with the marked side down.

“That way?” the keeper asked.

“It needs to catch what comes back.”

“Show me before I set it.”

Nereth indicated a place outside the first return. Maren moved the plate there, held it above the floor, and waited for the girl to nod.

The loop steadied when the plate touched stone.

Riarhin felt her own shoulders lower. She had been standing ready to take both women bodily from the room. Her hands hurt from gripping the sword.

A larger piece of plaster fell. It struck Nereth across the back. She flinched but did not let go. Riarhin kicked the debris clear of Maren’s foot.

“Again,” the keeper said gently.

The girl loosened the third finger. This time the loop held.

Maren lifted one of the loose ends with a wooden peg. The nearest loop bent. The foreign arch trembled along its edge but did not disappear.

“Next?”

“The low plate. Not the one beside your foot. The other.”

Maren reached for it.

“Wait.” Nereth seized her sleeve. “If you break it there, it pulls through the room.”

The keeper stopped.

“Then show me where.”

Nereth crawled around the stone. Riarhin moved with her. The girl put a finger beside a faint line nearly hidden beneath the dust.

“Here.”

“Why?”

“Because this is where I returned it. The first time. Before I knew it had to go lower.”

Maren looked at her.

“You left the first return.”

“I thought it was finished.”

The keeper closed her eyes briefly. Then she took out the little knife she used for cutting thread and severed the line at the place Nereth had shown.

A sound came from beneath the floor, like a heavy door closing far away.

The view of the gallery steadied.

Riarhin let out a breath she had not known she was holding.

They could stop feeding the expansion. They could not make the room what it had been that morning.

Maren tested the remaining connections while Nereth sat beside the wall, holding her wrist against her stomach. Riarhin crouched beside her.

“Let me see.”

The girl pulled away.

“I need to know if it’s broken.”

“It isn’t.”

“Let me see.”

Nereth held it out with visible reluctance. There was swelling along one side. She could move her fingers, though doing so hurt. Riarhin checked the bones as gently as she could and found no obvious break.

“We’ll bind it.”

“I need that hand.”

“You still have it.”

The words were harsher than she intended. Nereth looked at her mouth, at the blood on her lower lip, and looked away.

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

Riarhin tore a strip from the damaged edge of her own sleeve. She wrapped the wrist without immobilising the fingers. The girl watched the cloth go around twice.

“Kelva will hate me,” she said.

Riarhin did not answer at once. From the other room she could hear Haldren moving something heavy and Eddis telling him to wait. They were both alive. Cadan was somewhere below the foreign turn. Leth waited where he had not yet reached her. Werrin was on a path whose marks Nereth had made.

“I don’t know what Kelva will say.”

“She’ll hate me.”

“She asked me to bring you back.”

Nereth began to cry again, angrily this time, turning her face into the wall. Riarhin finished the knot and stood.

Maren had stopped beside the arch.

“It should be gone,” she said.

The fourth step remained. Beyond it, the dark stair turned away beneath its high lip.

“Are you certain you stopped the sequence?” Riarhin asked.

Nereth lifted her head.

“Yes.”

Maren nodded reluctantly.

“This side isn’t carrying it now.”

From below came three measured knocks.

All three women looked toward the stair. Riarhin went to the line and took it in both hands. Cadan appeared beneath the arch a moment later, climbing quickly enough that he needed her hand at the last step. His sleeve was torn. He stopped against the wall and drew breath before speaking.

“Leth. Behind a fallen screen. I can reach her.”

“Can she walk?”

“No. Her leg is held.”

He showed them with his hands, the position of the screen, the narrow place where he had got his shoulder beneath it. Riarhin checked the last cloth marker. He had returned within the interval, barely.

“I need more time to lift it. Then someone at this end to take her.”

“Does she know you’re coming back?”

“I told her what I intended.”

Maren looked towards the inner chamber. The passage could be held for a little while, she said, but not with Yrdis turning them from the wall whenever they approached the middle. They would have to ask her to remain while they worked.

Cadan set the cord down in loose coils beside the stair. Riarhin gave him the water and went to find Esvin.

Chapter 31 — The verse she refuses

Esvin began with a song Haldren knew.

He sang quietly, standing inside the protected approach, with the little bell resting on a stone beside him. Riarhin had brought Nereth to the alcove and left Maren checking the seat. There was a short interval in which no one needed her hands. She stood near the wall and listened.

The song had a river in it, and a crossing, and the woman who returned against advice. Haldren looked up at the first verse. By the second his lips were moving. He did not sing aloud.

Yrdis appeared at the inner arch.

Esvin reached the line about the enemy brought low beneath the golden thorn.

“No,” she said.

He stopped.

The last note hung in the chamber. Riarhin could hear a drip strike the same shallow pool three times before Yrdis spoke again.

“I didn’t finish it.”

Esvin lowered his head.

“No.”

“They went away.”

“The people in the song?”

“All of them.”

Haldren looked at the floor. Riarhin could not tell whether Yrdis saw him or someone who had once stood where he stood.

“You kept them from him,” Esvin said.

“I kept him.”

The spear angled toward the dark mass behind her. Now, from this side, Riarhin could see the shape within the roots: a body seated or held upright against a raised back of stone, its head bent, the gold point buried where the chest ought to have been. Roots entered the ribs and emerged beneath the seat. Some moved when the water moved. Others moved afterward.

Yrdis looked toward it and back.

“You want me to keep him longer.”

Riarhin came forward as far as the plate.

“We want to bring people through.”

“And then?”

“Then end this.”

“When?”

“When they’re through and the shelter is ready.”

Yrdis’s face changed. The attention she had given them in the boat room withdrew.

“No.”

Maren came to the plate and crouched beside Riarhin.

“We can finish it if she holds the middle.”

“For how long?”

“Until we’ve brought them across.”

Yrdis’s expression did not alter. She had heard those words already.

Maren tried to explain the shelter. She pointed to the connection, named the places it would protect, and described how the Golden Thorn could be taken from the old seat when the short return was carrying. Yrdis watched her hand rather than the places she indicated.

“And who takes it?” the woman asked.

“You,” Maren said.

“Who says when?”

The keeper hesitated.

“We have to see that—”

“No.”

Yrdis stepped back. The gold at her hand shifted; the buried point remained. Riarhin saw the woman’s wrist turn against the strain as though the distance between hand and weapon had hurt her.

“Don’t go,” Maren said before she could stop herself.

Yrdis looked at her.

“Where?”

The keeper’s face changed. She lowered her hand.

Esvin had set out the token, but he did not address Asket again. Riarhin watched him turn the little metal piece so its keeping mark faced the woman. She looked at it for a moment and then away.

“He was cross with you,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

“He wanted you to stay at the boat.”

“Yes.”

“Could you have?”

Yrdis looked toward the dark seat. For several breaths there was nothing in her face Riarhin could read.

“They were coming down the water. Not on it. In it. We pulled the boat up twice. They kept finding where we put it.”

She lifted her free hand as though indicating a bank much lower than the room’s floor.

“My sister had the children beneath the sail. She told them it was a game. They were too old to believe her. They stayed there.”

Maren looked at the token too now.

“The song says you went ashore alone,” Esvin said.

“I did.”

“After the others had left.”

“No.”

Yrdis’s fingers tightened. The movement seemed to draw at the roots behind her.

“I told them I would come back before the turn. I thought if I made him stop, they could go. I could see the high place from the river. It looked nearer.”

Riarhin thought of the foreign stair, the turn which had appeared within reach of her hand. She kept the memory to herself.

“I reached him,” Yrdis said. “I remember that.”

She did not say she remembered returning. There had been no return.

From the shelter came Brenn’s uneven breathing. A cup touched stone. Eddis was trying to make him drink without spilling. The ordinary little noises carried with painful clarity across the protected approach.

Yrdis listened.

“You will ask for one more,” she said.

Riarhin could have denied it. Instead she looked at the line coiled beside Cadan at the arch. He might find someone with only a short distance left to come. He might return asking for another minute with a living person just beyond the turn. She knew exactly what she would want then.

“We need a way to stop asking,” she said.

Esvin looked up.

Maren began to speak, then pressed her lips together. She looked towards the seat. Riarhin looked towards Cadan’s waiting line.

Yrdis waited.

Esvin moved the bell.

He placed it on the ground where there was room beside it for someone to sit. He set no token there, offered no name belonging to a dead person. Riarhin knew what he was doing from the chapel at the coast, though she had not seen him look so unwilling to begin.

He spoke a short invitation.

The empty place remained empty for a while.

Haldren rubbed his hands together. Eddis checked the line leading toward Cadan. Brenn slept with his mouth open, breath catching on each third or fourth exhalation. When he coughed, Esvin started towards the shelter. Haldren was nearer; he knelt and eased Brenn onto his side. The singer waited until the breathing settled before turning back to the bell. Nereth sat against the wall with the bound wrist in her lap.

Then there was a person beside the bell.

Riarhin could not have said when he arrived. Her eyes had been on that place, and then on Yrdis, and then on it again. A dark coat lay in folds around knees which might have belonged to an old man or a young one. His hands were bare. He looked at Yrdis before he looked at any of them.

Esvin bowed his head.

“Varnek Karn.”

The name made the chamber seem quieter, though none of its sounds had ceased.

“What are you offering her?” the seated figure asked.

Riarhin had expected a question about the dead below the stair, or the thing in the roots, or the damage which had brought them here. She glanced at Esvin. He did not help her.

“Release from the duty,” she said.

“After another duty?”

Yrdis made a small sound. It might have been laughter.

Riarhin looked at her. The woman stood with one hand on the spear, exactly where she had stood through every argument and measurement. They had been treating her continued presence as part of the room.

“A short interval,” Riarhin said.

“How short?” Yrdis asked.

Cadan lifted his working line clear of the stair’s lip. It had begun to catch where it had lain freely. He drew it towards him and laid the recovered length beside his knee.

Riarhin turned toward Maren, who had come back from the seat.

“How much do you need?”

The keeper stared at her.

“I can’t promise—”

“I know. How much?”

Maren looked toward the gallery, the shelter, the arch Cadan would have to pass again.

“Enough to finish the return. If nothing changes.”

“Things will change,” Yrdis said.

“Yes,” Riarhin answered.

She took out the spare measuring cord, cut to the length Cadan had used for his first passage. Three cloth strips marked it. His working line remained at the stair, held separately. She drew it through her hands at the agreed pace, once, while the others watched.

“Six of these,” she said. “After that you may go.”

Maren’s head came up.

“Riarhin.”

“Even if we haven’t finished.”

“No,” Maren said. “If the seat—”

“Then we have to be clear before it.”

The keeper looked furious. Riarhin let her. She was frightened herself, and there was no useful way to conceal it from the woman holding the spear.

Yrdis watched the cord.

“Who counts?”

“Eddis.”

The ferryman stared at her, then at the cord.

“I can count,” he said.

“Will he begin again if he loses it?” Yrdis asked.

“No,” Riarhin said. “He tells us where he is uncertain. We take the shorter time.”

Eddis swallowed. He took the cord from her.

The keeper did not agree quietly.

She drew Riarhin aside far enough that she need not speak across Yrdis’s face. There was nowhere far enough that the woman could not hear.

“You have given us a time without asking whether the work can be done in it.”

“I asked.”

“You asked what I needed. Then you chose something else.”

“What you needed had no end.”

“It ends when the work is finished.”

Riarhin looked toward the spear. Maren followed her gaze and looked away at once, furious at the ease of that answer.

“I know,” the keeper said. “I know how it looks. I still have people to bring home.”

“So do I.”

“Then let me have the time.”

It was the first plea Riarhin had heard from her. She would have preferred another angry demand. It was easier to refuse anger.

“Find the piece we can leave undone.”

Maren shook her head.

“There isn’t—”

“Look again.”

The keeper stepped back as though Riarhin had touched her. For a moment neither spoke. Then Maren went to the pattern and knelt.

Riarhin followed, staying out of the line. Nereth was already there. She watched Maren put one rod aside, bring it back, and put another beside it. The girl indicated a shorter return.

“That takes only the alcove.”

“And the gallery entrance?” Maren asked.

“While the seat holds.”

“After?”

Nereth shook her head. Maren reached for the longer rod again.

“Lay them both out,” Riarhin said.

They had to move a pack to make room. Maren set the rods beside each other, then used her finger to follow the shorter return. At the place where it left the gallery unprotected, she stopped.

Riarhin waited.

“We take the people over before the withdrawal,” the keeper said. “We don’t keep the whole passage after. We only keep this end long enough to leave.”

“How much longer?”

“I won’t promise it.”

“I heard you.”

They began moving the equipment which did not need to remain. Haldren carried the first bundle across the gallery and came back without being asked. Eddis laid the measuring cord in broad loops so it would not catch when the count began. Riarhin fetched the water they had put on the far side and left one cup at each end of the crossing.

When she returned, Maren was waiting beside the plate.

“I’ll try,” she said.

Riarhin nodded. There was nothing she could promise in return which would make the attempt less dangerous.

Esvin came to her while Maren was selecting the last pieces to move.

“The words need to be hers,” he said.

“I haven’t put any in her mouth.”

“Not yet.”

She looked at him sharply. He was pale, more than the cold alone accounted for, and had tucked his right hand beneath his left arm to keep it from shaking visibly.

“What do you need?” she asked.

“To hear her without you answering the part we want first.”

Riarhin glanced at the seated figure beside the bell. He was watching neither of them. Yrdis stood some distance away, looking towards the boat room. The hand which held the spear had not relaxed.

“Can he release her now?” Riarhin asked quietly.

Esvin did not look round.

“Ask him.”

“If he could—”

“Ask.”

She went as far as the edge of the cloth. The dark coat showed no damp where it touched the floor. A little run of water passed beside its hem without altering course.

“Can you let her go?” she asked.

The seated figure looked up. His eyes were ordinary enough that she could not recall their colour when she looked away from them.

“Has she been asked where she means to go?”

Riarhin turned towards Yrdis. The woman was listening now.

“Where do you mean to go?”

“Away.”

The word carried such force that Riarhin felt it in her own shoulders. She had been expecting a destination, a person, something by which they could recognise success.

“Do you need us to—”

“Away from here.”

Riarhin stopped. Yrdis looked towards the thing in the roots and tightened her grip on the spear until the knuckles changed.

“I don’t need you to tell me where.”

“No.”

“I need you to stop telling me when.”

Riarhin looked at the cord in Eddis’s hands. Six lengths. An end they could fail to reach in time, and a woman who would still be entitled to leave it behind her. She went back to Esvin.

“Let her ask,” she said.

He nodded and went to Yrdis. Riarhin carried the remaining bundle across the gallery. By the time she returned, the woman had begun asking her own questions.

Who would carry the remains? Whose account would be sung? Would someone be left holding the spear when she let go? She asked the last question twice, once of Riarhin and once of Esvin.

“No successor,” the singer said.

“Can you promise that?”

“I can promise we will demand none.”

“What they do after you leave?”

“I cannot promise for them.”

Yrdis looked at the seated figure. He gave her no answer in their place.

Riarhin said they would take what remains they could recover without sending someone else to die. They would record where the others lay. Esvin would keep the account of the boat, Asket’s part in it, the sister who could steer, the unfinished last trip. He would not sing the victory verse as though she had agreed to it.

Haldren cleared his throat.

“I can carry the tool.”

Yrdis looked at him.

“If you want it carried,” he added.

“Leave it with the boat.”

“All right.”

Yrdis stood without speaking. Haldren lowered the pallet to rest his hands. Riarhin wanted to tell him to keep it ready, but left him alone.

At last Yrdis nodded toward the cord in Eddis’s hands.

“Six.”

“Six,” he said.

“Begin.”

He began.

Cadan took up the working line and went beneath the arch.

Maren was already moving. Haldren brought the carrying pallet to the stair. Nereth got to her feet, pale and determined, and followed the keeper toward the seat.

Riarhin looked once at Varnek Karn. He remained beside the bell, watching Yrdis. He had offered no way to hold the roof, no assurance that the six lengths would be enough.

She went to the stair.

Chapter 32 — Those who come through

Leth came through on her stomach.

Cadan had freed her leg from whatever held it, but she could not bear to have it lifted. He brought her backward around the turn while she pushed with her elbows and told him to stop. Riarhin heard the words before she saw either of them.

“I know,” Cadan answered.

“Then stop.”

“I cannot stop here.”

Riarhin went down to the pale third step and reached for the carrier’s arm. Leth struck at her. The blow had little strength, but the intention was unmistakable.

“Don’t touch the leg,” the woman said.

“I won’t.”

They got her over the lip. Cadan came after her, bent almost double. A strip of his coat was missing, tied around the injured limb above the knee. Grey dust coated his face. He looked toward Eddis.

“First,” the ferryman said. “Nearly.”

Cadan nodded and turned back.

Riarhin caught his sleeve.

“What did you see?”

“Another passage. Davin may have gone that way.”

“May?”

“Leth heard him.”

“When?”

“She cannot tell me.”

Riarhin looked at the woman on the floor. Esvin had reached her, keeping his hands where she could see them. She was trying to explain that the room had moved while she was asleep.

“One return,” Riarhin said. “You report before another.”

“Yes.”

Cadan went down again.

For the first time since Cadan went down, Riarhin could put a living weight to the thing they had been waiting for.

Leth’s coat was stiff with dust. Her hair had been tied back so long that the knot had become a hard, matted ball at the nape of her neck. When Esvin tried to support her head she struck at his wrist, then apologised so quickly that the apology sounded like another protest.

“Tell me before,” she said.

“I will.”

He showed her the folded cloth, told her where he meant to put it, and waited until she nodded. Riarhin held the shoulders while he slid it underneath.

There was a strip of torn leather bound around the injured leg. Beneath it the flesh had swollen over the edge. Cadan had loosened the binding enough to free her from the fallen piece which held it, but not enough to see what damage lay beneath. Esvin examined what he could and left the rest alone.

“Can you move your foot?”

“I’ve been trying.”

“Does it hurt when I touch here?”

“Yes.”

“And here?”

She made a small, impatient sound.

“All of it.”

Riarhin sat behind her, supporting her while Haldren brought the pallet. The carrier’s hair smelled of closed stone and old sweat. She trembled each time someone moved near the leg. Riarhin knew better than to say it would not hurt. She told her when they were about to lift and let her swear.

They lowered her onto the pallet. Esvin arranged the makeshift support. Haldren asked Riarhin to move one hand while he passed a strap beneath, and she had to explain that hand’s purpose to Leth before the woman would let it go.

It all took time.

Across the room Eddis fed the cord through his fingers. He did not call another number. Riarhin was grateful for that silence and frightened by how much she wanted to hear it continue.

Leth caught the edge of her sleeve.

“Did he tell you?”

“Cadan?”

“The other way. Davin went looking. We said we’d stay.”

“He knows.”

“I told him to go before it shut. Davin. I didn’t mean—”

“You can tell me when we’re clear.”

“No. You need to know. We didn’t all go the same way.”

Riarhin knelt where Leth could see her face.

“I know he’s missing. Cadan is looking now.”

The woman searched her face. Then she let go of the sleeve.

“Don’t leave him because I’m slow.”

Riarhin could not promise that one person’s need would never cost another time. She adjusted the folded cloth beneath Leth’s head instead, keeping her hands visible.

“We’re moving you first.”

Leth closed her eyes. Riarhin did not ask whether she understood.

Leth wanted Brenn.

They brought her far enough into the shelter that she could see him. He woke when she said his name and began to cry. She told him not to be an idiot, then cried herself, angrily, while Esvin adjusted the support beneath her leg.

“You went,” she said.

“You told me.”

“I know what I told you.”

“I couldn’t get back.”

“I know.”

Riarhin left them as soon as she could. Their relief had made something ache beneath her ribs which she had no time to examine.

At the gallery Haldren had set a folded blanket over the roughest edge. The blanket he had kept dry at the first door. Riarhin recognised the red stitching along one side.

“That will catch,” she said, pointing to a fold.

He tucked it under the board.

“Better?”

“Yes.”

“Don’t let them put weight on the left end until I’m there.”

“I’ll tell them.”

He looked at the shelter, counting the people who would have to pass.

“Two loads. Then packs. Leave the stone if we have to.”

“The pattern?”

“Whatever you can carry in your arms. Not every useful lump in the place.”

Riarhin nodded. His bluntness helped. She went back and told Maren exactly what he had said.

The keeper selected two things and left a third beside the seat. Nereth reached for it, then stopped herself.

“Second,” Eddis called.

The working line slackened while Riarhin was returning from the gallery.

Eddis had the measuring cord in one hand and the passage line laid across his other wrist. He called her name without raising his voice. She came at once.

“It’s doing that.”

A loop of slack moved toward the stair, then away. Cadan had not pulled the return signal. The loose part lay on the stone as though someone had set it there.

Riarhin took it from Eddis and felt along its length. The cord was cold, but it had been cold before. She drew in a little, stopped at the first resistance, and called Cadan’s name.

No answer.

She pulled twice.

The answering movement came so promptly that she nearly gathered the line hand over hand. Then it stopped, leaving the same slack on the stair.

From the turn she heard a scrape followed by a knock. Work, Vessa had said. It sounded like work until it stopped.

Riarhin held the cord without pulling again. Haldren had come up behind her. She asked him to keep everyone clear of the line.

“Do we haul?” he asked.

“Not yet.”

The scrape came again. She wanted it to be Cadan freeing someone, moving a fallen piece, making the sound that would let her keep waiting. She could remember the first noise from beneath the gallery too distinctly. It had been the same interval between scrape and knock.

She looked toward the little hollowed stone Cadan had left beside the entrance. Its scratch faced the room. It had not moved. That told her only that something here remained where he put it.

“Cadan,” she called again. “Return.”

The cord tightened. This time the pull was uneven, carrying a weight which stopped and began. She paid out the small length he seemed to need, then held it when the movement ceased.

A child’s voice came from beneath the arch.

“I can’t.”

“Put your foot where mine was,” Cadan said.

“It’s not there.”

“Wait.”

Riarhin bent far enough to see the first turn. Cadan’s hand appeared against the wall, fingers spread. Beneath it a smaller hand gripped his sleeve.

“Stay where you are,” he called.

She stayed.

He brought the boy around the turn one step at a time, waiting whenever the child lost the next tread. Riarhin saw Cadan test each place before transferring weight. The cord dragged behind him. It had caught around a broken projection near the arch; freeing it accounted for part of the slack, though she could not tell whether it accounted for all.

She reached for the child only when Cadan nodded.

The boy gripped her forearm with both hands and climbed as though out of water. She set him on the pale stone and moved back to give him room. His knees folded at once. Haldren caught his head before it struck the floor.

The boy was older than Tessa, younger than Werrin, and wore a coat too large for him. While Haldren helped him sit, Cadan said he had been sleeping behind a fallen screen. He had thrown a stone when he heard footsteps.

“Who are you?” Riarhin asked.

He gave a name she did not catch.

“Again.”

“Osset.”

“From where?”

He named a place Maren knew. She turned sharply.

“The reed houses?”

He nodded.

“How long have you been missing?”

“Three days.”

Maren said nothing. Later, Riarhin understood that her silence had been because the place had been abandoned for much longer than three days. For now she took the boy’s shoulders, turned him toward Esvin, and told him to stay inside the plate.

Cadan held out a small object.

A wooden toggle, split along one side, with a loop of cord still through it.

Riarhin carried it to Leth. The woman opened her eyes when Esvin spoke her name, looked at the broken wood, and reached for it.

“Davin’s coat. That’s his fastening.”

Riarhin let her turn it before taking it back to Cadan.

“Where?”

“Beyond the division.”

“Any sign of him?”

“A place where someone rested. No one there now.”

“Third,” Eddis called.

Cadan looked at the stair.

“I could try the lower way.”

Riarhin looked at the line in his hand. Dust had entered the fibres. There was a dark patch where it had rubbed against something wet.

“How far?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then no.”

He remained still.

“We have time.”

“We have time to get these people out.”

“One more—”

“No.”

His eyes met hers. She had seen him accept instructions which disgusted her with less difficulty than this one.

At last he lowered the line.

“All right.”

Riarhin took the toggle. It fitted inside her palm. There was not much of it to bring home.

Osset heard his mother outside the shelter.

He had been sitting quietly until then, drinking from a cup held in both hands. Suddenly he stood and moved toward the plate. Esvin caught up with him without seizing him.

“Where are you going?”

“She called.”

“What did she say?”

“My name.”

Riarhin had heard something too, a woman’s cadence beneath the grinding in the roots. No word had reached her clearly.

“Can you see her?” Esvin asked.

The boy shook his head.

“Then answer from here.”

Osset called. A sound returned from the far passage. He took another step.

“Ask what she wants,” Esvin said.

The boy asked.

No answer came. He waited, trembling with the effort of staying still.

“She might not hear.”

“She might not,” Esvin said. “You can call again from this side.”

“Why won’t you let me go?”

“Because I can see the ground here.”

Osset turned towards Riarhin as if he had only now noticed her sword.

“Are you keeping us here?”

“No.”

“Then why can’t I go?”

She lowered Remorse’s point and let the blade rest beside her leg. He watched the movement with an attention which made her want to put it away entirely. Beyond the shelter, something scraped against stone. She kept the sword in her hand.

“We’re taking you out by the gallery. Where the carpenter is.”

“That’s not where I came.”

“I know.”

“Mother won’t find me.”

Esvin crouched beside him. He kept clear of the plate. Riarhin could see the measured line moving through Eddis’s fingers beyond the boy’s shoulder, each knot bringing the work closer to its end.

“Would she know your coat?” Esvin asked.

The boy looked down.

“It’s my brother’s.”

“Then she may know that too.”

Osset gripped its edge. He would not take it off. Esvin had not asked him to, but the expectation had frightened him.

“We can leave word at the entrance,” the singer said. “Where someone coming from the road can find it.”

“What word?”

“Your name. Where we’ve taken you.”

The boy looked towards the sound again.

“She doesn’t read.”

“Someone may read it to her.”

“Someone may not.”

Esvin swallowed. Riarhin could see him searching for another answer which would not be a lie. She knelt, keeping the sword turned away from the child.

“If you stay here, we won’t be able to come back for you tonight.”

His face changed. Anger made him older for a moment.

“You came for him.” He pointed at Brenn.

“Yes.”

“But not me.”

“Cadan found you. We’re bringing you.”

He pulled the cup against his chest until some water spilled over the lip. His lower lip shook. He bit it and looked away from both of them.

“Let me call.”

“From here,” Esvin said.

Osset turned back towards the passage. Riarhin rose slowly, moving out of his way while remaining between him and the plate. He noticed. She let him see that she knew he had noticed.

The boy called twice more. Nothing answered. At last he sat down and pushed the cup away when Esvin offered it again.

“Fourth,” Eddis said.

Maren lifted her head from the final connection.

“Nearly ready.”

Yrdis watched from beside the spear. Riarhin could not read her face. She went to Haldren and took the end of the first pallet.

Chapter 33 — The Golden Thorn

They moved Leth first.

She was heavier than Brenn and could not help them. Haldren had made a support beneath her leg from two straight pieces and a folded coat. She hated it until they lifted her, then gripped the sides of the pallet and made no further complaint. Esvin walked beside her as far as the gallery. Beyond that there was room for only the carriers.

Riarhin went backward. Haldren took the heavier end, one foot on the board and one on the surviving strip of floor. He told her when to stop, when to lift, when to let him change his grip. She obeyed without looking behind her more often than she had to.

Halfway across, the pallet caught.

Riarhin felt the halt through both hands. She stopped without pulling. Haldren looked down along Leth’s side, then toward the board beneath her heel.

“The strap.”

Riarhin could not see it. If she set her end down, the pallet would tip toward the unsupported part of the floor. She looked at Haldren. He nodded toward the wall.

“Put your elbow there. I can raise this end.”

She did. The stone was cold through her sleeve. He lifted until Leth cried out, then stopped and held the weight. A narrow piece of leather showed beneath the pallet, caught around the projecting peg they had used to keep the board from slipping.

Toothpick would reach it. Riarhin could not take her hand from the carrying pole long enough to draw the knife.

“Esvin.”

The singer had remained at the near end. He came onto the sound strip, keeping his body flat against the wall. Riarhin told him where the knife was. He looked at her, startled by the request, then drew it carefully with his free hand.

“Under the pole. Not near her leg. There.”

He bent. His shoulder pressed against Riarhin’s back. For a moment she could feel all their weights depending on the same narrow support.

The leather parted.

“Clear,” he said.

Haldren lowered his end a fraction. Leth breathed out through her teeth. Riarhin took the full weight again and moved one foot, then the other.

Esvin put the knife back in its sheath before returning along the wall. She felt the familiar weight settle at her hip. Nobody said anything about it.

At the far end Cadan took her place. They lowered Leth onto sound ground.

Leth would not let go of the pole when they set her down.

Cadan spoke to her quietly. She stared at him as though she could not quite place his face. He waited, then showed her that the pallet rested on sound stone, lifting his own hands away so she could see it was no longer being carried.

Her fingers opened one at a time.

“Where is Brenn?”

“Coming,” Riarhin said.

“Don’t let him come that way.”

“We have to.”

Leth closed her eyes. Cadan arranged a bundle beneath her shoulders and stayed beside her until she stopped gripping the edge of the pallet.

Osset came across after them, one hand on the lower line. He did not look down. When he reached the far end he sat beside Leth without being told. She opened her eyes long enough to ask his name, then put a hand against his coat so she knew where he was.

Riarhin went back for Brenn.

Brenn could not stand.

He had insisted he could while they prepared Leth. Now his knees folded as soon as they put weight on them. Haldren and Esvin caught him between them and lowered him onto the edge of the alcove. He apologised breathlessly.

“Don’t,” Haldren said. “Try it sitting first.”

He moved the carrier’s foot, testing whether it would remain where he placed it. Brenn looked down at his legs as though they belonged to someone who had misunderstood the instruction.

The pallet was on the far side with Leth. Bringing it back meant moving her again and carrying it across the gap empty. Riarhin looked toward the gallery, then at Eddis’s hands working the cord.

Haldren followed her glance.

“I can bring him along the wall.”

“Can he help?”

“He can hold on.”

“Brenn?” Riarhin asked.

The carrier nodded too quickly. Haldren crouched beside him and put the man’s arm over his own shoulders.

“Don’t grip my neck. Here.”

He moved the hand lower, onto his coat. Esvin supported the other side while they tried again. Brenn rose. His feet stayed beneath him for a breath, two. Then one turned outward and he sagged.

They lowered him once more.

Riarhin went to fetch a strap. Haldren took it, looped it around the carrier’s waist and his own, and arranged the fastening where he could release it without finding the knot behind his back. Brenn watched with a shame so naked that Riarhin had to make herself continue looking at him.
