“You’ll be against the wall,” she said. “Let him place your foot if you can’t.”

He nodded.

This time they got him upright. Haldren took most of the weight. Esvin stayed beside them as far as the turn.

Riarhin looked back toward Yrdis. The woman had not moved. She was watching the cord.

“Fifth,” Eddis called.

Maren stood beside the Keystone with the portable pattern wrapped and ready. Nereth had both hands on the last connection, the bound wrist held awkwardly. The line no longer ran toward the outer marks. It enclosed the short approach and returned to the seat.

“Is it ready?” Riarhin asked.

Maren looked at Nereth. The girl nodded.

“Yes.”

Riarhin turned toward Yrdis.

The woman had heard.

Maren needed Nereth’s hands for the last change.

The girl had been holding the temporary return clear of the seat. If she let it fall before Maren separated the branching end, the connection would go through the old growth again. Riarhin could see the strain in her wrist. The binding had darkened where the skin beneath was rubbed raw.

“Can I hold it?” she asked.

Nereth shook her head.

“You won’t feel when it turns.”

“Then tell me.”

“There won’t be time.”

Maren did not contradict her. She was working with a thin wedge beneath the gold, lifting by the smallest increments. Each time she stopped, the girl altered the angle of her hand.

Riarhin looked toward Haldren and Brenn. They were at the gallery entrance. Cadan had returned as far as the far end to receive them. Eddis stood where he could see both the inner room and the first board, counting with his hands while the rest of his body wanted to move elsewhere.

Nothing could be hurried without making some other part harder.

She knelt beside Nereth and supported the girl’s forearm, taking enough weight to ease the shaking without changing the angle. Nereth glanced at her, then returned her attention to Maren’s wedge.

“Like that,” she whispered.

Riarhin held still.

Across the chamber Yrdis had turned toward the throne. The dark body within the roots seemed smaller now that Riarhin could see it plainly. What she had taken for shoulders included stone. What she had taken for a crown was the branching end of an old growth which had entered above the ear and spread.

She could not recognise a face in it. There was a mouth, or the remains of one. It moved once as the woman came nearer.

Yrdis stopped.

For a moment Riarhin thought she would refuse to touch the spear again. There was room for that refusal in what they had promised, and nowhere in their plan to put it.

Esvin spoke from beside the bell.

“The passage is yours.”

Yrdis looked back at him. He did not beckon her toward the weapon or toward the exit. His hand lay beside the bell, open.

She took one more step.

Yrdis approached the body at the centre of the roots.

For the first time Riarhin saw her put both hands on the spear. One went above the other, close together. She set her foot against the stone beneath the throne and leaned back.

Nothing moved.

The roots tightened around the shaft. A sound came from the body, low and wet. Riarhin could not tell whether it had been a breath until she heard it again.

Yrdis changed her grip.

“Stay there,” she said.

Riarhin had not known she had taken a step.

She stopped.

The woman pulled again. Gold emerged from the mass by the width of a finger. Beneath it, something pale tore. The seated body lifted its head.

Nereth made a frightened sound. Maren put a hand on her shoulder without taking her eyes from the connection.

The room shook.

Dust fell from the arch. Haldren shouted from the gallery that the way was still clear. Eddis continued drawing the cord through his hands, slower now only because his fingers had grown clumsy. He corrected the pace with visible effort.

Yrdis pulled the spear free.

The point was dark. She held it before her, looking at it as though it belonged to someone else. Behind her the body sagged. The roots which had held it did not sag with it. They pulled in different directions, exposing gaps where flesh, wood, and stone had been pressed together for too long.

Water burst through one of the gaps.

“Move,” Maren said.

They moved.

At the edge of the chamber Yrdis turned toward them.

Esvin had the bell in his hand. Varnek Karn stood beside the place where it had rested. Riarhin had not seen him rise.

“Six,” Eddis said.

Yrdis lowered the spear.

No one asked her to wait.

She looked past them toward the boat room. Riarhin thought she might go there. Instead she set the butt of the weapon against the floor and let it fall. It struck the stone with an ordinary hard sound.

Then the woman was no longer beside it.

The gold remained in the failing light, one length among the fallen things. Riarhin looked for her farther down the passage and saw only water moving over the sill.

Esvin put the bell away.

Varnek Karn inclined his head toward the empty place. When Riarhin looked again, that place too was empty.

Maren pulled her away from the arch.

“The stair,” the keeper said.

Riarhin saw it.

The foreign stair remained open beyond the broken workshop wall. Nereth’s connecting lines had been severed; the girl carried the ends in her hands. Yet the dark fourth step was as clear as it had been during Cadan’s passage. A little stream of grey dust spilled from it onto the pale local stone.

“There,” Riarhin said to Maren. “You see it?”

“Yes.”

“We stopped it.” Nereth’s voice rose. “We stopped it.”

“Keep moving,” Riarhin said.

The roof above the workshop shifted. A jar fell from Nereth’s sleeping-room shelf and broke. Then another. The cloth across the doorway lifted in a wind which came from beneath the floor.

Riarhin took the girl by the upper arm and brought her toward the gallery.

Chapter 34 — The support gives

Brenn was on the crossing when the root moved.

Haldren had chosen to take him almost upright, one arm over the carpenter’s shoulders, because the pallet would take too long to bring back. Cadan waited at the far end. Riarhin had stayed behind long enough to get Nereth and the wrapped pattern through the turn.

She saw the board lift exactly as Haldren had shown her.

“Haldren.”

He looked down.

The movement came from the side. The great root beneath the gallery pressed outward against the wall, carrying a section of stone with it. The beam did not break. One end slid from its seating.

Haldren pulled the strap’s release and pushed Brenn toward the surviving strip against the wall.

The carrier’s foot missed it. Riarhin dropped the pack she had recovered and caught his coat with both hands. The fabric tore under one fist. She got an arm beneath his chest and dragged him onto the surviving strip.

Behind him Haldren went down.

The falling board struck his leg. He caught the handline, swung against the wall, and for an instant remained there with his head above the edge. Then the stone beneath his hip came away and drove him lower.

Riarhin reached for him.

Cadan had Brenn. Eddis came past on his knees and seized Riarhin’s belt before she could lean too far. She could see Haldren’s hand on the rope, the knuckles pale, the wedding ring pressed against the stone.

“Hold,” she said.

He was already holding.

They got a line under his arms.

It took too long. Riarhin went down on a second rope while Cadan braced above. The space beneath the gallery was full of water, broken stone, and pieces of the board. Haldren’s lower body was trapped against the displaced beam. He could move one hand. He used it to push at Riarhin when she reached him.

“Leg,” he said.

“I see.”

She did not see enough. The water was dark. She felt beneath it, found the edge of the board, found cloth, found the hard angle of something which should not have been there. Haldren made a sound through his teeth.

“Sorry.”

He shook his head violently. She could not tell whether he meant stop or continue.

Eddis took the upper line from Cadan, bracing it around the sound stone. Cadan came down beside her. There was barely room for both of them. He set his feet against the wall and lifted the beam enough for her to free the trapped cloth. Something shifted in the water. Haldren cried out once, then stopped making any sound at all.

“Now,” Cadan said.

Riarhin’s knife was in her hand before she remembered drawing it.

A strip from Haldren’s coat still held beneath the beam. She cut it close to the trapped edge, keeping the blade away from the flesh she could not see. The cloth parted. His body shifted toward her, too suddenly, and she caught him against her shoulder.

Cadan was still holding the wood. His feet had begun to slip.

“Clear,” she said.

He lowered it carefully. The movement sent a fresh wash of cold water around their legs. Haldren made a sound against Riarhin’s coat. She told him they were bringing him up, although he could see the rope and must have known.

Above, Eddis asked where to pull.

“Slowly. Straight above him.”

The line tightened. Haldren’s arm caught between his body and the wall. Riarhin freed it. Cadan took the weight at his hip as gently as the confined space allowed. There was no position which did not hurt him.

They stopped after the first lift because the carpenter could not catch his breath. He gripped Riarhin’s sleeve, stared up at the light, and shook his head.

“One more,” she said. “Then you can lie down.”

She hated saying it. It was all she could think of that described anything she could make happen.

He looked at her. There was no agreement in his face, but his hand loosened enough for her to move.

They hauled him upward between them.

At the top Esvin and Eddis pulled him clear. Riarhin followed, scraping her knee against the broken edge. She scarcely felt it. She crawled to Haldren’s side while the others moved him away from the gap.

His eyes were open.

“Cold,” he said.

They covered him.

Esvin cut the clothing where he had to.

Riarhin held the lamp. Her hand shook so badly that she set it on the floor and moved it from there. Haldren watched the flame. When Esvin pressed beside his hip he tried to rise, then fell back with a gasp.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said. “Stay still.”

He looked at her as though that were an unreasonable request. She had never seen such naked anger in his face. Then it was gone, replaced by effort.

“Brenn?”

“Across.”

He closed his eyes.

Haldren tried to take his boot off.

His hand moved toward the fastening, stopped short, and tried again. Riarhin reached for it, thinking he meant the injured leg, but he struck her hand weakly away.

“The other.”

She moved to the other boot. The fastening had swollen with water. She could not undo it with fingers which had begun to numb. She showed him Toothpick before cutting the strap.

“All right?”

He moved his head. She took it for assent and cut.

The boot came free with difficulty. Beneath it the green sock was wet and dark. She eased the cloth from the heel without pulling it off entirely, remembering the company beneath the tree, Haldren describing the dog with its dyed mouth. The memory arrived complete and unwanted while her hands worked.

He seemed to breathe more easily for a moment.

“Water?” she asked.

Esvin gave her the cup. She held it to Haldren’s mouth. Most of what she offered ran down his chin. He grew angry at that, made a sound, tried to lift his own head. She helped him only far enough to wet his lips again.

“Enough?”

His eyes closed.

She set down the cup where it would not be knocked over. That seemed suddenly important. She moved it farther from the edge, looked at it, and came back to his side.

Nereth stood behind Esvin. She had begun to say something about a fitting, a way of holding, then stopped when Maren shook her head. The keeper took her by the shoulder and moved her away.

Riarhin could hear stone falling in the rooms behind them. She wanted it to stop for long enough to let them do this properly. Esvin tore another strip of cloth. The first had already darkened beneath his hand.

“Haldren,” he said.

The carpenter opened his eyes again.

“Can you breathe more slowly?”

Haldren tried. The attempt made a wet noise in his throat. Esvin looked at Riarhin. He did not say what she could already see.

She took the carpenter’s hand. His fingers tightened once, painfully.

“Your wife,” she said, then stopped. She did not know what she was asking him to provide.

His mouth moved. She bent closer.

Nothing clear came.

“You don’t have to,” she said.

He was still trying when his grip weakened.

Riarhin stayed bent over him after Esvin moved his hand from the carpenter’s throat.

There was a voice far away.

She recognised the rhythm before she could place it. A pause, an impatient resumption, the sound of someone expecting to be interrupted and going on anyway. It might have been beyond the broken wall or beneath the floor. She could not make out a word.

She did not turn.

“Eddis.”

The ferryman came to her.

“Stay here.”

“I am here.”

“Beside me.”

He knelt. His shoulder touched hers. They remained like that while Esvin drew the blanket over Haldren’s face.

Then Maren said they had to go.

They would not leave him.

No one proposed it aloud. Riarhin knew the weight of the decision as soon as she tried to lift the carpenter’s shoulders. Cadan took the other side. Eddis and Esvin made a carrying sling from the blanket and two lengths of sound wood. The red stitching showed at one end, wet and dirty now.

Nereth offered her good hand. Riarhin put a pack into it instead.

“Carry that. Stay where Maren puts you.”

The girl obeyed.

They left the beam where it had fallen. Riarhin looked once at the empty seating in the wall. The wood had held. The wall had moved around it.

There was no time to retrieve the tools beneath the water.

At the next turn Brenn saw the covered load and began to apologise. Esvin told him to save his breath for walking. He said it sharply enough that Brenn stopped.

Riarhin took the front end with Cadan. The sling pulled against her fingers. They lifted together, waited for Eddis to find the first safe footing, and carried Haldren out of the room he had made passable.

Chapter 35 — The load they bring back

They did not reach the causeway that day.

The door through which they had entered the old workrooms opened onto a wall. Cadan checked it twice, then led them back to the lower chamber. Water stood higher there. The boat’s broken curve was half submerged, its new support invisible beneath the surface.

Riarhin could not look at it for long.

They found another way through a storage room whose shelves had fallen against one another. Getting Haldren’s sling between them required turning the load on its side. Riarhin hated the indignity of it and did it anyway. Behind her Leth swore at every jolt. Osset carried a lamp and kept asking whether they were going out now.

“Soon,” Esvin said once.

Then he stopped saying it.

At the far end of the storage room, Eddis found open air. It came through a split between two courses of stone. The gap was too narrow until Cadan and Riarhin removed three loose blocks. Each block had to be lifted, passed back, and placed where it would not obstruct the people behind.

When there was room, they sent Osset first.

He stood outside and held the lamp toward them. The light showed grass, a fallen column, and rain.

They camped beneath the colonnade because there was nowhere better.

Maren found a place where the broken stones made a shallow roof. She and Nereth laid the portable pattern around the entrance. The girl explained each movement before making it. Maren watched her hands.

Riarhin and Cadan set Haldren’s body beneath the driest part. Esvin arranged Leth and Brenn near enough to the little fire that their wet clothes would warm without scorching. Eddis went for water and came back without it.

“The pool isn’t where it was.”

“Another?” Riarhin asked.

“There will be.”

He took Osset with him. The boy needed something to do more than he needed another instruction to sit still.

Nereth tried to lift the cook pot with her bound hand and dropped it. Nothing spilled; it was empty. The noise made everyone look. She picked it up with the other hand and stood holding it against her chest.

Riarhin wanted to say something which would make her stop looking like that. She also wanted to ask whether the larger line had been worth it. She said neither.

“Give it to me,” she said.

The girl did.

They ate thin broth with bread softened into it. Leth could not finish hers. Brenn fell asleep before the bowl was empty. Esvin ate what remained rather than put it aside to spoil.

During the night Riarhin woke to the sound of someone vomiting beyond the stones. It was Nereth. Maren sat with her until she could come back inside.

No one slept well.

Before dawn Leth asked for the covering to be taken off her leg.

Esvin refused at first. He thought she was feverish. She said she was not, and when he touched her forehead she caught his wrist with enough strength to surprise him.

“I want to see it.”

“It hasn’t changed.”

“Then I can look.”

Riarhin woke during the argument and came over with the lamp. Esvin gave her a tired, irritated glance. She knew he had slept less than anyone except perhaps Leth herself.

“Let her,” she said.

He folded back the blanket.

The improvised support held the leg straight enough. The cloth beneath was stained, and the swelling had distorted the familiar shape. Leth lifted her head, looked, and lowered it again. She remained still for a while.

“Can I walk?”

“Not now,” Esvin said.

“After.”

“I don’t know.”

She turned her face toward the shelter wall.

Riarhin waited, holding the lamp steady. Rain ticked against the stones above them. Osset had woken and was watching from his blanket. She signalled him to turn away, then wished she had not done it so obviously. Leth saw the movement.

“Let him look too. Everyone’s been looking.”

“No one means—” Esvin began.

“I know what no one means.”

He closed his mouth.

After a while she asked for water. Riarhin helped her drink. The carrier’s hands were steadier than the evening before, but she could not quite hold the cup at the angle she wanted. Riarhin let her keep one hand on it.

“There’s a woman at the lower landing,” Leth said. “She keeps accounts for the carriers.”

“What name?”

Leth gave it. Riarhin repeated it quietly.

“She’ll know my sister’s address. Don’t let them send to the old one.”

“We’ll ask her.”

“My sister married. They always send to the old one.”

“I’ll ask.”

Leth looked at her. She was trying to judge the difference between a promise made to quiet someone and one which would survive breakfast.

“You write?”

“Yes.”

“Then write it now.”

Riarhin found the dry part of a page and wrote the name and instruction. She showed Leth. The woman could read enough to recognise it. She nodded and let Esvin replace the blanket.

When Riarhin stood, her knee almost gave. She caught herself against the wall. Leth saw that too and said nothing.

Breakfast was an argument over fuel.

Eddis wanted a larger fire. Cadan had found wood beneath a fallen section of wall, but some of it carried a pale growth which Maren would not allow near the food. Haldren would have known what could be burned. Nobody said that until Eddis did, with sudden anger at the wood itself.

“Well, he isn’t here to tell us,” Maren answered.

The silence afterward was dreadful. Haldren lay beneath the dry part of the broken roof, within sight of all of them. Maren turned away and began sorting the smaller sticks with her hands.

Riarhin brought the axe from the carpenter’s bundle. She cut away the outer part of one length, examined the exposed wood, and set it aside for a small fire beyond the ward rather than the cooking hearth. They could warm the wet clothing there without putting the smoke over the pot.

Eddis accepted the compromise by helping her carry it.

At the edge of the shelter he said, “I shouldn’t have said it.”

“She shouldn’t either.”

“No.”

They built the fire. It burned reluctantly, then well enough to steam the cloth spread beside it. Riarhin held one of Leth’s coverings near the heat and turned it before it scorched. The plain work occupied her hands while her mind kept returning to the gallery. She had caught Brenn first because he was nearer. She could remember the exact drag of his coat, the tearing seam, Haldren’s hand on the rope. She could change none of their positions by remembering them more accurately.

Eddis took the cloth when her arms tired.

“Eat,” he said.

She went back beneath the stones. Maren had made a little more room around Haldren’s body and set his recovered tools beside the covered shoulder. She was sitting with her hands in her lap.

Riarhin gave her a bowl. The keeper looked at it for a moment before taking it.

“I know he was there,” Maren said.

“I know.”

Neither said anything else.

Breaking camp took most of the morning.

The injured had to be moved before their bedding could be packed. The pattern had to remain until everyone was ready, but the wrapping for its pieces lay beneath Brenn. Osset lost one shoe in the dark and could not remember whether he had taken it off before or after the rain. Nereth found it beneath a fold of the carpenter’s blanket. She brought it to the boy without looking at Riarhin.

They reduced the loads. Food stayed. Water stayed. Spare vessels went. Esvin emptied a little jar and kept its contents in a scrap of cloth. The wrapped remains from beside the boat went into the freed space in his bag. Maren left two pieces of stone which might have helped later, after measuring them and making Nereth draw the marks from both sides.

Riarhin left her damaged pack’s outer frame. It had been useful for keeping a load clear of her shoulders, but it caught at every turn. She cut away the split part, kept the straps, and folded the leather around the papers instead.

Cadan took the heaviest remaining bundle.

“You have Haldren,” she said.

“For the first stretch. Then we change.”

“You need to be able to put it down.”

He considered, rearranged the knot, and showed her the release. She nodded.

Before they lifted the body, Esvin asked for a moment.

He did not sing. He checked the covering, tucked the loose end under, and removed a stone from the place where Haldren’s head would rest. Riarhin watched him do it and realised that he had been making such small adjustments since the gallery while she attended to routes and weights.

“Ready,” he said.

They lifted.

At the edge of the colonnade Eddis chose the path which looked wrong.

There were two ways from the colonnade. One led toward a pale marker Riarhin recognised from the causeway. The other entered a low stretch of wet ground beneath leaning trees. Eddis stood between them for some time, holding his pole horizontally across both palms.

“I’ve seen the first,” he said.

“So have I.”

“That’s why I don’t trust it.”

“What do you trust?”

He pointed toward the wet ground.

“The water runs out that way. The roots have mud on the same side. There’s a place where someone dragged something through before the last rain.”

“Us?”

“I don’t know.”

Riarhin looked at the marker. It was exactly where she wanted it to be. Beyond it she could almost see the causeway, the far bank, the two cloth strips they had left at different heights.

“How certain?”

“Not certain.”

She nodded.

“We take the water.”

It was slow. Haldren’s load sank when they set it down, so they learned to rest it across roots or stones. Leth travelled on the repaired pallet, carried in turns. Brenn walked between two people until his legs failed, then sat while they moved the others ahead and came back for him.

By afternoon everyone had carried something they could no longer bear to lift.

Riarhin’s hands blistered beneath the old calluses. She wrapped them in cloth. Cadan said he could take her end for a little while; she reminded him that he already had an end. He smiled faintly and said that was true.

They found the old cloth mark near dusk.

It was the lower one, Eddis’s, tied around a stone beside the water. The upper strip had fallen. Beyond it lay the causeway, narrower than Riarhin remembered and blessedly solid beneath the pole.

She sat down on the bank before crossing. Only for a moment. When she tried to stand again, her legs would not immediately obey.

Cadan waited without offering his hand. She was grateful for that, then angry at herself for being grateful, then too tired to care. She took the hand when he offered it on the second attempt.

The causeway did not bring them out of the wood.

They spent that night at the leaning wall. It was still there, and still leaned. The place where Riarhin had sat with Esvin’s strap was wet now, a run of water finding its way through the moss above. She looked at it while Eddis tested the ground for the pallets. No voice came.

They made no fire. There was bread enough for the night and they had neither dry kindling nor strength to search. Riarhin cut the portions while Maren and Nereth settled the pattern. The girl’s hand shook so badly that Maren made her speak the sequence and did the placing herself. Nereth corrected her twice. The second correction was almost a whisper.

Leth asked to have her pallet turned away from Haldren. Riarhin did it without asking why. When she had finished, the carrier caught the edge of her coat.

“I know he has to come.”

“Yes.”

“I know.”

Riarhin tucked the covering beneath her sound leg and went back for the bread. Brenn was awake, staring at his portion. He had been doing that since she gave it to him. She put a cup of water beside his hand and told him to soak it if he could not chew.

“I’m not hungry.”

“You’ll be walking tomorrow.”

He broke off a piece. She watched until he put it in his mouth, then made herself look elsewhere.

In the morning they went by the water gate. Cadan and Eddis walked ahead only as far as the next bend while the others carried the loads in short stages. They avoided the patrol crossing entirely, following the course the reed-cutter had scratched in the dirt and the marks Eddis had left where they could find them. The ground had changed less away from the ruined roads. It was also rougher, full of roots at the height of a tired ankle.

By noon the skin at Riarhin’s heel had begun to tear. She stopped long enough to wrap it and was still pulling on her boot when Osset came back along the track.

“The man says we can put it there.”

“What man?”

“The one with the knife on a stick.”

The reed-cutter stood beyond the next clump of alder, his hook across his body. Eddis was talking to him. He looked at the company one by one, counted their burdens, and kept looking after there were no more people to count.

“We didn’t find her,” Riarhin said.

He bent to pick up the bundle he had dropped.

“There’s a dry place. A little farther.”

She wanted to explain how little of the forest they had seen, how many routes remained, how worthless their failure was as evidence. He was already leading Eddis away to fetch his sled.

They rested beside the rack Riarhin had helped repair. The stone they had put beneath its foot was still in place. The man looked at Haldren’s covered face and then at the sled’s willow binding.

“Him?”

“Yes.”

He ran a thumb over the fastening. After a while he went into the shelter and brought out the second cup.

The sled could carry Leth. It could not carry all of them.

Eddis tried it with a load of reeds first, taking the narrow passage towards the bank and returning with one runner scraped nearly white. The old man watched and said he had gone too close to the stones. Eddis said there was nowhere else to go with that width. Together they shifted the load and tried again.

Riarhin sat beside Leth while the trial continued. The carrier had begun to shiver. Esvin wanted to change the dressing before they moved farther. He asked the reed-cutter for hot water and a place out of the wind.

The man cleared the space behind his reed bundles. He took the small coat from the bedding and held it for a moment before putting it inside a basket where nobody would sit on it.

Leth was taken in. Riarhin held the lamp. Esvin unwound the wet cloth and looked at the leg without speaking. His silence frightened her more than an oath would have done. He asked for the clean strips kept deepest in his bag and made her wash her hands again before passing them.

Leth said she could feel what he was doing and would prefer not to. He apologised. She told him to stop apologising and finish.

When it was done, they had very little clean linen left.

The reed-cutter came back with a folded length of coarse cloth. It had been washed until the weave was soft, then mended in three places with a darker thread. He gave it to Esvin.

“How much?” Riarhin asked.

He named a price. She paid it. He looked at the coins in his palm and added, “For the cloth. Not for the cup.”

She nodded. He put the money away and went to help Eddis lash the pallet onto the sled.

They stayed that afternoon and the next night. Leth slept after the dressing was changed, and Esvin would not move her while she could. Brenn ate two bowls of the reed-cutter’s stew and fell asleep sitting up. Osset helped bring in reeds. The old man gave him a short armful, then a longer one when he proved able to carry it. They spoke very little. At dusk the boy was asleep beside the doorway with dry stems caught in his hair.

Riarhin washed the bloodstained linen downstream of the shelter. The water numbed her fingers. She worked each piece between her palms until the cloth was no cleaner for rubbing, then spread it over a branch. Nereth came and knelt beside her.

“You shouldn’t get that hand wet,” Riarhin said.

“I can hang them.”

She could, with her good hand and some difficulty. Riarhin passed her the rinsed cloths. They went on until the branch was full.

On the bank above them, the reed-cutter asked Cadan about a place beyond the water gate. Cadan said he had not passed it. The man described it again, differently, and Cadan listened before saying the same thing. Riarhin bent over the last strip of linen. The cold water moved round her wrists.

At first light the reed-cutter took the front of the sled.

“Only to the firm track,” he said.

They thanked him and he told them to pull when he pulled, not after. Riarhin took one rear line, Eddis the other. Esvin walked beside Leth, watching the pallet at every bump. Haldren came behind with Cadan and Maren bearing the sling. Nereth carried Maren’s bundle. Brenn leaned on a cut pole and made no attempt to hurry.

The man knew which bends could be taken wide and which needed the front lifted. Twice he stopped them before a runner buried itself. Once he became furious with Eddis for pulling when told to slacken. Eddis shouted back. Leth told both of them to shut up unless they intended to carry her instead. They shut up for almost half an hour.

At the firm track the man untied his line.

“Hesvar,” he reminded Riarhin.

“I remember.”

“He knows the nephew.”

“Yes.”

He nodded. Then he looked at the sled, at the load they still had, and retied the line.

“The next turn.”

He came two turns farther. When he finally left them, he took no assurance that the sled would be brought back. Riarhin gave him money against it anyway. He counted the coins, returned one, and walked away without looking at the company again.

By evening they reached the track which had cost them the first detour. The flooded low road was still impassable, but Cadan could carry a message over the stony shoulder faster than the injured could be brought. Riarhin gave him the amended writ and told him where to leave a mark if he found nobody at the station.

He was gone until after dark. She had begun preparing another camp when two lanterns appeared above the track, separated by the width of a carrying pole. The station sergeant came behind them with Cadan.

“Six went out,” he said.

“Ten coming back,” Maren answered. “One dead.”

He counted again. Then he began dividing the loads among his people.

Riarhin had imagined relief as lying down. It took the form of answering questions while someone else examined her knots.

The soldier assigned to Haldren wanted the sling changed. He had never seen its particular fastening. Cadan showed him how it held. The soldier tried it, remained dissatisfied, and made a second fastening beside the first. Riarhin watched with a patience she had to manufacture breath by breath.

A young man took Leth’s rear line and asked her to tell him if the pallet tilted. She said she would. He promised to be careful and she told him that would be a welcome novelty. He looked wounded until Esvin reassured him that she had spoken worse to the rest of them.

The sergeant had brought food. Riarhin ate a piece of hard cheese so quickly that it caught in her throat. He passed her his water skin and looked tactfully towards the trees while she coughed.

They reached the station late. Its makeshift roof still stood outside the wall. The inner yard was dark. Riarhin heard what might have been a boot strike stone within and did not ask about the relief.

Leth was given the sergeant’s bed. He complained about this before anyone could thank him, which made it easier to accept. Brenn lay by the fire. Osset slept without removing his shoes. The others sat wherever they could fit and ate what the garrison had left from supper.

Maren put the papers on a dry board beneath the roof. The sergeant read the amended clause, looked towards the loads, and asked whether they had left anyone behind.

“One still missing,” Cadan said. “Davin.”

The sergeant repeated the name to the man keeping the watch. No one called the count complete.

They remained there until morning. Beyond the road station, carts could be used again. The sergeant lent one and sent a man to bring it back. Riarhin walked beside it, her empty hands swelling now that she no longer had to grip a pole. When she tried to close them, the skin cracked across two knuckles.

Cultivated ground returned gradually: first a drainage cut that someone had cleared, then a tied gate, then a field with people working in it. A woman looked up from the rows and watched the cart pass. She called to ask whether they had found the carriers. Eddis answered. By the next occupied frame, someone was already waiting with water.

At the outer line a bell was silent.

They heard the hammer before they saw the frame: metal struck against metal at irregular intervals, followed by shouted instructions. A repair crew worked beneath the leaning posts. Two soldiers stood beyond them with lamps. One raised his hand when the company came into sight.

“Stay there.”

Riarhin kept walking until Eddis touched her arm.

“Stop,” he said quietly.

She stopped.

The soldier looked at the loads, the injured, the girl carrying the wrapped book. He called for someone behind the frame. An officer came forward with a folded paper beneath one arm.

“Maren?”

The keeper stepped into the light.

“The lower span has failed,” he said. “We have orders concerning—”

“Open the admission.”

“I need to establish—”

“Open it. Then establish what you like.”

He looked at Riarhin. She could feel blood dried along her mouth and dirt ground into every crease of her hands. Haldren’s load hung between her and Cadan. She did not put it down.

“Who’s that?” the officer asked.

“Our carpenter.”

“Dead?”

“Yes.”

“And the girl?”

“Nereth,” Maren said. “She comes through with us.”

The officer glanced at his paper. Behind him a worker shouted that the upper pin would not take the weight. The frame shifted. Everyone turned toward it except Riarhin, who kept watching the officer.

He swore.

“Bring them through the side. One load at a time.”

Maren was already moving.

Inside, someone took the sling from Riarhin’s hands.

She tried not to let go. Cadan said her name, and she realised the people reaching for it were living men with a stretcher. She opened her fingers one at a time.

A woman brought hot water. Another cut the cloth from Riarhin’s palms. It hurt enough to make her hiss. The woman apologised without stopping.

Leth was taken into a small room where there was a bed. Brenn followed. Osset sat beside the hearth and began to eat before anyone told him he could. Nereth stood by the door until Maren placed her on a bench and put a cup into her good hand.

The officer came inside with the folded paper.

“Tomorrow,” Maren said.

“This cannot simply—”

“Tomorrow.”

He looked around the room. Then he put the paper on a high shelf, beyond the steam from the basin, and went back outside.

Riarhin sat while the woman cleaned her hands. Across the room Esvin had fallen asleep with his head against the wall. His mouth was open. Eddis covered him with a coat and sat beside him.

The water in the basin grew pink, then brown. The woman emptied it and brought more.

Part VI — What is still open

Chapter 36 — Arngrim’s carpenter

Arngrim came before the body reached the harbour.

Someone had sent a boy ahead. Riarhin did not know who. She had been walking beside the cart, one hand on its side because the road was uneven and she had not yet recovered the habit of trusting anything to remain where it was put. When she looked up, Arngrim stood beside the gate with his coat unfastened and a piece of cord in his hand.

He looked at her. Then at the cart.

“No,” he said.

She stopped.

“No.”

The carter took off his cap. Riarhin wished he had not, then could think of nothing else he might have done.

Arngrim came to the cart and pulled back the covering himself. Esvin moved as though to help, then remained still. The shipmaster looked for a long time. He touched Haldren’s forehead with two fingers, withdrew them, and put the covering back badly. One corner caught beneath the carpenter’s shoulder.

Riarhin freed it.

“What happened?” Arngrim asked.

“A support moved. He was bringing Brenn across.”

“Broke?”

“The wall shifted beneath it.”

“Was he on his own?”

“No.”

“Who was there?”

“I was. Cadan. Eddis. The others were close.”

Arngrim looked from her to the people behind the cart. Cadan stood with his hands at his sides. Eddis had taken off his cap too. Maren and Nereth had remained at the mill under the terms agreed at the outer line. Riarhin was glad the girl was not here, then ashamed of the relief.

“How long?” Arngrim asked.

She told him as accurately as she could. Not very long, and much too long. He asked whether Haldren had spoken. She said he had asked about Brenn.

“Of course he did.”

The words came out with such anger that the carter stepped back.

Arngrim looked at the cord in his own hand as though discovering it there. He wound it around his fist, unwound it, and put it in his pocket.

“Bring him aboard,” he said.

They cleared the space beneath the afterdeck.

A coil of rope had to be moved. A half-finished repair lay beside the bulkhead, the little tools still arranged where Haldren had left them. Arngrim stood over the work until a sailor asked what to do with it. Then he told the man to leave it alone, sharply enough that everyone nearby went quiet.

Riarhin helped carry the stretcher down. The narrow companionway forced them to lower one end first. She had dreaded another awkward turn, another moment of treating his body as a load which would not fit. Arngrim took the lower end himself and guided them through without a word.

Below, Esvin asked for clean water and cloth.

“We have both,” Arngrim said.

“I know.”

The shipmaster left. Riarhin heard him on the deck, giving instructions too quickly, then repeating one because nobody had understood it.

Esvin uncovered the carpenter’s face.

“You can go up,” he told her.

“I can help.”

“Yes. You can.”

She stayed.

They washed the mud from his hair. There was more of it than she expected. It had dried behind his ears and along the line of his jaw. Esvin worked gently, turning the cloth to a clean part each time. Riarhin cleaned the hands. The wedding ring would not pass the swollen joint. She looked at Esvin.

“Leave it,” he said.

She left it.

The work took time. Above them a bucket scraped across the deck. Someone laughed at something farther down the quay, then stopped as if another person had told him. Riarhin found a small splinter in Haldren’s palm and removed it with the point of a needle. She had done the same thing for him two days before the fortress, when he could still complain that she was making too much of it.

When they had finished, Esvin covered him with a clean sheet.

He did not take out the bell. He did not ask whether Arngrim wished to hear anything said through him.

Riarhin sat on the step until the water had been carried away.

Arngrim’s next questions were about the work.

Where had the beam come from? How had Haldren tested it? Had anyone seen the seat failing before they crossed? Riarhin answered all she could. She described the knife test, the sound stone at one end, the weaker seating at the other, the warning about the board lifting if the root moved sideways.

“He saw it, then,” Arngrim said.

“He knew it might.”

“And went on it.”

“Yes.”

“With another man.”

“Yes.”

Arngrim pressed both hands flat on the table.

“He knew better.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“He knew better than to put two men over something which might move.”

“We had injured people to get across.”

“So you let him.”

“Yes.”

The shipmaster looked at her. She did not try to improve the answer. She had accepted the repair, crossed it, put people on it, asked him to keep it open. He had been competent. She had relied on that. None of it gave her anything she could offer Arngrim now.

Outside, a sailor dropped something heavy. Arngrim shouted through the door that if they broke it they could pay for it. Then he sat down abruptly.

“His wife will ask me.”

“I’ll write it.”

“She’ll ask me.”

Riarhin looked at the table. A shallow cut ran across one corner, freshly planed at the end. Haldren’s work, probably. She did not ask.

“What is her name?”

“Thordis.”

Riarhin repeated it. Arngrim nodded, then gave her the name of the settlement and the house above the boat sheds.

He covered his face with a hand.

After a while he said, “He was supposed to mend that before we sailed.”

She looked where he pointed. The cupboard latch hung crooked. Every time the vessel moved, it tapped softly against the wood.

“I can—”

“No.”

“All right.”

They sat and listened to it.

Brenn came aboard in the afternoon.

He should not have walked that far. Esvin had told him so, and Brenn had asked for a cart. When none was available he had come with Leth’s borrowed stick and a young woman from the lodging house who supported him under one arm. Riarhin met them at the gangway.

“He isn’t receiving people,” she said.

“I need to.”

“I know.”

“No. I need to.”

She went to ask Arngrim. He stood beside the open sea chest, holding a shirt. Haldren had folded it around a little packet, perhaps to keep the packet dry. Arngrim put both back before answering.

“Bring him.”

Brenn could not stand upright in the small cabin. He tried and nearly fell. Arngrim pulled out the stool with his foot.

“Sit.”

The carrier sat.

He began with thanks. He stopped halfway through the sentence, unable to make its subject clear. Then he said Haldren had told him to put his foot where he put his and not look down. He had looked down. That was why he stumbled.

Arngrim’s face hardened.

“The wall moved,” he said.

“I know, but I—”

“The wall moved.”

Brenn began to cry. He tried to hide it by bending over his knees, which made him cough. Riarhin fetched water. Arngrim remained standing beside the chest.

At last the shipmaster put the shirt down and rested a hand on Brenn’s shoulder. The hand stayed there, awkward and heavy, until the coughing passed.

“Go back to your bed,” he said.

Brenn nodded.

“And use a cart.”

Before the meal, Arngrim asked Riarhin to help with the sea chest.

He said it was because her hands were smaller. The catch beneath the lining had stuck, and Haldren kept a second purse there. Riarhin could see the catch perfectly well. She thought Arngrim could have reached it. She knelt anyway and worked it loose with the little awl they had brought back.

The purse contained less than either of them expected. Arngrim counted the coins, frowned, and then found a folded tally beside them.

“Timber,” he said.

“For the ship?”

“For home.”

He sat on the edge of the berth and read the tally. Haldren had paid for lengths to be delivered before the next winter. Some had been crossed off, others marked with a second cut. Beside one stood a little drawing of a door.

“Thordis wanted the back room made larger.”

Riarhin set the purse beside him.

“He said she’d changed her mind three times. He liked complaining about it.”

Arngrim folded the tally, unfolded it, and looked again at the drawing.

“I suppose someone will have to do it.”

Riarhin almost offered money. She had enough to make a contribution if she sold something or delayed her own passage. She could imagine the practical uses of it and the way Arngrim might hear the offer in this room, beside the man she had brought back covered in a blanket.

“Will she want the timber?” she asked instead.

“I’ll ask her.”

They packed the tally with his papers.

There was a little bag of dried berries in the chest, a spare buckle, two socks which did not match, and a carved animal with one ear unfinished. Arngrim looked at the animal for a long time before putting it in a separate cloth.

“Who is it for?” Riarhin asked.

“Don’t know.”

He rubbed a finger over the unfinished ear.

“Someone will.”

They closed the chest. Riarhin worked the catch twice to make sure it would open at the other end. The awl fitted neatly in her hand. She set it with the rest of Haldren’s tools before standing.

At the door Arngrim said, “You could have asked someone else.”

She stopped.

“To go with you.”

“Yes.”

“He’d still have gone.”

She waited, but Arngrim seemed to have finished. He turned back toward the chest, put a hand on its lid, and stood there until someone called them to eat.

Before they ate, Arngrim asked Riarhin to come on deck.

The tide was turning. A sailor knelt by the gangway with a new length of rope, working slowly enough to make it clear that he was waiting for instructions. Beyond him the quay continued its day. A cart laden with empty casks had blocked the narrowest part, and two men were arguing about which direction it could reasonably move.

“I was going west first,” Arngrim said.

Riarhin waited.

“Cargo arranged. Half paid. Then home before the weather turns.”

“Will you still?”

He looked down through the open hatch.

“No.”

She said nothing. He rubbed the back of his neck, angry at the question although he had brought her up to hear the answer.

“There’s a ship going that way in three days. I can put the freight with her. Lose money on it. Pay people I haven’t used before. Tell the buyer to argue with someone he doesn’t know.”

“Can I help?”

“Can you make three days into yesterday?”

“No.”

“Then not with that.”

A shout rose from the cask cart. The sailor by the gangway tightened his knot and looked towards the noise, grateful for something to watch.

Arngrim leaned on the rail.

“His wife knows when I’m due. She comes down to the sheds even when she says she won’t. If another captain carries word before me—”

He broke off. Riarhin looked at the water between the hull and the quay. Bits of straw circled there, held by the small returning current.

“Who can tell her to wait indoors?” she asked.

He turned on her.

“I don’t want anyone telling her things before I do.”

“All right.”

After a while he said, “Her sister could. If I sent to the nearer harbour. She goes there to sell.”

He considered the arrangement without looking at Riarhin. She stayed by the rail. The cask cart finally moved, its driver walking beside the horse and abusing both men who had helped him.

Arngrim called the sailor over. The young man stood at once. They spoke about a packet for the nearer harbour, who would carry it, and whether that person could be trusted to take a message straight to the sheds. Riarhin stepped away while they settled it.

At the hatch she met the cook carrying a bucket. He waited for her to move and, when she did not immediately understand, said, “I need to get past.”

She moved. He lowered the bucket below and climbed after it. A few moments later she heard him ask Esvin which of the clean cloths could be used and which had to be kept. The ship was full of questions Haldren would not answer. She went ashore to fetch the remaining bundle of his tools.

They ate aboard because Arngrim told them to.

Riarhin would rather have left. She could have claimed Maren needed her, or that she had a message to deliver at the mill. Both were true in some loose sense. Instead she sat on the bench where Haldren had once sat with the bread between his knees, and accepted a bowl from a sailor whose name she did not know.

The stew was too salty. No one said so until Arngrim did. Then the cook told him Haldren had liked it that way.

“He liked everything too salty.”

“He said it was the only way he could taste your fish.”

For a moment nobody moved. Then Arngrim laughed once, badly, and wiped his mouth with the back of his hand.

The meal continued.

Later Riarhin wrote the account. She named who had been present, what they had seen, what Haldren had said. She left a space where she had almost written that he had saved Brenn’s life. It was true, but she did not want it to stand where the woman reading would look for her husband’s last words. She put it farther down, beside the account of the crossing.

She listed his tools which had been recovered and those left behind. The mallet from the old boat was not his and remained there. His own knife, the little awl, the straightening stone, and the needle case lay on the table beside her. One handle still held the warmth of her hand.

Arngrim read what she had written. He asked her to add the warning about the board. She did.

When she finally left the ship, the cupboard latch was still tapping below.

Chapter 37 — The grain still owed

Kelva struck Nereth across the face.

Riarhin had been halfway through the mill door when it happened. She stopped. Nereth did not raise a hand. The sound seemed louder than it could have been, followed by the rattle of a spoon dropped onto the table.

Then Kelva caught the girl against her and held her so hard that Nereth could barely move.

“You stupid child,” she said.

Nereth began to speak. Kelva shook her head against the girl’s hair.

“Not yet.”

They stood together beside the hearth. Riarhin stepped back into the passage and waited there until Tessa came past carrying a basket of clean cloth.

“Are they still doing it?” the child asked.

“Yes.”

“Pell says the bread will burn.”

“I’ll see to it.”

She went around by the kitchen door and moved the bread herself.

The mill was fuller than when they had left.

Two families still slept in the upper room. Another had been taken in across the lane, but their hens remained in Kelva’s shed because the other household kept a dog. Dorran had repaired the gate he had injured his hand upon. Rella’s clay bird sat on the shelf, its mended tail turned toward the wall so nobody could see the join unless they picked it up.

Riarhin picked it up. The child saw her and turned it around.

“That side’s better.”

“Yes.”

“Nereth did it.”

“I remember.”

Rella took the bird and carried it outside, where she had made a nest for it in a broken basket.

In the main room Sivrik and an Aeler clerk were counting sacks.

The clerk was not the official from the first inspection, though that man arrived later with the cut on his neck healed to a pale line. Sivrik had prepared three different totals, depending on what could be dried, what had already been advanced against, and what Kelva refused to release before the next grinding. He had written them on separate sheets and was becoming angry because the clerk kept laying one over another.

“Those aren’t the same sacks.”

“They’re the same grain.”

“Some of it is spoiled.”

“Then that should be entered.”

“It is entered. On the sheet you keep covering.”

Kelva came into the room with her face washed. She took the sheets, laid them side by side, and weighted each with a cup.

“There,” she said. “Now none of them can hide.”

The official almost smiled. He did not smile when Nereth appeared behind her.

Osset’s aunt arrived while the sacks were still being counted.

She came in a cart driven by a neighbour and would not let him help her down. She was perhaps forty, perhaps older; worry had made her face difficult to place. At the door she asked for the boy, then asked whether someone had played a cruelty upon her by sending the message.

Kelva brought her inside.

Osset was sitting with Tessa, watching the child mend a little net. He had not been much help. His hands knew the beginning of the knot and then forgot it, leaving Tessa to finish. When the woman entered he looked up politely, as he had looked up at every stranger since reaching the mill.

She stopped three paces away.

“Osset.”

He frowned.

“Aunt?”

The woman put a hand over her mouth. She had been ready for something, Riarhin thought, but not for that uncertain little question. She knelt in front of him and took both his hands before remembering to ask.

He let her.

“Your hair,” he said.

She laughed and cried at once. Her hair was shorter than he remembered, streaked with grey beside the ears. She touched it with one hand, then returned that hand to his.

“Yes.”

“Why did you cut it?”

“It caught in everything.”

“Mother said you were vain about it.”

The woman closed her eyes.

Riarhin moved nearer the doorway, giving them space. Tessa remained on the bench with the unfinished net in her lap. After a moment Kelva drew her away to fetch cups.

“How long?” Osset asked.

His aunt did not answer quickly enough.

“How long was I gone?”

She told him the number of winters. He looked at her and shook his head.

“No.”

“I know.”

“Three days.”

She gripped his hands. He pulled one free.

“I counted.”

“Come home,” she said, too quickly.

“Is she there?”

The woman looked toward Kelva. Riarhin saw the appeal and could do nothing with it. Esvin had come downstairs. He remained beside the stair rather than approaching the boy as though a priest’s presence could make the next answer less terrible.

“No,” the aunt said at last.

“Where?”

“At the end of the second winter.”

Osset stood. The movement nearly overturned the stool. His aunt rose with him, reaching for his arm, but he drew away and went into the yard.

Riarhin followed as far as the door.

He stood beside the fence, both hands on the top rail. Rella’s clay bird lay in its basket beneath him. He looked down at it without seeing it.

Behind Riarhin, his aunt said, “I shouldn’t have said it here.”

“Where could you have said it?” Kelva asked.

The woman began to cry properly then. Kelva took her to the bench.

Riarhin went into the yard. She stopped several paces from Osset.

“Do you want me to go?”

He shrugged.

She stayed where she was.

After a while he said, “I heard her.”

Riarhin looked at the fence rail, wet where his fingers had rubbed away the dirt.

“In there?”

He nodded.

“She said my name.”

Riarhin had not heard a name. Esvin had not said he heard one either. She could not decide what that entitled her to say.

“I heard someone I knew,” she said. “I couldn’t make out the words.”

“Did you go?”

“No.”

“Why?”

She thought of Ailsa beyond the wall, of how badly she had wanted to turn her head and find a door.

“Someone stayed beside me.”

Osset looked at her for the first time since coming outside. He was angry, and young, and too tired to keep anger fixed on one person.

“It might have been her.”

“Yes.”

He turned back to the fence.

They stood there until Tessa came out with bread and asked whether he wanted the end or the middle. He said neither. She put both on the rail and went away. After a while he took the end.

The aunt did not take him home that day.

The house she could offer was not the house he remembered. The reed houses were gone, the land surrendered after two failed seasons and a winter in which the old line could not be kept. She lived farther inland now, with people Osset had never met. She tried to explain the arrangement and stopped when she saw him listening as though she described a lodging for someone else.

They agreed on the next morning instead. He would sleep at the mill, she at the house across the lane. No one called the delay unreasonable.

At supper he asked for another bowl. Kelva gave it to him. His aunt watched him eat with such painful attention that Riarhin caught herself watching the woman, and made herself return to her own food.

Later Osset asked whether he could take the net. Tessa said it was not finished. He said he knew.

She gave it to him with the wooden needle still in it.

The demand for custody was read before supper.

Riarhin had expected soldiers. There were two at the door, but they had come because the official would not travel between the frames without an escort. One remained outside. The other stood near the hearth and looked embarrassed by how many people had to walk around him.

The paper demanded the journal, the dangerous fittings, and Nereth’s person pending examination by authorised keepers. It referred to the failed span, the displaced approaches, and the missing. It did not mention the houses which had slept safely under her work. Maren pointed that out.

“It is an order of inquiry,” the official said. “Not a history.”

“Then inquire here.”

“We cannot conduct the necessary examination in a mill.”

“You can conduct it in the keeper’s house.”

“Under whose authority?”

Kelva put a bowl on the table with unnecessary force.

“Under a roof which is standing.”

Riarhin stayed beside the window. She had given her account of the fortress earlier, including what Nereth had done. The girl had listened without contradicting it. Now she sat with her bound wrist against her stomach, looking at the paper rather than anyone’s face.

Maren had brought three other keepers. Two maintained old bell allotments and had no intention of abandoning them. The third had helped shelter the evacuated households. None wanted Nereth carried away before the new pattern could be properly understood. They did not all agree on what should happen afterward.

That disagreement occupied most of the evening.

Riarhin was asked twice whether she would guarantee the girl’s conduct. She said no both times. Nereth looked at her after the first refusal, then looked down. The second time Maren answered before Riarhin could explain.

“She won’t be working alone.”

“Who stops her?” the official asked.

“I do. Or someone who knows the stopping sequence. There will be more than one.”

“And the book?”

“Sealed when it isn’t being examined. Here.”

The official would not accept that as a final arrangement. Maren would not accept transfer as an interim one. At last they agreed on the next day, and the day after that: witnessed examination, copies of specific dangerous workings, no new installations without two keepers present. The larger question remained on the paper between them.

The soldiers ate before leaving. Kelva insisted. She gave them the same stew as everyone else and no warmth beyond what the bowl provided.

Werrin returned the following morning.

He had reached the upper frame before the path changed. The keeper sent a runner ahead with one copy and took the other herself. Mistress Aven and her husband had already begun preparing to go home for their seeds. The warning stopped them at the gate.

“They were angry,” Werrin said.

“At you?” Riarhin asked.

“At everyone.”

“Fair enough.”

His foot was worse. Esvin looked at it, told him what he thought of the journey, and sent him to sit beside Nereth, who had already begun telling him the same thing. For a little while their argument sounded like any two young people who knew one another too well.

Then Werrin asked about the carpenter.

Nereth stopped speaking.

Riarhin told him. He looked at the boot Haldren had let out for him. One of the new stitches had broken. He bent to touch it and remained bent for some time.

Nereth’s mother came on foot with a man carrying a sack of meal.

Riarhin met them in the yard. The resemblance was not immediate. The woman’s hair was darker, her face broader, and her gaze settled on a thing before she asked about it. Then she saw Nereth at the upper window, and the same quick movement crossed her mouth that Riarhin had seen in the workshop when the girl was frightened.

“She’s up there?”

“Yes.”

“Can I go?”

“It isn’t my house.”

Kelva appeared in the doorway. The two women looked at each other with the uneasy familiarity of people who had shared responsibility without ever agreeing how it should be divided.

“I brought meal,” Nereth’s mother said.

“You needn’t have.”

“I brought it.”

Kelva took the sack from the man and moved aside.

Riarhin remained in the yard until she heard the first raised voice. It was Nereth’s, followed by her mother’s lower answer. She could have distinguished the words if she tried. She went to help Pell instead.

He was mending a screen used to keep coarse matter from the flour. The old man had laid the frame across two stools and was struggling to pull the binding tight with his bad thumb. Riarhin took that end. He told her she was pulling too hard, then too little, then admitted the material had stretched and neither of them was at fault.

They worked while the voices upstairs rose and fell.

After a while Nereth’s mother came down. She stood beside the screen, watching them. Pell asked whether she had eaten. She said she had. He did not believe her and went to find bread, leaving Riarhin holding the frame.

“She thinks I wanted them to take her plates,” the woman said.

Riarhin waited.

“I opened the door. They were going to break the fastening. I opened it.”

“She told me they broke one.”

“They did. It fell. One of them apologised.”

The woman rubbed a hand across her forehead.

“That made her angrier.”

Riarhin could imagine it: the child watching her work put into a sack, the adult’s apology over an object he did not understand, her mother letting the men pass because the alternative would have broken something else.

“Did the old line protect you?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Then I expect you were glad to have it.”

“I was.”

She said it defiantly, as though Riarhin too might condemn her for surviving by the wrong protection.

Pell returned with bread and cheese. He put the plate on the dry end of the frame and told them not to get binding in it. The woman took a piece, ate without appearing to taste it, then took another.

“She was twelve,” she said. “I didn’t know what she had found. I thought the marks were copied from doorways. Children copy things.”

Riarhin set the frame down.

“You didn’t write the book.”

“No.”

The woman looked toward the stairs.

“She won’t come home.”

“She has to stay for the inquiry.”

“After.”

“I don’t know.”

“Neither do I.”

They finished the bread. Above them, a chair scraped across the floor.

When Nereth came down, her mother was still there.

The girl stopped on the last step. Her wrist was bare; someone had changed the binding and left it to dry. She held the clean cloth in her other hand.

Kelva looked up from the table, then returned to what she was doing with more attention than it required.

Nereth’s mother held out a small packet.

“Your comb. The good one.”

“I have a comb.”

“You have half a comb.”

Nereth took the packet. She unwrapped it enough to see the polished wood, then folded it again.

“I didn’t know it would do that.”

“I know.”

“You don’t. You weren’t—”

“I know you didn’t mean this.”

The girl looked at her mother’s face and seemed to lose the rest of the argument. She stood with the comb in her hand until her mother stepped forward and touched her uninjured wrist.

Neither embraced the other. After a while they went upstairs together. Kelva continued wiping the same place on the table until Pell told her it was clean.

“I can see,” she said.

He left her alone.

The smaller pattern went up at the mill first.

It did not replace everything. One old bell remained where it had always hung. Two households preferred to keep the Aeler maintenance outright; their keeper said he knew its faults and did not yet know the new one’s. Maren accepted that with less grace than she might have wished, but she accepted it.

Kelva learned the checks. She learned where to stand, which fitting to inspect before touching another, and how to recognise the change which meant people should leave rather than wait for a repair. She made Nereth show her twice. The girl did.

Sivrik missed the first lesson because he was at the landing with the grain. Kelva said he could take the second night watch to make up for it. He said he had already been awake half the night arranging the shipment. She said that was unfortunate.

Riarhin helped carry the posts. Her hands had begun to heal, and the weight reopened one blister. She wrapped it and continued. Dorran took the heavier end without comment.

Beyond the mill, the gardens remained out of reach. Mistress Aven came to look at the new line and asked whether it would get her home. Maren said not yet.

“Then don’t call it finished.”

“We aren’t.”

The woman stood beside the post until her husband persuaded her to come away. Before leaving she gave Kelva a small handful of seeds to keep separately from her own, in case something happened to the packet.

Kelva put them in a dry jar.

The first watch under the smaller pattern belonged to one of the sisters from the drying room and the keeper whose household had retained its old maintenance.

They did not get on well at first. He distrusted the new supports; she distrusted his habit of referring to them as the girl’s work while Nereth was standing there explaining a check. When he repeated a question already answered, the sister asked whether he wanted it sung.

“Yes,” he said. “Then I might remember it.”

Nereth gave a brief surprised laugh. The sister flushed and fetched the lamp.

Riarhin went out at dusk because Kelva had asked whether she could walk as far as the post and report who had brought food. It was an errand Kelva could have done herself. Riarhin accepted it gladly.

The watch place was a little shelter against the mill’s lower wall, with one side open to the lane. Someone had found a board for the bench and laid a sack over the worst splinter. The keeper sat at one end. The sister stood, peering out beneath the shelter roof.

“Soup?” Riarhin asked.

“Twice,” the keeper said.

“Bread?”

“Enough to feed the next watch.”

“Good.”

She sat for a while. The mill thudded behind them. Across the lane a shutter closed, opened again, and stayed open while someone shook crumbs from a cloth. The ordinary noise made the dark beyond the fittings seem farther away than it was.

The sister checked the lowest mark. Her lamp showed its small scratch clearly. She came back, sat down, and immediately rose again at a sound in the lane.

A cat crossed between the supports. Both keepers watched it. It sat to wash one foot precisely where Nereth had told them not to stand.

“Does it know?” the sister asked.

“It knows where we aren’t,” Riarhin said.

The animal looked at them with mild contempt and continued washing.

Later the wind brought a loose flap of washing against the upper support. The sister was up before the keeper, lamp in one hand, reaching with the other. He caught her sleeve.

“First look.”

“It’s cloth.”

“First look.”

She stopped. The mark had shifted because the support itself had leaned under the pull. There was scarcely enough movement to see from the bench. She called for Nereth and Maren rather than straightening it. Riarhin stayed at the post while the keeper went indoors.

The girl came without her notebook. She looked, asked the sister to describe what had happened, and had her lift the cloth while Maren attended the lower fitting. They reset the support together. The movement was small and slow. No one praised the watch for calling. Maren made the sister repeat which alteration she had seen, so the next watch could hear it too.

After they had gone, the sister sat on the bench with her knees together.

“I nearly did it.”

“Yes,” the keeper said.

“You could have said it less like that.”

“How?”

She thought, gave up, and took one of the bread pieces from its wrapping. After a moment she offered him the other half.

Riarhin returned to the kitchen. Kelva asked whether they needed more food. She said they had plenty, but another covering for the open side would help if it rained.

“Would it catch the post?” Kelva asked.

“We’ll put it farther in.”

They found a sheet which was too worn for a bed and sound enough to stop a draught. Riarhin carried it back with two pegs and waited while the keepers chose where it should hang.

Riarhin found Nereth in the little room above the mill that evening.

The journal was downstairs under a seal. Maren had the key to one fastening, another keeper the key to the second. Nereth had a clean sheet of paper and was trying to redraw a sequence from memory. She covered it when Riarhin entered.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t going to use it.”

“Then let Maren see it.”

The girl moved her hand reluctantly.

Riarhin set a bowl beside her. Kelva had sent it.

“She wouldn’t come up?” Nereth asked.

“She’s measuring grain.”

“She thinks I did it for nothing.”

“No.”

“She won’t say it was necessary.”

Riarhin looked at the clean paper, at the little smudge where Nereth’s bound wrist had dragged through the ink.

“It wasn’t necessary to do it that way.”

Nereth’s eyes filled. Riarhin sat on the edge of the other stool. She did not take the words back.

After a while the girl picked up the spoon.

Below them the mill worked late. Sivrik had obtained time to dry another portion of the grain before delivery, at a cost he would not explain while Kelva was in the room. The escort remained at the export stores. Riarhin could see its lamps through the window, steady above the wet lane.

Nereth ate half the bowl and pushed it away. Riarhin stayed until she took it up again.

Chapter 38 — The farewell ends

They could not find the woman Asket wanted.

For two mornings they looked in the wrong place.

Esvin had begun with what Haldren remembered of the name, the keeping marks on the token, and a description of the lower crossing. The present lower crossing had a keeper’s house, three old books, and a woman who knew the contents of all three well enough to tell them when they were wasting their time. She let them look anyway. Esvin turned the pages carefully, searching among names whose spelling altered from one entry to the next.

Riarhin found herself looking for the carpenter’s hand beside hers. He had heard the broken name. He would have said which sound she had misunderstood, complained that the room was too dark, asked for a better seat. The chair opposite remained empty until the keeper put a basket on it.

By the second afternoon they had found several names which might have been the right one and no reason to choose among them.

“Read the place again,” the woman said.

Esvin repeated Asket’s words as closely as he could. Riarhin corrected one relation between the crossing and the water. The spirit had said below, not downriver. She could hear it exactly. They had made downriver of it because they were looking at a river.

The keeper went to the door and called her son.

He was repairing a fence. He came reluctantly, carrying the short piece he had been fitting, and listened while his mother made Esvin repeat the account. Then he looked out toward the raised road.

“The old lower water?”

“Is there another?” Riarhin asked.

“There’s no water there now. Not except after rain.”

They went to look.

The old crossing lay beneath a field.

Riarhin could see the depression when the keeper’s son pointed it out: a shallow change in the land, marked by a different colour of growth. Beyond it stood a pair of stones so low she would have taken them for field clearance. One carried a worn groove across its top.

“The rope went there,” he said. “That’s what my grandfather said. They took the metal out when the road moved.”

Esvin knelt beside the stone. He set the token on top, without singing or asking anything of it. The keeping marks faced the groove.

Riarhin looked across the field. It was difficult to imagine a boat moving where the grain now grew. She had seen water leave land before, and land given to water, but here the change was old enough that even the lie of the crossing required someone to show her.

“Where would the houses have been?”

The man indicated the rise beyond.

“Most went into the road. You’ll find old stone in the lower drains.”

Esvin picked up the token again. Riarhin thought he would ask to examine the drains. Instead he asked whether any account of the old crossing survived.

“My mother will know.”

“She sent us here.”

“She wanted to know if I knew what I was talking about.”

He carried his piece of fence back with them, having kept it under one arm throughout. At the keeper’s house his mother listened to the report and said she had thought so.

The record they needed had been sent to the chapel years before, when damp entered the room where it was kept. Esvin looked at Riarhin. She began to laugh, quietly and without much pleasure, then could not quite stop.

“Tomorrow,” she said.

The keeper gave them bread before they left. It was still warm. Riarhin ate hers on the road and found she was less angry by the time they reached the mill.

At the chapel the old account was wrapped in a cover labelled as something else.

The white-scarfed woman found it after Esvin described the binding. She had been using the box beneath it to keep spare lamp cups. She made them move the cups first, one at a time, because the floor was uneven and she did not trust either of them not to step on one.

The book smelled of old damp. Its earliest leaves had stuck together at the corners. Esvin used a thin slip of wood to separate only those which would part without tearing. Riarhin held the cover flat.

There was the crossing. There were the keeping marks. A later hand had copied an earlier gift of boat shares, naming people who had long ceased to trouble anyone over their portion of the work. Among them lay the name Haldren had tried to retain.

Tovra, of the lower water.

Esvin put his finger beside it without touching the page. Riarhin read the surrounding lines aloud. The boat had ceased to exist long before the present chapel was built. The houses named alongside it had become the raised road. No living woman could be waiting for the message they carried.

Esvin read the line several times.

“Her people might still be here,” the keeper said.

“They might.”

“We could ask.”

“We can ask what they know. We aren’t giving them his waiting.”

The keeper looked at him, then nodded.

Riarhin sat nearby, sorting the copied notes from the fortress. Some were almost illegible. Water had entered Maren’s notebook and carried one page into another. The account of the boat survived better because Haldren had repeated it while she wrote, correcting a word she had mistaken for another.

She could still hear the correction.

Esvin placed the token beside the old record.

“I promised to seek her.”

“You did.”

He looked as though he wanted Riarhin to say more. She had nothing to add which would not turn the promise into something easier than it had been.

He copied the date and the name.

Someone at the chapel thought they knew a family descended from the woman.

The suggestion came while Riarhin was putting the lamp cups back. Esvin listened. He asked what was known, as he had promised, and received a sequence of marriages which lost its certainty halfway through. The speaker admitted that one of the names might belong to a different branch.

“They use it still. Tovra. You could take the message there.”

Esvin looked at the copied page.

“I could tell them what we found.”

“Wouldn’t that be the same?”

“No.”

He said it gently. The speaker looked disappointed, having offered a way to finish something which appeared unfinished.

Riarhin carried the last cup to its shelf. She had known families who would welcome any fragment of an ancestor, and others who would be burdened by an obligation before knowing what it was. She did not know this family. Neither did Esvin.

In the yard he sat with the token in his hand.

“I could ask Asket whether he wanted them to know.”

“You could.”

He glanced at her, surprised by the lack of objection.

“Would you?”

“No.”

“Why?”

“He asked for someone. We looked.”

Esvin turned the metal piece over. The cord hole had worn thin on one side. Asket had carried it long enough for a small bodily habit to change the metal, and that habit was now easier to see than anything which could identify his face.

“He’ll ask again.”

“Perhaps.”

“I may want him to.”

Riarhin sat beside him. Through the open door she could hear the white-scarfed woman returning the last of the cups to the box. One struck another with a bright little sound.

They remained until the singer wrapped the token and put it away.

The remains they had brought back were few.

A small bundle recovered near the boat room had been identified only by its place, not by a name. Maren had marked where the rest lay, as well as she could. No one was sent into the changed wood merely to make the account look complete. The reed-cutter received word of the routes they had searched and no invented certainty about his daughter. He sent back the message that he wanted the map anyway.

Riarhin made him a copy.

For Yrdis there was the account, the token, and the promise to leave the tool beside the boat. For Asket there was the message they had failed to deliver to its intended listener. Esvin wrote the words plainly, without adding a descendant’s name beneath them.

They prepared the place together.

No body lay beneath the cloth set out for Yrdis. Esvin made that clear to those who asked. Some remains had been brought from near the boat; they could not name them. The rest were still in the wood. He would say where they had been found and what had been left, and those were different things from saying who lay beneath the covering.

One of the keepers wanted him to use the old title. More people would know whom he meant.

“Use it at the beginning,” Riarhin said. “Then her name.”

Esvin looked at her.

“Can you say it for me?”

She did. He repeated it, stopped, and tried again. At the fortress he had been able to retain the sound while she stood before them. Here the old difficulty remained in places. He had written the name on a page and found later that he could not decide which mark he meant to follow.

Riarhin spoke it again. She remembered the sister’s quoted impatience, not a priest’s careful address. She let that shape reach her voice.

Esvin repeated it more easily.

“There,” she said.

“Stay close when I come to it.”

“I will.”

They practised no more. The benches had begun to fill. Kelva brought water, and Eddis set it where Esvin could reach it without leaving the cloth. Maren had copied the places onto a separate sheet in case the singer lost his way in the damaged notes. Riarhin put Haldren’s description of the mallet beside it, then moved it back into her coat. She could supply that part herself if she was needed.

A sailor from one of the northern vessels had come early to hear the song.
