He knew the older version. While the benches were being set, he hummed part of it and asked Esvin whether there would be a chorus. The singer told him where to join. When he reached the changed line, the sailor stopped.

“That isn’t it.”

“It’s the one I’m singing.”

“My father sang the other.”

“So did many people.”

The sailor looked at the little bundle on the cloth and lowered his voice. Riarhin had seen the same care from people approaching an injured person without knowing what injury they had sustained.

“Are you saying he had it wrong?”

Esvin drew the folded account towards him. His hand hovered over it, then moved away. He did not offer to settle the father’s memory with a document.

“I spoke with her,” he said.

The sailor looked at Riarhin. She nodded.

“Did she know the song?”

“She recognised enough to refuse a verse.”

“Which?”

Esvin spoke the line quietly. The sailor mouthed its beginning, following a familiar rhythm to the place where the words would have gone on without difficulty. Then he sat down on the front bench.

“My father never said she was still there.”

“No.”

“Did he know?”

Esvin said he could not tell him that. The man rubbed his palms together and watched the remaining benches being brought in.

When Kelva passed with the water, he rose to take one end of the last bench. He put it down facing the cloth and remained standing behind it. Riarhin wondered whether he would sing when the time came. Esvin caught her eye and shook his head very slightly. She left the man to choose.

At the farewell he sang the crossing first.

He had altered the song. Not enough to make it new to those who knew it, but enough that the familiar turn no longer led to the old claim of victory. The woman went back. The boat remained with someone else. Those who could steer took the wounded onward. The last journey did not finish.

Riarhin stood with her hands folded because she did not know what else to do with them.

There were not many listeners. Eddis, Maren, Kelva, two keepers, the white-scarfed woman from the chapel, and a few people who had heard there would be a song for the dead. Nereth remained under supervision at the mill. Esvin had asked whether she wished the account read to her afterward. She had said yes.

Near the end his voice faltered.

Riarhin saw him look at the empty place beside the bell. His lips moved as though beginning a question. The pause stretched long enough for someone at the back to shift their feet.

Then he sang the final line.

He let it end.

Afterward they carried benches.

The chapel needed its ordinary space back. Kelva took one end of a long bench and ordered Riarhin to take the other. Eddis complained that the thing had been made too heavy for its purpose. Maren told him it had been made to hold people, including him. He said it was doing so in reverse.

Esvin remained beside the bell until the white-scarfed woman touched his arm. He looked startled, then helped her fold the cloth.

Outside, someone had begun a work song while loading the smaller stools onto a cart. It was an indecent song if sung all the way through, which Kelva seemed determined it would not be. Esvin joined at the wrong verse.

Eddis stopped lifting.

“We haven’t got to her husband yet.”

“Have we not?”

“No.”

“Then I’ve spoiled it.”

“You’ve improved it. He takes too long.”

Esvin laughed. The sound was rough from singing. He began again where the others were, and Kelva told all of them that if they reached the third verse in front of Tessa she would leave the benches in the rain.

They reached only the second.

Riarhin carried the last stool to the cart. When she turned back, Esvin was listening to something beyond the chapel door. The laughter had gone from his face, though not the warmth of it.

She waited beside him until he turned away himself.

Chapter 39 — The Weeping Gate

The western gate wept down its inner face.

Water gathered beneath the crosspiece although the wood above it was dry. It ran in thin lines along the grain, darkening the salt laid at the posts. Maren sent for Riarhin before trying to renew it.

Eddis was already there when she arrived. He stood outside the marked approach with his pole across the entrance, preventing a young keeper from stepping nearer.

“I can see it,” the keeper said.

“So can I.”

“Then let me look.”

“You are looking.”

Beyond the gate stood a person in a coat which seemed to have been made from the same grey light as the mist. Riarhin could see the posts through it in some places and not in others. Its face was turned slightly aside, as though attending to a conversation elsewhere.

Maren laid fresh salt against the iron and spoke the exclusion she had prepared.

The figure thinned. For a moment Riarhin saw the whole gate through it. Then it was gone.

The young keeper let out a breath.

A voice answered from the drainage cut beside his feet.

“You have made this entrance inconvenient.”

Eddis pulled him back by the coat.

Riarhin did not answer at first.

The voice continued without requiring it.

“The farther work remains. You have seen that.”

Maren moved the iron piece to cover the drainage lip. The water changed direction but did not stop.

“There is a shorter way.”

Beyond the gate, where the lane should have run toward the abandoned houses, a dark step appeared. Then another. Riarhin recognised the high lip, the crack across the fourth tread. Above it a pale marker showed against a wall.

The young keeper leaned forward again.

“That’s ours.”

Maren looked sharply at him.

“The mark. From the tower. I cut it.”

Riarhin could see the cut. It lay on the far side of the visible stair, an answering mark where no road should have brought it so close.

“Don’t cross,” she said.

“No need,” the voice answered.

She ignored it. Cadan had come up beside her. He looked at the stair with an attention which made her wish he had not arrived until it was gone.

“Does it join your passage?” she asked.

“The first turn is the same.”

“And beyond?”

“I cannot see.”

Cadan wanted a lamp lowered to the first turn.

Maren refused to carry flame across the line. He said it need not cross; the light might reach far enough from this side. She considered, then gave him the shuttered lantern. Riarhin stood beside him while he turned it until a thin band fell across the visible steps.

The light showed a shallow hollow in the wall.

He had left the carrier’s frame there during the first recovery. A pale splinter remained along its edge. Riarhin could not see enough to be certain it was wood. Cadan could. He described the split before she moved to an angle where it became visible.

It was a way. That was the trouble.

“Could you bring something back from there?” she asked.

“If it stays as it is.”

The voice from the drainage cut made no promise. It waited while they looked, which Riarhin found worse than persuasion.

Maren put a short testing rod beside the salt and asked the young keeper for the measuring frame. He brought it forward, keeping its feet on the dry ground. Together they aligned the near cut with the far mark. Nothing crossed. No hand reached beyond the iron.

Eddis went to the drainage channel. Riarhin heard him test it with the pole, then do so again a little farther back.

“How far is the mark?” Maren asked Cadan.

“From here, I cannot tell.”

“The frame gives the angle.”

“It doesn’t give the ground between.”

The young keeper adjusted one foot of the frame so it stood level. Riarhin watched him. He remained well behind the threshold. She let him finish.

Maren looked through the fitted opening and described what she saw. The far mark stood where it should relative to the stair, but the old lane beyond appeared in a place the keeper could not fit to it. She asked Riarhin to look.

Riarhin bent. Through the narrow view, she could see the crack across the fourth step with extraordinary clarity. She could also see a patch of pale wall which Cadan said lay beyond the division, farther than they should have been able to look from here.

“If that’s the answering point,” she said, “we could—”

She stopped. She had already begun placing Maren beside it, Nereth at the near return, Cadan carrying a message between. She had no reason to believe the people would fit where the lines in her mind did.

Cadan lowered the lantern shutter a little.

“The left edge has changed.”

Maren looked again. The young keeper checked the feet of the frame, frowning because they had not slipped.

There was still nothing which required them to put a foot over the line. Riarhin stayed where she was and tried to find the change in the mark itself.

Maren asked the young keeper to describe his mark. He did, including a mistake in the lower cut which was visible even from where they stood. It was not a vague likeness. Riarhin felt the attraction of it: the long journey shortened, the damaged place within reach while the people who understood its making were still here.

She crouched outside the salt and studied the angle.

Eddis said her name.

She looked up.

The young keeper had moved only a pace. Riarhin had seen him do it and had not stopped him because he remained on their side of the iron. Now the iron lay behind his heel.

It had not moved. The approach had.

Riarhin caught his belt as the ground beneath him became a stair.

He shouted and tried to turn. That made it worse. His foot went down where there had been level earth, dragging both of them forward. Riarhin dropped to one knee and took his weight across her hip. Her healing hands opened under the strain.

“Don’t stand,” she told him.

He kept trying.

Eddis put the pole across them, braced between the posts. Riarhin hooked an elbow over it. Cadan caught the keeper’s other arm. Together they hauled him backward over the lip which now occupied the gate’s threshold.

Maren broke the interrupted line.

The visible stair narrowed. The far marker came toward them so quickly that Riarhin flinched, then vanished. Something metal struck the ground beyond the salt. The measuring frame the keeper had carried remained on the other side, one leg visible for a moment before the lane returned around it.

The keeper lay on his back, gasping.

Riarhin stayed on her knees until she was sure the earth beneath them was earth again.

“No one agreed to anything,” he said.

“No,” Maren answered.

She was already rebuilding the exclusion.

They did not retrieve the frame.

It had belonged to the upper keeper’s house and would be difficult to replace. Maren entered its loss with visible irritation. The young man apologised until she told him to stop wasting her concentration.

The gate dried slowly. Ordinary water resumed its course beneath the drainage lip. Eddis checked it from both sides while Cadan stood where the foreign stair had appeared.

“Davin?” Riarhin asked quietly.

Cadan shook his head.

“No sign.”

The voice had offered them a route, not a prisoner. She was glad of that much and disliked being glad of anything about it.

When the repairs were finished, Maren asked whether Riarhin meant to look for another approach.

“Yes.”

“You’ve lost the quick way.”

“I don’t think we ever had it.”

Eddis looked up from the channel.

“It was quick,” he said. “That part was true.”

Riarhin watched the water run past his pole. Then she helped carry the spare iron back to the keeper’s house, keeping to the middle of the old lane.

Chapter 40 — The other side of the mark

They laid the stone from the foreign stair beside the copied observations.

Nereth sat between Maren and the keeper who held the second key. The journal was open before her. Riarhin had brought the drawing she made after the first test, creased and watermarked now, with the crack across the fourth step still clear. Cadan added his account of the turn and the division beyond it. Esvin brought the old memorial cup from the chapel cupboard, not because it proved a road existed but because the band around its base repeated the same interrupted pattern.

Nereth had already copied the passage twice.

On the first sheet she had kept the spacing of the old marks. On the second she had arranged them as she understood the instructions. Riarhin read both, then put the second aside.

“That one is easier,” the girl said.

“Yes.”

“You think it’s wrong.”

“I think I can follow it without knowing where you made a choice.”

Nereth looked ready to argue, then drew the first sheet closer. Her wrist had improved enough to write. She still kept it stiff when she turned a page.

Riarhin indicated the word she had first taken for a measure of cold.

“What did you do with this?”

“Delay.”

“Why?”

“The repetition. It gets longer when that changes.”

“Longer in time?”

Nereth opened her mouth and stopped. She looked at the drawing of the stair.

“I thought so.”

Riarhin could remember the same word beside a woven pattern Ghere had once put into her hands. She had turned it the wrong way, looking for a length where he meant a relation between two turns. The memory supplied a possible question. It did not translate the page for her.

She drew two marks with a space between them.

“Suppose this belongs to the interval between places.”

Nereth leaned forward.

“Then this wouldn’t mean return.”

“What else?”

“Answering.”

Maren moved the stone nearer the first drawing.

“Does that change what you stopped?”

“No,” Nereth said quickly.

The keeper waited.

The girl read the line again, this time touching each group with the end of her pen.

“No. I can show you why. The stopping part uses the later mark. The one I made here.”

She turned to her own notes. Riarhin recognised the folded page she had found in the sleeping room. One corner had torn during the escape. Maren had pasted it onto another sheet, preserving the surviving words even where they made Nereth look least careful.

They followed the sequence together. Some of it they had already tested. Some depended on observations made in the fortress which could not now be repeated safely. Maren marked the difference rather than allowing the line to run smoothly across it.

At the end she put down the pen.

“You stopped what was passing out from your side.”

“Yes.”

“What was already there?”

Nereth looked at the stone fragment.

“I don’t know.”

Esvin fetched the cup himself.

The chapel keeper had wrapped it in two cloths and told him it was not to be placed where an elbow would strike it. He repeated the instruction before setting it on the table. Kelva moved the bread basket to make room.

The repeated border on the cup was more elaborate than the worn pattern on Cadan’s stone. Some of the turns ran the other way. Nereth wanted to turn the cup upside down to compare them. Esvin asked her to use both hands.

“It’s been dropped before,” he said. “It wasn’t improved.”

She held it carefully. Riarhin looked at the figures between the bands, too worn to distinguish easily. One seemed to carry a vessel. Another might have been opening a door or holding it shut.

“Where was it made?”

“The keeper can’t tell me. The account says where it was carried.”

“Obishaal?”

“Yes. That’s the name in the later hand.”

“Later than what?”

“The cup’s arrival. Earlier than the present cover. That’s all I can give you.”

Riarhin read the copied account. It concerned someone carrying an object from a departure, not a traveller setting down directions for those who followed. It confirmed the name associated with the border without giving them a road.

Cadan watched from the end of the bench.

“Have you been there?” she asked him.

“To places called by that name.”

“Was this one of them?”

“I didn’t recognise the turn.”

“You recognised something.”

He nodded.

“The way it continued when it should have ended.”

Nereth looked up sharply, wanting to ask about that. Riarhin raised a hand, not to stop the question but to keep it until they had finished the one before it.

“Can you find the place where you went?”

“From an approach that admits it, perhaps.”

“Not by going back through the forest.”

“The way I used may be there. It may admit something else.”

Maren took the cup from Nereth and put it upright again. It rocked slightly on its old base. She folded a scrap of paper beneath one edge, making it still before she returned to the drawings.

They worked until Kelva told them to eat or move the papers somewhere else. Riarhin had forgotten the time. Her stomach hurt when she smelled the bread.

At last they had enough.

Obishaal. An answering structure, approached by the high-sided stair. A connection older than Nereth’s work and made wider by it. The foreign stone had crossed into the local room; the local working had changed the farther approach. Removing the later connection had not restored either to its earlier condition.

Nereth showed them the stopping sequence again. Maren had already tested it under supervision. Nothing in the independent shelter depended on the severed extension.

“Could you reverse it from there?” Riarhin asked.

“I don’t know,” Nereth said.

“Could you?” she asked Cadan.

He shook his head.

“I can pass some places. I cannot make them as they were.”

“What would you need to learn?”

“What is holding the other end now.”

They looked at the drawing.

No one offered a useful name for that.

Hesvar’s packet arrived while they were still at the table.

The messenger brought oil as well, so Kelva admitted him before asking whom he wanted. He put the packet beside Riarhin’s elbow and said the reply had come through the factor she named before entering the wood. There was a small additional charge because it had been forwarded twice.

Riarhin paid it. She remembered leaving the inquiry, a few lines asking whether Aurine had heard of passages which continued answering after their local working was stopped. At the time she had expected a trading rumour, a name worth asking after, perhaps nothing.

Aurine’s handwriting covered three sheets.

The first sheet was almost ordinary.

Aurine disliked the address Riarhin had used. The factor had moved, the replacement had asked intrusive questions, and the packet had reached her because a woman at the next house remembered how to recognise the intended recipient from something less useless than a name. She had underlined useless. Riarhin could hear the emphasis.

There was a complaint about rain, a passage concerning an appointment which had not occurred, and a question about whether the northern roads had been as unpleasant as reported. Riarhin read those lines twice before finding the answer to her own inquiry on the second sheet.

I have not seen the stair you describe. Do not let anyone make me its authority because I answered your letter.

Riarhin smiled despite herself.

Aurine had been working among people whose bodies continued to change after the circumstances which began the changes had been removed. She described a woman with an altered hand who could no longer bear a closed glove. The healer first consulted had wanted to bind the hand into its earlier shape. The woman had refused. Another practitioner had helped her find a covering which allowed the changed joints to move.

She works with it. She is impatient with everyone who asks whether she can bear to look at it. She looks at it whenever she uses it. At present she wants a needle case that will open without catching beneath the thumb.

Below that was a different account, of someone who had accepted a further alteration and could no longer do a thing they had wished to preserve. Aurine had not made the examples agree. Riarhin could feel the effort behind that restraint more clearly than she might have felt a declaration of principle.

The second man was told what might happen. He understood a part of it. The person explaining also understood only a part. I was present for the explanation and did not know which part either lacked until afterward.

Riarhin lowered the page.

Across the table Maren was correcting a copied mark. Nereth watched the pen move, ready to object if the correction lost something she believed necessary. Their heads were close together. Neither looked up.

Riarhin took the third sheet.

Farther down was the name Isoka.

Aurine had followed that avenue through people she had treated, people who treated her, and a woman who had listened when she described a change rather than immediately deciding whether to reverse it. She named a practitioner who might receive a careful question and another who would charge heavily for pretending to know the answer. The directions were practical: whom to ask, where an answer could be left, which former address had become useless.

If you are coming to ask whether I would choose what has happened, ask me. Do not ask the man who offers to explain my condition to visitors. He has met me twice and understood less the second time.

There was a blot beneath the last line. Riarhin could not tell whether Aurine had paused there or whether the pen had merely needed wiping.

Riarhin read the sheets twice.

The letter did not tell her to go anywhere. It supplied an address and the warning that the person might have moved by the time she arrived. Aurine added that she was tired of hearing what she had become discussed only by people who had no intention of asking her.

Riarhin folded the letter and sat with it in her hand.

Maren looked up from the journal.

“Useful?”

“Possibly.”

“Good possibly or bad possibly?”

“I’ll have to ask.”

Riarhin carried the letter upstairs before answering.

The room she had first slept in had been given to another household while she was away. Her things occupied a smaller space now, beneath the slope of the roof, with a view of the mill’s rear yard instead of the lane. She preferred it. People coming to the front door did not look up and find her before she had decided whether she wanted to be found.

She laid the three sheets on the blanket.

Aurine had worked in houses where Riarhin had employed her for purposes no one admitted at the front door. She remembered the woman’s hands preparing a cup, cleaning something which had to leave no trace, doing useful work for people she might later help injure. She had trusted those hands and distrusted the messages Aurine sent elsewhere. Both memories remained plain. Time had not made them simpler.

From below came the sound of Kelva objecting to the way someone had stacked bowls. Riarhin waited for the reply, heard Sivrik say he had not stacked them, and smiled without intending to.

She read the first sheet again. Rain, the missed appointment, the badly addressed letter. It was easy to go past those lines toward the useful information. She made herself read them at the pace in which Aurine might have spoken them.

Then she fetched paper.

Her reply took longer than the first letter.

She described the foreign stair, the stopped sequence, the continued effect. She did not ask whether transformation was a cure. She asked who could inspect a changed answering point without using a living person to find out what it did. She asked what evidence would be worth bringing and what should be left where it was.

Then she wrote a line to Aurine herself.

She crossed it out. It sounded like forgiveness offered because the woman had become useful again. That was not what she meant, and she did not know whether forgiveness was hers to offer.

On a clean part of the page she asked whether Aurine was well, and whether she wanted Riarhin to come.

She let those questions stand.

Before supper, Nereth came upstairs with three copies of a drawing.

Maren followed her and waited in the doorway. The girl spread the pages on Riarhin’s bed, careful not to cover Aurine’s letter. Each showed the same pattern, with one branch darkened. At the corner of the last page there was a small hole where the pen had pressed through.

“Which one did you see?” Nereth asked.

“At the gate?”

“At the seat. When the stair stayed.”

Riarhin sat beside the pages. She had expected instructions and found herself being asked to remember something she had deliberately kept looking at while the room fell apart. She could retrieve the view exactly. That did not make the three drawings easy to distinguish.

“This one is higher.”

“Yes. Was it?”

“The edge was behind your wrist. I couldn’t see where it joined.”

Nereth looked disappointed.

“I need to know.”

“So do I.”

Maren came nearer. She put one finger beside the uncertain branch without touching the ink.

“Then leave it open.”

“If it went here, it means—”

“Write that separately.”

The girl took up the pen she had brought. For several moments she did nothing with it. Then she wrote two short lines beneath the diagram and handed the page to Riarhin.

“If it was behind the wrist. If it was below.”

Riarhin read them. Some words meant things to Nereth which she could not understand without another explanation. She marked those and asked the girl to show what they referred to. They worked through the list while the supper sounds below grew louder.

“Will you be able to ask someone?” Nereth said.

“I hope so.”

“Someone who knows the older work?”

“If I find them.”

Nereth ran the blunt end of her pen along the bed seam. She had cleaned the ink from her fingers, but a blue line remained beside one nail.

“I could read the parts I copied.”

Maren stayed silent. Riarhin waited for the rest of the offer.

“If you needed me.”

“You’re needed here.”

“That’s what everyone says when they mean I’m to stay where they can see me.”

Maren drew a breath. Riarhin spoke before the keeper could reply.

“I don’t know where I’m going after the first road. I don’t know whether the person there will let us stay. I can’t offer you useful work in a place I haven’t reached.”

The girl looked towards Aurine’s sheets.

“But you’re going.”

“Yes.”

“Then ask her.”

“Aurine?”

“Whoever knows. Ask them this.”

She took the pen and added a question beneath the alternatives. It concerned something the ancient writer had assumed and which six years of experiment had not settled for her. Riarhin asked her to read it aloud. She did, stumbling once over a word she had changed halfway through writing.

“I’ll take it,” Riarhin said.

“Will you send the answer to me?”

She looked at Maren. The keeper’s expression became weary.

“To both of us,” Maren said. “You can be there when it’s read.”

Nereth began to object. Then she looked at the drawing with its incomplete branch and folded the other two copies together.

“Put my question in your letter,” she told Riarhin.

Riarhin did it while she watched. When the girl had gone downstairs, Maren remained beside the door.

“She could spend a year working out that one turn,” the keeper said.

“Will you let her?”

“With someone else in the room.”

They looked at the clean page left on the bed. Maren picked it up and gave it to Riarhin. Then Kelva called both of them by name, and they went down to eat.

Cadan brought the route in the evening.

Not a secret way. A sequence of carriers, ports, and roads by which they could reach someone who knew an approach to the Obishaal side. Some of the names were familiar to Riarhin. Others meant nothing. They would need to verify them as they went. The first passage south could be bought at the harbour. After that there was weather, money, and the possibility that the next person would tell them the road had closed.

Riarhin looked at the list.

“No walking straight into a dead country from the next valley.”

“No,” Cadan said.

“And no promise that your route will still admit us.”

“No.”

She folded the list.

“Good.”

He looked puzzled.

“I’d rather know which part is a guess.”

He considered that, then took the page back and marked three names.

“These.”

She almost laughed. Then she saw that he was serious and asked him to explain the second.

They went to the harbour the next morning to check the first two names.

The first belonged to a man who had died. His daughter kept the business and disliked being asked for him, although the name remained painted above the door. She had painted over the given name and left the family name in place. Riarhin saw the difference in colour only after the woman pointed it out.

“He sold passages,” Cadan said.

“So do I.”

“Then perhaps you can help us.”

She could. She had two ships going south within the week and a third whose master had made so many promises that she would not take money on his behalf until the sail was raised. Riarhin asked about the first two. One went nearer the place they wanted; the other left sooner, stopped at three ports, and offered space in a crowded afterhold.

The woman put a board between them and drew the coast with charcoal. It was a shipping coast: shelters, bars, mouths, places to buy water. Riarhin recognised some names and none of their proportions. A short distance on the board might require a day if the tide refused it. A longer one might be sailed while they slept.

“Which is safer?” she asked.

The woman looked at her hands, their broken skin newly closed.

“From what?”

“Weather. A captain who drinks. A boat that should have been mended.”

“That rules out the one I won’t sell you. The others can both drown you. They try not to.”

She gave the names of the masters and told them when to return with their money. No deposit would hold a place after loading began. No promise included an arrival day. Riarhin found the terms almost restful after the fortress.

Outside, Cadan asked whether she preferred the closer landing.

“The ship leaves later.”

“Yes.”

“Would we gain the time back?”

“I don’t know the road from the nearer harbour.”

They turned back. The woman looked up from her accounts with an expression suggesting that she had expected this. She told them the road from the nearer harbour had a bridge under repair and that people were being put across in boats, one cart at a time. She had heard it from a carrier who complained more about waiting than paying. Riarhin thanked her.

They took the earlier ship.

Cadan’s second name belonged to a warehouse clerk who had once known the man at the inland approach. The clerk was at dinner. They waited beside bales of wool while a girl used a hooked tool to pull loose fibres out of the scales.

Riarhin watched Cadan watching the work. He seemed able to give it his whole attention. She could still feel the knife in her hand beneath the gallery, cutting cloth she could not see. The memory would arrive between one breath and the next, complete enough to obscure the scales.

“You didn’t tell me to leave him,” she said.

“Haldren?”

“Yes.”

“No.”

“Would you have?”

“If we could not bring him.”

She looked at him. His face held the same open consideration with which he had heard her question.

“And if I refused?”

“I would have asked what you intended.”

She almost laughed. It would have been an ugly sound. The girl at the scales glanced towards them and Riarhin lowered her voice.

“I didn’t know. I just knew we weren’t leaving him there.”

“We brought him.”

“Yes.”

The hooks clicked against the pan of the scale. A man came through the warehouse with a bale on his shoulder, calling for room. They moved apart to let him pass.

When they stood together again, Riarhin said, “I can’t do that with everyone.”

Cadan looked down at the dust on his boots. Some of it was still the pale powder from the stair, lodged above the welt despite washing.

“No.”

For a while neither spoke. Then he asked whether she had heard anything since they returned. She knew which kind of hearing he meant.

“Nothing I couldn’t place.”

He nodded.

“Would you hear it?” she asked. “If someone called me from there?”

“I might hear a voice. I would not necessarily know whom you heard.”

She remembered Esvin beside the wall, refusing to give her an answer he did not have. The resemblance made her look at Cadan more carefully.

“If I start following it, say my name.”

“I will.”

“From where you are. Don’t come after me without looking.”

He considered this, then said, “You should do the same.”

She nodded. The clerk emerged from the back room wiping his mouth and apologised for keeping them. Riarhin stood straighter before asking about the inland road.

The clerk’s information was two years old.

He made that clear before giving any of it. His former acquaintance had kept a house near a quarry road and received travellers recommended by certain families. He was not a priest, or had not called himself one. He knew approaches that were seasonally possible and others which cost more than the visitor usually meant to pay. The clerk had brought him oil once and declined an invitation to stay the night.

“Why?” Riarhin asked.

“He had no fire lit, and I was cold.”

Cadan asked about a mark carved above the door. The clerk drew half of it and could remember no more. Riarhin copied what he drew with the missing part left blank.

They came away with an address, a family to ask after if the address failed, and a warning that the road beyond the quarry was sometimes closed by the people living along it. The clerk did not know whether they would admit strangers now. He gave no reason to believe that Riarhin’s need would interest them.

At the mill she spread the two sets of directions beside the observations from the fortress. There were things she could pay for, things she could ask, and a long stretch of paper between the last known house and the foreign stair. She left that stretch empty.

Kelva brought her a plate and moved the papers out of the way before setting it down.

“How long are you staying?”

“Two nights. Perhaps three if the ship is delayed.”

“I’ll need the blanket back.”

Riarhin looked up. For an instant another quay, another boat, and Mab Tulley’s imperious hand were present so plainly that she smiled before she meant to.

“What?” Kelva asked.

“Nothing. You’ll have it.”

“Washed.”

“Yes.”

Kelva went back downstairs. Riarhin ate, looking at the empty stretch between the house and the stair without filling it.

Chapter 41 — The people who stay

Esvin refused before Riarhin asked him.

They were carrying washed cups from the chapel yard. He had taken too many, stacking them up his forearm until the top one threatened to fall. Riarhin reached for it.

“I can’t go with you,” he said.

She took two cups instead.

“I hadn’t asked.”

“You were considering it.”

“Yes.”

He nodded, satisfied at least by the admission.

“There are three farewells waiting, a woman who wants to know whether she can speak without her brother in the room, and a keeper who thinks a song will settle an argument over a roof.”

“Will it?”

“No. But he keeps asking.”

They put the cups away. Esvin rearranged one which Riarhin had set on the wrong shelf, then moved it back after discovering the shelf was already full.

“I also want to sleep in my own bed,” he said.

“That seems fair.”

“It seems wonderful.”

He smiled, and for a moment she saw the man who had mocked the grain collector in the mealhouse before any of this began.

At the door he touched her sleeve.

“If you hear them—”

“I know.”

“No. You know what I think they are. That wasn’t what I was going to say.”

She waited.

“Don’t be alone with it every time.”

She looked toward the lane. Two children were trying to persuade a reluctant dog through a gate. The dog had opinions about the matter.

“I’ll try.”

Esvin let go of her sleeve.

Cadan’s employer lived in a keeper’s house with a badly mended step.

The keeper had paid him to find the missing carriers and establish where the approach had gone. He listened to his account without interrupting until he reached Davin. Then he asked him to begin that part again.

He did.

Riarhin stood near the door. She had insisted on coming because she wanted to hear the obligation finished in front of the person who had commissioned him. Cadan had agreed without objection.

The keeper took the broken toggle. He turned it in his hand.

“So he may still be there.”

“Yes.”

“And you stopped looking.”

“At the agreed return.”

“Whose agreement?”

“Mine,” Riarhin said.

The keeper looked at her. Riarhin explained the interval, the evacuation, the way they had failed to locate him. She did not ask the keeper to approve.

At last the keeper put the toggle beside his account book.

“You found three.”

“Vessa, Brenn, and Leth,” Cadan said. “Osset was another.”

“I know who they are.”

Cadan bowed his head slightly.

The keeper counted out the remaining payment. Not all of what had been promised for four returned alive. More than the sum for locating the approach alone. Cadan accepted the amount he set down and asked whether he considered his present commission ended.

“Yes,” he said. “Ended. Not every part done.”

“No.”

Before they left the keeper’s house, Davin’s younger brother came to collect the account.

He had been waiting in the yard. Riarhin recognised him from the morning of their departure, though he had cut his hair and wore a different coat. He looked at Cadan first, then at the empty space beside him, with an effort so visible that she had to keep herself from speaking too soon.

The keeper handed him the toggle wrapped in a scrap of cloth.

“From his coat,” he said.

The young man opened it. He turned the broken wood over with a thumb, frowned, and held it closer to the window.

“I mended that.”

Cadan described where he had found it. The brother listened, asked him to say the distance from the turn again, and repeated the answer under his breath. He had brought a sheet of paper but no pen. The keeper lent one.

Riarhin stood while he wrote. Every sound in the house seemed intrusive: a woman moving a kettle in the next room, a dog scratching at the door, the keeper shifting his account book to give the young man more light.

“Leth said he went to find a way,” the brother said.

“Yes.”

“She didn’t see him go through this other turn.”

“No.”

“Then he might have gone somewhere else.”

Cadan said that was possible. Riarhin watched the young man write the word, then cross it out and write the whole sentence. He wanted to carry exactly what had been said. She was grateful to him for it and wished he would stop.

He looked up at her.

“You said to come back.”

“Yes.”

“Cadan wanted to go down again.”

“Yes.”

The brother put down the pen. His hand remained shaped around it for a moment after it was gone.

“I’d have asked him to.”

“I know.”

“You don’t. You weren’t asking for your brother.”

She felt the reply rise, the names she might use to show him that she understood loss, and let it go unsaid. Beyond the window the little dog had begun scratching with both forepaws. Someone opened the door to let it in.

Riarhin said, “No. I wasn’t.”

The young man looked down at the toggle. He folded it carefully so the broken edge would not catch the cloth.

“Will you take the description?” he asked. “If you’re going to look from somewhere else.”

“Yes.”

He began with the coat, then the shape of a scar on Davin’s chin, then the way one shoulder sat lower because he had carried the same side of a frame for years. The keeper gave Riarhin paper. She wrote while the brother spoke. Cadan corrected no part of it.

At the end the young man said Davin sometimes answered to a childhood name he had hated once he was grown. He hesitated before telling her. Riarhin wrote it on a separate fold, where a casual reader would not see it first.

The brother watched her put the paper away. He did not thank her. When he left, he took the toggle and the borrowed pen, returning a moment later to set the pen down with an apology which made the keeper reach for his arm. The young man moved beyond the touch and went out.

Riarhin remained by the table until Cadan asked whether she was ready.

“Not quite.”

He waited.

Outside, Riarhin asked what he understood by accompanying her.

“To reach the other approach. To discover what remains there.”

“And if I decide we cannot cross?”

“We do not cross.”

“If you are called?”

Cadan looked down the lane before answering.

“I tell you.”

She would have liked a stronger assurance. She was not certain a stronger assurance from him would have been worth having.

“Tell me before you go.”

“Yes.”

Eddis was repairing the ferry’s seat when she found him.

The woman who had kept it in his absence stood beside him, explaining that the seat had been loose before she took charge. He said he knew. She repeated it anyway. Riarhin waited until the woman had finished and left.

“She improved the landing,” he said.

“Will you tell her?”

“I did.”

“How?”

“I said it wasn’t bad.”

Riarhin sat on a coil while he fitted the pin. He tested the seat, frowned, and removed the pin again.

“Still seeing it?” she asked.

“Sometimes.”

“The causeway?”

“Other things too. Nothing useful.”

“Do you trust the ferry?”

“I trust the soundings. Most days.”

He set the pin in a different hole.

“This one doesn’t show me where it wishes it was.”

She stayed while he worked. When he had finished, he carried her across without asking for payment. On the far bank she held out the fare. He took it after a moment, then gave back half.

“You weren’t much trouble.”

She thanked him. He looked pleased and embarrassed and told her to step clear so he could push off.

Maren had begun teaching four people at once.

It made her impatient. She wanted them all to look at the same fitting at the same moment, and one always wanted to ask about something two movements earlier. Nereth, sitting nearby under supervision, answered before Maren could and was told to wait. Five minutes later Maren asked her to explain that same point because she had explained it better the first time.

Riarhin watched from the yard.

The girl saw her and came over when the lesson paused. The wrist had improved. She still guarded it when she moved.

“You’re leaving.”

“Yes.”

“Will you tell people what I did?”

“If they need to know.”

“All of it?”

“As much as I can honestly tell.”

Nereth looked toward the new line. It held a short stretch of lane and two houses beyond the mill. The rest remained work.

“I did make it hold.”

“Yes.”

“And I made it worse.”

Riarhin did not answer. The girl had not asked a question.

After a while Nereth said, “Kelva makes me eat with everyone.”

“Good.”

“It isn’t.”

“She’ll fetch you if you stay upstairs.”

Nereth gave her a look of such exhausted dislike that Riarhin smiled despite herself.

“I’ll leave you to it,” she said.

The girl almost smiled back. Then Maren called her, and she went.

Leth was still at the lodging house when Riarhin went to see her.

The message to her sister had gone by the right carrier. The woman who kept the addresses had checked the destination twice, annoyed that anyone expected her to use the old one. Riarhin had accepted the annoyance and paid for the message. Now Leth wanted to know whether she had made it sound worse than it was.

“I described what you told me.”

“She’ll come if she thinks I can’t manage.”

“Do you want her to come?”

“Yes.”

“Then perhaps she’ll come.”

Leth looked away. The answer had been too easy, but she had invited it. Riarhin set the little packet of cloth Kelva had sent on the table and took the chair Leth indicated.

The injured leg rested on a support made by someone at the landing. It had been adjusted twice. Leth could sit more comfortably now, and that made her less willing to tolerate everyone arranging the room around her. She told Riarhin to move the chair because it blocked her view of the window.

Riarhin moved it.

“Brenn’s gone home?”

“For now. His wife took him.”

“Good.”

“He’ll be back tomorrow.”

“I know. He’s promised to bring something I don’t want.”

“What?”

“Soup.”

“Tell him.”

“He needs to bring it.”

Riarhin sat without replying. Outside, someone was calling to a horse which had decided a barrel was an obstacle beyond its abilities.

Leth asked whether Davin’s family knew. Riarhin said his brother had received the account and the recovered fastening. There had been no new sighting.

“I keep thinking he was just beyond us,” the woman said. “One turn. He used to go ahead to see whether the load would pass. He always came back angry with us for waiting exactly where he’d told us.”

She looked toward the window.

“You’ll be going where he went?”

“I’ll be trying to reach the other side. I don’t know if it’s the same approach now.”

“If you find—”

She stopped. Riarhin waited until she began again.

“Don’t tell me you’ll bring him. Everyone wants to tell me something.”

“I won’t.”

“Tell him we waited. If you see him.”

“I can tell him that.”

Leth nodded. She pulled the covering higher over her knee, though the room was warm.

After a while she asked Riarhin to open the packet Kelva had sent. There were clean strips of linen and a small piece of soap wrapped separately so it would not scent the bread beneath. Leth laughed at the bread.

“She thinks no one feeds us.”

“She may be right.”

They ate some together. The horse outside finally passed the barrel, to a chorus of praise Riarhin thought excessive until Leth began laughing at it. The laughter hurt the woman’s leg. She pressed a hand against the support and carried on anyway.

Riarhin stayed until the light moved off the bed.

The letter to Coryd began with the names.

Riarhin wrote who had returned, who had not, and who remained uncertain. She wrote Haldren’s name carefully. She described the mill, Nereth, the small line which held, and the grain escort which had not gone away. She did not know how to describe Varnek Karn’s arrival without making it sound as though a patron had come to solve the matter. She settled for saying he had witnessed an agreement which they then had to keep.

She wrote that she was going south.

For a while she sat with the pen above the page, wanting to ask Coryd something and unable to decide which question belonged in a letter that might take weeks to arrive. At last she asked after the people at the table, the small matters she would have known without asking if she had stayed. She left room for an answer by giving the next address she expected to reach.

Then she sealed it.

Kelva took her first shared watch that night.

Riarhin sat with her on a bench beneath the little cover beside the mill. The air was cold but not bitter. Across the lane the fitting moved slightly whenever the wind came from the water. Kelva checked it each time until Riarhin could see her learning which motion mattered.

“You can go to bed,” the miller said.

“So can you, when Sivrik comes.”

“He’ll be late.”

“Probably.”

They sat.

After a while Kelva said, “I shouldn’t have hit her.”

Riarhin looked at her hands, folded around a warm cup.

“Have you told her?”

“Yes.”

“What did she say?”

“She said she deserved it.”

Kelva’s mouth tightened. She looked away toward the mill door.

“I didn’t know what to do with that.”

Riarhin had no answer which she trusted. She took the cup when Kelva offered it and drank. It was only hot water with a little leaf in it, but it warmed her hands.

Sivrik arrived late, carrying bread and cheese as though that might improve his reception. It did, a little. Riarhin stayed until they had finished eating, then left the two of them arguing quietly about which lamp used too much oil.

Chapter 42 — Passage south

The ship south carried grain, wool, three families, and a man who insisted his box contained nothing breakable.

It broke while being loaded.

He accused the porter of dropping it. The porter pointed to the split board and asked why, if there was nothing breakable inside, the box was full of crockery. The man said the crockery was packing. Riarhin stepped around them with her own bundle and found Cadan already aboard, watching a sailor tie it down incorrectly.

“You can tell him,” she said.

“I did.”

“And?”

“He said he had been tying loads longer than I had been alive.”

Riarhin looked at Cadan. His face revealed nothing useful.

“What did you say?”

“Nothing.”

“Good.”

They waited until the sailor moved away, then retied the bundle without making a ceremony of it.

Remorse lay wrapped beside her pack. Toothpick and the shortened Last Blade remained where her hands expected them. She had cleaned both, checked the edges, and spent longer than necessary repairing the severed pack strap. The new piece did not match the old leather. Haldren would have noticed the stitching. She pulled once on the join and left it alone.

At the far end of the quay soldiers watched sacks move from a guarded store to another vessel. Sivrik stood among them with two papers in his hand, arguing about which lot was being loaded first. The lamps over the store had been extinguished for the morning, but the escort remained.

Their allotted space was beside the after rail, beneath an awning which could be drawn forward if the spray came over. A family already occupied most of it. The father had laid bedding along the bench as if measuring ownership by the length of a blanket. His wife saw Riarhin looking and moved a folded coat before he could object.

“Two of you?”

“Yes.”

“We were told there’d be room.”

“So were we.”

The woman smiled without much humour. Her younger child was asleep across both her knees; the older stood holding a small pair of shoes, apparently uncertain where the wearer had gone. Riarhin put her bundle beneath the bench and asked the boy to pass her the end of a loose rope.

He gave her the shoes instead.

“Those can go under here,” she said.

He watched her put them in the space beside the pack. Then he found the rope.

Cadan went to ask the mate which parts of the deck had to remain clear. He returned with an answer which required the family’s bedding to be moved. The father began to argue. His wife told him she would rather move now than be stepped on all night. Together they rolled it more tightly and made a place for the shoes.

Riarhin helped the woman lower the sleeping child onto the blanket. The little girl’s hand caught in her hair. She stood bent over while the mother freed the fingers one at a time.

“Sorry. She thinks everything’s hers.”

“She’ll suit the ship.”

The woman laughed. Her husband looked up, decided the remark was not addressed to him, and resumed tying the bedding.

They were travelling to a sister’s house, perhaps for the winter, perhaps longer. The woman told Riarhin this while rearranging the child’s coat beneath her head. She seemed to want to say it aloud without inviting advice about whether to go. Riarhin listened. The sister had a yard, a husband who objected to visitors, and three children of her own. She had written that there was room. The woman had decided to believe her.

“Have you people there?” she asked.

“Someone to write to. Farther on.”

“That’s something.”

“Yes.”

The boy retrieved the shoes and put them beneath the sleeping girl’s feet, sole to sole. His father told him they would roll away. He waited until his father looked elsewhere and put them back.

Cadan returned with water and the news that one of the barrels aboard was for washing, not drinking. He showed everyone which. The woman thanked him; her husband said he could read a mark when he saw one. Cadan inclined his head and went to sit beside Riarhin.

“Will you sleep?” he asked.

“Eventually.”

“Here?”

“Unless someone gives us a better place.”

He examined the narrow strip they had made beneath the awning, then arranged his pack so it would take none of her room. She moved it back halfway. He looked at her, accepted the adjustment, and sat with his knees folded.

The harbour bell rang. The sleeping child stirred but did not wake. Riarhin leaned against the rail and listened to the sailors calling from one end of the deck to the other, learning which voices belonged to which tasks.

Esvin came to see them off.

He brought no token. He had a small parcel of food from Kelva, which she had apparently told him was for both travellers and which he made them promise to divide fairly. Riarhin said Cadan could have the part he disliked. Esvin said that was not what he meant.

“Will you want a song?” he asked.

“What does it do?”

“Passes the time.”

She smiled.

“Then yes.”

He gave her the beginning of one she half knew. A road, a late traveller, a household which had eaten before he arrived. The traveller complained, the household complained back, and someone eventually found another bowl. There were verses enough for a long journey if she could remember them, and no harm in inventing the ones she could not.

He stopped before the shipmaster had to ask him to leave.

Riarhin embraced him. He seemed surprised for a moment, then held her carefully around the shoulders, avoiding the places which still hurt.

“Write if you can,” he said.

“I will.”

“Not only when something has gone wrong.”

She did not promise that. He knew her well enough to sigh at the omission and let it pass.

At the gangway he turned back once, looking as though he had forgotten something. Then he shook his head and went ashore.

They would not sail directly to an entrance into Obishaal.

There was a coastal passage first, then a road, then someone who might still know the approach Cadan had been told of. Riarhin had enough money for the first two stages and an estimate for the third. Aurine’s answer, if she chose to send one, might catch them at the second port. It might not. The folded observations lay in a sealed cover inside Riarhin’s pack, protected from salt water as well as she could manage.

She knew what she was going to ask when they reached the farther side: what now held the answering point, and whether it could be closed without tearing open the shelter they had left behind. She knew which stair she was looking for. Beyond that her list consisted mostly of questions.

Cadan sat beside her and checked the knot on the sealed cover without opening it.

“Do you think she will answer?” he asked.

“Aurine?”

“Yes.”

“I think she may tell me I asked badly.”

“Will that help?”

“Sometimes it has.”

He nodded as though considering this as a practical method.

The ship moved beneath them. A rope came aboard wet, followed by a shouted complaint from the quay. Riarhin looked up. The gap between hull and landing had widened enough to show the water running through it.

For a little while she watched the harbour diminish: the guarded stores, the ferry landing, the narrow lane toward Hesvar’s, the roofs beyond which the mill stood. She could not see the new line. She knew who would be checking it this morning.

Then a woman carrying a basket tried to lift it onto the bench beside her and nearly lost its lid.

Riarhin caught it.

“Thank you,” the woman said. “Can I put this here?”

Riarhin moved her pack. There was room if she turned it on its side and put Remorse behind her knees. She did so, tested the new strap once more, and helped settle the basket between them.

Inside, something small and indignant scratched at the wicker.

“Hen,” the woman explained.

“So I hear.”

The woman laughed, sat down, and began to tell her how difficult it had been to persuade the owner to sell that particular bird. Riarhin leaned back against the rail and listened while the ship carried them south.
